The Message of The Old Testament

MAUDE BEELEY JACOB With an Introduction by

PREFACE 

THIS book has been developed to serve as part of the student reading for high school students in a course called "The Old Testament and The Problems of Life." It has evolved out of several years attempt to study the Old Testament in such a way that the highest religious and character values might be forthcoming in the lives of students. These attempts have been based upon the conviction that the religious faith and character of students are of greater concern than the mere acquisition of historical facts or knowledge of Biblical content. Furthermore, it is believed that within the Bible there are inherent significant spiritual values for present living which can be discovered and can serve to affect life if these values are made the central concern of the study and if a life-centered approach is made to them. This assumes that the student himself becomes the most important factor in the teaching process with the study material serving as a means to accomplish the religious and moral ends desired. The content of the courses needs, therefore, to be prepared in light of student experiences, interests and needs. Coupled with this view of education in this course is also the realization that the Bible has through the ages, represented the best source of the revelation of God to man and if any person is to build up his spiritual life and faith he should possess an appreciative understanding of this source of divine guidance. 

The fall of 1937 marks the beginning of an organized attempt on the part of the L. D. S. Department

vi 

PREFACE 

of Education under the direction of Commissioner 

Frank L. West and Dr. M. Lynn Bennion, supervisor of Seminaries to direct the course in Old Testament toward a student-centered approach. A faculty committee was appointed to think through the problem, make recommendations for procedure, and take initial steps in the production of needed course materials. As a result of a planning period the entire committee itself settled down during the summer of 1938 to the task of organizing a course which would center around the most basic religious areas of living as being experienced by younger high school students and which would utilize the Old Testament as a means of realizing the desired religious goals. The problem showed itself to be one which would require several years to complete under a spirit of continual study, experimentation, and planning. A preliminary organization resulted from the summer's intensive study and was published as a teacher manual entitled "Old Testament and the Problems of Life." This was used for a year in a larger percentage of the Old Testament classes and critical responses from the teachers were obtained on each unit of instruction as organized. 

As a result of a year's use of the outline, and the collected evaluations from those who used it, a revision of the manual was prepared in 1939 under the direction of the same committee. The revision narrowed somewhat the area to be covered in each division of the work and simplified the material for practical purposes. It furthermore tied the organization more closely to Bible stories themselves. In this revision the teachers were encouraged to utilize other existing collections of Bible stories pending the development of material that would serve more directly the needs of the course as it was organized. It was recommended that the minimum needed student reading should consist of (1) a lifecentered treatment of Old Testament material and (2)

PREFACE 

vii a collection of the best current treatments of life problems under consideration.

It is to fulfill the first of these needs that this student text is directed. It was intended to serve as part of the basic reading material of younger high school students. It is the hope that the stories included and the manner in which they are presented will not only appeal to the reading interests of this age group but will stimulate an emotional appreciation of the spiritual values in the Old Testament that will become an integral part of the moral and religious life of these students. 

This treatment purports to be much more than just another story book on the Old Testament. An attempt has been made to organize the Bible stories around larger comprehensive life experiences and spiritual values while maintaining the historical continuity of the Old Testament episodes. There has been an attempt to direct the content of each story or episode toward these spiritual values in such a way as to avoid the traditional moralizing approach and yet open the way for an appreciation of the basic message of the Old Testament. The author has tried to accomplish this by weaving the message into the total story itself by the use of elaborations and dramatic touches to bring the message gracefully to the surface. The use of soliloquies, conversations and other types of reflective deliberations on the part of the characters involved have been employed in the attempt to achieve the desired. results.

The implications and message in most stories are subtly worked into the material and thus do not always appear prominently on the surface. The student needs, therefore, to read the stories carefully in order to appreciate fully the total implications of the message. In the classrooms the teacher might well direct the reading by the use of appropriate guides and motivation. 

The basic content of this work is introduced by a

viii 

PREFACE 

general treatment of the Bible for the purpose of leading the reader toward an appreciation and understanding of the nature of the Bible, the romance of its preservation and its cultural and religious influence on the world today. This is followed by an overview of the political history of the Hebrews. This is intended to assist the reader to view continually the historical setting of each episode presented in the body of the text and to present the religious philosophy of this history upon which the episodes are built.

For classroom work the parallel teacher manual, The Old Testament and the Problems of Life, published by the L. D. S. Department of Education in 

1942 is available. This is designed to lead the student and teacher toward a creative life-centered approach to the Old Testament. The study suggestions, problems, and activities therein are intended to raise the message itself more prominently to the surface. The suggestions in the manual assume that the unit of study shall begin with a life-centered experience and shall proceed to a consideration of Old Testament materials as a means of arriving at a more religious way of living. 

Vernon F. Larsen 

Salt Lake City, Utah 

September 1942.

OUR BIBLE An Introduction

WE OUR BIBLE 

E ARE about to study the message of the Old Testament as it is expressed in the stories of God's dealings with the Hebrews. These stories have been preserved for man's benefit through centuries of religious experiences of a chosen people. These people lived over vast time spans in the world's history but their message comes down to us confined in one small book known as the Old Testament. Yet it has not always been known as just one book. In reality it is a collection of many sacred books preserved and assembled during the centuries by the hands of far sighted, inspired men who valued the message, and perhaps envisioned the universal and lasting contribution which this message could make to the generations to come. Now we have this collection, bound together with a second set of sacred writings, the New Testament, and we call this combined collection the Bible.

The Bible is a record so universally known and so widely circulated among the one-third of the world's total population who profess to be Christians that perhaps not a single person among them remains unaffected by its message. Its teachings have entered directly or indirectly into some phase of the cultural life of all socalled Christian people whether they are actively religious or not. It could be shown, for example, that the Ten Commandments themselves have been a directive force in determining the basic laws of Christian communities and nations. Other parts of the Old Testament could be indicated as directive forces in determining many of our social manners and standards.

xvi 

OUR BIBLE 

To those persons who are closer to the Old Testament as a result of reading its pages, studying its message, or even hearing its stories, the book has had even a more direct influence. Probably most of us come under this grouping. Most persons in our own groups have either heard its stories or read its pages from early childhood. The vast number of copies that have been published and circulated have made it possible, not only for every family, but every individual to own a copy of this sacred volume at a cost as low as a few cents. 

Publishing companies all over the Christian world have made it possible for a universal distribution of the Bible. It has accurately been referred to as the world's best seller. While the ordinary distribution of modern best sellers rarely exceeds 100,000 copies, each year sees the publication of about 5,000,000 Bibles in the United States and about 26,000,000 in the world at large. It has been reported that the American Bible Society alone shows a distribution under their own management of about 300,000,000 copies. It would be hazardous to try to estimate the total number of copies of the Bible that have been published in the whole history of its circulation the world over. It would certainly be enormous.

WHAT THE BIBLE IS 

Though the contents of the Bible have been widely circulated under one cover and one title we must not think it just a single volume. It is rather a collection of many volumes bound together under one cover because of their common religious message. The title "Bible" itself comes from the Latin word Biblia which means "books." Biblia is the plural of Biblion for "Book." Jerome in the fourth century shows that he clearly thought of the Bible as a collection of books when he referred to it as the "Divine Library."

AN INTRODUCTION 

xvii Anyone desiring to know the exact number of volumes which make up this "Divine Library" has but to turn to the table of contents of any Bible. Here he will also find the exact title of each volume and the order in which the volumes appear in this collection.¹ 

A study of the number of books in the Bible reveals thirty-nine in the Old Testament and twenty-seven in the New Testament making sixty-six in all.' 2 These sixty-six books can be conveniently classified into their varying types and content for study purposes. Such classification helps us to better understand their literary form, their purpose and their religious message.

In the first place the Bible is divided into the Old and the New Testaments. The term "Testament" generally refers to a "covenant" or "agreement." It sometimes means an agreement of God with man for his salvation. The Old Testament is used to indicate that covenant which was made before the time of Jesus. 

The New Testament signifies that part of the Bible containing the covenant which God made with man after the coming of Jesus.

Each of these parts of the Bible can in turn be divided into major sub-divisions. The Old Testament consists of four such divisions: The Law (five books), The Historical Books (twelve books), Poetry (five books), and The Prophets (seventeen books). This last division can conveniently be broken down into the Major and the Minor Prophets as indicated in the chart. 

The New Testament consists of three major divisions: 

The Historical Books (five of them include the gospels 'Some persons have found it helpful to memorize the names of the books in their order. 'An easy way to remember these numbers is to begin with the number of letters in the word "Old" which, of course, is 3. This can be multiplied by itself to make 9 which when written together makes 39 books for the Old Testament. We can then multiply the 3 and 9 together to get the number in the New Testament, 27. It then remains but to add 27 and 39 to get the total number of Books in the entire Bible.

xviii 

OUR BIBLE 

and acts), the Epistles or Doctrinal Books (twenty-one books), and the Apocalyptic section which includes only the Book of Revelation.

Each of these divisions has its own distinctive form, content, purpose, and message. Yet there is, running through them all, a unity which binds them together and enables them to make the contribution to man which God intended. The nature of the content which seems to hold these divisions and books together is suggested as we consider some of the other terms that have been used in referring to the Bible. Among these are the following: "The Scriptures," "Holy Writ," "The Oracles," "The Word," and "The Covenant."

Each of these terms implies a sacredness of the records themselves.

The Bible is a collection of sacred writings which has been inspired of God. The writings themselves repeatedly carry this idea of God's part in them as seen in such statements as the following: "Surely the Lord God will do nothing but he revealeth his secret unto his servants the prophets."—

Amos 2:7. 

"Prophecy came not in old time by the will of man: but holy men of God spake as they were moved upon by the Holy Ghost."-II Peter 1:21. 

"God who at sundry times and in divers manners spake in times past unto the fathers by the prophets hath in these last days spoken unto us by his Son.- Hebrews 1:1-2. 

The Bible thus represents itself as having come at varying times under varying conditions and places unto, and through, God's servants for the spiritual guidance of mankind. It is a religious book. Its message is of God and His dealings with man. It presents the religious faith, interpretations, aspirations, and experiences of God's people through the centuries. The purpose for which it was written and preserved was a

AN INTRODUCTION 

xix religious one. Today the basic message and contribution center around the relationship of God and man. 

Though the writings therein consist of poems, history, law, and oratory, yet their primary contributions are not poetic, nor historical, nor legal, nor oratorical. The 

Bible is important for its message of God's love and concern for man. It is an expression of the religious life and faith of the peoples represented in the various ages out of which the writings came. 

It has been referred to as a book in which God speaks "to men in and through men;" it is one which "begins with God in a sense, and to an extent, that is true of no other book in the literature of the world." 

THE WRITING AND EARLY GROWTH OF THE BIBLE 

A study of the growth of the Bible is strongly suggestive of the growth of a giant oak with its roots, trunk and branches typifying various stages in its development. Through the ages, the Bible, as also the oak, can be traced back to its deep and diversified roots, followed up through its main trunk to the varied, but interrelated, translations which have branched out for our use today.

Its origin, when traced back through the complexity of its original sources, reaches deep into the soil of the religious experience of the peoples and ages from which it emerges. The greatest values and truths from these ages and peoples are deposited and finally channelled up toward the large central manuscripts, from which the Bible, as we know it, branches out into many present day versions. If we would glimpse the true picture of the growth of the Bible it becomes necessary to understand some basic facts about each of these parts of the whole: the roots or original sources, the trunk or "Wilbur F. Tillett, "The Divine Element in the Bible," in the Abingdon 

Bible Commentary, Abingdon Press, Chicago, 1929, pp. 27, 28.

XX 

OUR BIBLE 

the major manuscripts, and the branches or translations of the Bible.

The years between the writing of the first material of the Old Testament and the writing of the last verse to go into the New Testament are so many that they can be counted best by centuries. This span is so great that the writers of the later books make reference to earlier writing as coming out of a dim past. We scarcely begin our reading of the first book of the New Testament until quotations appear from the Old Testament and reference is made to the "law and the prophets." 

Even Jesus in the Sermon on the Mount, when referring to various teachings in the law says, "Ye hath heard that it hath been said by them of old time." But we can go back even into the books of the Old Testament and find similar references to its own early records. 

Nehemiah discusses the things which they found written in the law and says that "day by day from the first day unto the last day he read in the book of the law of God." (Nehemiah 8:18) It is interesting to note that in the Book of Joshua, which is the sixth book appearing in the Bible, a quotation is made and then the author says, "Is not this written in the book of 

Jasher?" (Joshua 10:12-13) Although we do not now have this book, the fact remains that these early writers had access to books of scripture even in their day. Reference is made in the prologue of the early apocraphal' books, Ecclesiasticus, to the law, the prophecies and the rest of the books. We see, therefore, that even books in early Bible times these three parts of the scriptures were known and used very much as we know and see them today.

We do not know the complete story of how each of the various books came into being nor the nature of all the previous, still older, sources which these writers "The "apocrapha" is a collection of books written in Bible times but not included in our Bible.

AN INTRODUCTION 

xxi used. We do know that writing was a difficult task and that in all probability only the things which the writer felt were of greatest importance and value were preserved. We can imagine the difficulty of preserving these records without the use of convenient materials which we have today. Of course there was no paper as we know it and certainly no printing presses of any kind. Historians who have studied early writing have concluded that in some cases stones and clay tablets were used for inscriptions. Even the Bible speaks of 

Moses receiving the Ten Commandments on tablets of stone. There is evidence that another, and perhaps later method, was to write on skins of animals made into various types of parchments. Still later writers employed the use of papyrus, which was made from the fibers of the papyrus plant. This in reality was the first paper. Papyrus was generally prepared in great long sheets which was rolled up and after having been written on was read by unrolling one end and rolling the other. The Old Testament refers frequently to the rolls upon which their records were kept. 

Since writing was so difficult we can well assume that all written records were welcomed and preserved with the utmost enthusiasm and care. We also see why it would be that these would be carefully handed from one person to another for generations. Sometimes in this process of preservation, later writers would edit a previous record or try to combine several similar records into one. In the end an original record would turn out to be very much different from what it was at first. Many times the changes might be for the better although it would not be solely the work of the original writer. This has actually happened in some of the older materials of the Bible until it is now possible to trace the work of at least two or more different writers running through some of the particular books. 

These editings, modifications, and combinations

xxii 

OUR BIBLE 

of materials often added to the treasured value of these writings and led to still more care in their use and preservation. These writings were considered sacred and were used as scripture to guide the people in their struggle to understand God and live near to Him. They were read, quoted, memorized, and in some cases even put to music in order to utilize fully their inherent religious value.

Such sacred writings grew from the experience of many persons at varying periods of time. The various books and even parts of books arose at diverse times and often under very different situations. Therefore, the process by which they were finally all accumulated together into the larger collections must have been an interesting one. We have already referred to three collections that were known even in the Bible timesthe Law, the Prophets, and the Writings. These three collections still appear in our present Old Testament so in reality it is a collection of collections. 

THE FIRST GREEK TRANSLATION OF THE BIBLE 

As the Hebrews were keeping their early sacred records, they did so in their own language. It can quite safely be said that the original Old Testament was first written and preserved in Hebrew. This language had long served the Jews during their history as a united people. As early as 300 B. C., however, the Jews began to spread over the eastern world and to intermingle with surrounding nations. This began to affect even their language. Their constant contact with the Greeks soon led to wide usage of the Greek language among the Jews. It was not long until even in Jerusalem the Hebrew language was not used.

This naturally led to a need for all important records to be produced in the more popular language-the Greek. The pressure became greater among the many 

Jews who had moved to Egypt where they were living

AN INTRODUCTION 

xxiii and thinking in more liberal terms than had their fathers in Jerusalem.

It is said that a group of these Jews living in the city of Alexandria sent a committee to the High Priest at Jerusalem requesting him to send some elders from each of the twelve tribes of Israel to make a Greek translation of the Bible for the University at Alexandria. 

Various stories have been handed down to us concerning the way this translation was made. One account says that seventy elders worked on the project independently and came out with identical wordings in their translation. At any rate a translation into Greek was made between the years 250 B. C. and 50 B. C. which has since been known as the Septuagint.

This Greek translation included all the books of the Old Testament which we now have. In addition it included many books which are not in our Bible, such as Ecclesiasticus, Judith, Tobit, Baruch, Maccabees, and others. The order of the books is somewhat like our own 

Old Testament with the law books first. However, the prophetic books are scattered somewhat differently throughout the collection.

The Septuagint was the Bible which was used later by the Jews for centuries. It was used during the 

New Testament times. We are fortunate today to have a copy of the Old Testament which was made from the Septuagint. This copy is called Codex Sinaiticus. 

It was found by a German scholar, Dr. Tischendorf, in a pile of old manuscripts at the foot of Mount Sinai. 

It is now in the British Museum, but photographic copies of each page of this Codex are available today in some of the great libraries for persons who work on translations of the Bible.

THE NEW TESTAMENT ADDED TO THE BIBLE 

While the Septuagint was being read and studied by the Greek speaking Jews in Egypt and Jerusalem

xxiv 

OUR BIBLE 

Jesus was beginning a work that was destined to spread over the entire world. After Jesus' personal earthly mission was ended his Apostles took up the work. 

Through them and the missionary efforts of such men as Paul, the message spread rapidly. During his missionary activities Paul wrote a letter to his converts in Thessalonica which marked the beginning of a new book of scripture. Many other letters followed to other converts and saints. Finally they were all collected together to form a body of new scriptural and doctrinal treatments as part of the New Testament. The account of Jesus' own life and teachings was written by such men as Mark, Matthew, Luke, and John. These became known as a collection of Gospels which later formed the real heart of the New Testament message. 

Other books were added between those years of 50 A. D. to 110 A. D. These were eventually collected into one body of new scripture which has now been known for about over eighteen hundred years as the New Testament.

THE FIRST LATIN BIBLE 

As we could well expect most of these new sacred writings first appeared in Greek which, as we have seen, was rapidly replacing the Hebrew even in Jerusalem itself. It should be remembered, however, that the 

Latin influence was soon to begin spreading over the oriental world. Even the Gospels tell us that the inscription above the cross of Jesus was written in Hebrew, Greek and Latin. As the powers of the Roman 

Empire were extended Latin became more and more the language of the peoples of the New Testament world. Soon the entire Christian Church was to be under the direction of the Roman State. It is not surprising therefore that the Greek Bibles were soon to give way to Latin ones.

But there was no authorized Latin Bible—in fact

AN INTRODUCTION 

XXV there was no Latin Bible at all for some time. The more liberal Christians in Northern Africa, particularly those near the university centers, soon felt the need for Latin scriptures and such men as Tertullian and Cyprian were among those who began translating and using important parts of the Bible during the third century. It even seems that some of the scriptures became rather commonly known. We must turn to Rome, however, for the story of the authorized Latin Bible. 

It was during the days of a Pope by the name of Damasus who served the Church from 366 to 384. 

Damasus had made friends with a brilliant scholar whose name was Jerome. He was accomplished in the arts of Greek, Latin, and Hebrew literature. The Pope in 382 requested Jerome to prepare a Latin translation of the Bible which could be universally accepted by the church. The plan was to revise the current Latin versions and to make necessary corrections based upon the best available Hebrew and Greek texts. The thought of thus "correcting" the scriptures aroused criticism. 

But Damasus died and the new Pope had different ideas and the original plans fell through. Jerome left Rome and finally settled in Bethlehem. He did not give up his own ambitions to do the work on the scriptures that he knew was so much needed. But instead of merely "correcting" and "bettering" the former translations he went directly to the oldest Hebrew manuscripts that he could obtain. He translated truthfully and faithfully from these best sources and finally, after twenty years, delivered to the world the Latin Vulgate. This is the translation destined to serve the authorized Catholic church in some form even to this day. It has also served as one major source to which world Bible scholars have constantly turned in making even their modern English translations. Even today the Catholic Douay version is little more than an English translation of 

Jerome's Latin Vulgate.

xxvi 

OUR BIBLE EARLIER ENGLISH BIBLES 

When Christianity became established in England there developed the need for scriptural writings in the English language. But the production of such writings was not realized immediately. The Latin Church was the influence back of all religious matters. The worship in the church and the readings of scriptures were carried forward in Latin and it became difficult to change over to English.

The earliest step toward preparing an English Bible was the work of the poet Caedmon in the seventh century. Tradition tells us that at one time the Savior seemed to appear unto him and asked him to "Sing"to sing of "The beginning of created things." A power then seemed to be with him and he produced beautiful metrical paraphrases of parts of the Old Testament. 

It was but a few years after this that the Venerable Bede sat in his monastery room and diligently translated portions of the scripture. It is reported that as he lay on his death bed he was yet trying to finish his translation of the Gospel of John. Near the end his young boy scribe said, "There is yet one sentence, dear

Master." As Bede finished the sentence and as it was written the venerable monk spake his last words, “You speak truth. All is finished now.'

Among the other persons who gave the English people their earliest portions of scripture in their own tongue were, Alfred the Great 849-901, Aelfric the 

Archbishop of Canterbury 950; William Shoreham 

1327, and Richard Rolle of Hampole 1340. In all these cases the work consisted of the translation of but parts of the Bible. In most instances the translations were made into verse or metrical arrangement. It was probably Shoreham and Rolle who made the first English prose translations from the Latin Bible. 

The work of producing a complete Bible for the

AN INTRODUCTION 

xxvii English people waited until late in the fourteenth century. This was a time when much untold physical, moral, political, and religious corruption seemed to be common throughout England. The physical conditions in the cities and towns were unfit for decent healthful living. Moral standards were low even among the poltical and religious leaders. The churchmen were, in many cases, ignorant and selfish. They used the ceremonies and worship in the church for their own gain while wickedness continued rampant within and without the church. Into these conditions came a brilliant, clean, morally upright, socially sensitive, young scholar from Oxford named John Wycliff. Repulsed at conditions in England he began to push into action his convictions for clean religious living. He taught that virtue was important and that "each man that shall be damned shall be damned by his own guilt and each man that shall be saved shall be saved by his own merit.' 

He denounced the superstitious ceremonial saving practices which were being imposed by the clergy. He insisted that the people of England needed the word of God from the Bible in their own language, and not second hand from the Latin through the priests. 

Accordingly he set up the task of producing a complete English Bible from Jerome's Latin Vulgate. In the midst of his reform work he was tried and excommunicated from the church. During his later years as an excommunicant he continued his work on the Bible. 

With the aid of his disciple Nicolas de Hereford the first complete English Bible was completed in manuscript form. Since there were not printing presses the work of producing other copies required the untiring labors of many scribes. The people clamored to even get the privilege of reading from its pages. A load of hay was once given for the loan of a copy for a very short time. Though the Wycliff Bible was condemned by the church and severe punishments meted out to its

xxviii 

OUR BIBLE 

readers still hundreds of copies were made by hand. 

It was enough to whet the appetite of the people of England to the value of the scriptures. While Wycliff did not improve the Bible by going back to manuscripts earlier than the Latin Vulgate he nevertheless laid the foundation for a universal distribution of the Bible to English people in their own tongue. 

About 150 years after the time of Wycliff, the spirit of reformation which began in Germany began to spread to England. In the swing of these critical times against the established practices and teachings of the Church and some of its ignorant clergy came a brilliant scholar from Oxford. He was William Tyndale. Somewhat like Luther and the German reformers he criticized the church and its authorities for their teachings and practices which he felt were not truly Christian. He himself was an honest, truth seeking, upright man who sought to establish the finest religious principles in England. He reached a decision that there was a need to make the Bible available to even the plow-boy of England to be read in his own language. He felt that it was impossible to "establish the lay people in any truth except ye scripture were plainly laid before their eyes in their mother tongue.' He therefore sought to get authorization from the Bishop of London to make an English translation for the common people. But the 

Bishop of London refused such a request and probably reminded Tyndale of the dangers of such a heretical venture. Long before this, Wycliff's Bible had been destroyed and the law now forbade private persons from translating the scriptures. To study it in their original languages was even frowned upon. 

Being thus refused authorization, Tyndale left England and went to Germany where he settled at Cologne in 1525. Here he began his work of translation -working at first on the New Testament. Since the printing press had now been invented it became an easy

AN INTRODUCTION 

xxix matter for many copies of his translation to be made. 

His friends in England were happy over his work and sought to help him in every way possible. The new volumes were smuggled into England and circulated in great numbers. The authorities did everything they could to prevent this. The copies were confiscated and burned whenever they could be found, but the distribution went forward. Various stories are told of attempts made to prevent their entry in England. One agent of the English authorities went to Tyndale and purchased, for a substantial sum, all of the copies which had been published and they were accordingly destroyed. This only aided the work of Tyndale because now he had sufficient money to finance the publication of still larger editions.

The enemies of the reformation understood clearly the influence which Tyndale was having and sought every way to prevent his work from going forward. 

One day while he was out walking his enemies seized him and hurried him off to prison in a cold dungeon. 

From his cell he pleaded for clean and warm clothes but he was left cold and in rags. Even his Hebrew Bible, grammar and dictionary were denied him. On October 

6, 1536, he was taken from his cell to be burned at the stake. In mercy, however, he was strangled to unconsciousness before the burning took place. It is reported that the last words he spoke were: "Lord, open the

King of England's eyes." He died with no evidence of the influence his work was to have in the world. Though 

Tyndale's Bible translation was rejected during his own lifetime it was soon to be officially recognized by the same leaders who now condemned it all. 

Tyndale's work as a reformer and a translator made him one of the greatest figures of the reformation period. He was fearless and untiring in his efforts. He was not content to stop with the versions of the Bible which others had made and which, in the main, traced

XXX 

OUR BIBLE 

back to the Latin Vulgate. He sought diligently for the oldest and best Hebrew and Greek manuscript from which to translate. As a result he produced a work the influence of which has permeated nearly all later Bible translations. 

It was within a year after Tyndale's martyrdom that Miles Coverdale produced a translation of the scriptures in accordance to a request for an English translation. His work made no pretense of being an original translation but followed largely the work of Tyndale. 

The next year, 1537, the Matthew's Bible came out which likewise was really the work of Tyndale. The 

Taverner's Bible also appeared which was an edition. of Matthews.

The political and religious leaders by this time began to feel the need for a Bible that could be authoritatively accepted by the church. It was Archbishop 

Cranmer with some of the chief advisers of the King who selected Miles Coverdale to produce a National Bible. The work was done and came forth as the "Great 

Bible," because of its huge size. This again was really the work of Tyndale but this time it was officially sanctioned on the title page by the same ruler who had purchased and burned Tyndale's Bibles. England now had its first authorized Bible to be used in its churches but it was too large and cumbersome for private use. 

It was over in Geneva, Switzerland, in about 1858, that a more popular translation was made known as the Geneva Bible. It was smaller, printed in Roman type, and divided into verses. This edition was circulated widely and even today many copies of this are available in old libraries. A revision of this became known as the "Bishops Bible," which was about to become the base for a still greater translation-The King James, which we use most widely today.

AN INTRODUCTION 

xxxi 

LATER ENGLISH BIBLES 

England now had many Bibles, none of which was non-partisan and convenient enough for general use. 

They were either too large or they represented the work of private individuals or reform groups, and this did not command the respect of scholars. It was a Puritan leader by the name of Doctor Reynolds who suggested that the work of Bible translation really belonged to scholars in the Universities. Though some churchmen objected to this view, plans were laid during the rule of King James for a scholarly translation of the scriptures. Fifty-four learned men were selected impartially to carry forward the work. They were requested to seek the help of every Biblical scholar of note in all England. Those assigned the task were told to use the 

Bishop's Bible as the basis for the work. They were to depart from this text only as absolutely necessary. 

The work was divided into six parts with the scholars thus divided into companies to do their specific part of the text. In two years the first draft was completed and a smaller committee edited the work before printing. 

In 1611 the work of these scholars came forth as the "Authorized" or the King James translation of the

Bible. This is the one which is used in most Protestant churches today and the one to which most Bible lovers turn for inspirational reading of the scriptures. Of this translation John Livingston Lowes in his Essays in 

Appreciation says: "Its phraseology has become part and parcel of our common tongue. . . . Its rhythms and cadences, its turns of speech, its familiar imagery, its very words, are woven into the texture of our literature, prose and poetry alike." This remarkable scholarly work utilized the best versions and manuscripts then available and put them in the finest court language of King James' day. It has now lived and has been treasured for over four centuries and will probably yet live

xxxii 

OUR BIBLE 

for generations as the most sacred book nearest the heart of God-fearing people.

With the late recovery of many important old manuscripts since the work of these 1611 scholars, the field of Biblical research has been remarkably extended. 

Among the valuable documents which we have today but which were not known in 1611 are the Alexandrian, the Vatican, and the Sinaitic manuscripts. These and other old manuscripts of the Bible are now opening up vast treasures of Bible knowledge to the world and are forming the basis for many newer versions which are now available.

In 1870 a Revision Committee was formed in England for the purpose of utilizing all the latest found manuscripts for a revised version of the Bible. About seventy-five English and American scholars assumed the task and by 1855 the Revised Version appeared in England. In 1901 the American Revised was published.

The first forty years of our century has witnessed the publication of various modern versions of the Bible. 

Among these are the works by Weymouth, Moffatt, and Goodspeed. Some of these have been modern speech editions with the purpose of presenting the Bible text more nearly as it was spoken and written in Bible times instead of the scholarly court language of King James' day. Each of these is available and their effectiveness can best be judged by the reader and student of the Bible.

Through all of these centuries of Bible writings and translations we can recognize the God-inspired desires of man to preserve and transmit these sacred writings as accurately as possible for the religious value of mankind. It seems that God surely directed these efforts. 

No other book can show such universal concern for its preservation and such careful, prayerful work in its translation. One church writer said, "The real message

AN INTRODUCTION 

xxxiii of the Bible has been preserved unimpaired and is confirmed by every new translation."""

APPRECIATIVE USE OF THE BIBLE 

As we review these episodes in the centuries of endless toil and struggle that have brought us the Bible it should lead us to seek a greater appreciation of the Book itself. Those who toiled and suffered for its preservation did so because they felt its message would improve the life of the generations to follow. That such improvements in life have actually been realized has been recognized by many great leaders. Theodore 

Roosevelt showed his conviction of this when he said: "Every thinking man realizes, what many forget, that the teachings of the Bible are so inter-woven and entwined with our whole civic and social life that it would be literally-I do not mean figuratively, I mean literally impossible for us to figure to ourselves, what that life would be if these teachings were removed. Almost every man who has by his life added to the sum of human achievement, of which the race is proud, of which our people are proud, almost every such man has based his life work largely upon the teachings of the Bible. I plead for a closer and a wider and deeper study of the Bible, so that our people may be in fact as well as in theory "doers" of the word and not hearers only." 

Robert Millikan said, "I consider an intimate knowledge of the Bible an indispensable qualification of a well educated man. Contact with the finest influences which have ever come into human life can be obtained only in this way." Lincoln referred to the 

Bible as the best book that God has given to man. 

One of the influences from the Bible which has long sustained man in his personal struggles to live abundantly in his world has been the personal comfort and inspiration therefrom. Daniel Webster wrote: "John A. Widtsoe, Improvement Era, March, 1940, page 191.

xxxiv 

OUR BIBLE 

"I remember the time when at my mother's feet, or on my father's knee, I learned to lisp the phrases of the Scriptures. If there is anything in my life to be commended, the credit is due to my parents in instilling into my mind the sacred Scriptures." 

Henry Van Dyke says: "No man is poor or desolate when he has this treasure for his own. When the landscape darkens and the trembling pilgrim comes to the valley of the shadow of death, he is not afraid to enter; he takes the rod and staff of scripture in his hand; he says to friend and comrade, 'Goodby, we shall meet again;' and comforted by that support, he goes toward the lonely pass as one who walks through darkness into light."

The values which are obtainable from the Bible are continuously being recognized by the inspirational teachers of each age. There can be little doubt that its influence will be continually felt in ages yet to come. 

It but remains for each individual to so prepare his heart and mind for its message that its potential personal influence may be fully realized. Books and articles may be prepared upon the literary value of the Bible for in it are found some of the world's best literature. Men may discuss the historical aspect of the 

Bible and be able to convince us of its contribution in this area. Others, in a spirit of reverence and appreciation, may list other values which to them are paramount. Yet, along with these there towers the more significant reason for its existence-its message of God's loving concern for his people as this is revealed in all their experiences with Him.

The God-inspired men who preserved and transmitted the Bible down through the centuries intended that all who read its pages should understand its true purpose and message. In order to achieve this the Bible reader should approach its pages in such a manner that its religious significance can be fully understood and ap-

AN INTRODUCTION 

XXXV preciated. This requires the recognition of certain guiding principles that should underlie all such study. 

In the first place the reader should approach the Bible with a reverent spirit which will enable him to search for all the good which it contains. This assumes that he must recognize it, intelligently, as the word of God given to and through man. It represents the highest levels of the religious thought of its authors as they, through the Holy Ghost, received revelations from God. 

These revelations were preserved through the medium of human beings who thought and wrote through the finest religious experiences given them of God. Some writers undoubtedly lived and wrote in light of higher and truer experiences of God than others for inspiration and revelation comes to man according to the nearness of his life to God. Obviously then we can expect the human element to be mingled with the divine throughout the pages of the Bible. But the reverent reader will not allow the human elements to blind him to the great religious message from the divine source. 

The intelligent reader should do all in his power to put himself in the position of the writer of the message. He should try to understand the social situation and pressures out of which he wrote and the purposes which he had for writing as he did. With this frame of mind it is possible to appreciate more fully the force of his writings. Not only would it help the reader to get the true message of the author but it would help to avoid misunderstandings regarding any parts of the message which might seem not to conform to our best religious standards. Some peoples have criticized the Bible on the grounds that it seemed to contradict itself in places and that some of the characters are not as Godlike as they should be. Such criticism would vanish if the 

Bible were truly understood in light of the time and experience of the writer. The Bible presents a total picture of the life of its characters. We can thus expect

xxxvi 

OUR BIBLE 

human frailties to appear. However, many of these human elements reveal genuine religious purposes when they are understood in terms of the social standards of their own day.

The student who truly seeks to appreciate the Bible will study it always for the contribution of its message to our religious life today. It is not enough to be entertained by its stories unless these stories can reach deep into our souls to make better persons. The accounts in the Bible were preserved for the help which they can give to man in developing his faith in God and in following His teachings. The reader who misses the significance of Bible stories in present life is not a true student of the Bible.

Of course, the message of all episodes in the Bible are not always relevant to our own time. We must remember that the writers wrote to meet the needs of their day. They did not, write directly for our day. 

When Paul wrote his letters to the Corinthian Saints in answer to such questions as "Shall we eat meat that has been sacrificed to the God," or "Shall we marry before the second coming of Christ," we must interpret his answers in terms of the situations which Paul faced. 

When this is done the reader obtains a truer picture of the message of Corinthians. It is important, therefore, for the student of the Bible to be concerned as to whether the situation presented in the Bible has a counterpart in present life. Is it similar or relevant to situations today? As this test is made the reader will be pleasingly surprised to find that human life is near enough the same from generation to generation that it is possible to find numerous relevant situations today upon which the Bible can give valuable aid.

In reading the Bible it is highly important to do so in longer blocks of material whenever possible. The true significance of scripture is too often lost when a few verses are isolated from their total context. Some

AN INTRODUCTION 

xxxvii of our best Bible scholars advise reading an entire book at a sitting in order to feel truly its total significance. 

Readers of the Bible should not lose sight of its value for devotional purposes apart from any purely intellectual analysis of its content. The greatest Godlike men in our time use the Bible as a means of living near to their Creator. They read its verses in the eventide, at the break of day, or at any other time when they desire to turn their souls Godward. The inspiration therefrom arouses the divine urge within them which not only gives personal internal peace but which arouses them to do all in their power to help accomplish God's noblest purposes in the earth.

In all of his use of the Bible the student should do all in his power to attune himself to the greatest inspirational values within. At many places he may be helped by consulting the writing of more experienced students of the scripture. Such aids are found in the sermons and books of church leaders and Bible scholars. 

Bible commentaries, dictionaries, and concordances should be available for the help they can give and students should become familiar with their use. Thousands of valuable books have been written about the Bible, many of which are treasure-houses of wisdom about it and its message. However, it must be understood that the best way to really get near to any written record is to read it itself instead of a book about it. Turn, therefore, to the pages of this record which has come down through the ages. Follow the admonition of a 

Son of God who said, "Search the scriptures for they are they which testify of me."

THE OLD TESTAMENT THY WORD IS A LAMP UNTO MY FEET, A LIGHT UNTO MY PATH.-Psalms 119:105.

THE STORY of Israel is the story of a people whose life and thought have made greater influence upin the history of the world than that of any other people. The marvel of it being that a family of shepherds wandering from Babylon to Canaan should change the course of human history. 

The small tribal people calling themselves Hebrews has survived for almost fifty centuries, through the conflicts of the ancient world, the dark ages, and the changing scenes that made the modern world until today. It is not, however, a story of great conquests of land or of nations, it is a story of hardships and suffering, of persecution and bondage, of a people struggling to keep their identity. The marvel of it, however, lies in the great faith behind the struggles of Israel through the centuries to maintain their racial individuality, that they might retain their heritage as the chosen people of the living God, the Creator of the world and man. 

The Bible which has been called "A Divine Library" grew out of the life of the Hebrews, the people of Israel. This inspired book records the physical, social, moral, and spiritual struggles of the Hebrews as God sought to teach all mankind through them His purposes and His laws for their eternal happiness. It was the divinity of the message of the story of Israel that kept the Bible alive through the ages. 

For centuries the history of the civilizations of the ancient world, Egypt, Babylon, and Persia, the neigh-

bors of Israel, was shrouded in mystery. But in the last century the magic spades of archeology have uncovered their buried cities, temples, tombs, and monuments all bearing silent testimony of the peoples, places, and events of Bible times. The inscriptions written upon the walls of the buried city of Ur of the Chaldees, the home of Abraham, upon the walls of the temples and tombs of Egypt, upon the clay tablets of Babylon telling of the great Flood, and upon the rock of Behistun telling the story of the kingdoms of Babylon and Persia and their conquests all have helped to reveal Israel's story. Until today the Bible unlocked by those who have sought its secrets is in reality the greatest book in the world, the Book of Life.

The Old Testament of the Bible is a great human document for in it history and religion are strangely blended as it records over the centuries the inner life of Israel. Religion was the greatest influence in the life of this people, for they believed that God demanded more of them than worship, He demanded that they live righteously. In every book of the Old Testament record the presence and inspiration of Jehovah are to be found. 

In their joys and in their sorrows, in their successes and in their failures the people of Israel saw the loving or the chastening hand of their God as a Heavenly Father.

The message of the Old Testament is to be found in the thoughts of Israel of God and His place in the life of man. The influence of the life of Israel is found woVen into the religious, moral, cultural, and social life of the great civilizations of the modern world. 

The story of Israel as it is contained in the Old Testament record seems to follow the chief historical developments of Hebraic life and thought: 

From the Creation to Babel 

The Time of the Patriarchs

Bondage, Deliverance, and Wanderings of Israel The Rule of the Judges

The Kingdom of Israel The Divided Kingdom Exile and Restoration FROM THE CREATION TO BABEL 

The Bible story opens with the creation of the earth and man. The account of the Creation and the history of mankind to the time of Moses were kept alive orally before man learned the art of writing. From father to son, from generation to generation, the making of the first pages of the history of the world was passed along. When it was written by Moses much of the detail had been forgotten only the momentous events of God's dealings with mankind and the great human crises remained. Therefore much of the sacred record of the Mosaic writings is still shrouded in mystery.

After the sublime words "In the beginning God created the heavens and the earth" there follow the detailed order of creation, God's companionship with 

Adam and Eve in the first earth-home, the disobedience and punishment of Adam and Eve, of Cain and Abel, of the generations before Noah and their growing wickedness, of the account of the great Flood, and of the repeopling of the earth. Many of the details of places and happenings may be missing in the written record but the beauty and simplicity of the language make the message of it all unmistakably clear for it is the revelation of the nature and purposes of the Creator of the heavens and the earth, the God that Israel worshiped. 

THE TIME OF THE PATRIARCHS 

Abraham was the father of Israel. This patriarchal chieftain of his tribe and his people left Ur of the Chaldees to dwell in Padan-Aram the home of his fathers

about two thousand years before our era. Then at the command of God he went to Canaan which was to be the heritage of Israel. To these shepherd nomads of the deserts Canaan was a beautiful land. In the words of the writer of the Book of Deuteronomy it was a good land, a land of wheat and barley and vines and fig trees and pomegranates, a land of olives and honey." But the land of Canaan was also the home of many tribes: the 

Philistines, the Jebusites, the Ammonites, the Amorites, and the Hivites. So Abraham and his household moved from one place to another in Canaan with his flocks and herds. Keeping themselves pure they did not follow the customs of the people of the land. They built their own altars wherever they went worshiping God as He was revealed to them. 

The selection of a wife for Isaac, the son of Abraham is the beginning of the tradition which was to keep Israel racially pure, a factor of their miraculous endurance during the centuries. Jacob, the younger son of 

Isaac, became the patriarch of his tribe upon the death of Isaac, for Esau, his elder brother, had sold his heritage and had married wives from among the pagan tribes of Canaan. Of the twelve sons of Jacob his love for Joseph was the greatest. The brothers of Joseph sold him as a slave because of their jealousy of their father's great love. Later when Joseph had become a man of power and dignity in Egypt, the family of Jacob went to live with him there for before the death of Jacob there was a great famine in all the land. 

Under the leadership of the great tribal Patriarchs, Abraham, Isaac, Jacob and Joseph, Israel prospered and they lived together in harmony as a people. Preserved among the hostile tribes of Canaan and from the great famine they grew in numbers and in wealth, and they remembered their God and worshiped Him

THE PERIOD OF BONDAGE, DELIVERANCE, AND WANDERINGS OF ISRAEL 

After the death of Joseph the people of Israel did not return to the land of Canaan but continued to dwell in the rich valley of Goshen in the Delta of the river Nile. Here they honored no tribal leader or patriarch. The day came, however, when there arose in 

Egypt a Pharoah who did not remember the great service of Joseph and he made the Hebrews to serve him as slaves.

It was a hard and bitter bondage that was placed upon Israel for the Egyptian taskmasters were cruel and without mercy. And Israel had no leader to help them in their suffering or to intercede for them with the king. 

But at length Jehovah provided a leader for Israel, to deliver them from bondage and to lead them back to Canaan.

A young Hebrew named Moses tried to help his people but he failed and had to take refuge in the land of Midian. There God spoke to him and commanded him to return to Egypt to deliver Israel. The story of the deliverance of Israel from bondage is told in the Book of 

Exodus. Day after day the God of Israel performed miracles at the hand of Moses that His people might be freed. Plagues came to Egypt causing suffering and death before the tyrant king would let the Hebrews go.

Only when death came to all the firstborn of Egypt did he yield. Then Moses led Israel out of bondage, across the Red Sea, to the freedom of the desert. During the long journey to Mount Sinai the Hebrews were provided for and protected by the miraculous power given to Moses. But the greatest task of Moses was to direct his people as they lived together as homeless wanderers. 

He made laws for their guidance, he appointed leaders to help him, and he divided the great host into tens, fifties, hundreds, and thousands and placed leaders over them, to take care of all small matters.

At Mount Sinai Jehovah renewed with Israel the covenant He had made with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob. 

He gave them the Ten Commandments as a pattern of living whereby they might keep the covenant. Moses made an ark in which to place the tablets of stone whereon was written the Ten Commandments and the Ark became the symbol of God's presence and His covenant with them. Israel called this symbol the Ark of the Covenant, and they carried it before them as they journeyed each day. Also a special tent was made wherein the Ark might rest and this they called the Tabernacle or Tent of Meeting. Now as they journeyed toward Canaan they worshiped God and lived in peace together. 

The journey from Sinai to Canaan was long and difficult, but it was a period of trading for Israel, that they might know how to live among the pagan tribes of Canaan in loyalty to God and in righteousness. 

When they came to the outskirts of the land, however, the Israelites lacked courage to fight the fierce tribes occupying the land. Then they turned back to the wilderness with its security and its hardships. But a new generation demanded that Moses lead them to the land of their inheritance. And Moses directed his people to travel by the east of the Dead Sea through the land of 

Moab. Now the Israelites remained in Moab for some time because there was plenty of food for themselves and their flocks and herds. Conquering part of the country they lived in comfort, some of them never to leave to enter the land of Canaan.

When the great task of Moses was over Joshua was appointed the leader of Israel. Joshua was a great soldier as well as a man of God. He led Israel across the 

Jordan and began the conquest of Canaan. In those days every town or settlement in the land had its own chieftain. In the Book of Joshua we find a record of the kings subdued and the peoples destroyed; thirty-one kings, we are told. The cruelty of the conquest seemed

to be the only way the defenseless, unskilled Israelites could gain a foothold in the land against tribes skilled in warfare and in plunder. 

To complete his work, Joshua divided the conquered parts of the land of Canaan among the tribes of Israel as Moses had instructed him, that each might have his share of the heritage of the Land of Promise. Before his death this great soldier and prophet called the leaders of all the tribes together and reminded them of their covenant with Jehovah. He commanded them to destroy the altars of the worshipers of Baal in Canaan, to break down their idols, to cut down their sacred groves, and to burn their graven images with fire, that they might not be tempted thereby. He warned them of marrying with the people of Canaan as many had married with the people of Moab. And he reminded them in his last words of their supreme duty: "To love the Lord thy 

God, with all thine heart, and with all thy soul, and with all thy might."

THE RULE OF THE JUDGES 

The story of the early colonial life of the Hebrews has been told vividly by the historian of the books of 

Deuteronomy and Judges. 

After the death of Joshua the conquest of Canaan was continued. The struggle was a hard one. There were defeats as well as victories. The disadvantage lay in the fact that now Israel was without a leader, there was no unity. The only tie which held them together was their religious faith. From the record of the Book of Judges we learn that there was confusion, tumult, and bloodshed because "there was no leader in Israel, but every man did what was right in his own eyes." Of great significance also was the fact that the northern territory settled by the Hebrews was separated from the southern settlements by a chain of Canaanite strongholds.

From time to time, however, inspired leaders arose to help Israel in times of great peril. Ehud led the tribe to 

Ephraim to throw off the Moabite yoke. Deborah and 

Barak rose to the deliverance of the northern tribes and destroyed Jabin, king of Hazor. Gideon of the tribe of 

Manasseh defeated the Midianites. Jephthah triumphed over the Ammonites. But Samson failed where he might have succeeded over the great enemy, the Philistines, because he sought not the glory of Israel but his own satisfaction.

Sad to relate, the physical victories of Israel were intermingled with moral failures, for many forgot the covenant with Jehovah and adopted the religion of the Canaanites.

THE KINGDOM OF ISRAEL 

Out of a great crisis in the history of Israel, the menace of the military power of the Philistines, grew the establishment of the Hebrew kingdom. The national feeling of Israel was growing. They knew their weakness. They thought strength would come out of unity, a political unity such as existed among the peoples about them. And they came to the prophetic judge Samuel and demanded a king.

Samuel warned Israel of the evils that might come to them with kingship and exhorted them not to forsake the rule of Jehovah their heavenly king. But they were not to be changed in their demands and Saul, a warrior of the tribe of Benjamin, was chosen to be the first king of Israel.

At first Saul as a warrior-king was successful. He delivered his people from the Ammonites and the Amalekites and for a time checked the Philistines. At last 

Israel gained a measure of security and independence and as a result a patriotic unity grew to be a great bond among all the tribes of Israel. But in spite of the counsels

of Samuel, Saul ruled his people in his own way forgetful of their religious destiny. The disobedience of Saul, his cruel persecution of David, and the tragic death of Saul and his sons at the hands of the Philistines are among the doleful pictures of Israel's history. 

David, the second King of Israel, was a man of fearless courage and outstanding ability; also he was a man of great national loyalty and religious faith. To 

David, the hero of Saul's armies, fell the task of completing the defeat of the Philistines. David it was who became the builder of the empire. A statesman as well as a warrior, he kept peace with the neighboring peoples after he had subdued them by friendly relations, not by plundering. He established a national capitol for his people and built the city of Jerusalem, which is to this day the religious capital of the Jews. The benefactor of 

Israel's Golden Age, David, was deeply religious. His thought and feelings expressed in the words of psalms of worship lived longer than his kingdom and have blessed mankind in greater measure than his deeds of valor.

The Hebrews looked upon their nation as a democracy, their king was their servant. But Solomon the third King of Israel became the master of the people, not their servant. His reign was one of glory, wealth, magnificence, and extravagance. He was the building king of Israel, imitating the kings of Babylon and Egypt.

He built a magnificent temple for the worship of Israel, palaces for himself and his wives and great dwellings to house his large armies and chariots of war. The temple served to strengthen the religion of Israel but the pagan priests and altars of the foreign wives of Solomon brought evil among them. The heavy burden of taxation and forced labor that Solomon had laid upon his people for his magnificence and luxury was unjust and inhuman. Solomon, who was judged at the beginning of his reign to be the wisest of men, became a tyrant

stirring up the discontent which brought tragedy to the united kingdom of Israel. 

THE KINGDOM DIVIDED 

The evils of the reign of Solomon soon bore fruit. 

The people rebelled against Rehoboam, the son of Solomon, because his reign promised to be one of greater tyranny even than that of Solomon. For when they pled with him to lighten their burdens he answered, "My father chastised you with whips, but I shall chastise you with scorpions." Then the ten tribes of the northern part of the kingdom crowned Jeroboam their king and called their kingdom Israel. Only the tribes of 

Judah and Benjamin remained loyal to Rehoboam to form the kingdom of Judah. The separation of the tribes of Israel was a great tragedy, it led to defeat and exile.

The kingdom of Israel lasted about two hundred years. During a succession of nineteen kings confusion and decay grew rapidly. The worship of God declined also. Jeroboam thinking to strengthen his kingdom by making the people forget the worship of the Temple at Jerusalem set up rival temples at Bethel and Dan with altars and bull images. When Ahab became king he allowed his wife Jezebel, a princess of Sidon, to build altars for the worship of Baal throughout the land. Only the heroic efforts of Elijah and Elisha saved the worship of Israel during this great crisis. 

From time to time there arose in the land other prophets to continue the work of Elijah and Elisha. 

They went among the people rebuking them for their idolatry and evil living. Hosea's words of condemnation reveal the state to which the people of Israel had fallen: "There is no truth, nor mercy, nor knowledge of God in the land. By swearing, and lying, and killing, and stealing, and committing adultery they deny their covenant with Jehovah."

When Israel began to listen to the teachings of the prophets it was too late. They were disorganized as a nation. The Assyrians, now a strong nation, invaded the land of Israel and laid siege to the capital city of Samaria. Many were taken captive to Assyria and many people were brought from the land of the conquerors to colonize the land. It was an inglorious end for part of 

God's kingdom and people.

The story of the kingdom of Judah is also one of tragedy. It lasted about three hundred and fifty years. 

Among the twenty-two rulers were men of great power and integrity and men of exceeding wickedness. Luxury and oppression brought about moral and religious stagnation. The work of King Uzziah and the prophet 

Isaiah did much to establish a degree of righteousness once more. When Josiah became the king he did his best to bring his people to realize the dangers that could come to them from the conquering nations of Egypt and Babylon. But they refused to stir themselves and become united. Finally, like its rival of the north, Judah fell a prey to the invader. Jerusalem was destroyed and the people were carried away to captivity in Babylon. 

EXILE AND RESTORATION 

The captivity of the Hebrews was a period of great significance. During this period, one of suffering for all, to those carried away in exile and to those left isolated at home there came a new spirit. Now that they had no national home in Canaan and in captivity they had no voice in their own government, they began to realize that their only security was in the kingdom of God. In their suffering their hearts turned to the words of God through His servants the prophets. At last Israel was cured of idolatry. 

When Cyrus, the king of Persia, captured Babylon he granted the Jews permission to return to their native land. Meanwhile many captives in Assyria also had been

freed to return to Israel. It was a humble and chastened people that returned from exile. With eagerness and sacrifice they began to rebuild the temple and the city of Jerusalem. There were many handicaps, however, for the entire land was under the rule of Persia. 

The Book of Ezra and the Book of Nehemiah re cord many of the experiences of the Jews in the period following the Exile. Many of the people of Israel did not return to Canaan. Some of them had become established in the foreign lands and preferred to remain there. 

The Book of Esther is a testimony of the experience of some of those remaining in exile; it is also a testimony of a renewed determination in Israel to keep themselves racially pure..

The power of the Persian empire declined and a new age for the entire world came with the conquest of the ancient world by Alexander the Great, of Greece. 

This brought a new crisis in the religious thought of Israel. The Greek culture and learning soon dominated 

Egypt and Syria as Canaan was now called. The influence of Greek life, manners, customs, and wisdom was far-reaching. To this new influence the people of Israel were divided. Those Jews who resisted the influence of the Greeks became known as Pharisees, while those who sponsored the adoption of Greek manners and customs were known as Sadducees. Meanwhile the Jewish Sanhedrin was established, a form of city-state government. 

Once more persecution and suffering came to Israel. The Greeks attempted to make Syria a Greek colony and the Temple in Jerusalem a pagan sanctuary. 

Rising in rebellion many Jews were massacred. But many went on fighting valiantly to offset the work of the Greeks. Judas, a Maccabee, gathered an army and fought valiantly to drive the Greeks out of the land. At last he succeeded and the Jews took back their beloved Temple, cleansed it and rededicated it to God. But when the Romans became the conquerors of the Greek

world the hard earned freedom earned by Judas, the Maccabees was lost. Rome now was the master of the ancient world. This was the seventh empire to dominate the land of the people of Israel and the most cruel of all. One rebellion followed another, then Jerusalem and the Temple were destroyed once more. But when 

Herod became the king of the land the sacred city of Israel and Temple were rebuilt but this time by the love of Israel.

At the close of the Old Testament history we find the Land of Promise ruled over by Herod, the pagan tyrant, enslaving and degrading Israel to a degree unknown in their history. Yet now more brightly than ever there burned in the heart of Israel the belief that they were the chosen race of God and they awaited with eager longing the Messiah that the prophets had foretold would be sent to them as their Deliverer and their Saviour.

God Creates and Directs the World 

IN 'N THE beginning God created the heaven and the earth, and the earth was without form and void; and darkness was upon the face of the deep. And the Spirit of God moved upon the face of the waters. "And God said, 'Let there be light... let there be a firmament... let the dry land appear... let the earth bring forth grass. . . let the waters bring forth abundantly the moving creatures... let the earth bring forth the living creatures after his kind... let us make man in our image after our likeness, and let them have dominion over all the earth.'

So reads the sacred record of the Book of Genesis. 

Since the beginning countless generations have passed. The desire to know the method and purpose for the creation of the earth and man has stirred the deepest thought of all mankind in all ages. But still the surpassing beauty of the lilies of the field and the colors of an evening sunset, the mystery of life and death, and the ways of the Divine Creator are eternal mysteries.

Since the beginning science has made many conqests for man giving him dominion over the land and the sea and the air. Philosophy has made explanations of human behavior and destiny. But with all that discovery, science, and philosophy have done, the way of creation still lies unrevealed. But with the coming of

each dawn the soul of man, the world over, goes out to the Creator with a prayer of gratitude, "It is good." 

However, God did not leave man in darkness as to the reason for his creation. Many times through the ages God has revealed His purpose for the creation of earth and man. God made man and endowed him with intelligence that he might be the master of his own destiny. Created in God's image, free and unfettered, man's destiny is as limitless as his intelligence, his power of self-control, and his understanding of the meaning of life.

The Bible records the nature and purposes of God, the Creator. In its sacred record of an inspired race we see the loving grace of a Divine Father for His offspring; 

His efforts in the perfection of man, that he might gain for himself eternal happiness.

MAN'S FIRST EARTH-HOME-Genesis 2:8; 3:24 

And God planted a garden eastward in Eden and there He put man whom He had formed in His own likeness and to whom He had given breath of His own 

Spirit. And out of the ground the Lord God made to grow every tree that is pleasant and is good for food; also the Tree of Life in the midst of the garden, and the 

Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil.

And a river went out of Eden to water the garden. 

God blessed the man and woman He placed in the garden, and He said to them: "Be fruitful, multiply, and fill the earth and have dominion over it. Eat of the fruit of any tree in the garden for your food except of the Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil: from it thou shalt not eat, for in the day that thou eatest of it thou shalt surely die."

It was a beautiful place that God had prepared to be the earth-home of Adam and Eve. For a long time they lived in peace and innocence in the garden

of Paradise. Each day as they gathered the fruit of the garden for their food, they remembered the tree in the midst of the garden and did not eat its fruit. In their gratitude for their beautiful home Adam and Eve had no other desire but to do as God directed. 

Often the loving Creator came to Adam and Eve in the cool of the evening and walked and talked with them in the garden. As they learned more of their 

Heavenly Father's plans for the happiness of mankind they were full of joy and gladness.

Each day they were about their tasks caring for the garden that they might have food and learning the ways of the living things and naming them as they had been instructed. One day as she was working alone in the garden, Eve heard a voice speaking to her. It was the voice of the serpent saying: "Wherefore hath God said that ye shall not eat of every tree in the garden?" 

Eve who was not surprised or afraid of the voice answered: "We may not eat of the fruit of the Tree of 

Knowledge of Good and Evil lest we die." "Ye shall not surely die if you eat of the fruit," replied the serpent, "God doth know that in the day ye eat thereof, then your eyes shall be opened, and ye shall be as gods knowing good and evil."

From then on every time she came near to the forbidden tree, Eve remembered the words of the serpent and Eve knew no peace for the beauty of the fruit tempted her now as it had never done before. Now she began to wonder at God's command forbidding them to eat of the fruit of that tree. Then the moment came when Eve could resist no longer, she took of the fruit of the Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil, ate it, and gave some to Adam.

Now Adam and Eve knew that which was evil, for now the peace and contentment they had known

were all gone and they were full of fear. They had chosen to believe the words of the serpent rather than to obey the command of their loving Creator. 

That evening when they heard the voice of Jehovah calling to them as He was wont to do when He came to the garden, they hid themselves among the trees. They were so ashamed that they did not answer. 

A second time the voice called: "Adam, where art thou?"

Then Adam and Eve came from their hiding place, and Adam stood before God in great dread and answered: "I heard Thy voice in the garden; and I was afraid, and hid myself." "Hast thou eaten of the Tree, whereof I commanded thee that thou shouldst not eat?" asked the 

Lord God. 

Laying the blame upon Eve, Adam replied: "The woman thou gavest me she gave me of the Tree and I did eat." 

Then God spoke to Eve saying: "What is this that thou hast done?”

And she replied, "The serpent beguiled me and I did eat." 

Then the Lord God said unto the woman: "I will greatly multiply thy sorrow, for thy children shall be borne in suffering, and thy husband shall be thy master." 

To Adam the Lord said, "In the sweat of thy face shalt thou eat bread till thou return unto the ground; for out of it wast thou taken, for dust thou art and unto dust shalt return."

Now Adam and Eve knew good and evil, they knew that good came from obedience to God's commands and that evil came from disobedience. They had chosen to disobey God and now no longer could they live in the garden of Paradise. For God sent them out

of the place of beauty and purity to roam abroad in the world and make a home for themselves. 

As they left the garden home Adam and Eve had great sorrow in their hearts. Alone in the darkness they saw the flaming sword of the angels God had placed at the gate of the garden to guard the Tree of Life. Then they knew that because of what they had done they were separated from God, their loving father.

THE OFFERINGS OF CAIN AND ABEL-Genesis 4:1-18 

Adam and Eve had to make a new home for themselves when they left the Garden of Eden. It was a hard task, for now it was that they must till the soil to provide the food they needed. Often at first there was little to repay them for their toil but thorns and thistles. 

But later when they had learned to plant and to nurture the seeds, the earth yielded plants and grain for food, and the sheep and lambs they cared for in the cool grassy places yielded both food and clothing.
