In their loneliness and sorrow Adam and Eve did not despair for they had the knowledge of God's loving kindness to comfort them; also they had the memory of the things they had learned as they had talked with 

Him in their beautiful garden-home. And Adam and

Eve made it their custom to take the choicest of the fruits of their labors and make an offering to their loving Creator to show their thankfulness to Him for 

His goodness and mercy.

After a time sons and daughters were born to Adam and Eve. One of the sons was named Cain, another was named Abel, and still another was named Seth. Now out of their sorrow came once more joy and gladness, for as they watched their children grow to manhood and womanhood they knew that they had been obedient to the commandment of God to multiply that the earth might be peopled.

Cain and his brother Abel grew to manhood to-

gether. Adam delighted in the strength of Cain and the courage with which he worked at his chosen task as a tiller of the soil. But Eve wondered often at the difference between Cain and Abel, for Abel was as gentle as the lambs he tended on the hillsides, while Cain was easy to anger and was often resentful and jealous. 

Now it happened that when an harvest time came Adam and his sons prepared to make an offering to God in thanksgiving. The simple altars of stone were made and the offerings of the first-fruits of the fields and the flocks prepared. Abel prepared his offering with gratitude and thanksgiving in his heart for God's loving kindness to him. But Cain's heart was full of bitterness and jealousy as he prepared his offering. 

As the smoke from the altar fires ascended, Cain saw that his sacrifice was not pleasing to the Creator, for it rose not to the heavens as did that of Abel's offering. And Cain was bitter in his heart because God had rejected his sacrifice and he went on his way brooding and sullen. 

Seeing Cain's anger God spoke to him saying: "Why art thou downcast? Hast thou some sin in thine heart? Curb thy evil spirit that is within thee lest it master thee entirely." 

But Cain refused the warning the Lord God had spoken to him and in his heart his hatred for Abel grew deeper as the days passed, for he knew that his brother loved the Lord and obeyed Him in all things. Then one day when the brothers were alone together in the field, Cain killed his brother Abel. 

Then Cain heard the voice of God saying to him: "Where is Abel, thy brother?" "I know not," answered Cain. "Am I my brother's keeper?" "What hast thou done?" demanded the Lord. "Thy brother's blood crieth to me from the ground.

Now thou art cursed from the earth; for thou shalt be a vagabond and a wanderer till thou die." 

The punishment of Cain was very great for now he must leave the companionship of Adam and Eve and go out to make a new home. But the greatest suffering that came to Cain was the mark of his crime that was to be with him for all his life, for the Lord set a mark upon Cain that all men might know him and none might dare to lift a hand against him. 

So Cain and his wife went alone eastward from the Eden of the first earth home and made a new home. 

After many generations the family of Cain numbered many souls, and the men of the tribe of Cain were strong and resourceful. Two of the descendants of Cain in the sixth generation created for mankind two of its greatest crafts; Jubal made an instrument of music to gladden the hearts of mankind, while Tubal forged iron to make tools, the servants of man. But Tubal made also the first sword. 

The harp and the sword! Was it very strange that good and evil came to mankind through the lineage of Cain? No it is not strange, it is the heritage of mankind to be able to know good and evil. Good and evil exist side by side in the world. The choice is man's; to accept the one and to reject the other; to create or to destroy; to bring happiness or sorrow, to themselves and others. 

NOAH AND THE GREAT FLOOD-Genesis 5:1-9:17 

After many generations there were a great many people living in the world. Some of them remembered the teachings of Adam to his children of God and the creation of the earth and man. Some of them, however, like Cain, followed only their own desires whether they were good or evil. On every hand, therefore, there were those who lived only for the things that gave them satisfaction and pleasure. Yet these were all God's

children and if many had forgotten Him, He had not forgotten them nor His purposes for their creation. 

In the seventh generation from Adam a good man named Enoch lived in close communion with God. And 

God chose Enoch to be His messenger to plead with the people to repent and to forsake the evil ways they had learned and to remember the teachings of Adam. There were many who listened to Enoch and began to live righteously. But very many did not heed God's messenger. After a while the people missed Enoch for he came among them no more, God had taken him from the earth without tasting death that he might not suffer the judgment that was to come to the earth. 

Once more, however, Jehovah sent a messenger to the people of the earth. This time the messenger was to warn them of the punishment that was to come to them. For now the Creator was sorry that He had created the world and man and intended to destroy them. Choosing Noah, because of his righteousness, God told him of His plans saying: "The time is come to make an end to mankind, for they have filled the world with wickedness; therefore, I will destroy them. So, now, behold! I am bringing a flood of waters upon the earth to destroy all men; but with thee and all thy house I will make a covenant to save thee. Therefore, I command thee to build an ark after the fashion that I shall show thee; that therein thou, and all thy household and the living creatures of the earth, two of each kind, may be saved from destroying waters."

Noah and his sons, Shem, Ham, and Japheth believed the words that the Lord had spoken and they set to work to build the ark as they were instructed. It was a great ship with many compartments for the housing of the people and the animals to be saved and their food. Within and without the ark made of cedar

wood was coated with pitch so that the waters of the flood might not injure it.

It took many years for Noah and his sons to build the ark. When the people saw the work of the men they marveled at it. But when Noah explained the message of God that had come to him directing him to build the great ship, they mocked him and accused him of madness. But the men went on building and as they did so they never ceased to warn the people of the great disaster that was coming. But none listened, going about their way, doing as they pleased as was their custom. 

At length the great ship was finished, the birds of the air and the beasts of the field were gathered and the food for them stored away. Then God spoke once more to His servant Noah saying: "Now all is ready. In seven days will I cause it to rain upon the earth for forty days and forty nights, and the waters will destroy everything upon the face of the earth. Only thee and thy family will I save."

Thereupon Noah and his family, his sons and their families entered the ark. And God sealed the door that none other might enter therein. Then after seven days the rains came, floods of water such as never had been seen before.

At first the people were not afraid for they had not heeded the words of Noah. But when the waters covered the lands of the plains and valleys they were afraid and fled to the tops of the hills. When they saw the great ship builded by Noah tossing safely upon the waters they knew in very deed that it was God who had spoken to them through Noah. At last even the hilltops were covered and the punishment of God was complete. And then the rain stopped.

For many days the ark drifted about on the great waters. Then as the sun shone and the winds blew the waters abated inch by inch. At last the ark came to rest upon a mountain named Arrarat. Then Noah sent

forth a dove to see if the land was dry. But the bird returned for there was no place for it to rest. Again in seven days Noah sent forth the bird. This time it returned with an olive leaf in its beak and Noah knew that the waters had receded below the tops of the olive trees on the hillsides. The third time that Noah sent the tiny bird forth, it did not return and he knew that once more the earth was uncovered from the great waters of destruction.

When the ark was opened, the living creatures went forth to their own way of life. But the first act of 

Noah and his family was to build an altar and offer a sacrifice of thanksgiving to the Lord who had protected them. And God was pleased with the gratitude of Noah and He placed in the sky a beautiful rainbow. 

As Noah looked upward from the altar he saw the great arch of color across the sky. Then the voice of 

God was heard saying: "Behold! I will make with you a covenant. When

I do set My bow in the cloud across the earth thou and I shall remember the promise that I do make now, that no more shall I destroy with water all living things. 

While the earth remaineth, seed-time and harvest, and cold and heat, and summer and winter, and day and night shall not cease."

Once more the earth was new and ready for the service of mankind and Noah and his sons went abroad on the land to multiply and to replenish it also to make it fruitful once more.

THE BEGINNING OF NATIONS-Genesis 10:1-11:9 

After the great Flood, Noah and his sons and their families spread abroad upon the land until they came to the grassy plains of the great rivers later given the names of Tigris and Euphrates. Remembering the goodness of God to them they continued to worship Him and make offerings to Him. But the time came

after many generations when there were a multitude of families dwelling in that land. Then it was that some of them thinking of their own safety and forgetful of the covenant God had made with Noah decided to build a city that all might dwell within and be safe. Speaking one to another they said: "Come let us build a city for ourselves for our safety so that we may not be scattered from one another, but may abide in strength. And for our worship let us build a mighty tower whose head shall reach to the heavens.'

Then the people began to build the city. As there was no stone in the land of the rivers, they took the clay and molded it into bricks to build with. As they built the city their hearts turned with pride to their work. 

As they built the great tower of worship they thought more of its greatness than they thought of its purpose, until at length they forgot entirely that it was built for the worship of Jehovah, but thought only of the safety it would give them in case of another great Flood. 

When God saw the work of the people and the pride of their hearts, He was sorely disappointed for it was not in this way that the whole earth could be peopled. Then, too, God was displeased that so soon they should lose their trust in the covenant He had made with 

Noah for the security of mankind.

Thereupon God planned once more to help mankind to fulfill His purpose that all of the earth be made the home of man. To do this the Lord brought confusion to the minds and tongues of men so that they no longer understood the purposes of one another.

Now it happened that as the people continued with the building of their city with its great tower they found that they no longer understood the words of each other, and bickering broke out among them. Households began to quarrel among themselves, workmen. began to disagree over the plans of the city. It was not

long before confusion and jealousy filled the minds of the people and the building of the city was halted. Soon it came about that families could no longer understand one another, and many began to leave the city to go back to their plains following their flocks and herds with the seasons as their fathers had done before them. After a time all had gone. But the great tower remained standing aloft and, as the men of the plains looked upon it as it reached into the sky they called it "Babel" because of the confusion the building of it brought to the families of the earth.

In time the children of Noah were scattered abroad as God had intended. Some of the families of Shem followed the Euphrates to the land of the great rivers to be known later as Mesopotamia; some of the families of Ham followed the course of the great river to be named the Nile, while some of the families of Japheth followed along the shores of the great sea to be known later as the Mediterranean.

From these beginnings the great nations of the earth grew and thus the world was peopled. But what was more important, each nation took its place as part of God's plan for the progress and happiness of mankind.

Ят 

Abraham, A Man of God pur- used for the home of man. Man has achieved this 'T WAS God's plan that the whole earth be pose in many ways through the ages. Discovery, settlement, and conquest all have had a part to play. Many times, however, during the long history of the world God has had to reveal anew to man His purpose for the creation of the earth and His laws for the progress and happiness of mankind.

After the Flood the wandering life of the descendants of Noah led them from place to place along the great rivers where food was plentiful for themselves and for their flocks and herds. When some of the families found a desirable place to live they made settlements which as the generations passed grew into cities. It was in this way that the first nations and their civilizations began to grow.

Many families traveled southward along the fertile river plains of the great Tigris and Euphrates rivers and the races known as Assyrians, Babylonians, and 

Chaldeans with their great cities of Ninevah, Babylon, and Ur grew in wealth and fame. Many other family tribes traveled westward to the sea and settled. beauty and security of the valley of the Jordan river attracted some to make their homes there, living the wandering life of masters of flocks and herds. Others passed on still farther to the grassy plains of the Nile river and The

Egypt and the wonders that amaze us today, pyramids, temples, and tombs, grew apace. As families multiplied and became tribes, tribes became races and nations, in this way the peopling of the earth has gone on through the ages.

The stories of the races that have peopled the earth have much to reveal to us. Each race had its weakness as well as its greatness. Some rose to great glory, a glory never to be forgotten, and some passed away to be entirely forgotten; while some endured to become the races and nations we know today. But each in a strange way fulfilled God's commandment to his children: "To be fruitful, and multiply and replenish the earth, and subdue it; and have dominion over it." 

It was in this way that the earth as God created it, could be made a desirable home for mankind.

The forefathers of the Hebrews of the Bible story were the descendants of Shem, the son of Noah. It was during the third generation that the families of Shem began to separate. The family of Heber, the son Shelah, wandered to the west beyond the great river Euphrates. 

Later, this tribe became known as Hebrews, the name that has endured unto this day. Several more generations passed and Terah, who was the chieftain of the house of Heber, became restless and took his family and journeyed to the land of the Chaldeans near the mouth of the great rivers Tigris and Euphrates. It was near the city of Ur that Terah established his home and there his sons Abraham, Nahor, and Haran were born. 

ABRAHAM, SON OF TERAH-Genesis 11:27-31 

It was a strange setting, this new home of the Hebrews over four thousand years ago. The settlers in the land of the great rivers, known to us today as Mesopotamia, were the Babylonians and the Chaldees who had become wealthy and powerful nations. 

Their great

cities were very strange and exciting with many colored buildings and temples, huge statues, and busy streets crowded with traders exchanging silks and spices for cloth of camel's hair and wool and beautiful jewelry to take back to far away Egypt or Assyria. The luxurious, pleasure-loving ways of living of the people of the great city of Ur were very, very strange indeed to the plain and simple Hebrews of the desert.

The many-colored pyramid shaped temples of the city of Ur brought Abraham, son of Terah, the most to ponder about. Day after day he would watch the crowds of people who went there to offer gifts to the gods who were supposed to control the crops, the wind, the rain, the moon, and the sun. Often in the courts of the temples, Abraham would gaze at the huge statues of these gods. Very often he would spend hours reading the records of this people written upon tablets of clay to find the secret of the strange gods. But it was the festival days for the worship of Sin, the moon god, that had the greatest influence upon the thoughts of Abraham. Then thousands of people would follow whiterobed priests carrying a large golden statue of the moon god to the top of the great mountain-shaped temple over three hundred feet high. As the full moon rose in the east the chanting priests would place the god Sin on its throne. While the moon crossed the heavens shedding its silvery light over all, sacrifices were offered and strange ceremonies performed.

Perhaps it was the strange festival of the moon god that awoke in Abraham's memory the old stories of his people handed down from father to son, stories of one God, the God who made the earth as a home for man, the God who talked to Adam and gave him counsel and laws for the happiness of mankind. Or perhaps it was the wickedness of the worshipers of the gods of Ur that reminded Abraham of God's goodness to Noah and his family in saving them from the destruction of the

Flood. It all brought sorrow and great loneliness to Abraham and to his kinsfolk.

As the years passed the desire grew in the heart of Terah to return to the land of his youth, to the peace and contentment of its grassy plains dotted with groves of palm trees and Abraham yearned in his heart to know the true God, the living God, the creater of the earth, the loving God, many of the descendants of Noah had forgotten so completely. Abraham talked these things over with his father, Terah, and with Lot, his nephew.

To Sarah, his wife, he spoke often of the greatest desire of his heart to live with a people who knew and worshipped the true and living God. 

So Terah and Abraham led a great caravan of men, women, children, camels laden with possessions of all kinds, flocks of sheep and herds of cattle to a new home. 

The caravan journeyed northward by way of the long narrow plain between the great rivers to settle finally at Haran, for Terah was becoming frail with age. After a few years Terah died and Abraham became the chieftain of his tribe.

THE CALL OF ABRAHAM-Genesis 12:1-20 

The time was now at hand when God was ready to speak to mankind once more to give to them the knowledge of His being and His laws for their happiness. The sincere desires of Abraham to know God and to serve him had opened the way for the fulfillment of 

God's plan. So God spoke to Abraham saying: "Get thee out of thy country, and from thy kindred, and from thy father's house unto a land I will show thee; and I will make of thee a great nation. In thee shall all the families of the earth be blessed."

This command from God came as a great surprise to Abraham. But a far greater surprise was God's promise that from Abraham should come a great nation, as

Abraham had no son. The command to leave his country and kindred was a difficult one for now as chieftain of his tribe Abraham was a man of great wealth. To go to a new land meant sacrifice and hardships. What would Sarah, his childless wife say, and Lot, his nephew, whom Abraham looked upon as a son? Yet Abraham desired, before all else, to obey God's command.

After hearing God's command, Lot and Sarah were content to do as Abraham desired them. Soon all were ready for the great adventure. The caravan traveled westward across the desert to the mountains of Lebanon then on towards the Mediterranean Sea, a journey of four hundred miles. The first view of the land of Canaan with its tiny mud-walled villages on the rocky mountain slopes along the Jordan River was a disappointing picture. But Abraham continued on his way until he came to a beautiful valley, sheltered and green between mountain peaks. Here God spoke once more to 

Abraham saying: "Unto thy seed I give this land."

Then Abraham gathered stones and made an altar and offered a sacrifice of thankfulness for this new home in the beautiful valley of Shechem. On the foothills Abraham pastured his flocks and made his camp and called the place Bethel.

As time passed Abraham learned more of the tribes dwelling in the land of Canaan, who like himself were the descendants of Shem. He saw the crude ways of living of the Canaanites, and their cruel sacrifices of maidens and children to strange gods. This caused Abraham to wonder why God had brought him to this land. Perhaps, too, as he remembered the manners and customs. of the people of Ur, their gay clothing and rich jewelry, their homes and temples, and their festivals, he pondered much over the strange ways men had learned to live.

But Abraham lived in peace with his new country-

men. Often as they passed his tent as they crossed his valley home, he would invite them to share a meal of camel's milk, bread and dates with him. But it was when seasons came without rain, when the grass dried up and men and animals suffered for food that this "Promised 

Land" seemed so very disappointing. But Abraham never doubted the wisdom of God's command or that His promises to bless the children of men through him would be fulfilled.

Then there came a time when many seasons continued without rain in the land of Canaan and Abraham knew that he must plan to save himself and his family for God's service. So taking his flocks and herds and his servants he journeyed to the rich green lands of the Nile where food was plentiful. In Egypt, the land of plenty, Abraham found kings living in palaces while thousands of men served them as slaves; he found strange gods worshipped in great temples; and he found wickedness and great beauty strangely woven together in a new pattern of living. But here in Egypt life was easier for 

Abraham than ever before. The flocks and herds thrived on the grass lands of the Nile, the Egyptians were eager to buy the silken camel's hair cloth and soft woolen cloth woven by his servants, and thus his wealth greatly increased.

In due time, when the drought was over, Abraham returned to the land of Canaan, because it was the land God had told him to occupy. And once more he offered up sacrifices to God in thankfulness upon the altar at Bethel.

LOT'S CHOICE-Genesis 13:1-14:24 

Upon the return to Canaan all did not go well between Abraham and Lot. The herdsmen of Lot and those of Abraham began to quarrel because the green lands were more widely scattered than in Egypt and it was more difficult to feed the large flocks and herds. One

day Abraham saw the servants of Lot attack the men who were caring for his cattle so as to take for themselves the best feeding grounds. This grieved Abraham sorely, so he called his nephew to him saying: "Let there be no strife, I pray thee, between me and thee, between my herdsmen and thy herdsmen, for we be brethren. Is not the whole land before thee?"

And Abraham gave to Lot the choice of which land he would take. Knowing the land of Canaan as he did, Lot chose the Jordan Valley, the choicest place of all. Then Abraham and the nephew he loved as a son parted. As time passed Lot prospered and grew rich. 

Soon he left his tent home in the valley to dwell in the city of Sodom, a city known for its wickedness, its idleness, its drunkenness, and its cruel human sacrifices. 

When Lot left his own people to dwell among the Canaanites, God spoke once more to the faithful Abraham and blessed and comforted him with a new promise: "All the land that thou seest I will give to thee and thy children for ever. And I will make thy seed as the dust of the earth; so that if a man can number the dust of the earth then shall thy seed also be numbered."

Now Abraham took his household and went to the plains of Mamre, near the city of Hebron, to make his home. Although he and Sarah had no son and were growing old, the Hebrew chieftain never doubted but that God's promise would be fulfilled.

For many years the people living in the cities around Sodom, where Lot dwelt, had been forced to pay tribute to the powerful king of Elam. Each year a band of the king's warriors would come and take part of their cattle and goods. When they came on the thirteenth year, the leaders of the Canaanite cities refused to pay the tribute. The warriors returned to Elam emptyhanded but soon they came back again, this time in greater numbers. Not only did these plunderers take all

they wanted of cattle and goods, but many of the people were carried away to become slaves. Among these captives were Lot and his family. 

One of Lot's servants brought the news to Abraham of the sad plight of his nephew. Without hesitating Abraham gathered together his servants and his neighbors and went to the rescue of Lot. In a few days the robbers and their captives were overtaken as they camped in a small valley. Waiting until the darkness of night, Abraham and his small band surprised the warriors of Elam and not only rescued Lot and the other captives but their possessions also. 

The journey homeward was made with joyful hearts. As the company came near to the cities of the plains the King of Sodom came to meet them. In gratitude he offered to Abraham the plunder that had been regained, which was of great value. Also there came to greet Abraham and his servants, Melchizedek, the high priest of Salem, bringing bread and wine to refresh them. Lifting up his hands, the aged priest blessed Abraham in the name of the living God for saving some of His children from slavery. Thereupon Abraham took one-tenth of the gift offered to him by the King of Sodom and gave it to the high priest. But Abraham would take none of the gift for himself.

The captives returned to their cities with the King of Sodom and Lot returned with them. And Abraham continued his way rejoicing in the testimony that the blessing of Melchizedek had given to him, the knowledge that a witness for God had always been on earth and that in ways unknown to man God's holy priesthood would always remain on earth working surely if unseen to fulfill God's purposes.

GOD'S COVENANT WITH ABRAHAM- 

Genesis 15:1-19:26 

Many years had passed since Abraham obeyed God's command to come to the land of Canaan, the

land promised to him and his seed for ever. Yet after so long a time Abraham owned no land of his own, the 

Canaanites, Hittites, and Philistines still owned the land, and Abraham and Sarah had no son. But many times the heart of Abraham was very heavy, for now Lot had gone his way, there was only his servant, Eliezer, to be his heir. 

Once again Jehovah came to Abraham and renewed the promise to him that he should be the father of a nation as numberless as the stars and as countless as the sands on the seashore. And Abraham was commanded to prepare a sacrifice that a sacred covenant might be made. When the sacrifice was ready awaiting for the sign of the Lord, Abraham fell into a deep sleep. 

Then the voice of God came out of the darkness telling Abraham of the things that would happen to his descendants, of their home in a strange land, of their bondage and their delivery, and of their inheritance in the Promised Land. And as the voice ceased, out of the darkness came a flame from the sacrifice and Abraham knew that Jehovah had set a seal upon the covenant 

He had made.

HAGAR AND ISHMAEL-Genesis 16:1-17; 21:1-21 

Sarah, the wife of Abraham was growing old, still she had no son. At this time it was the custom of the Hebrews to have more than one wife. So Sarah gave her handmaid, Hagar, to her husband for his wife, thinking that in this way God's promise could be fulfilled. Perhaps it was the lonely heart of Sarah or it may have been her own disappointment that made her very unkind to Hagar, for Sarah drove her servant from her home. Sad and lonely Hagar set out on the road that led to Egypt, her own country. But as she rested by a wayside well in the desert Hagar heard the voice of an angel telling her to return to the home of her husband, Abraham, and her mistress, Sarah.

Then the angel said: "Thou shalt bear a son and his name shall be called Ishmael, because the Lord hath heard thy affliction."

The son that was born to Hagar grew up to be a strong, quick-tempered boy. Sarah's heart continued to be full of jealousy to see the son of Abraham that was not hers, growing so well. But Abraham took great joy in his son. Thirteen years passed and Abraham began to wonder if Ishmael was the son that God had promised to him.

After the birth of Isaac, Sarah once more demanded that Hagar be sent away.

So Hagar took the boy Ishmael and traveled towards Egypt, the land of her youth. The desert trail was hard and long and soon they became too weak to travel for want of food and water. Hagar went away from her son, not being able to stand to see his suffering. 

But God was watching over Hagar and her son. Speaking to the woman, he showed her a spring of water. 

Then Hagar was promised that her son should be the father of a great nation. So Hagar and Ishmael made their home in the desert and the boy became the father of a sturdy tribe of desert wanderers that have multiplied and have endured even to this day. Today they are known to the world as the desert wanderers, the Arabians.

Seated one day in the shelter of his tent at noonday, the aged Hebrew saw three strangers approaching.

As was his custom he hastened to greet them and urged them to pause and rest from the heat of the day. While the servants prepared meat for the guests Sarah baked cakes of fine meal. As the strangers partook of the meal in the shade of the great oak outside of Abraham's tent, one of them asked, “Where is thy wife, Sarah?” 

Abraham answered, "Behold, she is in the tent.'

And the promise made so often was made again, "Lo, Sarah, thy wife, shall have a son."

36 

And Sarah inside the tent hearing these words laughed in her heart, because of her great age. Then the speaker reminded Abraham of the promises made so often, that all the nations of the earth should be blessed through his descendants.

THE DESTRUCTION OF SODOM—Genesis 18-20 

When the heavenly guests left the tent, Abraham went with them and they traveled towards the cities of Sodom and Gomorrah. The two angels passed on to the cities and Abraham and the Lord were left alone. 

Then God told Abraham of his plan to destroy the cities because of the wickedness of the people. Abraham who remembered his nephew Lot and his family, pled with God to spare the cities if perchance any righteous people might be made to suffer with the wicked. 

And Jehovah promised that if the angels found but fifty good people, the cities would be saved. And

Abraham pled again and again, to save the cities if forty-five good people could be found; then for forty, for thirty, for twenty, or even ten good men to be saved. 

And God listened to the merciful pleading of this servant and promised to save the wicked cities if but ten righteous persons could be found.

Meanwhile the two angels had entered the city of Sodom and had found the household of Lot. Finding out the wickedness of the city, the heavenly messengers warned Lot to take his family and leave Sodom at once. 

When Lot came to warn his daughters and their husbands of the danger to the city they mocked him and took no heed of the warning. And Lot did not heed the warning but continued to linger over his home and his possessions. But the angels returned and led him out of the gates of the city with his wife and his two young daughters, saying: "Escape for thy life, look not behind, but flee to the mountains.'

Even as Lot and his family started on their way the lightning flashed from the sky and the wicked cities. were destroyed by fire. Instead of being grateful that she was safe, Lot's wife thinking of her home and her treasures turned and stood looking with longing to the burning city of Sodom. And when later her family looked back to where she had lingered there stood a pillar of salt.

As Abraham looked anxiously towards the cities of the plains and saw the smoke-filled sky, he knew that the Lord had not found even ten righteous men living therein.

ABRAHAM'S SUPREME LOYALTY- 

Genesis 21:1-20; 22:1-13 

At last God gave to Abraham and Sarah the son that had been promised to them so long. He was named 

Isaac because of joy that had come to them. When the boy was about two years old, Abraham gave a great feast in honor of his son. Perhaps the loving heart of

Sarah imagined that Hagar's son Ishmael treated her son unkindly, or perhaps Ishmael felt the change in his position in his father's household and was jealous over the homage given to the young child. So Sarah demanded that Hagar and her son be sent away. Abraham consented with sadness, for he loved his son Ishmael. 

Under the loving care of Abraham and Sarah,

Isaac grew up to be a thoughtful youth. He learned how the true and living God had directed the lives of his parents, of the great blessings that had been given to them. Also Isaac was taught to worship the Lord and to obey his commands. Many times the boy accompanied his father when he went to offer sacrifice to God. As the son of his old age grew so strong and so dutiful, Abraham's love for his son grew greater. As the years passed and the loving father saw the Canaan-

ites offer their first-born sons for sacrifice to their gods, he often wondered if he loved the son given to him by a miracle better than he loved the God who had given the son to him. Also Abraham wondered if the God he worshiped expected such sacrifices as the Canaanites made to their gods. The time came when Abraham was to know the answer to these problems troubling his heart.

God spoke again to Abraham, commanding him to take his young son to the altar on Mount Moriah and offer him as a sacrifice. Without hesitating Abraham made all ready to obey the command. The journey took three days. Arriving at the mountain 

Isaac asked, "My father, here is the altar and the wood, but where is the lamb for the sacrifice?" 

And Abraham answered his son, "God himself will provide a lamb for the burnt offering." Then together, father and son prepared the altar for the sacrifice. And Abraham took his son, bound him, and laid him on the altar preparing to do as he had been commanded. The same moment a voice from heaven came to Abraham saying: "Lay not thy hand upon the lad nor harm him. 

Now I know that thou fearest God since thou hast not withheld thy son from me."

In a nearby thicket Abraham saw a ram entangled in a bush, the burnt offering that Abraham had promised Isaac that the Lord would provide. In great thanksgiving the sacrifice was made, for now Abraham knew that God in His loving kindness did not require human sacrifice from His children in worship but only obedience. But what was more, Abraham knew that he had proved faithful to the test God had given to him, and his happiness was very great.

Isaac continued in the peaceful home of his parents to grow to manhood, learning from them all

they knew of righteous living. Sarah died when she reached the age of a hundred and twenty-seven years and Abraham bought a burial cave in the land of 

Machpelah, so that Sarah might be buried in the Promised Land. To all who pass this sacred spot today, if they be Mohammedan, Jew, or Christian, they pause in reverence to Abraham and Sarah, servants of the living God.

BY FAITH ABRAHAM, WHEN HE WAS CALLED, OBEYED-Hebrews 11:8; Romans 4:17-21 

Two thousand years later when the descendants of Abraham, the Jews lived in Palestine and the nearby lands, it was written by Paul, the Apostle, who was descended from Abraham, in the Epistle to the 

Hebrews: 

"By faith, Abraham, when he was called to go out into a place which he should receive for an inheritance, obeyed, and he went out not knowing whither he went. "By faith, he lived in the land of promise as in a strange country, dwelling in tents with Isaac and Jacob, the heirs with him to the same promise. "For Abraham looked for a city which hath foundations, whose builder and maker is God.' 

And Paul, the apostle, wrote also to the Romans: "Abraham, who against hope believed in hope that he might become the father of many nations. . . .

He staggered not at the promise of God through unbelief, but was strong in faith giving glory to God; and being fully persuaded that what God had promised, He was able also to perform."

God's Covenant With Isaac and Jacob 

THE 

HE strength of a people or a nation is builded upon ideals of marriage and family life. From family purity strong races were built, and from racial purity national unity and strength grew in older times. 

Among many of the peoples in ancient days it was the custom for parents to choose wives for their sons. and husbands for their daughters. It was in this way that tribal purity was preserved from generation to generation. Out of this ideal races grew in unity and strength. 

Family purity, however, is more than mating, it is the sacredness of the human relationships of man and wife. When marriage and family ties are held sacred purity of life is the strength upon which new generations come into being and truth is kept alive on the earth from generation to generation. Marriage is God's plan for the happiness and progress of mankind. 

Abraham became the founder of a great race, the Hebrews. But the building of a people through which all mankind might be blessed was the responsibility of the descendants of Abraham. And Abraham knew that the purity of his seed in marriage and family relations would in generations to come be the strength to build that people. In finding a wife for his son, Isaac from among his own people Abraham was obeying more than a tribal custom he was establishing the sacred

pattern of marriage revealed by God for all mankind to follow.

The long life of Abraham, the servant of God, was fast approaching its close. There was still one of the most important tasks of his life yet to be done, to find a wife for Isaac. And the heart of the aged father was very anxious that Isaac would love a maiden fitted to become a true companion and good wife, one who would pass on to her children the love of God and the knowledge of their great heritage. Because the women of the tribes of Canaan were idol worshipers and did not know the true and living God, Abraham decided to find a wife for his son among the maidens of his own people in far away Haran.

Abraham called his faithful servant Eliezer to him and said: "Go to my country, to the city of my kindred, and take a wife for my son Isaac."

Eliezer knew his master's purpose well for he, too, loved and worshipped the living God. So taking with him ten camels, servants, and many costly gifts, Eliezer set out on the long journey to Haran. The shadows of evening were falling when the little caravan neared the city, so Eliezer caused his camels to rest by a well. As he watched the shepherds and maidens as they came to the well for water he thought of the purpose of his coming to Haran and its grave importance. Then 

Eliezer went alone and prayed to God for guidance saying: "O Lord God of my master Abraham, behold I stand by the well and the daughters of Haran come hither to draw water. Let it come to pass that the damsel of whom I shall ask a drink shall not only say to me, 'Drink,' but let it come to pass that she shall say furthermore, 'I will draw water for thy camels also.'

And let that same damsel be she whom thou hast appointed for Thy servant, Isaac.'

Even as Eliezer prayed, a beautiful maiden with a water pitcher on her shoulder came down to the well. 

As she turned homeward after filling her pitcher 

Abraham's servant spoke to her saying: "I pray thee let me drink a little water from thy pitcher." "Drink, my lord," answered the maiden and she lowered the vessel of water from her shoulder that the stranger might drink. And when he had finished drinking she said: "I will draw water for thy camels also."

When the maiden's task was done, Eliezer took from the gifts he had brought to Haran some golden earrings and bracelets and gave them to her asking: "Tell me whose daughter art thou? Is there room in thy father's house for me to lodge?" 

When the maiden answered, "I am Rebekah, the daughter of Bethuel, the son of Nahor," Eliezer knew that God had surely guided him to achieve that for which Abraham had sent him. And the servant of 

Abraham bowed his head saying: "Blessed is the Lord God of my master, Abraham." 

Then Rebekah ran to tell her family what had happened at the well. When Laban, the brother of 

Rebekah, saw the costly gifts the stranger at the well had given to his sister, he hastened to invite him to come to his father's house, saying: "Come in, thou blessed of the Lord; wherefore standest thou without? I have prepared the house, and room for the camels.' said:

When the evening meal was set before him, Eliezer "I will not eat until I have told my errand. I am 

Abraham's servant. The Lord has blessed my master greatly in the land of Canaan, giving him flocks and herds, silver and gold, and many servants. Now hath

my master sent me to you, his kinsfolk, to find a wife for Isaac, his son."

Then Eliezer told of his coming to the well and his prayer for God's help. As Laban and Bethuel, the father of Rebekah, listened they became assured that 

Eliezer had spoken truthfully and answered saying: "I know that this is God's sending. Here is Rebekah; take her and go, and let her be the wife of Isaac as God hath willed."

Eliezer gave to Rebekah and her family the gifts his master had sent, and the meal became a feast of rejoicing in Bethuel's house.

The next morning Eliezer wished to make ready to leave Haran. Laban and his mother pled that 

Rebekah might stay a few days longer in the home of her loved ones, but the maiden consented to do as Abraham's servant desired. So the caravan started on its homeward way. And Rebekah and her maids riding on camels traveled the same road that Abraham and Sarah had passed over to the Land of Promise many years before.

Many weeks had passed since Eliezer had left the camp of Abraham and his return was awaited eagerly. 

Then one evening the outline of a moving caravan was seen in the sunset sky. Isaac came to welcome Rebekah with great honor, and gave her the tent of his mother for her own. When Abraham and his son listened to 

Eliezer's story, they were well pleased with Rebekah, that she came so willingly to a new home far away from her family. But Abraham was comforted most of all in the knowledge that God had guided the choice of a wife for Isaac.

It was a time of great rejoicing in the camp of 

Abraham, the wedding of Isaac. Isaac loved Rebekah tenderly and Abraham was greatly comforted in his old age by his son's wife.

After twenty years the twin sons of Isaac and

Rebekah were born. Soon after Abraham died in peace, believing that all God's promises to him would be fulfilled through Isaac and Rebekah and their sons. 

SELLING A BIRTHRIGHT-Genesis 25:19-27:41 

The family of Isaac lived for many years on the plains of Mamre following the way of life Abraham had established. The wealth of Isaac increased greatly until, in fact, his neighbors envied him. So Isaac took his family, his servants, and his flocks and went to live in another part of Canaan. Following the custom of 

Abraham, the first thing that Isaac did in his new home was to build an altar and worship God. The name given to the new home was Beersheba.

The twin sons of Isaac, Esau and Jacob, grew up much as their father had done. They were strangely different in many ways. Esau, bold and reckless and full of energy, the first born of the twins, became the pride of his father, Isaac. Jacob, quiet and thoughtful, became a source of joy to his mother, Rebekah. This family so small, with blessings so great grew up, not in peace and harmony but in discord, the unhappiness that grows out of selfishness and misunderstanding. 

Often Isaac called his sons to him at the close of the day and told them of God and His purposes for His children, the creation of earth and man, the disobedience of Adam and Eve in the Garden of Eden, and the wickedness that brought about the Flood. But when Isaac told the story of God's dealings with Abraham and the promises made to him, he always urged his sons to remember that it was through them and their children that these blessings would come to mankind.

Esau, the first-born, listened restlessly to the words of his father, such things meant much less to him than a hunting trip. But with Jacob it was different, he thought about them day after day. He often asked himself the questions:

"Why is Esau the first-born? Why is it that he, Jacob, was not the first-born son? Why was it the custom of his tribe to give so much to the first-born? 

Why should he, Jacob, have no such birthright?" 

And Rebekah, who knew of God's promises to the seed of Abraham, wished in her heart that Jacob might be the one to receive the birthright and blessings of the first-born of his tribe. Over and over in her mind she questioned: "Why should Esau, the reckless and careless son, be the head of the tribe when he cared so little about 

God's promises? Why was it that Jacob, her favorite son, could not have been the first-born?"

One day Jacob was preparing a meal of lentils in his tent. It was the savory smell of this food that greeted Esau as he came in tired and exhausted from hunting. "Give me some of the food," Esau asked, “for I am faint."

Before he knew it Jacob framed the words and he answered, "Sell me thy birthright, then I will give thee the food." "If I die of hunger what can a birthright profit me?" questioned Esau weakly because of his hunger. 

So Jacob fed his hungry brother. Esau refreshed went on his way thinking very little over what had happened.

The everyday life of Esau had become a source of great sorrow to Isaac. He was a reckless youth. It seemed that he would rather mingle with the Hittites, the plundering tribes of the desert, or the pagan Canaanites of the plains, than with his own people. It was strange that the promises made to Abraham meant so little to him, the first-born of his tribe, perhaps they seemed too far away to worry about now. It was when 

Esau married two of the Hittite women that Isaac's heart was troubled most. Now it would be more difficult than ever for Esau to mend his reckless ways. These

two women who were pagans and knew not the God revealed to Abraham could have little place in helping Esau to be worthy of his birthright and its responsibilities. Then, too, there would be the children of Esau. 

What of their place in God's plan? Many times Isaac spoke to God in prayer of this great sorrow that had come to him.

As Isaac grew older, a sore affliction came to him, he became blind. Now he left the care of his many possessions to his sons and his servants. Thinking that he was to die soon, Isaac felt that the time was now come to follow the custom of his people and give to his first-born son, Esau, the birthright and blessing. So 

Isaac called for Esau to come to him saying: "My son, behold, I am old and know not the day of my death. Therefore, I pray thee, take thy quiver and thy bow, and get me some venison. Make me savory meat such as I love; that I may bless thee before 

I die. 

Now Rebekah heard the words of Isaac to Esau, and she made the plans for Jacob instead of Esau to receive his father's blessing. In a few hours Jacob dressed in Esau's clothing that smelled of the outdoors, his hands and neck covered with heavy skins lest his father touched him, carried a dish of delicious venison to his father saying: "I am Esau, thy first-born son. Eat of the venison 

I have prepared as thou commanded me! 

Then Isaac said: "Bring me the venison that I may eat and bless thee."

When Jacob came near to his father, Isaac smelled the clothing of his son, it was the smell of the out-ofdoors which always marked Esau. Then Isaac blessed 

Jacob with the blessing given by God to Abraham. 

It was only a little while later that Esau came to his father bearing a meal of venison. Then Isaac found

out that he had given his blessing to Jacob. Esau cried out in bitterness: "My brother has taken away both my birthright and my blessing." Now Esau realized that the birthright he had sold to satisfy his hunger was actually taken away from him. Then he begged his father saying: "Hast thou only one blessing? Bless me, even me also my father!" 

And Isaac gave his son Esau a blessing promising him the blessings of wealth and conquest by the sword. 

Henceforth Esau hated Jacob and awaited but the time of his father's death so that he could kill his brother and take back his birthright. 

The first fruits of the blessing Jacob had gained brought to him his father's sorrow and his brother's hatred. Rebekah fearing for her son's life urged him to flee to her brother Laban at Haran until Esau's anger cooled. Rebekah pled with Isaac that Jacob be sent to 

Laban to take a wife from his own kinsfolk that he might not marry as Esau had done, women of Canaan.

Isaac knew that Rebekah spoke wisely for now Jacob must carry out God's purpose. It was his birthright, so he called Jacob to him and said: "Arise. Go to Haran and take a wife from the daughters of Laban, and may God Almighty bless thee. 

May He make thee fruitful and multiply thee, that thou mayest inherit the land which God gave unto Abraham."

So Jacob left his home in loneliness and sorrow, and Rebekah, who had dreamed so much for the son she loved so dearly, saw him go to return to her no more. 

And Esau seeing his brother leave for Haran to marry a wife as Isaac wished, let his anger grow even against his father. To displease his father more, Esau took other wifes from among the Canaanite women. 

Taking his wives and all his possessions, Esau

went away from the land of his people and established himself by conquering the desert tribes. The land of 

Esau was known as Edom henceforward. 

WORKING FOR A WIFE 

Jacob, the son of Isaac, was a strange character, a strange mixture of faults and virtues. His behavior to his father and brother seemed to grow out of the jealous desires of his heart. Yet Jacob must have believed in the God of Abraham and Isaac, perhaps it was because he believed in the promises given to Abraham, believed in them so much that he stooped to deceitfulness to get the birthright to be the head of his tribe. 

Now Jacob was on his way to Haran. Alone he traveled the way Abraham had passed. When darkness came he paused to rest, taking a stone for a pillow. As he slept he dreamed of steps leading from earth to heaven and angels descending and ascending the steps. Then 

Jacob heard the voice of God saying: "I am the Lord God of Abraham and Isaac. In thee and in thy seed shall all the families of the earth be blessed. I am with thee and will keep thee in all places whither thou goest.

Jacob awoke from this wonderful vision filled with amazement. As he recalled the things that he had seen and heard, Jacob was filled with a new understanding and new hope. Then he took the stone upon which he had rested and poured oil upon it as an offering and made a vow: "If God will be with me and keep me as He promised, of all that God gives me to Him I will give a tenth."

The place of the vision and his vow Jacob named Bethel. It was a new beginning for Jacob, a beginning from which the house and tribes of Israel were to grow. 

At last Jacob came to Haran, the home of Terah, of Abraham, and of Rebekah. Coming upon some shepherds, Jacob asked for his uncle Laban. But even

as he asked, they saw Laban's daughter, Rachel, coming to the well.

Overjoyed at finding his own kinsfolk, Jacob kissed Rachel and told her who he was, of his mother

Rebekah, and of his grandfather Abraham. When Laban heard of his nephew's arrival he came to meet him and to make him welcome. For a month Jacob stayed in the home of Laban as his guest, yet helping with the flocks and proving himself to be a fine herdsman. And

Laban desiring to keep Jacob with him as a servant, offered to pay his nephew wages. But Jacob who had loved Rachel ever since the day he saw her coming to the well answered: "I will serve thee seven years if thou wilt give me 

Rachel for a wife." 

And Laban made a bargain with Jacob, and Jacob worked willingly and eagerly for Rachel. And the time passed quickly for he loved her so much. 

When seven years had passed Jacob asked Laban for his wife. Then Laban prepared a marriage feast as was the custom of his people. But it was Leah not Rachel who was taken to the tent of Jacob to be his wife. 

After the feasting was over Jacob went to his tent and found it was Leah and not Rachel he had wedded. 

Jacob had been deceived and he sought Laban in anger to know why Rachel was not given to him. The cunning Laban answered: 

It is not the custom of my people to give the younger daughter before the elder in marriage. I will give thee Rachel also if thou wilt work for me still seven years.

Since Jacob loved Rachel so much, he consented to Laban's bargain. Thereupon a second wedding feast was planned and this time Rachel was brought to the tent of Jacob. Because of the great love of Jacob for 

Rachel he continued to work willingly and honestly for Laban until the second seven years was completed.

After fourteen years of work Jacob had but his wives and children, he had no wealth. So he made another bargain with Laban, this time for a share in the flocks and herds. Because of his skill and his diligence as a herdsman, the flocks of Jacob grew in size in spite of the fact that Laban kept changing the bargains so as to make his own share greater than that of Jacob. In fact, so greatly did Jacob prosper that the sons of Laban accused him of stealing their father's wealth.

By this time twenty years had passed and Jacob was filled with a deep longing for the place of his childhood. He desired to take his wives and his children to a home away from Laban's family. Speaking with 

Leah and Rachel of his longings, all agreed on a plan to leave Haran. So when the time came that Laban went to a distant part of his land, Jacob took his family and his possessions and started on the journey to Canaan.

When Laban found that Jacob had gone, he followed him in anger taking his servants with him so as to overpower Jacob and force him to return. But 

God spoke to Laban in a dream and warned him not to harm Jacob. The next day when uncle and nephew met, Laban only accused Jacob of unkindness in taking away his daughters and their children without letting him bid them farewell. Then Jacob and his uncle. 

Laban, parted as friends.

JACOB, FATHER OF ISRAEL— 

Genesis 32; 33:1-16; 35:9-15

As he neared the home of Isaac in Canaan, Jacob began to realize how greatly he had wronged his brother Esau. So he decided to send a message to his brother acknowledging him as the chief of the tribe. Sending some of his servants with the message Jacob told them to say to Esau: "Thy servant Jacob has been staying with Laban

for twenty years and now returns and desires that he may be friendly with thee.'

When the servants returned with the word that 

Esau was coming to meet him with four hundred armed men, Jacob was greatly distressed. In his trouble 

Jacob prayed to God for aid, confessing that he was not worthy of the blessings of Abraham. That night when the caravan came to rest Jacob chose the best of his camels, goats, sheep, kine, and asses and sent them as a gift to Esau. Then Jacob sent his wives and his children with his servants and his flocks and herds across the river to safety.

As Jacob waited alone on the hillside the memory of his wrongdoing to Esau made his heart full of sorrow. While he prayed and watched, an angel came to

Jacob and appeared as if to strike him, but Jacob struggled to prevent it. Then the two wrestled together. All night long Jacob tried to rid himself of the angel.

As day began to break Jacob was at length overcome. 

Instead of fear and guilt of the night before in his heart, Jacob felt as a new person full of hope and love. As the angel started to leave him, Jacob clung to him pleading: "I will not let Thee go, except Thou bless me." "Thy name shall be called no more Jacob, but

Israel, for thou hast striven with men and with God and hast overcome," the angel answered.

Fearing nothing now, Jacob joined his family across the river and awaited the coming of Esau. As 

Esau came near, Jacob went forward to meet him and stood with bowed head. Esau, no thought of vengeance in his heart, threw his arms around Jacob and kissed him and the two men wept together. Then Esau remembering the gift that his brother had sent asked: "Wherefore dids't thou send me of thy flocks and herds? I have enough of my own!"

When Jacob pled with Esau to accept the gift as

a token of forgiveness, Esau consented. Then the brothers separated, Esau going southward to his mountain home and Jacob towards Shechem. 

Now there came to Jacob the greatest sorrow of his life, the death of Rachel. Many and great had been the trials of Rachel. Her sister, Leah, had been given six sons, but for many years Rachel had no children.

Then a son, Joseph, was given to her. Now in the shadow of the Promised Land a second son was born, and death came too. The sorrowing father named this, the youngest of his twelve sons, Benjamin. Jacob buried his beloved Rachel in a place which afterwards became known as Bethlehem.

At last Jacob came to his home. The rejoicing of the aged Isaac knew no bounds in the twelve sons of Jacob. But Rebekah was not there to greet the son she had sent away as she thought for but a few months. 

Again deep sorrow came to Jacob for he loved his mother greatly.

When Isaac died Esau came back to his old home. 

Together the brothers buried their father with Rebekah, his wife, Abraham, his father, and Sarah, his mother, in the cave of Machpelah. Then Esau returned to Edom to the way of life he loved, hunting and plundering, and Jacob became the chief of his father's tribe.

A long period of learning had come to Jacob before he was worthy of his birthright, the heir of the promises made to Abraham. He had conquered the weaknesses of his character; he had built his family life upon a belief in the true and living God; he had seen 

God and had vowed to serve Him and keep His commandments.

As the years passed the wandering tribes of Canaan still occupied the "Promised Land," but Jacob knew that some day God would give the land to Israel as He had promised. Many years later in far-away

133 Egypt Jacob spent his last years and dying blessed his children saying: "God shall be with you and bring you again unto the land of your fathers."

Joseph's Companionship With God

JAC ACOB, the Father of Israel, brought his family and his possessions to the home of his fathers, the grassy plains of Hebron. It made a large encampment the four households of Jacob: Leah and her sons, Reuben, Simeon, Levi, Issachar, Zebulun, and Judah; 

Zilpah and her sons Gad and Asher; Bilhah and her sons 

Dan and Naphtali; and the motherless household of 

Rachel with Joseph and Benjamin.

The neighboring tribes of Canaan looked upon Jacob as a great chieftain because of his wealth in flocks and herds. But Jacob and his family lived apart from the Canaanites because of their worship of the living God.

In all things Jacob sought the companionship of the Lord God who had blessed him so greatly. His children were never allowed to forget the greatness of their inheritance. Old and young alike were gathered to the tent of their father from time to time to hear of Abraham and of Isaac, their experiences and their testimonies of God and his promises to them.

JOSEPH, THE DREAMER-Genesis 37:1-36 

Joseph now a youth of seventeen years had grown to be the joy and comfort of Jacob. It was the older sons of Jacob who became the herdsmen of the large flocks of their father. It was a most natural thing for Jacob to

give a great deal of love and tenderness to his youngest sons, the motherless children of the beloved Rachel for whom he still sorrowed. But it was Joseph the firstborn of Rachel who became the favorite of Jacob, beloved above all his sons.

Joseph grew up sharing very little in the life of his brothers. However, now he was old enough, Jacob often sent him with food and messages to his brothers herding their sheep in nearby valleys. It was on these errands that Joseph saw that his brothers had become rough and uncouth in their language and their actions, like the Canaanite shepherds, but so unlike their father Jacob. Jacob often counseled with his older sons urging them to remember their heritage as descendants of Abraham and not to follow the ways of the rough herdsmen of the plains. So it was that Joseph's brothers began to dislike him partly because of the difference in their lives and partly because of Jacob's great love for Rachel's son.

The faith of Jacob in the promises of God to Abraham made a deep impression upon Joseph. He was proud to be one of the sons of Israel, and in his heart he was anxious to please God as his fathers had done.

Now Jacob did a strange and unwise thing. To show his love for Joseph, he gave him a beautiful tunic or coat such as those worn by rich chieftains who did no work. When the sons of Leah saw the beautiful coat of camel's hair richly embroidered in many colors they were angry and said one to another: "Why should our father treat Joseph so?" Are we not the sons of our father also? Do we not tend his flocks with care? Is our brother, Joseph, better than we are?" 

There came a day when Joseph told his brothers of a strange dream saying: "Hear, I pray you, this dream which I have had!

We, the sons of Jacob, were binding sheaves in the field,

and lo my sheaf stood upright while your sheaves bowed to my sheaf." 

Once more Joseph dreamed, and this dream he told to his father and his brothers saying: "Behold, I have had another dream! It seemed that the sun, moon, and eleven stars did bow down to me."

After this the hatred of his brothers knew no bounds. Why should they bow down to Joseph? Was he not the second youngest son and not the elder son? 

How could be become the head of the family? Even Jacob, his father, openly rebuked Joseph saying: "Shall I and thy mother and thy brothers bow down ourselves to thee as if thou were a king?" But in his heart Jacob believed that the dreams would come true for he knew that God revealed His purposes in dreams to His children.

The season came for the flocks of Jacob to go to the valley of Shechem, fifty miles away. After they had been away some time Jacob sent Joseph to visit his brothers to see how they were getting along. Joseph went gladly to do his father's bidding, and not finding his brothers at Shechem he followed them along the hillside to Dothan. When the brothers saw the figure of a young man coming towards them they knew it to be Joseph because of the coat they envied so much. The hatred in their hearts rose up and they said to one another: "Behold! The dreamer cometh! Now let us slay him and cast him in the pit! Let us say to our father, 'Some evil beast hath devoured him.' Then we shall see what will become of his dreams." 

But Reuben, the eldest brother, would not consent to kill Joseph so he coaxed his brothers to put Joseph into a pit that was nearby. For it was in Reuben's heart to rescue his brother later and let him return to their father. The shepherds agreed and Joseph was lowered

into the pit. Not being able to stand to hear the cries of his brother, Reuben went away alone among the flocks. 

Later as the brothers were eating their noonday meal, a caravan of traders on their way to Egypt came by. Judah saw a chance now to get rid of Joseph without killing him by selling him as a slave. When the Arab traders saw the handsome Hebrew youth they were glad to pay twenty pieces of silver for him, because they knew that he would bring a good price in the slave markets of Egypt. So Joseph was sold into slavery by his own brothers.

But Reuben knew nothing of the selling of Joseph and when darkness came he went to the pit to rescue him. Finding the pit empty he cried out in great sorrow knowing the grief that would come to Jacob when it came time to return home the men brought to their father, Jacob, the coat of Joseph covered with blood saying: "This have we found." 

And Jacob saw it was his son's coat and cried: "It is Joseph's coat. An evil beast must have devoured him."

Jacob's grief was so great that he did not cease to mourn for his son. 

Strange changes had come to Joseph, a happy care-free youth, secure in his home, beloved by his father, and a son of Israel. Now he was going to be sold as a slave in a strange land. His heart was very heavy as he traveled with the Arab traders across the dreary desert. Over and over he tried to find an answer to the questions: Why did my brothers do this cruel thing to me? Shall I ever see my beloved father and little Benjamin again? Then the stories he had heard of the cruel treatment of slaves in the land of Egypt crowded his thoughts bringing great fear with them. 

But as the caravan came to the grassy plains of the great Nile river the heart of Joseph was cheered for he remembered how Abraham had been led to this land

of plenty and mystery. Now his heart found great comfort for he remembered the words of his father Jacob as he had told his sons of the many times God had spoken to him and to his father Isaac, and to Abraham directing them in their wanderings, comforting them in their trials, and never failing to remind them of the great blessings in store for Israel. Then, too, Joseph remembered his dreams, these, too, must have come from the kind Father in heaven who had spoken in dreams to his people so often. Surely, God had a great purpose in bringing him to a new land, just as He had when Abraham was led to the land of Canaan. As these thoughts came to the captive boy they brought with them the comforting assurance that God was with him guarding and protecting him.

JOSEPH, A TRUSTWORTHY SERVANT-Genesis 39 

It was a strange land, the land of the Nile, with its grassy plains, its busy cities, its great temples, and its mysterious statues. It was to be the home of Joseph for the rest of his life.

When the traders came to the city of Avaris, where the Egyptian king held his court, they sold Joseph to Potiphar, the captain of the king's guard. Now a strange new life was awaiting the young slave. To live in a palace with courts and gardens and fountains was very different from life in the shepherds' tents on the plains of Hebron. Then, too, the Egyptians were so very strange, somehow. Joseph liked these bustling men in their clean white linen skirts and ornaments of gold around their necks. But it was when Joseph was taken with his master Potiphar on a trip down the great river and saw mile after mile of grassy plains, the mysterious silent desert, the enormous pyramids, and the cities teeming with people, that the wonder of it all changed to a feeling of great satisfaction that all was well, it, too, was God's world.

At first the Egyptian servants in Potiphar's household looked down upon the Hebrew slave. He was so likeable and so willing to do whatever he was asked that he soon made many friends, however. After a while he was allowed to wait upon Potiphar. Soon the great captain of the guard noticed the care with which Joseph performed every service for him. Often 

Potiphar would talk with the young Hebrew about his home and his people, and after Joseph had left his master, Potiphar would ponder over the words of his slave about the living God of the Hebrews. 

From time to time Potiphar tested his young slave by giving him important tasks, tasks which would test his loyalty and honor. And Joseph worked very hard and served his master faithfully. Potiphar was well pleased with the honesty and industry of his slave and made Joseph the chief servant of his household. Joseph was happy in his new freedom, for now he was no longer a slave.

But now a greater test than any his master had given him was to come to Joseph. The wife of Potiphar became very interested in the young Hebrew who had become the master of her husband's household. Now 

Joseph, no longer a slave, wore fine clothing and made a striking appearance. In time Potiphar's wife, who was an Egyptian and knew not the ideals of purity of the Hebrews, tried to tempt Joseph to a great sin. But 

Joseph refused to listen to the wickedness of his master's wife and said to her: "Behold, my master hath committed all that he hath to my hand. How can I do this great wickedness and sin against God?"

But again and again this woman tempted Joseph, but he did not listen to her. Now Potiphar's wife became angered by Joseph's honor and she went to her husband accusing Joseph of the sin which was in her own heart, which Joseph had refused to commit. Immediately,

without giving Joseph a chance to speak, Potiphar ordered Joseph to be cast into prison. What a strange reward for being faithful to his master and to the teachings of his youth!

JOSEPH'S RISE TO POWER-Genesis 40:1-41; 44 

Even in spite of this new suffering that had come to him Joseph never lost his courage. For he had kept alive in his heart his belief in God's interest in his wellbeing; he had his dreams and he had his faith in God's promises to Israel. So with a brave spirit he worked. hard and willingly as before, even though he was a prisoner.

Before very long the keeper of the prison saw the fine spirit of the young prisoner and he ordered the fetters of Joseph to be loosened and he placed the care of all the other prisoners in his hands. After a time two new prisoners were brought to Joseph, the butler and baker of Pharaoh who had angered the great king. One morning Joseph found the butler and baker sorely troubled for they had dreamed strange dreams and Joseph said to them: "Only God can interpret dreams. But tell me of your dreams and perchance through the words of my mouth God will give you the meaning."

And Joseph listened to the dreams of the butler and baker of Pharaoh. 

The chief butler said: "In my dream, behold, a vine of three branches appeared before me bringing forth grapes. And the King's cup was in my hand and

I pressed the juice of the grapes into the cup and gave it into Pharaoh's hand." 

Then the baker told Joseph of his dream: "Behold

I had three baskets on my head, and in the uppermost basket were all manner of baked dainties for the King's table. And the birds came and ate the food from the basket on my head."

Then with the words God put into his mouth Joseph interpreted the dreams of the butler and the baker.

After three days the interpretation of the dreams came to pass even as Joseph had spoken. For Pharaoh gave a great feast on his birthday for all his servants. 

Remembering the butler and baker in prison, he sent for them also. The butler he ordered to take up his work once more but the baker was ordered to be hanged. 

The butler, however, when he returned to service in the king's palace, forgot the promise he had made to 

Joseph, to speak to Pharaoh in his favor that he, too, might be released from prison. 

Two years passed and Pharaoh dreamed two strange dreams. The dreams troubled the king, so he sent for all the wise men of Egypt to find some one who could interpret the dreams. But no one was able to interpret the strange dreams. It was then that the chief butler of Pharaoh remembered Joseph and hastened to tell the king of the young Hebrew prisoner who had interpreted the dreams of the butler and the baker, and all had happened as he had spoken. 

Pharaoh sent to the prison for Joseph. And when 

Joseph came before the throne Pharaoh said: "I have dreamed a dream, and none can tell me its meaning; but it is told me that you are wise to interpret dreams." 

Joseph answered the king in humility: "It is not in me that the wisdom to interpret the dreams of Pharaoh lies; but God shall give the answer." 

Then Pharaoh spoke and said: "The first time I dreamed I stood on the bank of the great river Nile. Then, behold, there came up out of the river seven cattle, sleek and fat, and began to feed in the meadow. And as I looked lo! seven other cattle came up on the bank, but these were lean and starved. 

The seven lean cattle did eat up the seven well-fed cattle.

and when they had eaten them they were still as lean as at the beginning. "Moreover, I dreamed a second dream. This time

I stood in a corn field and I saw seven well-filled ears of corn growing on one stalk. Then, behold, I saw seven thin and wasted ears spring up beside them. And the thin ears devoured the seven good ears." 

When Joseph had heard the dreams he said unto

Pharoah: "In two dreams which have the same meaning God hath showed Pharaoh what is to come to pass. The seven fat cattle and the seven fat ears of corn mean that God is going to give to this land seven years of good harvests. The seven lean cattle and the seven empty ears of corn are seven years of famine. So great shall be the famine that all the stores made in the years of plenty shall be used up. Very great shall be this famine for God sent the warning in two dreams.'

But God had more than the interpretation of the dreams to reveal to Pharaoh. He had words of counsel that the people of the land be provided for in the days to come. So Joseph spoke on saying: "Now, therefore, let the king choose a wise man and give him power over the land. Let him direct the storing of one-fifth of the harvests in the years of plenty that when the days of famine come there shall still be corn to eat." 

Pharaoh and the wise men marveled at what they had heard. Turning to those who stood around the throne the king said: "Can we find any with more wisdom than appears in this man, to whom God has given of His spirit of wisdom?" 

And to Joseph Pharaoh said: "Since God hath showed you all that is to come and given you so much wisdom, you and none other shall rule my household. According to thy word shall

my people be ruled, and only on the throne will I be greater than thou." 

Then Pharaoh took his royal ring and placed it upon Joseph's hand. Robes of fine linen and a gold chain were brought for Joseph to wear and when he rode abroad in the land Joseph was given the chariot next to the King and the people bowed to the ground in great respect as he passed. 

Thus it was that at thirty years of age Joseph became the governor of all Egypt, to which land he came as a slave. The dreams he had never ceased to believe were beginning to come true, "for his sheaf of corn now stood upright and his star was high in the heavens." In all his dealings with the people over which he ruled Joseph never forgot the teachings of Jacob, to deal justly with all men and to trust in God for guidance in all things. And all the people of the land were blessed through the righteous service of Joseph.

For seven years the harvests were abundant, and each year under Joseph's direction one-fifth of the harvest was stored in great storehouses throughout the land. Then came the years of famine throughout all the lands as Joseph had foretold.

JOSEPH, A SAVIOUR IN ISRAEL-Genesis 42-45 

The great famine came to Egypt and all the lands about. Joseph opened the storehouses he had filled as 

God had counseled and the people of Egypt were provided for. But in far-away Hebron the family of Jacob was in great distress, for the fields had yielded no corn and the valleys and hillsides no grass for the cattle. 

Then the word came to Jacob that there was corn in 

Egypt. So the ten brothers went at their father's bidding to procure food lest all of them die.

When the Hebrews arrived in Egypt they were sent to the Governor, for none save he could sell corn

to those not of the land of Egypt. When the brothers came to the presence of Joseph they bowed low before him and begged to be allowed to buy corn to take to their families in Canaan lest they die of hunger. 

Joseph knew his brothers at once, but they knew not Joseph dressed in the royal garments of the Governor of Egypt. As he saw his brothers before him bowing to him as he had seen them in his dreams so long ago, Joseph was deeply moved. It was in his heart to give them a loving greeting. But also Joseph wanted to know whether they had changed in heart from the evil men he had known them to be. So Joseph spoke harshly to them saying: "Ye are spies, come to see the nakedness of the land." 

They answered him: "Nay, my lord! We are no spies. We are twelve brethren, sons of one man; the youngest is with our father, and one is dead." 

But Joseph insisted saying: "Ye are spies. Let one of you go hence and bring your youngest brother to prove your words." ing: 

The brothers pleaded with Joseph once more say- "We speak the truth, we are no spies. Our youngest brother cannot leave his father for our father loveth him so that if any harm came to him his father would surely die.' 

Then Joseph told his brothers to choose one of their number to stay in prison while the rest returned to Canaan with the corn for their families. But he warned them saying: "For except you bring your youngest brother you shall buy no more corn in Egypt."

In their distress the brothers spoke to one another in Hebrew, not knowing that Joseph could understand them. They said, reproachfully: "This has come to us because of the evil we did to

our brother, Joseph. He pleaded with us to spare him and we would not listen. Now mercy is denied to us in our great need." 

When Joseph heard the words of his brothers he turned away that they might not see that he wept. Later Simeon was chosen to be kept in prison and the rest of the brothers started homeward carrying grain for their families and provisions for the journey. 

At the first resting place one of the brethren opened his sack of grain and there was the money he had paid for the grain. Then the other sacks were opened and every man's money was found in his sack. The men were sorely troubled for fear that they might be accused of stealing, for they knew not that Joseph had ordered the money to be returned to each man in his sack.

Reaching their home in the land of Canaan the brethren related to Jacob the things that had happened to them; how they had been taken for spies; how Simeon had been forced to stay in prison until Benjamin be brought; and how the money had been found in the sacks. It was all more than the aged father could understand and in his great grief he cried: "I am bereaved of my children. Joseph is dead, Simeon is gone, and now ye will take Benjamin away!"

The famine continued and the wives and children of the men of Israel were suffering of hunger once more. 

And Jacob said to his sons again: "Go to Egypt to buy grain for food lest we die."
