The sons of Jacob argued with their father that they dared not return to Egypt without Benjamin. 

Then Judah pledged himself for the safety of his youngest brother. At last Jacob consented to send Benjamin, his beloved son, for the suffering in his camp was very great. Also as a present to the great Governor 

Jacob sent fruit, spices, honey, myrrh, nuts, and al-

monds. Also Jacob sent double the money for the grain to pay for that for which the money had been returned. 

Now Joseph saw his brethren as they came again to the storehouse in Egypt. When he saw that they brought Benjamin with them he commanded that a great feast be prepared. Also Simeon was brought from the prison to be with his brothers. At the feast the kindness of the Governor set aside the fears of the Hebrews. 

Joseph spoke much with Benjamin about his father Jacob as he served him with food from his own table. 

Then the brothers went on their way rejoicing at their good fortune; they had food for their people, Simeon had been released from prison, and Benjamin was safe with them. But Joseph had planned once more to know the hearts of his brothers. So he had commanded his servants to put back the money for the corn in the sacks of the Hebrews and in the sack of Benjamin to place a silver cup from the royal table.

It was not long before the little group of travelers were overtaken by the servants sent by Joseph, saying as their master had commanded: "Wherefore have you returned evil for good by stealing my lord's cup?"

The astonished brethren pleaded with the servants to believe their innocence vowing to return to Egypt as bondmen if the cup be found with them, and that he who had the cup should die. Then the servants searched the sacks of grain, and found the silver cup in the sack of Benjamin. Despair and great fear filled the hearts of the brothers as they returned to the house of Joseph. 

Joseph looked upon his brothers with great pity in his heart as they fell to the ground before him so great was their fear. And Judah cried out: "My lord, how shall we clear ourselves? We are all thy slaves." 

And Joseph answered, "The man in whose sack

the cup was found shall be my slave. The rest of you can return in peace unto your father."

Then Judah came and knelt before Joseph and pled with him, saying: "Let thy servant speak. We have a father, an old man, and this youth is a child of his old age; and his brother is dead, and he alone is left of his mother, and his father loveth him. We persuaded our father to let the lad come with us as thou commanded. Now if we return without our brother our father will surely die in his sorrow."

Then Judah offered himself in place of Benjamin pleading: "Let thy servant stay behind as a bondman and let the lad return with his brethren; for I fear evil shall come to our father."

When Judah had finished speaking Joseph ordered all the servants from the room so that he could be alone with his brothers. Then Joseph, unable to refrain longer, wept aloud and said: "I am Joseph, your brother, whom ye sold into 

Egypt! Doth my father yet live?"

Then Joseph spoke words of comfort to his brothers who stood speechless before him and were sore afraid: "Be not grieved nor angry with yourselves that ye sold me hither; for God sent me before you to save your lives. It was not you that sent me hither, but God.

There shall yet be five years of famine so hasten ye to my father. And ye shall tell my father of all my glory in Egypt. And say to him that he must come to Egypt with all of his children and his children's children and his flocks and herds and I will provide for them." 

So great was his joy that Joseph threw his arms around his brother Benjamin and kissed him and they all wept together because of the great gladness of their hearts.

When Pharaoh learned of what had happened he

was greatly pleased because of his love of Joseph and he said to him: "Give wagons and provisions and clothing to your brothers to take to your father so that all may come to Egypt in comfort.' 

And Joseph sent gifts to his father of food, clothing, and the good things of Egypt. 

At first Jacob refused to believe that Joseph was alive and the Governor of all Egypt, but when he saw the caravan of wagons filled with gifts and provisions that Joseph had sent he said: "It is enough. Joseph my son is alive. I will go to see him before I die."

ISRAEL IN EGYPT-Genesis 45-46 

All was made ready and the journey to Egypt started, seventy-six persons with all their possessions. 

In time they came to Goshen and Joseph came to meet his father, and the reunion of the aged patriarch and his beloved son was tender, indeed. Pharaoh received Jacob and his sons with great kindness and gave to them the land of Goshen, the rich land at the mouth of the River 

Nile, for their home.

The remaining years of the famine were hard and trying, but Joseph was able to provide for all the needs of his people. For seventeen years Jacob lived in Egypt and then came the time that he knew that he was to die. 

So he sent for Joseph to come to him and to bring his sons, Ephraim and Manasseh, to him that he might bless them. And Joseph seeing that his father laid his right hand upon the head of Ephraim, the younger, removed his hand to the head of Manasseh saying: "Not so, my father, for this is the first born; put thy right hand upon his head." 

And Jacob answered: "I know it, my son; he also shall become a great people, but his younger brother

shall be greater, for his seed shall become a multitude of nations."

Jacob then called all of his sons together and blessed them. And to his son Joseph he said: "Joseph is a fruitful bough, even a fruitful bough by a well, whose branches run over the wall. The archers have sorely grieved him, and shot at him and hated him; but his bow abode in strength, and the arms of his hands were made strong by the hands of the God of Israel."

When Jacob had finished the blessing of Israel his life closed and Joseph and his brothers took their father to bury him in the cave of Machpelah in the land of 

Canaan, where Abraham, Isaac, Sarah, Rebekah, and 

Leah were buried.

The brothers on their return to Egypt feared Joseph now that their father was dead because of the evil they had done to him in his youth. And Joseph, deeply hurt that his brothers should doubt him, comforted them saying: "Fear ye not. For as ye thought evil against me,

God meant it for much good. I will nourish you and your little ones.'

And the brothers of Joseph dwelt in the land of 

Goshen and Joseph lived in the palace of Pharaoh. The children of Israel lived together in peace and happiness. 

Ere he died Joseph called his people to him and said: "God will surely bless you and bring you out of this land, to the land which he promised to our fathers. 

Swear to me that when that time comes, you, or your children after you, shall carry my bones with them, for 

I too, would be buried in the Promised Land."

Moses and The Deliverance of Israel FOUR 

OUR hundred years! How many changes can come to the world in four hundred years! In the changing world of today much has happened to mankind and nations in less than a century, even in a decade. 

Even so the world over three thousand years ago was a changing world. Peoples were growing into nations while nations were passing away. Just as some nations grow by the power of conquest, others are crushed and die; as some grow and endure by that which is good, evil causes the downfall of a people. 

The world of Egypt, Canaan, and Mesopotamia was a changing world over three thousand years ago. 

The Pharaohs of Egypt had gone forth and conquered the land from the source of the Nile to the plains of the great rivers Tigris and Euphrates, peoples and nations had become enslaved. Then the greatest of all the 

Pharaohs, Ramses the Great, a fierce warrior and a great builder became the ruler of Egypt. While his warriors. collected great wealth in tribute from the conquered countries, thousands of slaves built great temples to the war-god, Amon, and erected immense granite statues of the mighty ruler all along the land of the Nile. 

Meanwhile the children of Israel, secure in the land of Goshen, had prospered greatly. From twelve families they had increased to many thousands of souls. 

Very little change had come to their lives, however, for

they still followed the manner of living of Abraham and of Jacob. Each tribe had its leader, but over all the tribes of Israel there was no leader. Now, in the time of Ramses the Second, the world was no longer a place where a small tribe could follow a simple way of living. 

It was the time for the Israelites to become united and strong that through them all mankind might be blessed as God had promised to Abraham, to Isaac, and to Jacob.

THE BONDAGE OF ISRAEL-Exodus 1 

The blessing of freedom and comfort made life desirable for the Israelites in Egypt. Even after the death of Joseph, the Governor, they continued to live in the land of Goshen, given to them by Pharaoh. It seemed almost as if they might have forgotten that Canaan was the land God had promised to them for their home. Or perhaps the reason that they did not return to Hebron was the fear of the desert tribes that roamed the land between them and the home of their fathers. Then came the time when the powerful warriors of Egypt entered the land of Canaan and brought all the tribes under their power. But strange as it seems, the Israelites lived in peace for many generations, almost as if they had been forgotten by the conquering Pharaohs.

It was when Ramses the Second became the ruler of Egypt, that the great tragedy came to Israel. Ramses knew the strength of the Hebrews, and fearing that they might help the tribes of Canaan who had risen in rebellion, he said to his people: "Behold the children of Israel are akin to the people of Canaan. Let us deal wisely with them, lest it come to pass that when there falleth out a war they join also unto our enemies and fight against us."

But above all things Ramses was a mighty builder

and desired to build two great cities as storehouses for his treasures. To carry out these plans he needed many strong men as slaves. So the Children of Israel were taken from their homes and their flocks and herds and placed in bondage. Now they lived in mud huts along the banks of the river. Day after day, driven by the lash of cruel slave drivers, they made bricks or dragged huge blocks of stone over the burning sands of the desert. 

And the cities of Pithom, the sacred city of Atum, god of the Setting Sun, and Ramses, city of the mighty king grew at the hands of the suffering Hebrews. 

As the days passed many of the Israelites died, because of the great hardships inflicted upon them, and many became broken in spirit. But those remaining never lost faith in the living God or forgot His promises to Abraham, and they prayed always for deliverance from their cruel bondage unto the Egyptians. In His own due time God answered the prayers of Israel in bondage.

A DELIVERER IS BORN-Exodus 1:1-2; 10 

The years of bondage brought great suffering and sorrow to the Israelites. At times they wondered if God had forgotten them. But in spite of the cruel treatment they received they grew in numbers and remained strong. Now Pharaoh began to fear what might happen if he could not crush the Hebrew slaves completely. 

So he sent forth a wicked command: "Every son of the Hebrews that is born ye shall cast into the river."

The command brought great fear to every Hebrew family because they knew that in this way the race of Israel would be destroyed. But they remembered that 

God preserved Israel from famine when He brought them to Egypt, and they trusted in God to protect them again.

The family of Amram, of the tribe of Levi, however, defied Pharaoh's cruel command. For when

Jochebed looked upon the face of her new born son she felt the spirit of the Lord within her inspiring her to guard and protect his life from destruction. So the child was kept hidden by its parents and the secret guarded bravely by Miriam, his sister, and Aaron, his brother. When the time came that the babe could be hidden no longer the mother made a basket of bullrushes and placed the babe therein. Then the basket was placed in the reeds near the place where Pharaoh's daughter came to the river to wash. And Miriam waited nearby.

When the princess came to the river she saw the babe and knew it to be one of the Hebrew children condemned to die by her father's cruelty. And Miriam who had waited nearby spoke to the princess saying: "Shall I call a Hebrew woman that she might care for the child?” 

And Jochebed was given the care of her babe for a little while longer. Then he was taken to live in the palace of Pharaoh, and named Moses. Instead of growing up as a slave Moses grew up as a royal prince; instead of working as a slave beaten by cruel taskmasters, he was educated by the wise men of Egypt. 

From time to time Moses returned to the slavehut of his parents, to the joy and pride of his sister Miriam who had guarded him so well. There Moses was told the story of his people, of Abraham, of Isaac, of Jacob, and of Joseph, and of God's promise to bless mankind through the seed of Israel.

Moses grew to manhood as a prince of Egypt, but he never forgot that he was a son of Israel. The bondage of his people laid heavy on his heart, for he would often mingle with them as they toiled in the brickfields or on the great buildings. When he heard them spoken of with scorn by the servants of Pharaoh, who seemed

74' 

to delight in the cruelty of the Hebrew slave drivers, he longed to be able to do something to help them. 

It happened that one day Moses saw an Egyptian lashing one of the Hebrews. In great anger Moses slew the Egyptian and hid his body in the sand. Then he went on his way thinking that no one had witnessed his actions.

The next time Moses visited his people at work he saw two Hebrews fighting, and he spoke to them thinking to end the quarrel. And one of the slaves answered sullenly: "Who made thee judge over us? Intendest thou to kill me as thou killedst the Egyptian?"

Moses knew that his anger had brought trouble instead of good to the Hebrews. More than anything else he wanted to help his people. Now he feared the anger of Pharaoh for his people and for himself. So 

Moses made a great choice, he left Egypt and became a wanderer.

THE PREPARATION AND CALL OF MOSES- 

Exodus 4

Weary, homesick, and lonely, Moses passed the long stretches of rocky foothills of Mount Sinai unto the desert lands of Arabia. Then he came to the land of Midian, the home of the descendants of Midian, the son of Abraham and Keturah, whom Abraham married after the death of Sarah. In this place there were no cities or houses, only the tents of the herdsmen. 

Now Moses was ready for the next period of training that was to come to him to prepare him to be the deliverer of Israel. Although Moses did not understand it, his life and education at the court of the King of Egypt was the first step, that the deliverer of Israel might know how to follow God's directions to make the tyrant king release the Hebrew slaves. But in the luxury of the life of the palace there were many things

that Moses had not learned, these could only be learned away from the bustle of cities and the ways of men, in the stillness of the desert night, with the stars, and with God their creator. 

Then Moses came to the tent of Jethro, the highpriest of Midian, and was made welcome. Changing his Egyptian garments for the rough woollen clothes of the desert dwellers, Moses became a herdsman. Later Jethro gave Moses his daughter Zipporah to be his wife.

Even in that far-away place the news of the happenings in Egypt came to Moses. The caravan drivers told him of the death of Ramses and that the new king was more cruel than even his father had been to the Hebrew slaves.

As Moses watched the flocks of Jethro in the desert pastures his thoughts dwelt upon the experiences of Abraham and Jacob, how they had been directed by God to the beginning of Israel. As the days passed the faith of Moses grew stronger in the promises made to Israel. When he came to realize that the delivery of 

Israel must be in God's time and of His making, he prayed without ceasing that that time might be near at hand.

One day Moses led his flock across the desert until he came to Mount Horeb. Then as he walked he saw a bush that was burning and the bush was not destroyed. As he came near to see this strange thing a voice came from the burning bush calling: "Moses! Moses!" 

And Moses answered: "Here am I." 

Then the voice spoke again saying: "I am the 

God of Abraham of Isaac and of Jacob."

Then Moses hid his face in his cloak for he was afraid to look upon God. And God spake again: "I have seen the affliction of my people which are in Egypt and have heard their cry by reason of their

taskmasters; for I know their sorrows. I am come to deliver them, to lead them out of the land of Egypt unto a good land and a large, a land flowing with milk and honey, unto the place of the Canaanites. Come now, therefore, and I will send thee to Pharaoh that thou mayest bring forth the Children of Israel out of Egypt."

Moses knowing the great power of the tyrant king of Egypt and remembering his own weakness in killing the Egyptian in anger, he felt unequal to this great task and answered saying: "Who am I that I should go unto Pharaoh, and that I should bring the Children of Israel out of Egypt?" 

And God answered Moses: "I will be with thee." 

And Moses answered and said: "Behold, they will not believe me, nor hearken unto my voice; for they will say "The Lord hath not appeared unto thee.'

God knew that what Moses spoke was true; so 

He gave to him a sign, he was able to turn his shepherd's rod into a serpent and back again. And if perchance that they should not believe the first sign, a second was given to Moses, his hand would become white with leprosy and clean again as he desired. said:

And still Moses was afraid of the great task and "Behold when I come unto the Children of Israel what shall I say unto them? How shall I speak to them when I am slow of speech?" ing: 

Again God sought to stay the fear of Moses, say- "Now, therefore, go, and I will be thy mouth and teach thee what thou shalt say."

In spite of all God's promises, Moses still hesitated, until at last God was angry. Then God took pity on 

Moses and told him to take his brother Aaron as his

spokesman to the Hebrews. Now Moses grew steadfast and accepted the task of freeing the Children of Israel from their bondage in Egypt. 

And Moses took his wife and his sons and left his home in the land of Midian with Jethro, the highpriest. When he came to the wilderness Aaron came to meet him. As the brothers traveled towards Egypt 

Moses told Aaron all that God had spoken to him. 

When the brothers came to the land of Goshen they called together the elders of Israel, and Aaron told them what the Lord had promised to Moses. And the hearts of the people were filled with joy for they believed that at last God had heard their prayers and had raised up a deliverer to lead them out of bondage to the land promised to Abraham. 

THE DELIVERANCE OF ISRAEL-Exodus 5-15 

It was a superhuman task that God had given to Moses, the deliverance of the Children of Israel from bondage. The tyrant king of Egypt was not going to give up the slaves who were so valuable, whose work was to be a monument to the greatness of Egypt. And indeed it was a monument because today the remains of the cities, temples, tombs, and statues built during the bondage of Israel are one of the wonders of all the works of man in all the ages. 

But Moses was God's servant and Aaron was the spokesman. And Moses followed the commands of 

God in all things. First, he went to Pharaoh requesting the freedom of Israel asking: "Thus saith the Lord God of Israel. 'Let my people go for a three days' journey into the desert that they may hold a feast unto me in the wilderness." "Who is the Lord that I should obey his voice? 

I know not the Lord, neither will I let Israel go," 

Pharaoh answered.

And Moses and Aaron continued to plead with the king. Angered at the words of the Hebrews 

Pharaoh cried: "Why do ye hinder the people from their work? 

Get you unto your burdens."

Thenceforward Pharaoh made the burdens of the Hebrew slaves heavier, for he commanded that they be given no more straw for making of bricks. Now they were to find their own straw and they were to make as many bricks as before. And the taskmasters showed the 

Hebrew slaves no mercy, beating them with whips. At last the slaves cried out in anguish: "Moses, why have we been made to suffer even more than before? Ye have made us hateful to Pharaoh."

And Moses was greatly troubled over the added burdens of his people and he sought God to know the reason saying: "Since I came to Pharaoh to speak in Thy name, he hath done evil to this people; neither hast Thou delivered Thy people at all." 

And God spoke unto Moses and said: "I am the Lord. And I appeared unto Abraham, unto Isaac, and unto Jacob. And I established a covenant with them to give them the land of Canaan, wherein they were strangers.

Also God instructed Moses to say to the Children of Israel: 

"I am the Lord, and I will bring you out from under the burdens of the Egyptians. And I will take you to me for a people, and I will be to you a God; and ye shall know that I am your God which bringeth you out from under the burdens of the Egyptians. And I will bring you to the land which I did swear to give it to Abraham, to Isaac, and to Jacob, and I will give it to you for a heritage." 

Then Moses returned to Pharaoh once more to

plead for the deliverance of Israel as God had commanded, and the King seated among his wise men demanded that he be shown a miracle. Then Aaron cast his staff on the ground and it became a serpent. But 

Pharaoh called to his wise men to cast their rods upon the ground, and they, too, became serpents. But that was not all. The serpent of Aaron's staff rose up and devoured all the other serpents, to the amazement of the wise men of Egypt.

Still Pharaoh did not understand that this was a sign of God's power, and he refused to free the Childre of Israel. 

And God said to Moses: "Pharaoh shall know that I am God." 

And through His servants, Moses and Aaron,

God brought upon the land of Egypt plagues that were grievous and horrible; swarms of lice, flies and frogs came to harass the people; hail, rain, and locust came to destroy the crops; disease came to the cattle; and a pestilence of boils came to man and beast alike. 

When each plague would become unbearable 

Pharaoh would send for Moses and Aaron and say to them: "If thou canst remove this plague, I will let thy people go." 

And each time after the plague was removed

Pharaoh hardened his heart and refused to let the Hebrew slaves go. 

Then there came to the land of Pharaoh a strange and awful manifestation of God's power, in the midst of the day the sun was hidden and a darkness lay over all the land but over the land of Goshen. 

This time Pharaoh was in great terror because the sun-god of Egypt was hidden. Again Moses was called. "Go ye, serve your God in the desert, only let your flocks and herds remain in Egypt," cried the King.

"Nay," answered Moses. "Not a hoof shall be left behind. How shall we live; how shall we offer sacrifices to our God unless we take our cattle?" "Get thee from me!" cried the angered Pharaoh. "Take heed that thou show thy face no more in my presence, for in the day thou seest my face thou shalt die."

Then Moses spoke as God commanded him: "Thus saith the Lord: About midnight shall the first-born in Egypt die, from the first-born of Pharaoh even unto the first-born of the humblest manservant. There shall be a great cry throughout the land and men shall cry, 'Let the Children of Israel go.'

Then Moses went to his own people and told them that freedom was at hand and to make all things ready. Then he spoke to them as God had given him counsel: "Let every man take a lamb that is without blemish. Let the lintels of the door be marked with the blood of the lamb. And let the flesh of the lamb be prepared for food, for the last meal ye shall eat in 

Egypt. For the destroying angel shall pass through the land and only the first-born of the houses marked with the blood of the lamb shall be saved." "This day shall be unto you for a memorial, and ye shall keep it a feast to the Lord throughout all generations . . . . And ye shall call the feast the Passover because the Lord passed over the homes of the Children of Israel when He smote the Egyptians." 

And the Children of Israel obeyed the commands of Moses and made ready everything as he had said. 

As the night passed cries came from every side, from 

Pharaoh's great palace and from every house save those of the Hebrews, for as God had threatened all the first-born in the land of Egypt died. And from all the land the stricken Egyptians cried out: "Ye of Israel begone, before we are all dead."

Straightway Pharaoh sent his messengers to

Moses telling him to take his people and all their possessions and begone out of the land. 

Therefore the Children of Israel left Egypt, old men and women bowed with years of slavery, youths and maidens rejoicing at the thought of freedom, fathers and mothers and children, flocks and herds. Also there went with them a large number of slaves and others who were eager to leave the tyranny of Egypt. 

In all it was a great multitude, And as they started on the journey the pledge made to Joseph was remembered for they bore his body with them that it might be buried with Abraham and Jacob and Isaac in the Land of Promise. 

Moses led the great multitude towards the upper end of the Red Sea, and at evening they made a camp and rested. But it seemed that even now their deliverance was not complete, for in the distance the warriors of Pharaoh were seen approaching. For when the first sorrow of the death of all their first-born had passed, the Egyptians realized that the slaves who did so much work for them had gone. So Pharaoh ordered his warriors to follow the Hebrew slaves and bring them back. 

Now in their great terror the Children of Israel cried unto Moses: "Wherefore hast thou dealt with us thus? For it had been better for us to serve the Egyptians than to die thus." 

And Moses said unto the people: "Fear ye not! Stand still, and see the salvation of the Lord, which He shall show you this day. For the 

Egyptians whom ye have seen today ye shall see them again no more for ever." 

Then Moses stretched out his hand over the sea and God sent a mighty wind from the east and the waters of the sea were divided. And the Children of Israel

marched forward across the pathway between the waters to safety.

After the people of Israel had crossed safely, the wind ceased and the waters returned to their course. 

And Pharaoh and all his warriors who were following the Hebrews were destroyed. 

Now the Children of Israel knew without a doubt that God was with them and with His servant, Moses, for they had a testimony of the promises made to Abraham and to Moses which had been fulfilled. 

In their joy and thanksgiving led by Miriam, the sister of Moses, they sang a great song of praise: "I will sing unto the Lord, for He hath triumphed gloriously:

The horse and the rider hath he thrown into the sea. 

The Lord is my strength and song, And He is become my salvation: 

This is my God, and I will praise Him; 

My father's God, and I will exalt Him."

PROVIDING FOR ISRAEL IN THE WILDERNESS— 

Exodus 15-17 Many new experiences awaited the Children of Israel as they journeyed towards the land of Canaan. 

For generations they had lived in Goshen, either as free tribes or as slaves. Now they were wanderers, there were deserts and a wilderness to cross, there were enemies to overcome. The problems were many and difficult and they were ill prepared. There is no doubt that the Israelites expected to be led at once to the land promised to them. But God had in His mind to prepare the Israelites for their first task, the journey to Canaan, and also for the greater task, of becoming a nation of God's children. Thus God was directing their leader Moses in all that he did.

For three days Moses led his people along the rocky wastes of the land of Sinai. For three days they found no water to drink and they suffered great thirst. 

At last they came to water and shade. With eagerness they stooped to drink, but the water was exceedingly bitter and they were sore troubled because of the disappointment. But God instructed Moses how to sweeten the water with the leaves and branches of a tree that grew nearby. Then the people and the cattle drank freely and were refreshed. Before long they came to a beautiful valley named Elim, with green palm trees and wells of sweet water. Now the huge caravan could rest awhile in comfort.

Once more the Israelites began to journey, this time the way crossed a barren wilderness. Now the food they had brought with them from Egypt was all gone. Then they began to cry out to Moses saying: "Who shall give us food to eat? In Egypt we did eat freely of bread to the full and of cucumbers, melons, and onions. Now ye have brought us into this wilderness to kill us with hunger."

Moses was sorely grieved at the ingratitude of the people whom God had delivered from bondage. 

Then Aaron pled with them to bow themselves in humility before God and ask for the things they needed. 

As the Israelites looked towards the wilderness they saw a pillar of cloud and they knew that God had not forgotten them.

The Lord spoke to Moses to relieve his great anxiety: "At evening these people shall eat flesh and in the morning they shall be filled with bread."

As the sun set upon the camp of Israel, huge flocks of quail came and settled upon the ground. And the people ate flesh as God had promised. In the morning the ground was covered with small grains that looked like frost. When they tasted it they found it

sweet and knew that it was the bread they had been promised. Then Moses spoke to them saying: "This is the bread which the Lord has given you to eat. Let each gather enough for the needs of his household for one day, and on the day before the Sabbath a double supply. Let no man gather more than he needs."

Whereupon the people called the sweet food "manna," and they collected it fresh each day and baked it into bread. But some disobeyed the instructions of Moses and gathered more than they needed, and the next day they found the manna had soured and was unfit to eat. Throughout the journey of Israel the manna was on the ground each morning, except upon the Sabbath.

Now Moses led his people out of the wilderness towards the foot of the great mountain Sinai, where they came to rest at a place called Rephidim. At this place there was no water. Once again the Israelites began to murmur, forgetting all that had been provided for them before.

Moses went alone among the rocks of the hillside and spoke to God saying: "What shall I do to this people. Behold, they are ready to stone me?"

Returning to the camp Moses did as God had told him, taking his sacred rod in his hand he struck a great rock, calling upon the name of the Lord. Instantly water poured forth and the people drank and were satisfied.

While the Israelites were camped at Rephidim Moses had to prepare to protect his people from the robber bands of the Amalekites who roamed the desert in search of caravans of traders traveling between the cities of Egypt and the cities of Mesopotamia. Straight way Moses chose Joshua to be the Captain of Israel. Bondage.

And Joshua was instructed to gather and train a band of fighting men for the protection of the people as they traveled toward Canaan.

When Joshua and his warriors went out to meet the Amalekite robbers, Moses went up to a hill-top and prayed to God for their success. As the warriors looked back and saw Moses with his sacred rod raised high over his head their courage grew and they fought without wavering. The enemies were defeated and the 

Children of Israel took the upper valley of Rephidim for their camp. Because it was a choice and grassy valley with wells of sweet water the people of Israel rested in comfort and were contented. 

While at Rephidim, a great happiness came to Moses, for here he was reunited with his wife and his sons; for when the plagues began to punish Pharaoh

Moses had sent his family back to Midian to the home of Jethro. Now Jethro came to the camp of Israel bringing with him his daughter, Zipporah, the wife of Moses, and her sons, Gershom and Eliezer. 

THE FIRST JUDGES OF ISRAEL-Exodus 18 

When Jethro saw the problems of Moses and that which he had accomplished with God's help, he marveled at the evidences that had been given to this people of God's power and loving kindness. Also Jethro saw how human were the people of Israel, how the problems of everyday living, food, shelter, possessions, and security bothered them. He saw their dependence upon Moses for every small matter, and that the patience and long-suffering of Moses was taxed greatly. So Jethro counseled with Moses to choose good men to become the leaders of groups of the people and to act as judges in everyday matters. 

Moses selected able men and made them captains. of thousands, of hundreds, of fifties and of tens to

help him to look over the welfare of the people and to administer the affairs of everyday life. When problems were too great for the captains or judges, as they were called later, Moses became the counselor and judge. It was in this way that Israel learned to govern itself.

It was a great testimony that came to Israel in the journey from Goshen to Rephidim; a testimony that God had brought them from bondage to safety, had provided food and drink for them, and had helped them to conquer the Amalekites. Never again could they doubt if they remembered God's loving care in the wilderness that the promises made to Abraham would be fulfilled.

Ten Commandments Given To Israel 

GOD chose the Children of Israel from among all the peoples of the earth to become a holy nation. 

But in order to attain this glorious destiny it was necessary that this people learn God's laws and how they must be obeyed. Now when they were away from the rest of the world as they were traveling towards the land of their inheritance was the time chosen by God to teach them of Himself and His will. 

During the journey from Egypt to Mount Sinai the Israelites had came to know that God was always ready to answer the call of His servant, Moses, and to provide for the needs of the wanderers. But there was much more for the Israelites to learn, for life is more than food and shelter or than comfort and security. 

They had to learn to live righteously with man and with God. To live in peace and harmony with their fellows meant to deal with honesty and kindness; to live in mindfulness of God's purposes for man's happiness meant knowing and obeying the great laws of life.

Now the Children of Israel had come to God's school wherein they might learn these great lessons. 

It was indeed a strange school, but it was a glorious school, alone with the beautiful and majestic creations of God, led by God's chosen teachers, and instructed by the Divine Creator of the earth and man.

THE TEN COMMANDMENTS GIVEN-Exodus 19, 20 

Three months after the deliverance from Egypt the camp of the Israelites was made on the grassy plains in the shadow of Mount Sinai. There they could rest in security and comfort and for a long period of time. Now Moses felt that his people must be taught how to live together in peace and harmony, that they must learn to know more of God and His purposes for them. So Moses went up to the top of the mountain to be alone and to pray for guidance so that he might be able to teach his people right.

The Lord told Moses to go to the people and to prepare them to hear His voice from the mountain.

Moses did as he was commanded and instructed the Israelites to cleanse themselves and their garments and to be ready before the mountain on the third day to hearken to the voice of the Lord. 

On the morning of the third day a dark cloud rested on Mount Sinai and thunder and lightning shook the earth. Then the voice of a trumpet sounded loudly. Below in the valley Moses had assembled all of Israel. It seemed as if the mountain burned with fire when from the cloud on the mountain peak the voice of Jehovah was heard speaking to Israel: "Ye have seen what I did unto the Egyptians and how I bore you on eagle's wings, and brought you to myself. Now, therefore, if ye will obey my voice and keep my covenant then ye shall be mine own possession from among all peoples: for all the earth is mine; and ye shall be unto me a kingdom of priests and a holy nation. "I am the Lord thy God which have brought thee out of the land of Egypt, out of the House of Bondage. age. 

Thou shalt have no other gods before Me; 

Thou shalt not make unto thee any graven im-

Thou shalt not take the name of the Lord thy 

God in vain. 

Remember the Sabbath day to keep it holy. 

Honor thy father and thy mother. 

Thou shalt not kill. 

Thou shalt not commit adultery. 

Thou shalt not steal. 

Thou shalt not bear false witness. 

Thou shalt not covet."

The hearts of the people were stricken with fear as they listened to the voice of the Lord God and they withdrew from the mountain, and cried unto Moses: "Let not God speak to us in the voice of thunder. 

Be thou God's messenger to us.

Moses spoke to them so as to comfort their fears explaining God's desires that they knew Him and how to serve Him, explaining to them that the commandments which had been given to them were for their good.

The next day Moses prepared a sacrifice to God and one of each of the tribes of Israel came to the altar to make a sacrifice of oxen. Then Moses spoke to the people repeating all that God had spoken the day before. And all lifted their hands and made a covenant with God: "All that the Lord hath said will we do, and be obedient."

Then the people left the sacred mountain and returned to their tents. Moses took Aaron and his sons and seventy of the elders of Israel and went up into the mountain to hallow the covenant which the people had made. There they saw the glory of God in all its brightness. Then Aaron and his sons and the elders came down from the holy mountain, but Moses remained that God might instruct him further as to how

the Children of Israel should serve the Lord as they had covenanted.

For forty days Moses dwelt on the mountain with the Lord. And as he received the laws which were to be a guide to Israel to help them to be true to their covenant and to become a holy people, Moses wrote them into a book, the Book of the Covenant. Many other great and glorious things were also revealed to Moses at this time. Also God gave Moses instructions to provide a place wherein the Children of Israel might worship Him. All the details of the Holy place, the building and the furnishings, Moses saw in a vision; and Moses was instructed in the manner of worship to be held in this House of God. 

Then Jehovah inscribed on two tablets of stone the ten commandments as He had spoken them to the Children of Israel. Each of the commandments was explained to Moses, so that he might know that these were the laws for all mankind in all times and places. 

Then Moses knew that if men would know God and serve Him by loving obedience to these laws they would love one another also, and that it was in this way alone that the Hebrews could become a holy nation.

AARON AND THE GOLDEN CALF-Exodus 32 

The Israelites waited on the plains before Mount Sinai while Moses remained upon the mountain with 

God. Day after day they looked at the clouds of fire and awaited the return of their leader. After many days they began to fear that Moses would not return so they turned to Aaron for guidance. 

Now the Israelites had heard the voice of their

God; they wanted to worship Him. They had helped to build great temples and statues to the gods of the Egyptians; they had seen the great festivals and sacrifices made in honor of the gods of Egypt. They knew,

also, that the Egyptians believed that their gods had made them a great nation, strong enough to conquer many other nations and take their wealth in tribute and their people as slaves. So they gathered together the tribes of Israel and the Egyptians which had come with them out of bondage, and came to Aaron in great concern saying: "Make us a god. How can we worship the God we have never seen? The people of Egypt have stone statues of their gods and they can see them when they worship. Make us a god that shall go before us, for as for this man, Moses, we know not what is become of him." 

Perhaps Aaron, too, feared that Moses might not return to lead Israel for he had been gone so long. Or perhaps Aaron feared what the people might do to him if he refused. But whatever was the reason, Aaron agreed to the demands of the Israelites and forgot that the voice of God had said, "Thou shalt not make to thyself any graven image."

Aaron commanded the people to bring to him all the ornaments of gold they possessed, their earrings and their bracelets. Then Aaron melted the gold and molded the image of a calf such as he had seen in Egypt. All the people gathered together for a great festival in honor of the golden image. Aaron built an altar for the golden image, burnt sacrifices were offered to the idol, and the people made merry with feasting and dancing.

God saw what was happening among the Israelites and spoke to Moses saying: "Get thee down, the people have corrupted themselves. I have borne with their stubbornness long enough, now I will destroy them and of thy children, Moses, will I make a great nation."

When Moses looked down upon the dancing and feasting of the people and saw the golden image,

he had pity on them for he knew wherefore they had seen the worship of idols in Egypt. So he pled with 

God saying: "Have mercy upon them, abate Thy anger. Remember the promises made to Abraham, to Isaac, and to Jacob to make them a great nation and to give them the Land of Promise for an heritage forever.' 

Jehovah listened to the pleading of Moses and repented of the punishment He had thought to impose upon His people Israel. Then Moses came down the mountain to where the Israelites were dancing and singing before the golden calf. And his anger became very great. Flinging the tablets of stone upon which 

God had written on the ground they were shattered to pieces. Then he cast the golden image into the fire and destroyed it. To his brother Aaron he cried: "Why hast thou brought so great a sin upon thy people?"

But Aaron could find no good reason for his weakness but that he had done only as the people had begged him to do. 

On the morrow when his anger had gone, Moses called his people to him and said: "Ye have sinned greatly, yet I will beseech the 

Lord for you; it may be that I can make atonement for your sin.'

Then Moses went once more to the mountain to intercede with God, beseeching Him to forgive the sin of Israel. For now the anger of the Lord was softened by the willingness of Moses to suffer instead of Israel, and He spoke words of comfort to His servant 

Moses saying: "Whosoever hath sinned against me, him only will I blot out of my book. I will not forsake Israel. 

Thou shalt lead them unto the place I promised. And

I will go with thee. Hew then two tablets of stone like

unto the first, that I might write my commandments once more for Israel."

THE TEN COMMANDMENTS AND THE TEACHINGS OF MOSES—Exodus 20 

When Moses came again to his people he brought the tablets of stone upon which God had written once more the commandments He had spoken. Also, he brought the laws which were recorded in the Book of the Covenant.

Then Moses began to teach the people the great lessons of God's commandments and laws; to worship the only true God in spirit and in truth; to live righteously with all men; and to become a holy nation keeping God's word alive in the earth. 

The First Commandment, Thou shalt have no other gods before me, was a difficult law for Israel to learn. On all sides of them in Egypt they had seen the worship of many gods. Because they could see the sun, the moon, the stars, the rain, and the growing things of the earth they could understand why the Egyptians believed that there were many gods in the heavens. whose business it was to govern all the different things of the earth. But it was much more difficult to think of one god over all the earth, the heavens, and mankind. All they had to help them was the testimony of 

Abraham and of Jacob until Moses became their leader. 

Now before Mount Sinai the Lord Jehovah, the only true God, had made His presence known to Israel. 

Now they knew the source of the power given to their leader Moses for their deliverance from bondage and for their protection in their wanderings. How could they forget? But there were generations yet to come. 

What of them? Throughout the years the First Commandment stood as a challenge to Israel and to all mankind to accept one God, the Creator. It took Israel

many generations to learn this first great commandment but when they did they carried the great truth to all mankind. 

The Second Commandment, Thou shalt not make unto thee any graven image, was also necessary for Israel to learn because of the forms of worship they had seen in Israel and were to see in Canaan. Because people thought of their gods as living beings they made images of them so that they might see them. But what was more, it was the custom to worship such images offering sacrifices to them, thinking thereby to gain blessings and favors from them, also, to prevent the anger of the gods and the evil they might do. 

Moses knew the human appeal of such worship, and the Children of Israel were very human, desiring to have the good things and fearing the hard, unpleasant things. But Moses knew also that worship for good gifts was not the true form of worship, so when he taught this great commandment to Israel he added an explanation, also a blessing and a penalty to the words spoken by Jehovah. "Thou shalt not make unto thee any graven image, nor any likeness of anything that is in the heaven above, or that is in the earth beneath, or that is in the water under the earth: thou shalt not bow down thyself unto them, nor serve them; for I the Lord thy

God am a jealous God, visiting the sins of the fathers upon the children, upon the third and upon the fourth generation of them that hate me and showing mercy unto thousands of them that love me and keep my commandments."

Moses taught Israel to worship as Jesus taught mankind: "God is a spirit, and they that worship Him must worship Him in spirit and in truth.' 

The Third Commandment, Thou shalt not take the name of the Lord thy God in vain, had for its purpose the keeping of the name of the Lord God sacred.

It was the custom to use a sacred name for emphasis or as a proof of one's honesty. If the thoughts of God are to be kept sacred and exalted then the name of God must be kept sacred and used only in worship or with reverence. Thus it was that there was added a penalty to this great law: "Thou shalt not take the name of the Lord thy

God in vain; for the Lord will not hold him guiltless who taketh His name in vain."

The Fourth Commandment, Remember the sab bath day to keep it holy, was an ancient Hebrew law. 

Abraham had taught it to his children. Out of this holy observance of a day set apart for God Israel had become strong in spirit. But when they lived in bondage in Egypt the custom had to be abolished. In Egypt the people held feast days in honor of their many gods but the feast days were not holy days they were times of feasting and merriment.

The law of the Sabbath was the law established by God at the creation of the earth. 

Now Israel was to return to the sacred law of the sabbath and keep it holy in every detail that they might be blessed with God's spirit. 

Remember the sabbath day, to keep it holy. Six days shalt thou labor and do all thy work; but the seventh day is the sabbath of the Lord thy God: in it thou shalt not do any work, thou nor thy son nor thy daughter, thy man servant nor thy maid servant nor thy cattle nor thy stranger that is within thy gates; for in six days the Lord made heaven and earth, the sea, and all that in them is, and rested the seventh day: wherefore the Lord blessed the sabbath day and hallowed it. 

The Fifth Commandment, Honor thy father and thy mother, was the law upon which the racial integrity of Israel was founded. It was the law upon which

96 God's holy nation was to be builded. Out of the sacredness of marriage and family relations, purity and righteousness could continue in the earth, for when children followed the righteous teachings of their parents, truth is kept alive in the earth. 

Now a great tribal law was to become a sacred commandment that the eternal progress and happiness of mankind might be assured. It was upon this commandment that a righteous nation could be built and could endure and thus it came to be recorded: "Honor thy father and thy mother that thy days may be long in the land which the Lord thy God giveth thee." 

The Sixth Commandment, Thou shalt not kill, is the great law protecting God's greatest blessing, the gift of life. Jealousy, hatred, and fear bred in the heart of man leads to violence and murder. Such was the experience of Cain. Moses understood these things and taught Israel to respect human life as a sacred thing, a God-given blessing to man that he might learn, and progress, and gain immortality. 

The Seventh Commandment, Thou shalt not commit adultery, is the great law of individual purity. 

To the Hebrews impurity was one of the greatest sins. 

The penalties that were laid down for the violation of this law were severe and merciless. Because of their knowledge of the sanctity of marriage and family life, adultery was a grievous sin, for the individual and for society. But what was more, Moses knew that the spirit of God could not dwell in impurity. There are no penalties or blessings attached to this great law, it is a commandment carrying its blessings or its punishments with it, inescapable and far reaching for good or for evil.

The great commandments of righteous living, the Eighth Commandment, Thou shalt not steal; the 

Ninth Commandment, Thou shalt not bear false wit-

ness against thy neighbor; and the Tenth Commandment, Thou shalt not covet, are the laws upon which all social and righteous living must be built. As Moses saw the actions of his people day after day he saw the great need for men to curb their desires, to covet the things of another led to jealousy, to stealing, and to lieing. If all these actions could be curbed Moses knew that everyday life could become a beautiful, kindly thing. He knew also until all men loved their fellowmen righteousness could not be established in the earth. 

Moses also instructed the children of Israel to teach the commandments to their children, even to write the words of them on the posts of their houses that every household in Israel might know and obey them.

Into their sacred records the Hebrews wrote the commandments as they were pronounced by the voice of God and as they were taught by God's great servant, Moses.

THE BUILDING OF THE TABERNACLE AND THE WORSHIP OF ISRAEL-Exodus 25, 26, 40 

While Israel remained at Mount Sinai Moses revealed the vision God had given to him concerning the worship of Israel. And when they heard of the plans for the building of the Tabernacle or Tent of Meeting, their joy was unbounded and the time of the building of the holy sanctuary was the happiest of all the years spent in the journey to the Promised Land. 

Moses chose Bezeleel and Aholiah to direct the building of the Tabernacle because they were skilled craftsmen. The people brought of their treasures for the adornment of the building, gold, silver, bronze, and precious stones. The women under the direction. of Miriam, the sister of Moses, wove the fine linens needed for the curtains of the inner and outer courts of the Tabernacle and the garments of the priests. All

worked together faithfully and eagerly that the house of worship be acceptable unto the Lord. 

All was done as Moses had been instructed by God. 

First there was made a box of acacia wood about four feet long, two feet wide, and two feet deep, overlaid within and without with gold. Inside the box the tablets of stone were placed upon which God had written the commandments. The sacred box and its contents was known henceforth as the Ark of the Covenant.

The Tabernacle was made on three sides of wood and bronze, the front or entrance was a curtain of fine linen richly embroidered, and the roof was of fine cloth of goat's hair and skins of rams.

A veil separated the Tabernacle into two chambers, the first was called the "sanctuary," the second was called the "holy of holies." Around the Tabernacle was a large court surrounded by curtains of linen hung from sixty bronze posts. The Tabernacle and the court were so made that they could be taken apart when it was necessary to move from place to place. 

When all was completed Moses called the people together. The Ark of the Covenant was placed in the 

Holy of Holies. A sacrifice of thanksgiving was offered before the door of the Sanctuary. To the sons of Levi was given the care and worship of the Tabernacle and Aaron was chosen and anointed as the high priest of the worship of God. In the words of the Lord used by 

Moses: "This anointing was to be an everlasting priesthood throughout all generations."

Then Aaron spoke to those assembled in the court of the Tabernacle saying: "The Lord bless thee and keep thee; 

The Lord make his face to shine upon thee; 

And be gracious to thee!

The Lord lift up His countenance upon thee 

And give thee peace!"

Even as the high priest spoke a pillar of cloud from the heavens settled down upon the Tabernacle and the glory of the Lord filled the holy place. 

And from that time forward when the children of Israel were to take up their journey the cloud was lifted; but if the cloud were not taken up they did not go forward but remained until it was taken up. And at night the Lord placed a pillar of fire upon the Tabernacle that all might see it and know that He was with them.

The Tabernacle and its worship with the priesthood of Aaron were very great blessings to be given to Israel. Now they went on their way in humility and obedience.

LIVING ABOVE THE EVILS OF ENVIRONMENT The Israelites In Canaan Under the Judges DURING URING the journeyings of Israel from Egypt to the borders of Canaan they had lived away from the rest of the world. It was a period of learning that God had planned for them, the people He had chosen to be 

His own.

In this school of experience God had chosen and directed the leaders of His people, Moses and Aaron; He had delivered Israel from slavery to the Egyptians; Hė had brought them unharmed across the Red Sea; He had provided them with food and water in the desert; He had spoken to them giving them the Ten Commandments, the great laws of life; and He had given them a sign of His spirit to go with them every day, the pillar of cloud over the Tabernacle.

All of these manifestations of God should have given the Israelites a testimony of God's loving kindness, of His power, of His being, and of His spirit. When they made the holy covenant with God, He promised that He would never forsake them if they kept the commandments which He had given to them. It was a glorious promise and a great challenge was given to Israel.

Now that they were at the borders of Canaan, a new life confronted the Hebrews, another great period in their history. All that had gone before was but a preparation for the great task of growing into a nation.

It was not a beautiful new world to which they came, this Land of Promise. It was a world of man's making. On every hand Israel was confronted with new customs, new ideals, and new values; for people of Canaan worshipped many gods thinking to gain thereby food, security, and possessions. Honesty, purity, and justice had little or no place in their dealings with their fellowmen. Instead of good, much evil abounded in the land of Canaan.

Israel had been taught during the wanderings in the wilderness God's pattern of righteous living. Furthermore, it was God's purpose that Israel grow into a righteous nation. The challenge that came to Israel in making a new home in the land of Canaan was one of grave import. In this new environment the people of 

Israel had many choices to make whether to follow their own way of life or to adopt the ways of the Canaanites. 

But they had always a guide to follow, a testimony of the true God, and a knowledge of His commandments. 

Through obedience to the Ten Commandments, Israel grew in strength and power but when they followed the way of the Canaanites they fell into darkness and sorrow. Many times during this period of Israel's history they had to be taught the true meaning of obedience as God requires it from His children.

PREPARING TO ENTER CANAAN-Numbers 13-15 

The Promised Land lay ahead of the Israelites.

Moses chose twelve men, one of each tribe, to enter the land of Canaan and bring back a report of the problems which lay before the people. And they did as they were instructed by Moses and the men searched the land from border to border. Then they came back to their people awaiting at Kadesh on the edge of the desert. And

Moses called the Israelites together and they listened to the words of their tribesmen: "Surely this is a land flowing with milk and

honey. Look at the choice fruit we have brought, grapes, melons, and dates. But the land is occupied by mighty tribes who have built great walled cities, the Amalekites in the southland, the Hittites, the Amorites, and the Jebusites in the mountains of the north, and the Canaanites in the valley of the Jordan." 

But Joshua and Caleb spoke out against the others saying: "The land is a goodly land. God is with us and we can possess the land. They cannot match the strength of God. Let us go up at once." "Nay," cried the other men sent by Moses, "we cannot take the land, the tribes are bigger and stronger than we are. And we saw the sons of Anak who are a race of giants. If we go up against them we shall be destroyed."

When the Israelites heard these words they wept in their disappointment. Then some of them became enraged and cried out saying: "Would we had died in Egypt or in the desert.

Why has God brought us here to die by the sword and our wives and children to be taken captives. Let us choose a captain and let us return to Egypt."

Moses and Aaron were overcome with grief to see that this people should forget the goodness of God so easily. And the Lord spoke to Moses giving him words to say unto Israel: "Ye shall not come into the land concerning which I sware to make you dwell therein, save Caleb and 

Joshua. But your little ones, them will I bring in, and they shall know the land which ye have despised."

On the next day many of the men of Israel came to Moses and begged to be allowed to enter the land. 

But Moses refused them saying: "It is too late. 

Go not up for the Lord is not with you; that ye may not be smitten and fall by the sword of your enemies."

Nevertheless, the men went. But the Ark of the

Covenant stayed in the camp. The tribes of the Amalekites came down from the mountains and the Israelites who had ventured against them were driven back to the desert and many were killed.

THE WANDERINGS OF ISRAEL-Numbers 20, 21, 23 

Now Moses led his people away from the Land of Promise to the wilderness. There they lived until 

God prepared the way for the conquest of Canaan. The years passed slowly and wearily. After almost forty years the wanderers found themselves once more at Kadesh. Suffering for food and water for themselves and their flocks and herds, they were rebellious and cried unto Moses for relief. And the Lord instructed Moses to gather the people together and to command the rock before them and water would spring forth.

But when the people were assembled Moses cried out in anger, "Hear, Now ye rebels; must we fetch you water out of this rock?"

Then Moses struck the rock twice with his staff and the water flowed out abundantly. The people and their animals drank and their suffering was relieved. 

But what was more their courage and faith were sustained at the evidence of God's providence. However, the Lord was sorely grieved that Moses should have forgotten all that had gone before and should have disobeyed Him and glorified himself, and He said to 

Moses and Aaron: "Because ye believed Me not and did not sanctify

Me in the eyes of the children of Israel, therefore, ye shall not bring them unto the land which I have given them."

When the fortieth year came Moses called the people together for now they were strong in spirit and ready to fight to possess the land of the inheritance God had promised their fathers.

So Moses sent to the King of Edom asking that the Israelites be allowed to pass through his land. Because the people of Edom were the descendants of Esau, Moses did not wish to shed the blood of the kinsmen of Israel. But the King of Edom sent his soldiers to keep the wanderers out of his land. Then Moses turned southward to find another way to Canaan. At this time a great loss came to Israel for Aaron the great leader, died. This was a great sorrow because the people loved Aaron for his understanding and his loyalty. 

Now Eleazer, the son of Aaron, became the high priest of Israel.

When the Israelites came to the land of the Amorites, God was with them and they took all the land and the royal city of Heshbon. Also when they came to

Bashan, the land of the mighty men they were victorious and were able to take possession of the land. But when they came to the land of Moab, Moses took not a foot of the land because the Moabites were the descendants of Lot. So the Israelites pitched their tents on the plains near the waters of the Dead Sea. 

VIEWING THE PROMISED LAND-Numbers 27-33 

Now the Israelites pitched their tents on the plains of Moab near the waters of the Dead Sea. From the nearby hills the beautiful valley of the Jordan and the magnificent city of Jericho could be seen. 

It was a glorious sight for the wanderers. But what was more it was the beginning of the fulfillment of the promise made to Abraham and to Jacob. 

And the Lord took Moses unto the mount to show him the heritage of Israel and He said to His servant: "See the land I have given unto the Children of

Israel. Now thou shalt be gathered unto thy people for ye rebelled against my commandment in the desert of Zion.'

When Moses knew that his work was over he

asked the Lord to appoint a leader for Israel that they might not be as sheep without a shepherd. The Lord answered Moses saying: "Take thee Joshua, the son of Nun, a man in whom is the spirit, and lay thine hand upon him." 

And Moses took Joshua before the people and Eleazer, the high-priest set him apart as the leader of Israel.

THE FAREWELL OF MOSES-Deuteronomy 1 

Now for the last time Moses called his beloved people to him. Then over the people assembled Moses spread his hands in blessing; blessing first the tribes, each in turn, telling them which portions of the land of Canaan should be theirs. To Israel, the nation, he spoke saying: "The eternal God is thy refuge. Be strong and of good courage. Jehovah, thy God, He will go before thee and He will not fail or forsake thee."

And Moses spoke to them at length of the experiences of their fathers, even of Abraham and of Isaac, and of Jacob. Then he counselled them not to disobey any of God's laws but to be faithful in all things that they might live long in the land of their inheritance. 

Then once more Moses reminded the people of the meaning of the great laws of God, the Ten Commandments, instructing the priests of Israel that the sacred records of the covenants and laws of God be kept in the Ark of the Covenant and that they should be read aloud to the people at the end of every seven years at the Feast of the Tabernacle.

Now that he had come to the end of his counseling Moses proclaimed his testimony that all might remember. "Hear O Israel! The Lord our God is one Lord 

The Lord thy God requireth of thee, to fear the Lord thy God, to walk in all His ways, and to love Him and

to serve Him with all thy heart, and with all thy soul. "I call heaven and earth to record this day that I have set before you life and death. Therefore choose life that thou and thy seed may live."

After the great prophet and leader had spoken to his people for the last time, he went alone upon the mountain called Nebo, for he knew the time was near for him to die. Then God fulfilled His promise to His servant and showed him all of the Promised Land. And as Moses gazed upon its valleys and its hills and its plains, its palm trees and its date groves, even as far as the silver shimmer of the waves of the great Sea, he knew that the heritage of Israel was indeed a goodly one. Filled with thankfulness and satisfaction, Moses died on the lonely peak and the Lord buried His faithful servant in the valley of Moab, but no man knoweth of his sepulcher unto this day.

All of Israel mourned for their prophet and leader, who had led them out of bondage, who had guided them in all their wanderings, and who had taught them to know the true and living God.

SERVING TWO MASTERS-Numbers 22, 24 

While the Israelites dwelt on the plains of Moab they grew in numbers and in strength, in sight of the land they were to occupy when God opened the way for them, and they were united in purpose. 

But Balak, the king of Moab was greatly worried over the strength of the Israelites and feared that they might take his kingdom as they had taken the land of the Amorites and the land of Bashan. Therefore, he sent for Balaam, a prophet and a seer to come and curse the Hebrews.

The messengers of Balak came to Balaam bringing with them much treasure. Now Balaam, who dwelt in the land of Midian, believed in the God of Israel. So he said to the men of Moab:

"Lodge here with me this night, and I shall tell you on the morrow what the Lord bids me do." 

During the night God spoke to Balaam saying: "Thou shalt not go with the men of Moab; thou shalt not curse the people: for they are blessed."

And Balaam told the messengers that he could not do their king's bidding.

Balak sent the messengers back to Balaam promising him great wealth and honor if he would but come to Moab and curse Israel. Again the wise man asked

God's will, but in his heart he desired to serve Balak, for he saw the wealth of the land and the pleasures that abounded there.

God was displeased with Balaam because of his desire for the wealth of Balak, so He spoke to him saying: "Go with them; but only the word which I speak unto thee, that shalt thou do."

So Balam saddled his ass and went with the king's messengers to Moab. And when Balak heard that the wise man was coming he went out to meet him. 

On the next day the king took Balaam to a high hill where there was one of the places of sacrifice of the god, Baal. "Build me here seven altars and bring a ram and a bullock for each," ordered Balaam.

When the burnt offerings were made the wise man went alone to a nearby cliff that God might impart to him the words to say to Balak.

When Balaam spoke the words of God praising Israel for their strength and their purity, the wrath of 

Balak was very great, and he cried: "I bade thee curse mine enemies and thou hast blessed them." 

And Balaam answered the king: "What the Lord hath put into my mouth, that only must I speak.

Then Balak demanded that another sacrifice be

made in another place, so the seven altars and the oxen and the rams were made ready. As the smoke ascended from the altars once more Balaam sought God to know the words that he should speak to the King of Moab. 

And Balaam returned to Balak and said: "God is not a man that He should lie, neither a son of man that He should repent. What He hath said He performs, and 

His utterances shall He make good. Bless them He bids me. And if this is His commandment, can I reverse it?" 

And again, a third time, Balak demanded that seven altars be built and seven sacrifices be offered: 

Now Balaam knew in his heart that it was God's will that Israel be blessed. So turning his eyes to the camp of Israel on the plains below, once more Balaam blessed Israel saying: "How goodly are thy tents, O Jacob and Israel. 

Blessed be every one that blesseth thee, and cursed be every one that curseth thee.' 

In his anger Balak cried: "I called thee to curse mine enemies and thou hast blessed them three times.

Get thee home to thine own place. I thought to advance thee to great honor, but the Lord hath held thee back from it."

Even though Balaam had spoken the words of God to Balak he desired to please the King of Moab because he wanted more than anything else the great treasure the king had offered to him. So he counseled the king to be friendly with the Israelites and not to fight them, to mingle with them and live in peace with them.

And the people of Moab followed the counsel of Balaam and treated Israel with friendliness instead of enmity. Soon all were living in peace together. The way of living of the people of Moab became pleasing to many of Israel. Many young men married the women of Moab and made homes for themselves; many joined with their neighbors in the worship of Baal and forgot

Jehovah, while many others forgot the ways of honesty and of purity. And when the time came for them to leave Moab to enter the Promised Land they forgot their inheritance and remained behind. 

But Balaam did not go unpunished for his evil counsel. He became openly a traitor to God and joined the Midianites to help them to fight against Israel. But the Midianites were defeated and Balaam was killedout of his choice of good or evil the wise man had chosen foolishly.

THE GREAT CAPTAIN Joshua 1-10 

The land of Canaan lay before the Israelites in all its variety and wonder. As they looked upon its grassy plains dotted with palm trees and its hillsides bearing grapes, olives, and pomegranates, they saw a choice land of God's making, the land promised to the seed of Abraham. They saw also a land occupied by many tribes and covered by the works of man, homes and cities. To a people who were dwellers of the wilderness, walled cities of stone houses were a great wonder, but a greater source of wonder was the many gods of the Canaanites and the strange and cruel sacrifices used in the worship of these gods. This was the place where 

Israel was to become a great nation. To occupy the land of Canaan and to conquer the people was a great task for the Hebrews, and God appointed Joshua to be the leader of Israel for this great undertaking. 

Joshua, the faithful servant of Moses, had had a long period of training during the journey to Canaan. 

He had shown his loyalty to God's plans and his ability as a warrior many times. When called to the leadership of Israel upon the death of Moses, Joshua knew that 

God's promises would be fulfilled when He said: "As I was with Moses, so will I be with thee. I will not fail thee or forsake thee. Be strong and of good courage."

Many times during their wanderings had the Israelites seen the courage and loyalty of Joshua, at Rephidim, at Sinai, and at Kadesh. They knew his understanding of God's purposes and his sincere desires for their success in attaining their inheritance, so they rejoiced greatly when Joshua became their leader promising: "All that thou commandest us we will do. According as we hearkened unto Moses in all things so we will hearken unto thee. Only the Lord thy God be with thee as He was with Moses." 

Then Joshua prepared Israel to leave the land of

Moab and to cross the river Jordan into Canaan. Also he sent spies ahead to bring him word of the land nearby and of the city-fortress of Jericho. Soon a great caravan of about forty thousand souls was on its way. 

And the Ark of the Covenant carried by twelve men, one chosen by Joshua from each of the tribes of Israel, led the way.

And when they came to the river Jordan, Joshua ordered the leaders to continue. As the feet of the bearers of the Ark of the Covenant touched the waters of the Jordan the waves parted leaving the bed of the river dry. As they came to the middle of the river-bed the 

Ark was rested upon twelve large stones, and all of Israel passed by. Then once more the Ark took its place at the head of the caravan and the waters of the Jordan came together again. Now Israel knew without doubt that God was with Joshua as he had been with Moses, and they went forward with great courage. 

Joshua caused his people to camp at Gilgal to the east of the city of Jericho to await the return of the spies and to select a band of warriors from the men of Israel. 

When the spies returned to the camp of Israel they told Johsua what had befallen them inside the walls of the city; how they had been discovered by the king's men, how Rahab had hidden them on the roof of her

house under bundles of flax, and how she had lowered them to safety from the walls of the city after nightfall. 

Also the spies brought the word to Joshua that many of the tribes of Canaan were living in great fear of the coming of Israel.

When all was ready God instructed Joshua how to proceed to take the city of Jericho: 'Ye shall march around the city, first the men of war, then seven priests blowing trumpets of ram's horn, then the priests with the Ark, and then the rest of the people of Israel. This shall ye do for six days. 

And the seventh day ye shall march round the city seven times. Then shall all the people shout a great shout and the wall of the city shall fall down flat, and the people shall go up and take the city."

It was a strange way to take a city, but all happened as Joshua commanded his people. The city of 

Jericho was taken and only Rahab and her household were saved from destruction.

Then the warriors of Israel, under Joshua's direction went all through central Canaan taking the smaller cities which were ruled over by independent kings. Later, the King of Jerusalem, fearing for his city, made an alliance with five kings of the tribes of southern Canaan, thus hoping to be able to defeat the Israelites. But Joshua was able to defeat them in a great battle. Then he went and took their cities.

In this manner in seven years Joshua, under God's direction had taken most of the land of Canaan from the Dead Sea to the Sea of Galilee. Then he divided the land among the tribes of Israel as Moses had given him instruction, by casting lots before the Lord. And each tribe went to live in a different part of the "Promised Land" which was their particular inheritance. 

THE MESSAGE OF JOSHUA-Joshua 23, 24 

Then Joshua set up the Tabernacle of the Lord

at Shiloh, and the Levites, who served as priests of the Tabernacle, were instructed to live among the people of the cities to teach them of God's laws and to direct the worship of God at the sacred altar at Shiloh, the only one upon which their sacrifices must be offered to God. And Joshua made his home at Shiloh.

For many years Israel lived in peace occupying the land of their inheritance. Then Joshua, who was growing old, sent word to all the tribes to gather together once more so that he might speak to them in farewell. They came together at Shechem, the home of 

Abraham, the priests, elders, judges, and many of the people of Israel. And Joshua spoke to them in farewell.

First he reminded the Israelites of Abraham, of Isaac, and of Jacob, of their knowledge and faith in the only true God; of the beginnings of Israel in the land Canaan; of Joseph, who became the Governor of Egypt; of the great suffering of Israel in bondage in Egypt; of 

Moses and Aaron raised up to bring about their deliverance; of the building of the Tabernacle at Mount 

Sinai; and of the protection of God during the long years in the wilderness. Then Joshua read to them from the book of the law of God explaining the commandments written upon the tablets of stone and the laws given to the Israelites through the prophet Moses. 

Then Joshua pleaded with the people saying: "Be very strong and resolve to do just as Moses commanded you, swerving there from neither to the right nor to the left. The Book of the Law must never be off your lips that you may be careful to comply with all that is written therein. For then you shall succeed and make your life prosperous. In all things I command you to be strong and to be brave for the Lord your God is with you in whatever you undertake.' 

Before Joshua died he fulfilled a great trust given to Israel. The body of Joseph brought out of Egypt

was now laid to rest with his fathers, Abraham, Isaac and Jacob.

No leader was appointed to take the place of Joshua, the great captain, because of the scattered condition of Israel. But from time to time leaders known as judges were raised to administer the affairs of everyday life and to assist the priesthood of God in directing the lives of the people in honesty and righteousness. 

THE WAYWARDNESS OF ISRAEL-Judges 1-3 

For four hundred years the Israelites continued to occupy parts of the land of Canaan which Joshua had gained for them for their inheritance. It was as the great captain had warned them, when the Israelites were left to themselves "every man would do what was right in his own eyes" and forget that God's laws were given to them as the only way to right living. 

Those who covenanted with Joshua at Shechem to serve God and obey Him remained faithful. But a new generation settled down to enjoy the blessings of the land for which their fathers had struggled. They did not obey God's command to drive the heathen tribes out of Canaan and to destroy the altars of worship to their pagan gods. And the gods of Canaan became as Jehovah had forseen: "Their gods shall be a snare unto you. I will not drive them out before you; they shall be as thorns in your sides."

As time passed the Israelites forgot to gather at Shiloh and worship the living God, the Creator of the earth and man. Instead they worshiped the god Baal and the goddess Ashtoreth as the Canaanites did, because these gods were supposed to give them their crops and the increase in their flocks and herds. 

But this disobedience to the commandments of God written upon the tablets of stone and kept in the sacred Ark of the Covenant at Shiloh was not all. Soon the sons and daughters of Israel married with the Ca-

naanites and forgot how God had protected them for many generations through famine and through bondage and from their enemies that they might become a holy nation.

So it came about that through following the way of living of the Canaanites the Hebrews became weak and defenseless against the enemies who sought to drive them out of Canaan or to hold them in bondage. 

And God saw the foolishness of Israel and left them alone waiting until they should call upon Him.
