Many times, however, God inspired leaders called judges to help them to defeat their enemies. Because of these leaders raised up by God, the tribes of Israel managed to survive in the land of Canaan. Othniel was the first judge, Samuel the last. But for many generations at a time Israel had no prophet to bring them to God's counsel and comfort.

Cushan, a strong king, came from the east and held the tribe of Judah in bondage for eight years. God inspired Othniel to gather together an army and Cushan was defeated and driven out. Then Othniel continued to guide Israel ruling over them as a judge and keeping them united.

After forty years of peace another enemy threatened Israel, the King of Moab. He ruled over them for eighteen years taking most of their fruit, wine, grain. and cattle in tribute. Once more God saw the suffering of Israel and a leader was inspired to free them. Ehud, the Benjaminite.

Then came the most powerful enemy of all to try to crush Israel. Jabin, King of the Canaanites who dwelt in the north. For twenty years he punished the 

Hebrews with his cruelty. This time no tribe had the courage to face the great armies of their cruel enemies. 

Nor would the tribes forget their differences and unite together.

DEBORAH, THE VALIANT WOMAN LEADER Judges 4, 5 

During this period of suffering a good woman named Deborah had become a great comfort to her people of the tribe of Ephraim. Because she knew the laws of her people many came to her for counsel. Her fame spread throughout Israel and day after day she sat under a palm tree near her home in Shiloh serving her people. 

And Deborah learned of the suffering of Israel in bondage to Jabin as they cried to her in their distress: "We dare not leave our homes lest the robbers come.... The soldiers of Jabin came and carried away my wife and children. . . . My sheep have been stolen 

My crops have been burned." 

And Deborah answered them: "Ye have chosen new gods. Therefore this trouble has come to you. Why do you hide in your tents?" 

And the people answered: "We have no horses and chariots like the warriors of Jabin. We have no leaders." 

Then Deborah said to her people: "Put your trust in the Lord our God, who gave Joshua dominion over the mighty. Strengthen your courage.' 

Straightway Deborah went to find Barak, a great and good man who was also a valiant soldier. 

When she came to the soldier she spoke as God inspired her saying: "This is the word of the Lord God of Israel. Gather ten thousand men of the tribes of Naphtali and Zebulun and lead them to Mount Tabor.

But Barak knew the strength of the armies of the enemy under a great captain, Sisera. So he hesitated to undertake the task alone and he answered Deborah saying: 'If thou wilt go with me to put courage into the

hearts of Israel, I will go. But if thou wilt not go with me I will not go." thee." 

"Surely," Deborah answered, "I will go with

Then Deborah and Barak went to the land of the north calling the men of Israel to arise and fight for their freedom. An army of ten thousand soon came to the call, and the valiant Barak and the inspired Deborah led them to Mount Tabor where the army of Sisera was gathered.

From the slopes of the mountain the men of Israel looked upon the vast army of Sisera with the prancing horses and chariots of war with wheels set with fearful knives.

Seeing the fear settling upon the Israelites, Deborah commanded Barak to lead his army to battle saying: "Doth not the Lord go before thee? Up! For this is the day which the Lord delivereth Sisera into thine hands."

As Barak led his army onward a great storm broke in the hills and the camp of Sisera was flooded. Unable to move the great chariots of war the soldiers of the enemy were stricken with fear. The Israelites came down upon them and many were killed and others fled along with their captain, Sisera.

After much wandering Sisera came to the tent of Jael, the wife of a Kenite. Weary and exhausted he sank to the floor and asked for a drink. And Jael gave him milk to drink. Asking the woman to guard the door of the tent Sisera fell asleep. 

Then Jael killed Sisera with a large wooden tent pin, while he slept. Soon Barak came to the tent of Jael having followed the defeated captain and found his body covered with a mantle. Seeing this strange fulfillment of God's promise, Barak continued to lead Israel until the power of Jabin, the Canaanite king, was completely destroyed.

And Deborah led her people in triumph to Shiloh and celebrated the victory in a great song of thanksgiving: "For that the leaders took the lead in Israel. 

For that the people willingly offered themselves. 

Bless ye the Lord. 

Hearken O Kings; and give ear, O ye princes

Unto the Lord to Jehovah of Israel. 

Praise will I offer."

For many years Deborah continued as a judge in Israel and the counsel of her lips was good and true because in all things she trusted the Lord.

GIDEON DELIVERS ISRAEL FROM THE MIDIANITES Judges 6-12 

Once more when a new generation grew to manhood in Israel and all was peaceful they forgot how God had raised up leaders to deliver their fathers from their enemies. Once more Israel followed the ways of the Canaanites. When a new enemy came they were not prepared, or were they united.

Now great hordes of Midianites came from the South. They grazed their cattle and sheep upon the land of the Israelites and harvested their fields of grain. 

Nothing was left for Israel and the suffering was very great. In some places the Midianites drove them from their homes and villages and they were forced to take refuge in the mountains.

In their great distress the Hebrews remembered 

God once more and called upon Him for aid. And God looked for a leader for Israel who was faithful and strong. Seeing Gideon, a brave and good man of the tribe of Manasseh, God sent an angel to him. And the angel found Gideon as he was threshing wheat in a wine press so that the enemy might not find it. And the angel said to Gideon: "The Lord is with thee, thou mighty man of val-

or. Go in thy might and thou shalt save Israel from the Midianites.' 

And Gideon answered, "Wherewith should I save 

Israel? My family is the poorest of the tribe of Manasseh and I am the least in my father's house. 

Then the Lord spoke to Gideon saying: "Surely, I will be with thee and thou shalt smite the Midianites." 

Now Gideon hesitated no longer but asked God to give him a sign. And Gideon made ready an offering of the flesh of a kid and unleavened cakes and brought them to the angel. 

And the angel said: "Take the flesh and the cakes and lay them upon this rock." 

And Gideon did as he was instructed. Then the angel touched the offering and a flame sprang up and consumed the flesh and the cakes. Then the angel disappeared leaving Gideon in great fear. 

That night God sent a vision to Gideon bidding him to destroy the altar of Baal and the sacred pole of Ashtoreth which his father Joash had built, and Gideon was instructed also to build an altar to God. 

When the people came the next morning to make sacrifices and saw their altars destroyed, they demanded that Gideon be delivered to them in order that they might kill him. 

But Joash answered: "If Baal be in truth a god he can do battle for himself when someone destroyeth his altar."

But Baal did not answer the men of Aphrah. And many came and offered to help Gideon to serve his people as their leader. Then Gideon called to the men of Israel to rally to the standard of freedom and defeat the Midianites.

From the great numbers that came to his call, Gideon chose three hundred men. They surrounded the camp of the Midianites at night, each man with a

trumpet and an empty pitcher with a lamp in it. Blowing a loud blast upon their horns they shouted, "The sword of the Lord and Gideon." Then every man broke the pitcher he carried in which was a lighted torch. The noise of the shouting and the glare of the lighted torches threw the enemy into confusion and they fled in terror. Then the large army of Gideon followed the Midianites and drove them out of the land of Canaan. 

All Israel rejoiced at their freedom. They came back to their fields and their homes once more. Days of peace and contentment came to Israel under the rule of Gideon, for he ruled with a firm hand. As the people enjoyed the great security that had come to them they thought it had come to them because of Gideon and they desired to make him their king. 

But Gideon was loyal to Jehovah and refused to be the king over Israel saying: "Neither I nor my son shall rule over you. The 

Lord is your king and rules over you.'

But Gideon continued to serve his people as a judge. And the people respected and honored Gideon and they brought to him the golden ornaments and gems taken from the Midianites. Then Gideon, thinking to keep the minds of Israel upon God who had saved them from their enemies, made a great mistake.

He made an idol of gold and set it up in Aphrah for the people to worship.

Then Israel forgot the wrong-doing of Aaron in making a golden calf and the commandment of God concerning the use of graven image in His worship, for they began to worship the golden image at Aphrah and idolatry took the place of righteousness in their lives. And Israel began to forget the living God once more.

JEPHTHAH'S FOOLISH VOW-Judges 11 

There came a time during the rule of the Judges

in Israel that the tribes to the east of the land were being sorely oppressed by the Ammonites. It seemed there was no warrior among them to whom they might turn to lead them in battle against their enemies. When they cried unto the Lord for help He bade them to call upon the false gods that they had begun to worship. 

But God did not forsake Israel at this time. Seeing that they needed a leader He inspired the leaders of the people to go to ask Jephthah, an outlaw chieftain, to come to their aid. But when the messengers came to 

Jephthah, he answered: "Did ye not hate me and drive me away from my father's house? Now in your distress you seek me.'

Before Jephthah would promise to help them he demanded that he be made a captain over Gilead, his own tribe. Then he made a strange vow, a foolish and unnecessary vow, yet a vow made sincerely and honestly. Jephthah because of his mother had grown up in idolatry and such was the custom of the pagans in requesting a favor of their gods. Knowing nothing of the ways of the Lord God he said in his vow: "If thou shalt not fail to deliver the people of

Ammon unto my hands then it shall be that whatsoever cometh forth from my house to meet me as I return I will offer it up for a burnt offering." 

The Israelites were successful and the Ammonites were defeated. Then Jephthah returned to his home and his daughter hearing of the success of her father came to meet him. When the warrior saw his only child coming towards him, he remembered his vow. The pride of his great victory left him and he tore his clothes in his great agony.

When the daughter learned the cause of her father's distress, she spoke bravely to him, saying: "My father do to me according to thy vow inasmuch as Jehovah hath given thee victory over the men of Ammon.”

Jephthah was loyal to his vow sinful as it was because he had spoken it and Jephthah did unto his daughter according to his vow.

The price of the victory over the Ammonites, the sacrifice of an innocent maiden, brought a lesson to Israel that was difficult to learn, that God needs no vows to temper His mercy and to secure His blessings, loving obedience and righteousness are all He asks of His children.

SAMSON, THE JUDGE WHO FAILED ISRAEL Judges 13-16 

There was a hero story which was told and retold from generation to generation to the youth of Israel, the story of Samson. Samson's story is a sad one for it is the story of a young man who failed in his responsibility to serve his people. And the story lives today as one of the best known of the world's great stories. 

Many of the Israelites continued loyal to God whatever befell them. Some of the members of the tribe of Dan pledged themselves to keep apart from the 

Canaanites to drink no wine, and to serve God at all times. As an evidence of their pledge these men followed an ancient law of Israel calling themselves Nazarites: "There shall no razor come unto a Nazarite's head. He shall be holy and let the locks of hair of his head to grow."

An angel came to Manoah and his wife and told them of the son that was to be born unto them that he should be a Nazarite and that he should deliver Israel from the hands of the Philistines. When the son was born he was named Samson. And Samson grew up in the knowledge that he was pledged to God's service. 

Samson was exceedingly strong and full of courage and believed with all his heart that as long as he kept the sacred vows of the Nazarites he would be filled with the spirit of God. As Samson grew to manhood, his

strength was a wonder to all who knew him and his spirit a source of rejoicing to his people, the tribe of Dan.

At this time the Philistines, who had settled on the sea-coast plains to the south of Canaan and bordering on the land of the tribe of Dan, became the new enemy of the Hebrews. These Philistines were a race of sailors who had grown rich and powerful through trading with Greece and Egypt. They were skilled also as builders and workers in metal. They built fine great cities in their new home, the greatest being Gaza. In this city was a great temple where they worshiped the god Dagon, a sea god, part man and part fish. 

When the Philistines went to war their metal armor, spears, and swords gave them easy victory. They did not make war on the people of Israel at first, however, they raided and robbed the people taking their crops and their cattle. And the Israelites were afraid to resist the Philistines because of their weapons of war and their great numbers.

While in one of the cities of the Philistines, Samson met a beautiful maiden. When he returned home he asked his parents to arrange it that he might marry the Philistine maiden. The parents were sorely troubled over their son's desire and reasoned with him saying: "Is there never a woman among the daughters of thy people that thou dost wish to marry a Philistine?” 

But Samson returned again to Timnath to see the Philistine maiden. On the way a young lion sprang out at him from the grapevines on the hillside. Without any ado Samson killed the lion with his hands as easily as if it had been a lamb.

In spite of the counsel of his parents, Samson made ready to marry the Philistine maiden. At the wedding feast of seven days there was much feasting and merry making. During the fun-making, Samson got in a quarrel with the Philistines over the answer to

a riddle and Samson, in anger, left the feast and went back to his own home.

When the anger of Samson had abated, he returned to Timnath to get his wife. But the father of the maiden had given her to another. Now the anger of 

Samson knew no bounds and he planned a cruel revenge. Samson took three hundred foxes and tied them together by their tails with firebrands between each two tails. Then the foxes were turned loose to set fire to the grain fields and olive orchards of the Philistines.

When the Philistines looked upon the ruins of their fields and orchards they sought Samson to slay him. First, however, they came to the home of the maiden Samson was to have married and they set it on fire, burning the father and his daughter for causing Samson's anger. 

Straightway Samson returned to the city of Timnath and killed many Philistines in revenge for the death of the maiden. Then he took refuge in a cave in the mountains of the land of Judah. 

Now the Philistines raided and plundered the farms of the Israelites with more cruelty than ever before. When the men of Judah found where Samson was hiding they came to take him to deliver him to the Philistines. And Samson permitted the Israelites to bind him and take him to their enemies. 

As the Philistines shouted in triumph at seeing 

Samson delivered unto them, Samson broke the cords that bound him. Seeing a jaw-bone of an ass lying on the ground, Samson seized it and killed the Philistines. as fast as they came to take him. And the slaughter was very great.

When Samson returned to his people they marveled at his great strength and chose him to be their leader and judge for they said: "Behold the power of the man. He is a Nazarite.

devoted to keep the word of the Lord. Surely he is able to judge us; and to drive out those that oppress us." 

And Samson became the judge over Israel and ruled for twenty years and the Philistines did not plunder the land any more because they were afraid of the strength of Samson.

But Samson did nothing to free his people from the Philistines. He was content to think that fear of him might keep them away always. So in pride and vainglory Samson annoyed the enemies of his people rather than using his strength in wisdom to subdue them. 

On one occasion Samson went into the wicked city of Gaza in the quest of pleasure. The guards saw him and barred the gates, determined to trap him as he left the city. When Samson came to the gates at midnight he placed his arms around the huge gate-posts and tore them down and carried them to the top of a nearby hill and left them there for the Philistines to see when day came.

As time passed once more Samson came to love a 

Philistine woman, Delilah. The Philistines soon learned that Samson came often to the valley of Sorek to see 

Delilah. They came and bribed Delilah with large sums of money to find out the secret of the strength of Samson. Now Delilah loved her own people more than she loved Samson, but she loved money most of all so she accepted the bribe of the Philistines. 

Three times as he visited her Delilah coaxed Samson to tell her the secret of his strength. And three times 

Samson told Delilah a false story. And three times

Delilah bound him as she had been told, and then betrayed him to the Philistines for money.

Three times Samson knew that he had been betrayed, for three times he found himself bound as he had instructed Delilah to bind him. And each time Samson broke the bonds and jeered at the enemies who sought the secret of his strength.

But Samson would not learn wisdom and in his conceit returned again to visit the faithless Delilah. 

This time she wept and said unto him: "How cans't thou say, 'I love thee,' when thou hast mocked me three times and told me lies, and hast not told me truly the source of thy strength." 

And Samson in vexation or foolishness told Delilah his secret. Then Delilah in haste called the lords of the Philistines and while Samson slept they shaved off his hair, the secret of his strength. And Samson awoke to find his strength gone because his hair had been shorn. 

Straightway the Philistines took Samson to Gaza, his eyes were blinded and his feet fettered in chains of brass. Now he spent his days walking round and round, pushing the great handle of millstones grinding corn. 

What a sad picture! 

As he stumbled in his blindness. Samson realized that in his foolishness he had broken his vow. In his selfishness he had betrayed his people. He had forsaken the ways of righteousness for pleasure and for satisfaction. He had sought the approbation of evil men in his conceit over his great strength. But greatest of all he had been disloyal to Israel as its leader. 

For the first time a light came to the spirit of Samson as to what he might have done to serve Israel, and he called upon God in sincere humility. Now day by day as he toiled in weakness there grew in his soul a desire to be given another chance to serve his people. 

When the great feast of Dagon was held the Philistines brought Samson from his prison that they might taunt him. And Samson heard their shouts and jeers but his heart did not fail him. His love of Israel once more flooded his soul, this time he would not fail them. 

To the boy who was leading him he said: "Let me feel the pillars upon which the great temple stands that I may lean upon them."

With his hands on the pillars of the temple of 

Dagon, Samson lifted his blind eyes to heaven and prayed: "O Lord God, strengthen me this once more that 

I might die with the Philistines." 

And as he prayed his great strength returned and with a mighty crash the temple of Dagon fell in ruins and three thousand Philistines died also.

Samson died a great hero, a physical giant but a moral failure. Because of his great strength he slew many of the enemies of his people but because of his weakness he was not able to deliver Israel from its enemies. To him the ways of the Philistines were more pleasing than the ways of God, power was greater than service, and satisfaction greater than obedience. 

Samson failed Israel because of the strength that became weakness.

The Early Kings of Israel 

THE history of any people or nation is in a large part a record of the lives of its leaders. 

In the early days of civilization when families were growing into tribes, the patriarchal or family leader was the head of the tribe. To this leader, all looked for counsel and guidance; he led them as they wandered from place to place with their flocks and herds, he directed them as they made settlements by streams and hillsides; and he led them in battle when it was necessary to fight when their rights were threatened by other family tribes.

After many generations, family tribes grew very large. Often a member of the tribe was chosen to be the leader or chieftain because of his power or because of his wisdom. As centuries passed, tribes became races and nations. Some tribes became races because as they grew in numbers, they remained united and racially pure. Other tribes grew into powerful nations by the conquest of other peoples and their land and possessions. And as races and nations grew, new patterns of leadership arose, tribal-chieftains being replaced by warrior-leaders, and these later by kings. Such was the history of Babylon, Persia, Egypt, and Greece, the great nations of the ancient world. 

But what of God's plan for the leadership of mankind? In the Bible story we can find the answer.

Thus far we have followed the growth of Israel from a family tribe to a numerous people, but what is more, we have seen how God, the Creator of the world and man, provided leaders for His children. We saw 

His companionship with Adam as He taught him the laws of heaven and of all created things; we listened to 

God's word to Abraham as He led him to become a family apart that Israel might grow as a blessing to all mankind; we saw the loving Father's protecting care over Israel in Egypt we followed Moses through the wilderness teaching the children of Israel the great laws of life, the Ten Commandments; and we saw one leader after another provided for Israel as they lived in the Promised Land among the Canaanites. At all times we saw a patient and loving Creator watching over mankind providing direction and council. But what shall we say of Israel with all the blessings that had come to them since God spoke to Abraham?

Joshua gave to each tribe of Israel an inheritance in Canaan, the Promised Land. As the generations passed the tribes increased in numbers and in possessions. From a wandering people they were fast becoming settled as an agricultural people, but they had made little or no progress in becoming a united people in the land of their inheritance. In time of crisis they sought 

God's aid and sustained the leaders He provided for them. But when all was well they neglected the counsel of the priests and judges of Israel. Now after four hundred years in the land of Canaan they were in grave danger, for the powerful Philistines held many of the tribes in subjection.

To save His people God provided a new leader for them, the prophet Samuel. But it was not long before 

Israel demanded a king to lead them, thinking only of power in battle as a source of national strength. A king was chosen and given the task of serving Israel. 

Now we see kings and prophets taking part in the 1

leadership of Israel. It was the golden age of the history of Israel for Israel became a united kingdom, leaders of Israel and the loyalty of the people made the golden age of Israel a reality.

SAMUEL IS PREPARED TO LEAD ISRAEL-I Samuel 1-3 

In the time of Eli, the high-priest of the Tabernacle at Shiloh, a good man, Elkanah, came every year with his family to worship God and to offer sacrifice. 

One year when the time of sacrifice came Elkanah gave a larger portion to his wife Hannah for her offering than he gave to his wife Peninnah and her children, for he loved Hannah dearly and he knew that she sorrowed greatly because she had no children. After the sacrifice was over, the family of Elkanah gathered at the inn to eat together. But Hannah was not comforted for she remembered the many times Peninnah had jeered at her because she had no son. So Hannah left the feast and went alone to the House of the Lord to pray. Kneeling at the altar, she wept and made a vow to Jehovah: "O Lord, if Thou wilt remember Thine handmaid and give me a son, then I will give him to Thee for all of his life." 

Now Eli, the aged priest, saw the moving lips of the woman at the altar but he heard no words. Thinking the woman must have been drinking wine during the feasting, Eli said: "How long wilt thou be drunken? Put away wine from thee." 

But Hannah answered: "Nay, my lord! I have drunk neither wine nor strong drink, I have poured out my soul before Jehovah."

Then the high-priest spoke gently as if ashamed of his hasty judgment, blessing the sorrowing woman and bidding her to go in peace saying:

"May the God of Israel grant thee thy petition which thou has asked of Him."

Then Hannah returned to her husband and her home in Ramah, near Jerusalem. When a son was born to Hannah, she called his name Samuel because he was "Asked of God." For three years the young child was cared for with loving tenderness and great rejoicing. 

But all the time the vow she had made was in the mother's heart. When he was old enough, however, Hannah never faltered in her loyalty. Taking three bullocks, a goatskin of wine and a measure of flour as an offering, she and Elkanah took the child to the House of the Lord at Shiloh. "My lord," said Hannah to the high-priest, "I am the woman who stood beside thee here, praying unto 

Jehovah. For this child I prayed and the Lord hath given me that which I asked. Therefore, also, I have returned him to the Lord to serve Him, as long as he liveth." 

Elkanah and Hannah returned home leaving their little son with Eli, to be the servant of the Temple. 

Now Hannah's heart was filled with gladness. Each year as the time approached to go to Shiloh, Hannah would weave the finest of linen and make it into the garments worn by Samuel in the service of the Lord in the Tabernacle. As time passed other children, three sons and two daughters brought more happiness to the worthy parents.

Meanwhile Samuel was learning many things as he helped Eli in the work of the sanctuary. As he sat with the aged priest day after day and listened to the history of Israel, to the promises made to Abraham, to the words of God to Moses, and to the experiences of his people in the land of Canaan, Samuel's heart was deeply touched. How he longed to help in God's work!

But very often Samuel was very sad, for Eli's sons were wicked men. Even as priests of the House of the

Lord, Hophni and Phineas were dishonest. When offerings were brought for sacrifices, they would take the best meat and wine for themselves. When the sacrifices were offered, the ceremonies were not sincere in word or in deed. And Samuel knew also that because the people saw the gluttony and drunkenness of the sons of Eli many of them stayed away from the worship of God in the Tabernacle.

Now Eli was a good man and often pled with his sons to cease their evil ways, but because of his great love for them, he did no more. Even when God warned 

Eli of the punishment that would come to him and to his sons, he did not remove them from the service of 

God's holy sanctuary. Then God ceased to speak to

Eli for the guidance of Israel. 

It came about that one night Samuel lay down to sleep as was his custom in the inner room of the sanctuary where the Ark of the Covenant rested. He was awakened by a voice which called his name. Thinking that the old priest needed him, he rose and went to Eli answering: "Here am I." 

But Eli said, "I called thee not, my son; lie down again."

As Samuel lay down, the voice called again, and Samuel went again to Eli. But Eli sent him to rest as before.

Then a third time the voice called to Samuel and he went to the couch of Eli once more. Now Eli knew that it was God that was calling to Samuel so he answered his servant saying: "Go and lie down. And if He shall call again thou shalt say, Speak, Lord, for thy servant heareth."

Once again the voice came out of the darkness calling to Samuel. And the young servant answered as Eli had instructed him. Then the voice of God

continued to speak, telling Samuel of the evil that was to fall on the house of Eli. 

So Samuel lay until morning sorrowing over Eli, because he loved the old priest of Israel. of day the boy went about his duties. the old priest called to him and said: 

With the break 

Soon, however, "What is it that the Lord hath said to thee? I pray thee; hide it not from me.”

Then Samuel told all that had been spoken to him. 

When he heard, Eli answered: "It is Jehovah; let 

Him do what seemeth Him good."

Samuel grew to manhood in the service of the House of the Lord. From time to time God spoke to him giving him instructions and counsel in all things that the leader of His people should know. And Samuel was faithful in his dealings with the people of Israel and they came to know that he was chosen of God to be their prophet and leader.

THE ARK OF THE COVENANT TAKEN FROM ISRAEL I Samuel 5-7 

The Philistines, the powerful enemies of Israel, now made war upon them. In a great battle four thousand of the Hebrews were slain. In great terror, those who were saved decided to secure the Ark of the Covenant from Shiloh that it might bring them a victory over their enemies. Thinking only of a way to defeat their enemies, they did not remember that when the Ark went before Israel as Joshua led them at Jericho, it was God who was directing the welfare of Israel. This time Israel had not asked for God's help, they were acting alone.

The wicked sons of Eli took the sacred Ark from its resting place in the Tabernacle in spite of the pleadings of their aged father. When the Ark was brought into the camp of the Israelites, they shouted in great

joy thinking that by some magic power they would be able to defeat the Philistines.

Once more the battle raged, Israel fighting with noise and great tumult. At first the Philistines were afraid when they saw the Ark for they were idol worshipers. They cried out: "Their God is come to help them. Woe unto us; for this is the God that smote the Egyptians! Be strong and quit yourselves like men, O ye Philistines, lest ye be slaves unto the Hebrews as they have been to you.' 

But God was not with Israel, for they had not sought His aid. Once more the Philistines were victorious, but this time the slaughter of the Hebrews was very great. Among the slain were the sons of Eli. But what was the greatest disaster, the Ark of the Covenant was taken away from the Israelites.

Far away in Shiloh Eli sat by the Tabernacle awaiting news of the great battle, his heart sorely troubled for the safety of the sacred Ark. At last a messenger reached the city bringing the sad tidings of the battle. A great cry of agony arose from the people at the news and Eli heard the cry. When the messenger was brought to him, and the old priest heard the dreadful word that the Ark of the Covenant had been taken he fell from his seat and died. Throughout the land as the news spread, men cried out in great sorrow: "The glory is departed from Israel, for the Ark of God is taken.”

Hopeless despair seemed to settle over all Israel and they had no more courage to fight against the Philistines. Now that there was no need of Samuel remaining at Shiloh, he returned to Ramah, his home. There he gathered together some worthy young men to serve as teachers for his people. Samuel trained the young men to have faith in the Lord God, to listen for His voice, and to be pure in thought and action. When the young teachers were ready to leave the school of Samuel, they

were sent throughout the land to encourage the people to take heart and to remember God's goodness to Israel in times gone by and to return to the ways of obedience and worship. In this way Samuel kept alive in his land, during its darkest days, a tiny spark of faith in 

God and His promises to Abraham. 

Meanwhile the Philistines celebrated their victory over the Hebrews by setting up the Ark before the statue of their fish-god Dagon, in the temple at Ashdod. 

The next morning the priests of the temple found the god Dagon fallen from his exalted position and lying on the floor. With great ceremony the fish god was replaced on its throne. The next morning the priests found the same thing had happened, but this time the great idol was broken into pieces. Great fear seized the people of the city that such evil should befall their god. 

When a sore pestilence of boils broke out in the city of Ashdod a few days later, the people demanded that the 

Ark of the Israelites be taken from thir midst.

For seven months the Philistines carried the Ark from city to city, but wherever it went the plague followed and many died. At last the people demanded that the priests return the Ark of Israel lest they be destroyed. With the Ark, the Philistines sent an offering of gold that the anger of Jehovah might be turned away from them.

SAMUEL, THE LEADER OF ISRAEL-I Samuel 7, 8 

When the Ark of the Covenant was returned to Israel, there was great rejoicing throughout the land. 

Then Samuel called to his people to lift up their hearts to the living God in thanksgiving saying to them: "If ye will return to the Lord and serve Him only, He will deliver you out of the land of the Philistines."

The Children of Israel listened unto Samuel and many gathered together at Mizpah, fasting and repent-

ing, to renew their covenants to serve Jehovah and Him only.

Even as they were gathered together at Mizpah, the Philistines came upon them hoping to defeat them. 

But this time Israel trusted in God's aid and they were victorious.

Once more all Israel looked up to the prophet of 

God as their leader. And Samuel continued to travel throughout the land teaching the people to seek happiness through obedience to God's laws and administering justice so that men might live in peace together. Because of his wisdom, justice, and righteousness, Samuel was held in honor by all men at this time. 

But the time came when Samuel could no longer administer all the affairs of Israel for he was getting very old. So it became necessary that others be appointed to serve the people. Now it was that Samuel made a grave mistake, instead of selecting men of wisdom and righteousness to serve Israel he appointed his sons as judges. 

The sons of Samuel, Joel and Abiah, were not like their father, they were dishonst, wicked men. When they were given authority in the land they gave false judgments in cases of dispute and took bribes from the people. Yet in spite of the evil ways of his sons, Samuel allowed them to continue in authority as judges. 

At last the elders of Israel came to Samuel saying: "Behold thou art old and thy sons are not like thee; now make us a king to rule over us like all the other nations."

Now Samuel was greatly troubled over the discontent that had grown among the people. But instead of removing the cause of the unrest and providing righteous leaders for the people he sought to please them. 

So Samuel laid the matter before the Lord. And 

God bade him to warn the people of the evils that would come to them if they chose a king to be their leader. 

Then Samuel spoke to the elders saying:

"If ye make you a king, this will be the manner of his rule over you. He will take your sons for his chariots and to be his horsemen and warriors. He will set some to plough his ground, and to reap his harvests, and to make his instruments of war. He will take your daughters to be his cooks and his servants. He will take your fields and your vineyards and your olive orchards and give them to his friends. He will take the tenth of your flocks and of your crops and ye shall be his servants. Say not, 'A king shall reign over us' for the Lord your God is your king."

But the hearts of the people were set upon having a king like other nations, and they rejected the counsel of Samuel saying: "Nay, we will have a king to reign over us, that he may judge us and go before us and fight our battles.'

Then the Lord bade Samuel to heed the request of 

Israel saying to him: "Hearken unto the voice of the people in all that they say unto thee. Make a king for them. They have rejected me that I should no longer be their king."

Samuel was exceedingly grieved because the people of Israel desired to be governed by a king instead of being directed by a leader chosen by God; that they were ready to depend upon the power of a king to deliver them from their enemies rather than to put their trust in God who had promised them victory over their enemies if they would but serve Him in heart and mind and soul. 

Sorrowing at the wilfulness of Israel, Samuel said in his heart: "I am the last of those judges, through whom God hath governed His people."

SAUL CHOSEN KING OF ISRAEL-I Samuel 9, 10 

Samuel was to find a king for Israel. The aged prophet had returned to Ramah, there in the simple

sanctuary he had built for himself in the hills he awaited God's guidance in the selection of Israel's first king. 

One day at sundown, Samuel sáw two strangers following the road along the hillside that led to his abode. One of them was a tall, handsome young man, head and shoulders above his companion, he was Saul, the son of Kish, of the tribe of Benjamin. 

Even as the young man came up to Samuel to speak to him God made it known to the aged prophet that this was the man who should rule over Israel. 

As Saul was about to speak to Samuel to inquire concerning the cattle he was seeking he was surprised at the words he heard: "I am Samuel, thou seekest. Fret not over thy father's asses for they are already found. Go up before me to the sanctuary. All the desire of Israel is upon thee to make thee their king.'

Saul thought that the prophet was mocking him and he answered him saying: "Wherefore should I be king? Is not my tribe the smallest of Israel, and my family the least of all the families of my tribe?"

Samuel led Saul to the sanctuary and a feast was prepared and many guests assembled. There Saul was seated and served with great honor. After the feast the prophet took the young man alone and spoke to him at great length concerning the past of Israel and of the things to come to pass in order that this people become a united nation. It seemed as though a great inspiration came to Saul for he said to the prophet: "Hath God redeemed Israel from bondage in Egypt for them to be slaves in the land of their inheritance? Surely some good thing must come."

Alone in his chamber, Samuel pondered through the night over the young man who had been brought to him. The loyalty of Saul for his people pleased him greatly as did his great strength and courage. But still

the prophet wondered. Would this man accept his great responsibility as a service to God? Would he seek God's aid in all he did for Israel? Would he think more of the glory of victory in battle than of the welfare of his people?

Early the next day, Samuel accompanied Saul to the gate of the city and as they came to part the prophet took a vial of oil and annointed Saul's head saying: "It is because the Lord hath appointed you to be the king over Israel. The spirit of the Lord will come upon thee. Thou shalt become a different man."

When Saul reached his home he found that his father's cattle had returned as Samuel had said. Even after this assurance of the truthfulness of the words of Samuel, Saul's amazement at what had happened to him at Ramah continued. But he spoke to no one of his kingship.

After the direction which had been given to him in finding a king for Israel, Samuel sent messengers throughout the land calling the people to gather at Mizpah to choose a king. When the people had come together, Samuel spoke to them reminding them once more of all God's blessings to them and admonishing them saying: "Now you are asking for a king to rule over you instead of letting God rule over you. You are not honoring God when you ask for a king. But God will grant your command; He will choose a king for you today.”

Then Samuel told the tribes of Israel to choose the king by the casting of lots. The lot fell to the tribe of 

Benjamin. Then the lot fell to the family of Matri of the tribe of Benjamin; and of the family of Matri the lot fell to Saul, the son of Kish. When Saul was brought to stand before the people of Israel Samuel said: "Behold! this is the king the Lord hath chosen." 

Then the people shouted: "God save the king."

Thereafter Saul returned to his home and followed

his everyday tasks awaiting the call of Israel. One after another there came to Saul young men of all the tribes of Israel to show their loyalty to form his body-guard and do his bidding.

But there were some who were not satisfied with Saul as their king. They did not want a king chosen by 

God, they wanted to choose their own king. 

SAUL SERVES THE MEN OF JABESH-I Samuel 11, 12 

It was not long, however, before a challenge was to come to Israel and its new king. The fierce, plundering desert tribe of Ammonites laid siege to the city of Jabesh. The men of the Jabesh knew that they were not strong enough to defeat the Ammonite king, Nahash, so they sent messengers asking for mercy. The answer came back: "We will spare your lives, but in seven days the soldiers of Nahash will come and put out the right eyes of all your people." 

In great terror, the men of Jabesh sent word to

Saul telling of their sad plight and asking for help. 

When Saul heard of the cruel threat to his people, his anger was very great. Taking the oxen he was driving he slew them and sent parts of them to all the tribes of 

Israel with the threat: "Whosoever will not come forth with Saul and

Samuel to fight the Ammonites, so shall it be done to his oxen." 

The Israelites who heard this strange call from their new king came out of fear to join his army. Sweeping down upon the Ammonites, Saul's army was victorious slaying many and driving the others out of the land.

The Israelites were pleased with their king for. they saw that he was brave and fearless. Some would have killed the men who had opposed his choice to be

their king, but Saul restrained them in thanksgiving because of the great victory that had been given to Israel. 

Now Samuel said to the people: "Let us go to 

Gilgal and make Saul our king." 

At Gilgal, the first camping place of the Israelites in the land of Canaan, Saul was crowned king with great rejoicing.

Samuel ruled no longer as a judge over Israel, but as God's prophet he spent his energies to help the new kingdom to grow strong in righteousness never forgetting to urge Israel to be loyal to their earthly king, but to remember at all times their heavenly king. 

Saul now took his place as the ruler of Israel. He lived apart from them in a royal house of many rooms and courts. He gathered together a large army of warriors to be ready at all times to fight. Every man was required to do his bidding. As time passed, Saul sought 

Samuel's aid and counsel less and less for he was growing very wise in his own eyes. The time was not far distant when the King of Israel was to forget that he was leading Israel for God's purposes; he became the ruler of 

Israel for his own purposes.

HOW SAUL FAILED-I Samuel 13, 14 

The Philistines began to trouble the Israelites once more. They had built strong fortresses throughout the land and kept soldiers in all of them to keep the people in constant fear. Many times, even the people were driven from the small villages to live in caves in the hills. It was natural that now they had a king Israel looked for help in their troubles.

When the Philistines gathered six thousand horsemen and thirty thousand chariots near Gibeah where Saul lived, the king sent messengers to call the soldiers of every tribe together. When Samuel heard the message, he sent word to Saul saying: "Wait until I come in seven days that I might of-

fer sacrifice, that the armies of Israel might take courage and know that God is with them when they go forth to fight." 

Seven days Saul waited and yet Samuel did not come. Saul became impatient at the delay, fearing that his armies might leave him. As he waited, Saul grew angry and said to himself: "Why should I, the King of Israel wait upon 

Samuel? Is not a king mightier than all?” 

Then Saul called to his servants saying: "Bring hither the burnt offering unto me, I will wait no longer."

The sacrifice was made as Saul had seen the priests of Israel perform the ceremonies. The men of the armies looked upon a strange sight for they knew that by the law of Moses none might offer sacrifice save the holy priesthood. Instead of courage, dismay entered the hearts of all; for the words of Saul were without meaning for them.

Even while the sacrifice was still burning upon the altar of Gilgal, Samuel came. He asked, "What hast thou done?" 

Saul replied, "Because thou camest not I was forced to offer sacrifice. I feared that my people were scattering to all parts of the land."

Samuel's heart was filled with sorrow to see the change that had come over Saul. He knew that Saul had not been forced to break the laws of God and he said: "Thou hast acted foolishly, thou hast set thyself above the Lord thy God. Therefore thy kingdom shall be taken from thee and given to another who will obey God."

Instead of going to battle against the Philistines at this time. Saul returned to his home at Gibath. Now his soldiers were afraid of the enemy, for now they had neither courage to fight nor confidence in their leader.

As the days passed and the Philistines continued

to harry the people, Jonathan, the son of Saul, sought to help his father. Jonathan was a valiant young soldier whose heart was filled with faith in God. When he saw the fear that had overcome the warriors of Israel, he said to his armor bearer: "Come let us go to the camp of the Philistines at

Geba. It may be that God will help us for He can save the people with two soldiers as well as with many.” 

Then the two soldiers left the camp of Israel and made their way to the fortress of the enemy across the narrow valley. Climbing on their hands and knees over the rocks of the outer wall, they came to the guard and fell upon him and killed him. Twenty others were killed also. The warriors within the fortress seeing the fate of the guards thought the army of Israel was upon them and great confusion and panic took hold of them. Many of the Israelites who had been forced to fight in the enemies ranks seeing the courage of Jonathan came to his aid and soon a battle was in progress. 

Saul's watchmen seeing the confusion in the camp of the Philistines ordered his armies to battle. When the men knew that Jonathan and his armor bearer were fighting the Philistines they did not hesitate. Rushing upon the confused and panic stricken Philistines they slaughtered many and drove the others out of that part of the land.

Once the courage of Israel was aroused they continued to fight until many of the fortresses of their enemy had been destroyed. Once more the brave men of 

Israel rallied to the banner of their king once more to fight for their inheritance, the Promised Land. 

The growing strength of Israel in the land of Canaan caused the Amalekites grave concern. Bands of cruel 

Amalekite warriors began to harrass the people who lived in the south. These desert warriors had created trouble and suffering for centuries, since the time Moses was leading the wanderers in the wilderness. The

prophet, Samuel, was instructed, that now the time had come to destroy this great evil from the land. So Samuel went to Saul with the inspiration he had received and said to him: "The Lord remembers the treachery of the Amalekites and the evil that they have wrought in the land. 

Go forth and destroy them utterly. Spare nothing, neither man nor beast; neither shalt thou take any spoil of them."

Saul gathered all his armies and prepared for a great battle. Then he warned the Kenites who lived in the land of the Amalekites to leave their homes because they were of the descendants of Abraham. 

When the great struggle began, Saul's armies swept through the land of Amalek even to the borders of Egypt. The Amalekites were destroyed as Saul had been commanded. But Saul spared the life of the great robber king, Agag, that he might bring him to Israel to show him to the people adorned in his golden armor. 

The soldiers of the army were allowed to save many of the choice cattle and sheep of the Amalekites for themselves.

It was a strange caravan that came towards Canaan. This victorious army and its spoils led by two kings Saul, the victor, and Agag in chains. When they came to Gilgal, Samuel was awaiting them and Saul spoke in full of pride saying: "Blessed be thou servant of the Lord I have obeyed the Lord's command."

But Samuel knew of Saul's disobedience and he said to Saul: "If thou hast performed the commandment of the

Lord what is the meaning of the noise of bleating sheep and lowing cattle that I hear?" "The soldiers spared the best of the flocks and herds to offer them as sacrifice to the Lord," the King answered.

When Samuel looked into the eyes of Israel's king, Saul knew that his wickedness was known to God's servant. Then Samuel spake: "Who made thee king over Israel? Why hast thou disobeyed the commands of the Lord?"

Again Saul tried to cover his disobedience and he answered: "I destroyed the Amalekites. I feared the people and obeyed them when they demanded to keep the best of the sheep and cattle make a sacrifice to the Lord." swer: 

The stern voice of the prophet of God made an- "To obey the Lord is better than sacrifice and to hearken is better than the fat of rams. Because thou hast rejected the Lord, He also hath rejected thee from being king over Israel."

Saul cried out in great distress and pled with Samuel to pardon his great sin. In his great sorrow he fell on his knees and caught at the cloak of the prophet to keep him from leaving him alone. A piece of the garment tore away and as the King clutched it Samuel cried. "See the Lord God hath torn the kingdom of Israel from thee. He will give it to another that is better than thee."

Then Samuel called for the King of the Amalekites to be brought before him. With his own hand he killed the robber king before all the people. In great sadness Samuel turned away and returned to Ramah grieving for the failure of the king he had anointed for Israel. After that time Samuel came no more to Saul to serve him as God's propret.

DAVID IS CHOSEN KING OF ISRAEL—I Samuel 16:1-13 

Samuel's sorrow at the unworthiness of Israel's first king was very great. Now a great anxiety for his

people laid heavy upon his heart. He knew that Israel would follow Saul. He feared, also, that the Philistines might not be subdued and Israel might perish. 

After a time God spake again to Samuel and said: "How long wilt thou mourn for Saul, seeing that

I have rejected him from reigning over Israel? Take a horn of oil and go to Jesse the Bethlehemite; for I have chosen a king for Israel from among his sons." 

So Samuel went to Bethlehem and found Jesse who was a grandson of Ruth of Moab. The prophet bade Jesse to bring his sons before him and Jesse brought seven of his sons to Samuel, leaving David, the youngest, who had not yet grown to manhood, watching the sheep in the fields outside the city. 

When Eliab, the first born of Jesse, came to Samuel, the prophet was pleased with the bearing of the sturdy young man and thought that this must be the son chosen to be the new king. 

But the Lord said unto Samuel: "Look not on his countenance or upon his stature, for the Lord seeth not as man seeth, man looketh upon the outward appearance but the Lord seeth the heart of man.'

One after another of the seven sons of Jesse came before Samuel and not any one of them was the choice of God. Then the prophet requested that David be brought from the field.

David, the youngest son of Jesse, was a sturdy youth, happy in the simple life of keeping his father's sheep. Each day as he searched for the choicest pastures on the green hillside or by the cool refreshing streams his heart was gay and thankful. The glory of the sunrise and the sunset, the mystery of the stars, and the wonders of the living things seemed to put a song in his heart. To the simple notes of his beloved harp, his songs became living things. 

When the shepherd boy, David saw the messenger hurrying towards him, he wondered what had hap-

pened that he should be needed. Had the Philistines attached the little city of Bethlehem? His seven brothers had gone on a strange mission that morning to meet the prophet Samuel, had something happened to them? 

As David hastened along with the messenger he could not help but wonder why Samuel had sent for him. When he came to the prophet, David knelt to pay homage to the servant of the Lord for so Jesse had taught his sons to do reverence. Then the words of Jehovah came to Samuel: "Anoint this lad, for he is the one I have chosen." 

There with his father and wondering brothers around him, David was anointed to lead Israel and David knew that some great thing had come to him. It was the new spirit God had given to him. Thereafter as David tended his flocks it seemed that God was very near to him helping him to understand the things that had puzzled his youthful mind and comforting his heart with a knowledge of the goodness of things. 

There on the hillside of Bethlehem, David came to know that God watched over the world as a shepherd guards his flock. There were great things ahead of David, trials and disappointments, victories and gladness.

But the simple song he sang in the days of his youth was always in his heart bringing him assurance of God's protecting care: "The Lord is my shepherd, I shall not want!

He maketh me to lie down in green pastures: 

He leadeth me beside the still waters; 

He restoreth my soul!"

THE EVIL THAT TROUBLED SAUL-I Samuel 16-19 

Saul continued to rule Israel as its king. But all was not well with Saul. The great punishment that was come to him through his own disobedience weighed heavily on his mind. Day after day he sat alone in his

tent brooding. The knowledge that his kingdom was to be taken away from him that his son Jonathan would not be king after him haunted him. At times a great rage seemed to take possession of him at other times a spirit of deep despair, so that his servants believed that an evil spirit had taken possession of their king. Seeking to help their leader in his sore despair, they spake unto him saying: "Behold an evil spirit troubleth thee. Let thy servants seek out a skillful player upon the harp that his music might comfort thee that the evil spirit will leave thee and thou wilt be well."

One of the servants of Saul had heard the sweet singing and playing of David, the shepherd lad, and he suggested that the king send for him. 

So Saul sent messengers to Jesse in Bethlehem asking that David be sent to him in his palace at Gibeah. 

And David came to the King as he had been commanded. 

Day after day he went before the brooding king and sang his songs, sometimes he sang the songs of the shepherds of the hills, sometimes he sang the songs telling of the history of Israel, sometimes he sang the songs of worship and thanksgiving beloved by those who remembered Jehovah, and sometimes he sang the songs his own making of the beauty of the earth and of God's loving kindness. of In time Saul roused himself and began to think of the welfare of his people. Making David his armor bearer, he kept the youth near to him that he might have his sweet music to cheer him. And Saul grew to love the young singer very much and the king's son Jonathan grew to love him also.

Once more the Philistines began to trouble Israel in different parts of the land. David learned a great deal of the ways of battle from his service to King Saul, but it was from his association with Jonathan that his faith in God's help was strengthened because Saul's son was

a valiant and true soldier of Israel. After a time Saul made David the captain of a thousand men and he was sent to do battle with the Philistines.

It was not long before David's warriors grew to love their captain, his faith and courage inspired them to fight bravely and unselfishly for Israel. Many times they were successful in small encounters with the enemy. 

Then it happened that Saul called all his armies. together for a great battle, and David fought side by side with the king. As they came home from the battle the people came out of the cities along the way to welcome the victorious warriors. They came singing and dancing with great joy and some of them sang: "Saul has slain his thousands!" 

While others of them sang: "David has slain his tens of thousands!" 

When Saul heard the people singing praises to

David greater than to himself he was overcome with a great jealousy. Over and over again the words of Samuel came back to haunt him "Thy kingdom shall be taken away and given to a better man." Soon the mind and heart of Saul were clouded with evil, the evil of hatred.

The next day at the palace at Gibeah, Saul called David to play on his harp and to sing for him. This time the music did not soothe the unhappy ruler, rather did it make his hatred turn into madness. For he picked up a spear and threw it at David. But David saw the spear in time and stepped aside. Again the player returned to his harp and again Saul attempted to kill him and failed. As David left the presence of the angry king unharmed he knew that the Lord had protected him, and Saul knew, now, without a doubt, that God was with David.

It was not very long, however, before the king called the valiant young captain to him. Saul knew that

David loved his daughter, Michal, so he said to him:

"Go forth once more against the Philistines. If it be that thou shalt defeat them, I will give Michal unto thee."

David went to do the king's bidding, for he was eager to serve his people, also he had grown to love Michal but he had never felt himself worthy to become the son-in-law of the King. Later when David returned unarmed and victorious, Saul was disappointed for he had hoped that the young captain might be killed. But the king kept his promise and Michal became the wife of David.

After this the King hated David more than ever. 

Each time he came back from the wars, it seemed that the people grew to love the young captain more and honored him openly. And Saul became more determined that David should die. Jonathan knowing the wickedness in his father's heart warned David to stay away from the palace.

David fled to his home and his wife Michal guarded the house day and night. It was not long, however, before the soldiers of Saul came to spy upon the house. When Michal saw them, she helped David to escape. After David had gone, Michal took a large stone statue and placed in David's bed covering it with a cloth of goat's hair. The next morning when the soldiers came to take her husband, she showed them David's bed saying: "David is sick; he cannot be taken."

But Saul demanded that David be brought before him on his bed when he heard the story of his servants. 

Later when Saul learned how Michal had tricked his servants he sent for her and gave her to another man for his wife.

Meanwhile David had found his way in safety to the prophet, Samuel, at Ramah. Samuel was glad to welcome the young man that was to be Israel's king, so he took him away to Naioth where he kept the school

he had started to teach the youth of Israel in the ways of God. Here David remained for many days learning many things from the aged prophet preparing him for the leadership of Israel.

It was not long, however, before Saul learned of David's hiding place and he went to capture him. But when Saul came outside the simple dwelling, he heard voices raised in prayer and in worship of Jehovah and Saul's strength left him and he was unable to enter. 

Once more Saul remembered the Lord God of Israel and humbling himself he knelt down on the earth and prayed.

When Saul returned to Gibeah it seemed for a time that his anger against David was forgotten. Hearing this David returned and sought his friend Jonathan for advice as to whether he should return to the palace. But 

Jonathan knew that David's life was in danger and warned him to never let Saul find him. This time David went away an outlaw, to find a new home for himself for no longer could he fight for his people in the armies of the King.

SAUL FORCES DAVID TO BECOME A WANDERER-I Samuel 21, 22 

In his wandering David and the few faithful men who had joined him came to the city of Nob where the Ark of the Covenant was kept by the high-priest Ahimelech. The men were faint from hunger and David begged the priest to give him bread. The only bread in the Tabernacle was the shew bread that had been blessed for the service of the Holy Place. Now it was the law of 

Moses that only the priests could partake of the sacred bread but Ahimelech seeing the great need of David gave him the bread.

Then David asked the high-priest if he might find a sword for him because he had no weapons with which to protect himself. And the keeper of the Tabernacle

brought to David the sword of Goliath, the Philistine. 

David took the sword of the enemy he had killed with the pebbles from his sling many years before, and girt it about himself.

It was indeed a strange happening that brought Doeg, a servant of Saul, to the abode of Ahimelech as David and his men were there. Now the outlaw knew he must flee once more for safety. This time he went to the city of Gath in the land of the Philistines. 

But there was no safety for David in the city of Gath for as soon as they saw the sword of their hero, Goliath, they planned to make David a prisoner. It was only by acting as if he had lost his reason and become a witless fool that David made his escape from the city. 

David now returned to the land of Israel alone and took refuge in the cave of Adullam near to the home of his parents. Many days in hiding, David was hungry and desolate. Many times he wondered about the strange things that had happened to him: Why was he forced to live as an outlaw? Why did Saul seek his life? 

Then he remembered the words of Samuel when he anointed his head with oil, and once more David's heart took courage and to his lips came the words: "O God, in Thee do I put my trust! 

Thou wilt show me the way of life!"

When the word came to the men of Bethlehem of David's hiding place they came to his aid. In all there came about four hundred men and they made David their captain. The first thing that David did before he led his new army was to take his mother and father to safety in the land of Moab lest Saul vent his anger upon them.

In the meantime Doeg, the servant of Saul, had reported to his master that Ahimelech had aided David to escape. Straightway, Saul sent his soldiers to the sanctuary at Nob and they killed Ahimelech and all who dwelled there. Only Abiathar the son of Ahimelech

escaped. When David heard of the terrible thing that had come to God's servants because of the help they had given to him, he was greatly distressed. After this David protected Abiathar and kept him as long as he lived. 

Now it happened that the Philistines came and plundered the grainfields of the people of Keilah. The people came to David and asked for aid as Saul had forgotten to protect his people any more. Before he went to the call of the people David asked Jehovah whether he should go to fight the Philistines. And the answer came to David to go and save the men of Keilah. So 

David took his army and attacked the raiders, slaughtering many and driving the others away. 

Then David and his men went to live in the city thinking that the people would protect him because of his service to them. As soon as Saul heard what had happened at Keilah he sent an army to demand the surrender of David. But God warned David through 

Abiathar, the priest, that the men of the city he had helped would deliver him to the King out of their great fear. So once more David and his loyal men were forced to flee for their lives.

This time David found refuge in the mountains of the wilderness that was near to the Dead Sea. And

Saul went from one place to another seeking David. 

Many times through his servant, God delivered David from the soldiers or the spies of the King. The days were hard and the suffering was severe for those who followed the young captain of Israel but as time passed more and more came to join him. Somehow it seemed that David was able to inspire them with his own courage for he never ceased to believe in God's goodness. 

Perhaps it was at this time the words of the beautiful psalm came to his lips to comfort them: "The Lord is my light and my salvaton! 

Whom shall I fear? 

The Lord is the strength of my life!

Of whom shall I be afraid?

Though an host should encamp against me, my heart shall not fear!"

DAVID SPARES THE LIFE OF SAUL-I Samuel 23-27 

While David and his men were hiding in the wilderness they dwelt in a great cave called Engedi. One day they were surprised to see Saul and his soldiers on the mountain side. As they watched they saw the King leave his men and come towards the cave. Not knowing that anyone was in the cave Saul lay down and went to sleep. In the darkness, David came and stood by Saul's side and softly cut off a large piece of his cloak. Then he went aside to await the awakening of the King. 

The followers of David pled with him to let them kill Saul, but David restrained them because he knew that Saul had been chosen of God and anointed the King to rule Israel as the people had requested. When 

Saul awoke and left the cave, David followed him and bowed low before him and said: "My Lord, my King! Behold, this day the Lord delivereth thee unto my hand. I cut off a piece of thy robe but I killed thee not, for thou art the Lord's anointed. Now wherefore doest thou seek me to kill me?"

Saul was so moved at David's reverence that he wept. Then he said to the young captain he had once loved so dearly: "Thou art more righteous than I, my son David. 

God reward thee for what thou hast done this day. Now 

I know that thou shalt surely be king of Israel. Swear to me to deal as mercifully with my family as thou hast dealt with me."

Then Saul took his soldiers and returned to his home at Gibeah. For a time David was free to go where he desired. So he took his followers and went to the land

of Paran to the south of Canaan, where there were many shepherds and their sheep. And David and his men were helpful to the shepherds driving the desert robbers away from their flocks.

It was not long, however, before the word came to David that the spies of the king were seeking him once more. In the stillness of the desert night David sat alone and tried to understand the things that had happened since Samuel called him from his father's flocks in the fields of Bethlehem, and what course must he follow now that Saul was seeking him once more. Then there was the longing for the peace of his boyhood, for his wife Michal, and his beloved friend Jonathan. 

There were his loyal followers also to be thought of and always there was Israel in desperate need of a righteous leader. Always there came to David the assurance that the Lord God of his fathers would direct the way. Now 

David gathered his men and left the wilderness of Paran and wandered from place to place. At last they came to the land of the Ziphites and took refuge in the hills. It was not long, however, before David's men warned him that Saul's army had gathered in the valley nearby. 

More than anything else David wanted Saul to know that he was his friend and wanted to help him to serve Israel.

So when night came he took his servant Abishai and went to seek the tent of Saul. They found the king fast asleep with his spear by his side with his captain Abner asleep nearby. When Abishai saw the sleeping king, he begged his master to let him kill Saul with the spear. But David answered: "Nay, I cannot take the guilt of striking the Lord's anointed. Let Jehovah deal with him, for his day shall come to die. Take thou his spear and let us begone. 

Saul aroused by the familiar voice cried out: "Is that thy voice, my son David?” "It is my voice, my lord," answered David.

"O King why wilt thou pursue me? What ill have 

I done thee? Who has set this strife between us; that I am a wanderer from my heritage in Israel and that the great King of Israel is come out to seek my life as one doth hunt a partridge in the mountains?"

When Saul saw that David had spared his life once more, he acknowledged his sin and begged David to return with him to Gibeah. But David was inspired not to trust the king, because in his heart Saul had no desire to control his own feelings and impulses. So once more 

David became an outlaw, this time in the land that lay near to the home of his own tribe of Judah. And Saul and David met no more. 

"HOW ARE THE MIGHTY FALLEN!"-I Samuel 28-31 

The closing scenes of the life of Saul, King of Israel were fast approaching. The Philistines now held the people more completely in their power than ever before. Now they plundered and robbed at will. They feared Saul no longer so they planned a great battle thinking to defeat Israel completely and make them as slaves. One after another the Philistine warriors and their great chariots passed over the roads towards the beautiful valley of Esdraelon. All of Israel called upon their king to come forth and save them.

Saul called all the soldiers of each tribe to gather near the foot of Mount Gilboa. But when he saw the great strength of his enemy, his courage failed him. 

There was no one to help him, Samuel was dead, the priests of the Tabernacle had been slain at his command, and he knew not where David was. In his great despair he called upon the Lord to know what to do but he received no answer.

Now it was the custom of the pagan Canaanites to call upon witches or enchanters who pretended to be able to speak to the dead to obtain information and

counsel about the things that were to come. The priests of Israel had been commanded to destroy all such evil doers from the land, but many still remained. The desperate king now turned in his terror to one of them, the witch of Endor, for help. Dressed in the clothes of a poor man, Saul went with two of his guards to the cave of the witch by night and demanded that she bring

Samuel to him that he might speak with him. 

A voice came out of the darkness and Saul knew that it was the prophet who spoke to him, and Saul called out saying: "I am sore distressed; for the Philistines make war against me; God has forsaken me and answers me no more. I have called thee to seek thy counsel."

There was no comfort or counsel in the words Samuel spoke to Saul, only the assurance of the tragedy that would come on the morrow. When the voice ceased Saul fell helpless to the ground in his great suffering.

All the next day the great battle waged. The 

Philistines drove the Israelites before them with great slaughter. At eventide the weary king stood on the hillside sorely wounded. Jonathan and two other brothers lay mortally wounded by his side and the enemy bowmen were closing in upon him. Fearing to be taken alive, Saul said to his armor bearer: "Draw thy sword and kill me lest the enemy slay me and make mockery of me."

But the armor bearer was afraid to kill the King of Israel. Then Saul took the sword and fell upon it and died.

After three days the evil tidings reached David and his sorrow was very great because of the great love he bore to Saul and to Jonathan. In the days that followed David remembered the great things that Saul had done, his victories and his great love for his people. 

But when he thought of the weakness of Saul, his pride

and his arrogance, his brooding suspicions, and his love of power, David's heart was very sad because he knew that Saul had forgotten that he was called to lead Israel for the God of Israel and was not self appointed for his own glory.

Then David made the words of his heart into a song of lament: "Thy glory, O Israel, is slain upon thy high places! 

How are the mighty fallen!

Saul and Jonathan were lovely and pleasant in their lives, And in their death they were not divided;

They were swifter than eagles, They were stronger than lions. 

Yet they have fallen!

WHEN DICTATORS DESTROY MAN'S FREEDOM 

Solomon and the Rulers that Enslaved Israel FRE REEDOM is one of God's greatest gifts to mankind. It is because of this great gift that each individual can earn the greatest of all blessings, happiness. 

Freedom of living, which means freedom of thought and action, is part of the divine plan for growth and progress. To be a blessing, however, actions must be directed by a knowledge of good and evil, and by ideals that grow out of that knowledge. 

God, the loving Father, has revealed to His children the knowledge of right living because of His great purpose for their lives: "Man is that he might have joy."

How strange it is that out of the great blessing of freedom men make bondage for themselves, slavery to false ideals of living! On the pages of history we find inscribed the records of men and nations who have risen to greatness because of the right use of individual liberty; while on the other hand we find there countless examples of tragedy and failure because of the bonds of slavery men have forged for themselves out of their freedom.

Government and leadership are necessary to help mankind in all the affairs of life that they might live in harmony with their fellows. But leadership is not dominion nor is government slavery. It is service, service that is directed to help the individual to make the best

use of his knowledge and his ability for the good of all and for his own success and happiness. 

He who assumes the government or leadership of his fellows in whatever phase of activity, family, social, national, or spiritual, must remember that freedom is man's divine heritage and that governments were instituted of God for the benefit of man, that He holds men accountable for their acts, both in making laws and administering them for the good and safety of society. 

Freedom was the heritage of Israel in the land of Canaan. The leadership of the people was invested in the servants of the Lord God they worshiped. But the time came when they desired a new type of leader, a warrior-king, that they might triumph over their enemies. The prophet Samuel knew the evils of such leadership from the record of the kings of Egypt, Babylon and Assyria, the neighbor nations of Canaan, so he warned Israel against choosing an earthly king and rejecting God's plan of leadership. But they did not listen to Samuel.

Saul, the first king of Israel, was a patriotic Hebrew and a brave warrior, but he ruled for himself, not for the welfare of the people. Not able to control his emotions and feelings he was not able to direct his actions intelligently for the good of Israel. At the end of his reign the Philistines help the people of Israel in their power as they did at the beginning. 

David was a great king. In the forty years of his reign he overcame the enemies of Israel, the Philistines, the Moabites, and the Edomites. Israel became a united nation with a capital city, Jerusalem. At all times 

David ruled Israel for God through inspiration and prophetic guidance.

Solomon, the third king of Israel, fulfilled the warning of Samuel, for Solomon followed the pattern of the ancient kings of Egypt and Babylon. The strength of his people was spent in raising magnificent

buildings and monuments, while the wealth of Israel was spent in maintaining luxurious palaces for hundreds of wives and thousands of warriors and servants. 

It was a terrible burden that Israel carried under Solomon, the tyranny of oppression and taxation. 

When Rehoboam, the son of Solomon, ascended the throne, the people begged him to lighten their grievous burdens. They were given an answer of unthinkable cruelty: "My father chastised you with whips, but I will chastise you with scorpions."

Once more the Hebrew people became divided for all but the tribes of Judah and Benjamin revolted and chose a new king, Jeroboam. Never again was Israel to be a great and united nation. 

DAVID: KING OF ISRAEL-II Samuel 1-10 

David, the shepherd-warrior, had had a long preparation before he was called to rule as the King of Israel. 

Foremost in the heart of David came his love of God and his belief in the destiny of Israel. Service in the armies of King Saul had given him a knowledge of warfare. 

While his experiences as an outlaw, hiding from the hatred of Saul in all parts of the land, had given to him an understanding of his people, their problems and their way of life. But it was the teachings of the prophet 

Samuel who had anointed him, which had given to him the true ideals of kingship.

When Saul had fallen and the armies of Israel were defeated, David returned to Hebron. There the men of Judah, his own tribe, crowned him as their king. But 

Abner, the faithful captain of Saul's hosts, declared that it was the fallen king's son Ishbosheth, who by patriarchal rights should be the King of Israel. So a long struggle continued between the house of Saul and the house of David.

At this time, however, there was little of the land of Canaan that belonged to the Hebrews, for the Philistines occupied the fruitful valley of the Jordan. But what was most disheartening now, the land of the children of Israel was divided into two portions, Israel to the north and Judah to the south. 

David gathered together all who would follow him and set about driving the Philistines out of Canaan. 

One victory after another came to the loyal band, for

David was a wise and courageous leader whose trust in 

God's help never faltered.

Meanwhile among the hosts of Ishbosheth there was strife and bickering. Now Abner was weary of fighting, for his captains sought only to gain favors for themselves rather than to serve Israel. So it was that after seven weary years Abner sought to make peace with David and he came to Hebron. David received the valiant soldier of Saul with great kindness, but Joab, David's captain, had a grudge against Abner for the death of Asahel, so he slew him.

Without Abner, Ishbosheth could not rule his armies let alone his people, for he was a weak man.
