Two of the captains of the host of Israel, thinking to gain favor with David, killed their king. Then they took the head of their master to David expecting a rich reward. But David ordered the traitors to be hanged.

Now that Ishbosheth was dead, the men of the tribes of the north came to David eager to end the strife that had divided Israel. And David was anointed king over all Israel.

As yet there was no central city or capital for the Hebrew nation. So David sought to unite the people by making a capital which belonged to neither portion of the land where all the tribes might meet. A few miles from Bethlehem, in the very heart of the land, was the fortress of the Jebusites, an ancient tribe of Canaan.

Taking thirty thousand warriors, David laid siege to

the fortress. But it was Joab who led the men to the top of the stronghold and took the city. 

David went to dwell in Jerusalem and called it the City of David. The city was extended beyond the small fortress and out over Mount Zion and a new wall was built around the city with watch towers overlooking the plains. The people of Israel in their joy sang of the beauty of their first city: "Beautiful for situation, the joy of the whole earth, Is Mount Zion on the sides of the north, The city of the great king!" Then

The fame of David spread throughout the land and all feared him, even the Philistines. Once more, however, they tried to subdue Israel. Gathering all their warriors and chariots of war in the valley of Rephaim, they prepared for a great battle. But David took his armies and drove them out of the land. he went and subdued the Moabites and made them pay tribute; then followed the subduing of the Edomites, the Syrians of Damascus, and the King of Zobah. Now at last the land was safe for Israel; it was a powerful united country extending over sixty thousand square miles.

The fame of David spread far and wide in the land. Hiram, the king of Tyre, sent a gift of cedar wood to build him a royal palace. Skilled carpenters and masons were sent also from among the clever craftsmen of the Phoenicians. The palace of David brought beauty to the city of Jerusalem and pride to the people of Israel.

Then David desired to bring the Ark of the Covenant to Jerusalem. So he said to his people: "Let us bring the ark of our God to Jerusalem; for in the days of Saul we knew it not.' 

A place was prepared for the Ark on the top of Mount Moriah, high above the city. Then the people

went to Kerjath-jearim, where the Ark had rested since the Philistines returned it and they brought it up to Jerusalem amid great singing and rejoicing. And when the procession entered the city they sang the psalm: "Lift up your heads, O ye gates; 

And let ye lift up thy everlasting doors; 

And the King of glory shall come in. 

Who is the King of glory? 

The Lord of hosts, He is the King of glory."

And when the Tabernacle was established and the Ark within it, David brought the Levites to administer in the ceremonies of the holy place. Then David ordered that a choir of singers with musicians take part in the worship of God in the Tabernacle because more than anything else he sought to glorify the Lord God of Israel. David wrote a beautiful psalm of worship for. the singers and bade them to sing it: "Sing unto the Lord, all the earth! 

Glory and honor are in His presence! 

Strength and gladness are in His place! 

Worship the Lord in the beauty of holiness!"

Now that the Ark was among the people once more, the hearts of men turned in worship to Jehovah. 

And David thought to bring them even nearer to the goodness of God that they might be more united. So he sent for Nathan the prophet and said to him: "It is not well that I should dwell in a palace of cedar and the Ark of God in a tent." 

Nathan was pleased with David's thought to build a temple to the worship of the Lord. But it was revealed to Nathan that it was not for David to build the House of the Lord, but that to the son of David would be given the task. For David was a man of war

who had shed much blood, and his son would be a man of peace.

David was surprised and disappointed at the answer to his request. Nevertheless, he gathered together much treasure in gold, silver, brass, and precious stones in preparation for the building of the House of the Lord. 

In the days of his fame David never forgot his love for Jonathan. One day he said to his servants: "Is there yet any left of the house of Saul that I might show him kindness for Jonathan's sake?"

Then David was told of the son of Jonathan, Mephibosheth, a cripple, and the young man was sent for to come to the palace of the king. When he came before David, Mephibosheth trembled at the thought of what might happen to him because he was the grandson of Saul. But David was very tender with the son of Jonathan, his beloved friend, and he said: "Fear not for I will show thee kindness for thy father's sake and will give thee the property of Saul, and thou shalt be a member of my court and eat at my table."

All went well with David in the early years of his reign. He sought constantly to know the will of God and to do it. He built a great empire for Israel and he ruled his people justly. Holding his kingship as service for God, he established the noblest ideals of kingly leadership, and all of Israel loved and honored David. 

THE SIN OF DAVID-II Samuel 11, 12 

But in the last years of the reign of David trouble and sorrow came. For he did that which was evil and his sons were wicked and rebellious. 

When Abigail, the most beloved of the wives of David, died, the king was very lonely. Soon after, however, he saw the beautiful Bathsheba and desired to make her his wife. When he learned that she was the wife of Uriah, a brave soldier of Joab's army, it

made no matter. Yielding to the temptation that had come to him, David sent a message to Joab saying: "Set Uriah in the forefront of the battle and retire ye from him, that he may be smitten and die."

Joab did the evil will of his master and soon the word came unto David saying: "Thy servant Uriah is dead.”

When Bathsheba had mourned for her husband as was the custom, David sent for her and made her his wife.

The thing that David had done to satisfy his own desires was evil in the sight of the Lord. And Nathan the prophet came to the king and spoke to him of his great sin saying: "Thus saith the Lord to you: I anointed thee king over Israel, and I delivered thee out of the hand of Saul. 

Wherefore hast thou disobeyed the commandment of Jehovah? Thou hast killed Uriah and taken his wife to be thy wife. Therefore, the sword shall never depart from thine house! Evil shall spring up against thee out of thine own household.' 

David saw how great was his sin and he cried out in his misery: "I have sinned a great sin."

David's sin was the sin of a man, for the commandments, "Thou shalt not commit adultery; Thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's wife," were for all mankind in all ages.

All his life long until now David had kept clean hands and a pure heart. But now his hands were stained with the blood of Uriah. Repenting of his sin David sought forgiveness in the House of the Lord. There in the anguish of his soul he cried: "Have mercy on me, O Lord, according to thy loving kindness! 

According unto the multitude of thy tender mercies, blot out my transgressions.

Wash me thoroughly from mine iniquity and cleanse me from my sin:

For I acknowledge my transgressions and my sin is ever before me. 

Create in me a clean heart, O God, and renew a right spirit within me! 

Restore unto me the joy of Thy salvation, and uphold me with Thy free Spirit!"

THE REBELLION OF ABSALOM-II Samuel 13-24 

Even after his sincere repentance trouble and disaster continued to crowd into the life of the aging king. 

The evil coming this time from within his household. 

For it came to pass that Absalom killed his brother

Ammon in revenge for a wrong he had done. David's grief was great for he loved both of his sons. As the years passed he yearned more and more for the handsome, impetuous Absalom whom he had been forced to banish for his wrongdoing.

After three years, however, the King sent for Absalom and forgave him his sin. But now instead of gratitude in the heart of Absalom there was evil. Fearing that Solomon, the second child born to Bathsheba, might become the king of Israel, Absalom began to plot against his father.

First, he sought to gain the favor of the people of Jerusalem, by taking part in the life of the city. Then he gave counsel to those seeking judgments in disputes. 

Little by little the king's son became known throughout the land for his friendliness and his sympathy. 

Then Absalom went to Hebron and calling together his followers he set himself up as king over Israel. 

Many came flocking to his standard, among them his father's chief counselor, Ahithophel, who sought vengeance on the king for the shame he had brought upon his house, the kin of Bathsheba. After this war raged

between the followers of David and those of Absalom. 

So that his son would not strike at Jerusalem and destroy it, David took his family and servants and fled from the city. But when the priests of Levi carrying the Ark of the Covenant followed him, David commanded that they should return to Jerusalem: "Carry back the Ark of God into the city. If I shall find favor in the eyes of Jehovah, He will bring me again and show me both it and His house; but if He has no longer any pleasure in me, lo, I am in His hands, let Him do unto me as He deems best."

David went to Mount Olivet to await the outcome of a great battle. And when all was ready he summoned his captains to him saying: "Deal gently for my sake with Absalom."

With the news of the defeat of the rebels came the word of the death of Absalom. Then David went up unto the chamber above the gate of the city that he might be alone with his sorrow. Wrapping his face in his mantle, he cried aloud: "O my son, Absalom! My son, my son, Absalom; would God I had died for thee, O Absalom, my son.' 

When the loyal captains of the armies of Israel heard the lamenting of the king and saw that he had no joy at their victory they were greatly displeased. 

Then Joab came to his master and rebuked him saying: "Thou hast shamed this day thy servants who have saved thy life. In that thou lovest thine enemies and hatest thy friends, thou hast shown that if Absalom had lived and all we had died this day, then it had pleased thee well. "Now therefore arise, go forth and speak comfortably unto the soldiers and the people, for I swear that if thou go not forth there will not be a man left to thee by nightfall; and that will be worse unto thee than all the evil that befell thee from thy youth until now.'

The wise counsel of Joab brought David to realize

that he had forgotten his people. So rousing himself he went and sat in the gate that all might speak to him and comfort him. Now once more the hearts of the people turned to David, and the king returned to Jerusalem and took care of is duties and ruled his people with wisdom and justice as he had done before. Now that peace was restored in the land, Israel was once more a united kingdom.

Throughout the history of the Israelites the name of King David has been honored as a great king, a great leader, a great warrior, and a great singer. In spite of his weaknesses and his shortcomings the magnificence of the faith of David in the living God and the destiny of man stands as a beacon light keeping the belief in God alive in the minds and hearts of mankind even unto this day.

SOLOMON BECOMES KING OF ISRAEL—I Kings 1-8 

Solomon became King of Israel while his father was still alive. It must have cost the aged king a great deal to give up his kingdom to his youngest son. But in this last heroic action, David revealed the depth of his love for Israel; he did it to save his people from rebellion and bloodshed.

Adonijah, a son of David, hoped to be made the ruler of Israel after the death of his brother Absalom. 

But when he saw that the youngest son of his father, Solomon, was being trained by Nathan to succeeed David he plotted against the king. Soon Adonijah had many followers. Even Joab, David's loyal captain, joined Adonijah, as did Abiathar, his faithful friend. 

When he felt that the time was ready Adonijah called all his followers to a great feast outside the city. 

When Nathan brought the news of the rebellion to David, the aged king made all preparations for Solomon to be crowned immediately. Straightway, Nathan

brought Solomon to Gihon and anointed him king. 

Then David sent forth the trumpeters of the royal court to announce the coming of the new king to Jerusalem. 

And the people shouted with one voice: "Long live King Solomon."

The followers of Adonijah fled every one his own way when the word came to them of the crowning of Solomon. Left alone Adonijah sought refuge in the 

Tabernacle were none might touch him, until Solomon should promise him his life. Solomon, following the example of his father, sought to avoid strife at this time so he forgave his brother and sent him on his way. 

As Solomon took his place on the throne of Israel, the aged King bowed low before his son and prayed: "Give, O God, thy righteousness unto Solomon, my son, the King of Israel. Let him judge with righteous judgments and deliver the needy when they cry. 

In his days let the righteous flourish and abundance of peace endure.'

Then turning to the people David spoke to them saying: "Solomon, my son, hath been chosen to sit upon the throne of the kingdom of the Lord over Israel. Also the Lord hath chosen him to build a temple for the sanctuary. Be strong and consecrate your lives to the building of the House of the Lord, that His truth may live in the earth.”

David gave to Solomon the pattern for the building of the temple, also the great wealth and the treasures he had gathered for its building and adornment. 

The people rejoiced greatly when they saw all that David had prepared for the Lord's service, and they offered their labor and their goods willingly that all might be done as David had prepared. 

As the end of his days drew nigh, David spoke to 

Solomon in farewell saying: "I go the way of all the earth. Be thou strong

therefore, and show thyself a man. Keep the commandments of the Lord thy God that thou mayest prosper in all that thou doest. He that ruleth over men must be just, ruling in fear of God."

Although Solomon was young he had learned much wisdom from his father and from Nathan the prophet. In his heart he desired to rule justly and to serve Israel. In the beginning of his reign he was humble and exceedingly righteous and followed the statutes of David.

THE WISDOM OF SOLOMON-I Kings 3, 4 

Moreover, God was pleased with the young king. 

One night after he had been offering sacrifices, the Lord appeared to Solomon in a dream and said to him: "Ask what I shall give thee?” 

Solomon answered: "Jehovah, my God, Thou hast made Thy servant king instead of David my father; and I am but as a little child. Give, therefore, Thy servant an understanding heart, that I may know what is good and what is evil, so that I may govern Thy people. For what man is able to judge so great a people?"

The humility of Solomon pleased the Lord so He said to him: "Because thou hast not asked for long life, riches, or honor for thyself, or for the death of thine enemies, but hast asked understanding to judge thy people, behold I have done according to thy words. Lo, I have given thee a wise and understanding heart. I have also given thee that which thou hast not asked, both riches and honor, so that there shall not be any among the kings like unto thee all thy days. "And if thou wilt walk in My ways to keep My statutes and My commandments, as thy father did walk, I will lengthen thy days."

As time went on the judgments of Solomon became known throughout the land for their justice. The words of counsel he gave upon matters great or small became as proverbs repeated over and over again because of their wisdom. Many of the judgments of 

Solomon were recorded in the chronicles of the time, but the wisdom of his utterances kept them alive for generations and even to this day they are repeated as proverbs. One of the great judgments of Solomon excelled all others in the depth of human understanding it revealed, it too is remembered today.

There came to the King's throne one day two women bearing with them two children, one living, the other dead. Both of the mothers cried out to the king: "The living babe is mine. The dead babe belongs to the other woman.'

So they argued and contradicted one another before the King, that there was no way to discover which woman was the mother of the living babe.

For a while Solomon was at a loss to know how to render judgment. Then the king called to his servants: "Bring me a sword." 

A sword was brought. Then the king said unto a guard: "Both these women lay claim to the living child. 

Therefore cut the babe in two. Give half to the one and half to the other."

Then the woman who was the mother of the living child cried aloud: "O my Lord, slay not the child, rather give it to the other woman.'

But the woman who was not the mother answered sullenly: "Let the child be cut in twain. Neither mine nor thine shall it be."

Now Solomon knew to whom the living child belonged. And he gave the judgment:

"Give the child to the first woman, for she is its mother."

As the word of this judgment of Solomon came to be known in the land, the people feared and respected the young king for they knew that the wisdom of Jehovah was with him.

THE BUILDING OF THE TEMPLE—I Kings 5-9 

Early in his reign Solomon set himself the task of establishing Israel as a great nation after the pattern of Egypt and Assyria. To do this he felt that it was necessary to have a large army. So twelve thousand men were selected to serve the king, many of them trained Philistine soldiers. But this large army was to be different than the foot soldiers of Israel in the past, there were to be horses and chariots also. And for these Solomon made a trade treaty with the King of Egypt. 

How was Israel to pay for the army of Solomon? 

It was in part as the Prophet Samuel had foretold, the people had to pay for it. In times past each tribe had supplied the needs of the king, but now Solomon divided the land into twelve districts and over each was placed an overseer or collector of tribute. And each man had to provide his share for the maintenance of the king, his household, and his armies.

Now Solomon undertook the building of the Temple. To do this he sent to Hiram, the king of Tyre, who had helped build the palace of David at Jerusalem. 

The great cedars were to be brought from the forests of Lebanon. The great, huge blocks of marble from the quarries of Phoenicia, and the gold, silver, ivory, precious stones, and silks came from Arabia, Africa, and India. For all this, Solomon had to supply Hiram with twenty thousand measures of barley, twenty thousand vats of wine or oil each year during the seven years the Temple was being built. The burden of the labor to be used in the building was from among the men of

Israel, one hundred and eighty thousand forced workers. 

When the Temple was finished Solomon called a great feast of dedication. It was a great day for Israel and the wonder of the people at the richness and beauty of the temple was beyond expression. As they saw the great pillars cast in bronze in front of the building, the walls of cedar plated with shining gold, and the beauty of the carved figures of cherubims around the altar they were filled with deep emotion and pride. 

Then the priests of Levi took the sacred Ark, builded at the word of Moses and containing the Tablets of the Commandments, and placed it in the holy sanctuary of the Temple. And Solomon kneeled beside the altar and prayed aloud that all heard his words: "Lord God of Israel, there is no God like thee in heaven above or on earth beneath! Thou keepest covenant and shewest mercy unto Thy servants that walk before Thee with all their hearts. Now, O Lord God of Israel, let Thy word be proved which Thou didst speak unto Thy servant David. But will God in very deed dwell with men on earth? Yet hearken then to the supplication of Thy people Israel when they shall pray toward this place. And when Thou hearest do unto every man as he desires in righteousness for Thou alone knowest the hearts of all the children of men.' 

And Solomon blessed all of the people assembled and exhorted them saying: "Blessed be the Lord, that hath given rest unto

His people Israel according to all that He promised by the word of His servant Moses. Let your hearts therefore be perfect with the Lord your God, to walk in His statutes and to keep His commandments. "The Lord our God be with us, as He was with our fathers: let Him not leave us or forsake us.'

As the prayer ended, the glory of the Lord filled the Temple as a great cloud and the people bowed low with their faces to the ground and cried:

"O give thanks unto the Lord; for He is good: For 

His mercy endureth for ever."

SOLOMON'S UNRIGHTEOUS DOMINION -I Kings 10, 11 

The Temple was not the extent of Solomon's building program by any means. There was a huge structure, the House of the Forest of Lebanon made with rows of cedar pillars exquisitely carved, the Court of the Priests, and a house for the high priest, all connected with the Temple, the Hall of Judgment, Solomon's Palace, a palace for Solomon's wife, the daughter of the King of Egypt, and palaces for the rest of his wives were built also. All these buildings took twenty years to build, so that half of Solomon's reign was spent in building. 

The splendor of the palace that Solomon builded for himself was greater than that of the House of the Lord. In the central hall of the palace stood a great throne made of ivory overlaid with beaten gold. By the throne were two golden lions. Even the vessels used at the King's table were of gold. And when Solomon rode abroad in the land, his armor bearers carried shields of beaten gold.

The fame of Solomon spread throughout all lands, for the splendor of his court and for the wisdom of his judgments. The ships of Solomon went to the lands of the Mediterranean and along the eastern shores of 

Africa. His caravans went beyond the land of the two rivers into India. Israel became rich, even as Babylon and as Egypt. Thinking to increase his wealth and his power Solomon made treaties with the kings of the nations surrounding Israel. And to make his alliance the more binding he took many wives from among the princesses of the foreign courts.

In the days of the glory of Solomon there came to Jerusalem the Queen of Sheba. The wisdom of Solo-

mon had reached her in her kingdom far away in the mountains of Arabia. With her she brought a caravan bearing gifts of gold, jewels, and spices. And the King of Israel received her with much pride in his heart. 

After the Queen of Sheba had seen the beauty of the Temple, the magnificence of the palace, and the splendor of the king's service she was greatly amazed. 

But when she listened to the words of Solomon in answer to her questions concerning the world and mankind she said to the King: "All that I heard of thine acts and thy wisdom are true. How be it I believed not the words until I came, and mine eyes had seen it, and behold the half was not told me; thy wisdom and thy prosperity exceedeth the fame which I heard. Happy are thy people which come before thee and hear thy wisdom.

But all was not well in Israel. The hearts of the people were heavy for the burden of taxation the King laid upon them and the forced labor he required of them made them despair. In their suffering they were all but slaves. As for Solomon his concern was greater for the horses for his royal chariots than for the wellbeing of the people who supplied the food for his table and the tribute to pay for his splendor. 

As time went on it seemed that the splendor and luxury of his court was all that interested Solomon. 

His wives became more and more powerful; to please the Moabite wife he built an altar for the worship of Chemosh; to please the Ammonite wife he built an altar to Molech; and for another he built an altar for the 

Phoenician goddess Astarte.

But the building of the altars to the pagan gods in the shadow of the great Temple was not all, Solomon worshiped them. Twice God warned Solomon of the wickedness of his ways but he did not listen. A third time the Lord spoke unto him and said: "Forasmuch as thou hast not kept my covenants

and my statutes which I have commanded thee, I will surely rend thy kingdom from thee and give it to thy servant. Yet for David's sake I will take it from the hand of thy son. One tribe only shall be left to him and that for the sake of Jerusalem."

The wisdom of Solomon availed him nothing for he continued stubbornly in his wickedness. In his heart he thought only of revenge for the words God had spoken. Now he set more cruel taskmasters over the people of the northern tribes of Israel, creating heavier burdens of taxation and labor. But for Judah, his own tribe, he lightened their burdens. 

In the days of his wisdom Solomon had said: "It is an abomination to a king to commit wickedness, for the throne is established by righteousness." 

The admonition of David had been forgotten also, instead of showing himself a man in strength of purpose, he had sought power, glory, honor for his own. satisfaction. To the people of Israel he had brought suffering, despair, and evil. Instead of ruling his kingdom as a charge for Jehovah for the welfare of the children of men, he ruled it as a dictator for the satisfaction of his own desires.

The light of God was kept burning in Jerusalem, the City of David, but the great kingdom of Israel was beginning to fall into decay.

THE KINGDOM OF ISRAEL DIVIDED -I Kings 12; II Chronicles 10 

For many years the people suffered in silence the heavy burdens placed upon them because of the splendor and pride of Solomon. They feared the power of the king and his great armies and were silent. But at last there arose a leader among them, Jeroboam, the tax gatherer and overseer of the tribes of Ephraim and 

Manasseh. In many ways Jeroboam had shown mercy

to his people and had given them sympathy instead of harshness as he had followed his duties. 

One day the prophet Abijah came to Jeroboam.

The two men talked over the sufferings of Israel and sought earnestly for some plan whereby help could be given. Then the prophet rose up and taking the cloak the overseer was wearing he tore it into twelve pieces. 

Ten of them he gave to Jeroboam saying: "The Lord God has spoken! Take thou these ten pieces. For ten tribes will be rent out of the hands of Solomon and given to thee, unto his son I will give but one tribe."

When the news of the prophecy of Abijah came to Solomon, he sought Jeroboam to kill him. But the overseer being warned left the land and took refuge in Egypt until the death of the king. 

The reign of Solomon ended amid threatening storms of unrest and with shadows of pagan gods throughout the land. When Rehoboam, the son of 

Solomon, became the king, the tribes of Israel gathered at Shechem for his crowning. At that time Jeroboam led a great gathering of the men of Israel and presented a petition to Rehoboam begging him to relieve them of their burdens. "Thy father," they said, "made our yoke heavy. 

Make the yoke of our service lighter we pray thee, and we will render thee loyal service.'

The king ordered them to come back in three days so that he might seek counsel in the matter. 

The men of Solomon's court counseled the new king to grant the petition of the people because they, too, were afraid that Israel could suffer no longer. But the young friends of the king who desired to share in the luxury and glory of the court counseled him to deny the petition. So on the third day when the people came 

Rehoboam spoke as his foolish friends had advised him, answering the request with scorn and insolence, saying:

"My father laid a heavy yoke upon you. I will add to your yoke; my father beat you with whips, I will scourge you with scorpions."

The northern tribes went to their homes and only the tribes of Judah and Benjamin remained to crown Rehoboam as their king. The ten remaining tribes gathered at Shechem and crowned Jeroboam, of the tribe of Ephraim, to be the king of Israel. 

The division of Israel into two kingdoms: Israel and Judah came about less than a century after its founding. Only for a short time did strength and unity come with kingship. Then because of unworthy and selfish leaders came weakness and division. The kings of Israel and Judah were political leaders only. Henceforth, Israel must return to the leadership of the prophets of God if their inheritance in the promises made to 

Abraham was to endure.

Elijah and Elisha Dedicate Themselves to the 

Service of God 

NATIONS like individuals are known for their integrity and their influence. Because the record of history is chiefly an account of kings and governments, armies and battles, it is necessary to look at the life of the people if we would understand the individuality of a nation and how it developed. 

The nations of the ancient world are examples of national individuality that developed in a small section of the world. The love of power, the love of beauty, the love of learning, the love of wealth, and the love of God are to be found in the records of remnants of the civilizations that were created. Behind these great nations of the past were its leaders molding the pattern of life of the people, kings and conquerors, prophets, and teachers. The Pharaoh's and the Cæsars, Xerxes and 

Alexander, David and Solomon, Moses and Isaiah all had their influence.

The integrity of Israel grew out of its religious life. 

However, it was military supremacy over the enemies of Israel that made the kingdom over which David ruled; later its commercial growth made the glory and grandeur for which Solomon was famous. If it had been possible for Israel's kings to have been dedicated to God's purposes all might have been different. It was 

David, however, who came nearest to this goal, under

his rule the kingdom of Israel reached the peak of its greatness spiritually and nationally. The decline and fall of Israel as a nation were brought about by those rulers whose desires were for individual power and glory. As a result, this period of Israel's history is a record of revolution and strife, sorrow and degradation. 

The destiny of Israel, however, lay not in its national greatness but in its influence for good upon all mankind. Israel was saved for this great mission during the period of national decline by the fearless prophets and teachers who dedicated their lives to keeping alive in the hearts of their people the knowledge of the true and living God.

THE DECLINE OF ISRAEL AND JUDAH-I Kings 12, 13 

The kingdoms of Judah and Israel differed in many ways. From the time that the Canaanite strongholds made two natural divisions of the tribes of Israel rivalry had existed. The land of the south was small and hilly, fitted chiefly for the pasturage for flocks and herds. The people therefore lived simply, there were very few cities and very little wealth. On the other hand, the land of the north was three times as large as the southern division. First agriculture developed and then commerce, cities grew up and great wealth came to the people. The caravan routes from the Mediterranean ports, from Syria, and from Egypt went through the northern division. As a result new ideas crowded in upon the people of the north and life became very complex. It is not surprising then, that the kingdom of 

Judah lasted much longer than that of Israel. The kingdom of Israel continued for almost two hundred years, while Judah endured for three hundred and fifty years.

Jeroboam was made the king of the Northern Kingdom because he was a reformer. He had taken

the leadership of the people in their rebellion against the continuation of the tyrannies of Solomon by his son Rehoboam. But Jeroboam had no interest in the religion of Israel.

As the time passed, however, Jeroboam began to realize the place that the worship of Jehovah, the God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, had in the lives of his people. He was afraid that if they went to Jerusalem three times a year to worship at the Temple and mingled with their old friends at the great Feasts of Israel all differences would be forgotten and they might desire to become a united kingdom once more. 

To prevent such a possibility Jeroboam undertook to control the worship of Israel. So he made two golden calves like the image set up by Aaron in Horeb. One he placed at Dan for the people of the north and the other he set up at Bethel near the south of his kingdom. Then he said to the people: "It is too far for you to go to Jerusalem to worship 

God. Behold here are the gods which brought thy fathers out of the land of Egypt. Now you may worhip in your own land."

Then Jeroboam built sanctuaries for the golden idols and set up altars of sacrifice. Priests were chosen for the religious ceremonies and feast days were named for the same time as the Feasts of Israel at the Temple of Jerusalem. 

It happened that one time when Jeroboam was burning incense upon the altar at Bethel after the pattern of the ceremonies of the worship of Baal, an unknown prophet came to him and warned him of the punishment that would come to him and to Israel because of the idolatry he had established. As a sign that the words of the prophet were true, God caused the stones of the altar at Bethel to fall apart.

In his anger Jeroboam called to his servants to seize the prophet, but no man moved to obey him. So

the king stretched out his hand to do so and in that moment his arm became stiffened as if turned to stone. 

Then he cried out to the prophet: "Intreat the Lord, thy God, for me, that my arm may be restored."

As the prophet prayed the power of the King's arm was restored to him again. Then the man of God went on his way refusing to be rewarded or to eat at the King's table. Jeroboam heeded not the strange warning that had come to him, but continued to lead Israel into idolatry. In time the suffering and punishment foretold by the aged prophet came to the house of Jeroboam. 

For his son Abijah took sick and died. And when his son Nadab became king, he and all the household of Jeroboam were assassinated by the man who became the third king of the Northern Kingdom.

It was a great evil that Jeroboam brought to Israel, for many accepted the idolatrous pattern of worship he had established in spite of the commandments of Moses. The sin of idol worship helped to bring about the fall of the Northern Kingdom, and the name of Jeroboam became known as "The Man Who Made 

Israel Sin."

Soon it seemed that no king could rule very long in the land for there were rebellion and treason among the people. One after another the kings were killed or driven away by another who wished to rule. When 

Omri, a captain in the army, took the throne, however, he brought order in the land. He also built Samaria and made it the new capital of the Northern Kingdom. 

But his son Ahab was one of the most wicked kings to reign over Israel.

Ahab was a capable man and a successful leader of the armies of Israel against the enemies who were beginning to harass the kingdom. He determined also to bring back the glory and splendor of Solomon to Israel. To do this, he made a treaty with Ethbaal, the

powerful King of Sidon, and married his daughter, Jezebel.

When Jezebel came to Samaria she brought with her many of the priests of Baal, the god to whom the Phoenicians prayed for wealth and power. It was not very long before Ahab set up a temple for the worship of Baal in Samaria at the demand of his wife. It was in the mind of the Queen to establish the worship of Baal throughout all Israel. Soon there were the huge stone pillars of Baal and the evergreen posts of Ashtoreth near every village and settlement in the land. Then she ordered the prophets of Jehovah to be slain. Had it not been for Obadiah the wickedness of Jezebel would have been complete, but he took a great number of God's servants and kept them hidden in a cave and fed them secretly.

The people were amazed at the prosperity which came to Israel because of Ahab. When they saw the splendid palace of ivory that he built at Samaria many were foolish enough to think that all of it came as gifts of Baal. So the worship of Baal grew rapidly until it threatened to overcome the worship of Jehovah. If 

Jeroboam pointed the way for the decline of Israel, Ahab did much to hasten its downfall, for it is said of him in the record of the Book of Kings: "Ahab did more evil in the sight of the Lord than all the kings of Israel before him."

Meanwhile what had happened in the kingdom of Judah? Rehoboam, the first king of Judah, was the son of King Solomon and an Ammonite princess who was a worshipper of Baal. While Rehoboam remained loyal to the worship of Jehovah, he did nothing to stop the evil influence of his mother's priests at Jerusalem. Even after the death of Rehoboam his mother remained the Queen of Judah.

During the reign of Abijah, the son of Rehoboam, the worship of Baal began to spread in the Southern

Kingdom. But when Asa, the grandson of Rehoboam, became king he began to rule wisely for he loved his people. First, he commanded that every idol in the land be destroyed. When he found out that his grandmother, the Queen, was doing all she could to spread the worship of her god, Baal, in the land, he ordered that she be queen no longer and that her temple and altars be destroyed. Throughout his long reign Asa did a great deal to restore the worship of Jehovah and to destroy the worship of Baal. Also his son Jehoshaphat followed in his footsteps and ruled well but not very wisely, for he became an ally of King Ahab of Israel when Jehoram, his son, married the daughter of Ahab and Jezebel, a woman with the wicked ambitions of her mother.

The beginning of the downfall of Judah, however, came not entirely from the faithlessness of the people; it came about largely as a result of the unwise political policies of the righteous king, Asa. When he allied himself with Syria against Israel long and costly wars brought evil to the land and the kingdom weakened in power and in influence.

ELIJAH, THE FEARLESS PROPHET—I Kings 17, 18 

In the time of Israel's great need a prophet of God was sent to Ahab. He was Elijah and he came from the desert hills of Gilead beyond the Jordan. Coming before the throne of the ivory palace at Samaria, the fearless messenger spoke to Ahab and his court saying: "As Jehovah of Israel liveth, there shall be neither dew nor rain these years in the land save it be at my word."

Then the speaker was gone before the astonished king could answer him. Ahab sent his servants throughout the land to find the man of the wilderness, but none knew him or where he had gone. But Elijah had gone

to a rocky cave by the brook Cherith near Jordan. There he dwelt among the rocks and the ravens brought him food each day.

Month after month passed and no rain came. The great drought brought famine and suffering to both man and beast. The angry king still searched in every corner of the land for the prophet but to no avail. For when the brook of Cherith had dried up and there was no longer food for the ravens to bring to Elijah, God had commanded him to go to the land of the Sidonians saying: "Arise, get thee to Zarephath and dwell there; I have commanded a widow there to sustain thee."

When Elijah came to the little sea-coast village of Sidon he saw a woman dressed in the garb of a widow gathering sticks and he said unto her: "Bring me, I pray thee, a little water to drink and a morsel of bread also that I might eat.'

The astonished woman looked at the stranger and knew that he was an Israelite because of his garments. 

Then she answered him bitterly: "As Jehovah thy God liveth, I have but a handful of meal left and a few drops of oil. I am gathering these sticks to make one last cake for me and my son, that we may eat and then die.” "Fear not," answered the prophet, "make me first a little cake of thy meal, and afterwards make cakes for thee and thy son. For thus saith the Lord God of Israel, 'The barrel of meal shall not waste, neither shall the cruse of oil fail until the day that the Lord sendeth rain upon the earth.'

Straightway, the woman went and did the bidding of Elijah. And each day thereafter the widow, her son, and the prophet of God were fed from the wondrous store of meal and oil which never grew less. But in Samaria Ahab and Jezebel suffered greatly from hunger.

After a time the son of the widow sickened and died. In great distress the woman came to Elijah and cried: "O thou man of God, art thou come to call my sin to remembrance, and to slay my son?" "Give me thy son," he answered tenderly.

Elijah took the child from the arms of the mother and carried it away to his own chamber. The prophet wondered, too, if he had brought misfortune and sorrow to the woman in return for her kindness. Then this fearless man of the wilderness prayed unceasingly for the life of the little child. The Lord heard the prayer of His servant for soon the cold, still body grew warm again and the child revived. 

As the mother clasped the little son in her arms once more, she exclaimed: "Now by this I know that thou art a man of God and that the word of Jehovah in thy mouth is true."

The power and goodness of God had been made manifest to Elijah once more. But Elijah had learned a great lesson also, a lesson that was to give him strength and faith for the tasks ahead of him.

After three years the words of God came again to 

Elijah saying: "Go, shew thyself unto Ahab and I will send rain upon the earth."

By this time the famine was so severe in the land that Ahab and his servant Obadiah went about by the brooks and hillsides to find grass lest all the horses for the chariots of war perish. It happened that Obadiah came upon Elijah when he was returning to Israel. The prophet requested that he be taken to the King. At first Obadiah was afraid, for he knew the wickedness of Ahab. But Elijah was fearless and went into the 

King's presence. When Ahab saw the prophet, for whom he had searched so long, standing before him he cried out in bitter anger:

"Art thou he that troubleth Israel?" "I have not troubled Israel," answered Elijah sternly. "It is thou and thy father's house who have troubled Israel, in that ye have forsaken the commandments of God, and have followed Baal." 

Ahab made no answer to the prophet but shrank in fear. Then once more the voice of Elijah rang forth: "The time has come for men to know the true God, Jehovah or Baal. Send therefore and gather all Israel unto Mount Carmel, and the four hundred and fifty prophets of Baal and the four hundred prophets of 

Ashtaroth which eat at Jezebel's table."

Ahab did as Elijah commanded for he dared not disobey. It was a strange sight, on one side were the prophets of Baal and on the other the solitary figure of Elijah! On the slope of the mountain the people from far and near had gathered to witness the great conflict. 

Over all was a fearful silence. Then the voice of the prophet of God rang out: "How long will ye, the people of Israel, halt between two opinions? If Jehovah be God follow Him, but if Baal, follow him!" 

No answer came from the people. Then Elijah spoke again: "I, only I, remain a prophet of Jehovah; but Baal's prophets number four hundred and fifty. Here be two bullocks. Let them take one and prepare it and lay it on the altar putting no fire under it, and I will do likewise with the other. Then call ye on the name of your gods and I will call on Jehovah. The god that answers by fire, let him be acknowledged God."

The prophets of Baal prepared their offering in the sight of all and began to pray. From morning until noontime they besought their god without avail. As the day wore to its close they cried louder and louder unto Baal and some even slashed their bodies with swords seeking to move their god to action with their

suffering. But no fire fell from the sky upon the sacrifice. On

When eventide came, the hour of sacrifice in the Temple of Jerusalem, Elijah took twelve stones, one for each of the tribes of Israel, and built an altar. the stones he laid the wood and upon the wood the flesh of sacrifice. Then the prophet ordered that water be poured over the sacrifice and the wood three times that the burning might be more difficult.

When all was ready Elijah came near to the altar and knelt and prayed: "Lord God of Abraham, Isaac, and Israel, let it be known this day that Thou art God in Israel and that I am Thy servant and have done all these things at 

Thy word. Hear me, O Lord, that all this people may know that Thou art Jehovah God." 

Then a fire fell from heaven upon the altar, and the flames consumed not only the wood and the sacrifice but the stones of the altar and the water around it burned away and nothing remained. When the people saw what had happened they fell on their faces and cried aloud: "Jehovah, He is God! Jehovah, He is God!" 

Then Elijah called out unto the people: "The earth must be cleansed from the prophets of Baal. Seize them and take them forth and slay them."

Ahab looked upon the wild scene in silence as the people seized the priests of Jezebel, for he knew that they would do as Elijah had commanded. Then the 

King went on his way to the feast of the sacrifice, but Elijah climbed to the top of Mount Carmel and prayed for the rain to come as the Lord had promised. Then to the servant who was with him he said: "Go now and look towards the sea."

So the servant went and climbed to the highest peak of the mountain and looked over the western sea. 

But there was not even a sign of a cloud in the sky.

Seven times Elijah prayed that rain might come and seven times he sent his servant to look for a sign that God would fulfil his promise. And on the seventh time the servant came back and said: "Behold there ariseth from the sea a little cloud no bigger than a man's hand." 

Soon strong winds blew and the heavens grew black with the coming storm. Then the rain began to fall in torrents, once more the brooks were filled to overflowing and the parched land drank in the water.

And Elijah in his great joy ran as with the strength of youth down the mountain side and across the plain to the city of Jezreel.

Now that Baal was overthrown Elijah felt sure that the people of Israel would remain faithful and worship the true God. But it was not to be so. When 

Jezebel heard of the slaughter of the priests of Baal she sent a messenger to Elijah saying: "So let the gods do to me and more also, if I have not thy life by tomorrow at this time."

When the people heard the words of Jezebel they also sought to kill Elijah. The people who had shouted for God only the day before became faithless once more. 

The prophet's spirit failed him now and he fled once more to the wilderness. There all alone he cast himself in the shade of a juniper tree and cried aloud in his great disappointment: "It is enough! Now, O Lord, slay me, for I stand alone in the land!"

ELIJAH AND THE STILL SMALL VOICE-I Kings 19 

What had happened to change the fearless prophet who had challenged the King of Israel and all the priests of Baal on Mount Carmel? Why had he fled to the wilderness? God had cared for him, and nurtured him for three years. But what was more God had sustained

Elijah in his challenge to the priests of Baal, also He had sent the rain that was promised. Was he ashamed that a woman had defeated him and driven him out of the country?

The prophet Elijah had learned a very great lesson. 

He had misused the authority he had been given. He had been sent to teach Israel the ways of God and he had sought to frighten the people into obedience. He had used the power of God shamelessly.

Now he wanted to be alone, to find God again and to learn the way to do His service. After a time the prophet fell into a deep slumber. When he was awakened by the touch of an angel he found by his side a fire with bread baking on the hot stones and a cruse of water nearby. And Elijah ate and drank and was refreshed. Now as he thought of God's loving kindness to him he desired more than anything else to go to find God. Remembering the experiences of Moses with God on Mount Sinai, he decided to go thence to seek Him. 

Once more Elijah fell asleep and rested. Then a second time an angel came and prepared food for Elijah. 

This time the heavenly messenger spoke to him saying: "Arise and eat lest the journey is too great for thee."

Now that Elijah had eaten the food the angel had prepared he was strengthened greatly and he began his journey. After traveling for forty days and nights he came to the place where Moses had talked with God. 

Finding a cave in the mountain side, he waited until God should speak to him. Soon the voice of God came to him saying: "What doest thou here Elijah?" 

Then Elijah answered Jehovah saying: "I have been very jealous for Jehovah the Lord of Hosts. The men of Israel have forsaken Thy covenant, thrown down Thy altars and slain Thy prophets with the

sword. I, even I, only am left, and they seek my life to take it away." 

Again the voice of the Lord came to Elijah saying: "Go forth and stand in the Mount before Jehovah." 

When Elijah came to the top of the mountain a mighty wind shook it and brake in pieces the rocks; but the voice of Jehovah was not heard in the wind. Then after the wind had passed there came an earthquake and all the earth shook and trembled, but the voice of Jehovah was not heard in the earthquake. Then lightning flashed, but the Lord spake not from the flames of fire. Then there came a great silence over all the place and a still small voice spoke to Elijah. And when he heard the voice Elijah wrapped his face in his mantle. 

Once more the words came to him: "What doest thou here, Elijah?" 

Once more Elijah tried to defend himself instead of confessing his mistakes. And the still small voice spoke again to Elijah saying: "Why has thou said, 'I stand alone?' There are yet seven thousand left to Me in Israel that have not bowed the knee to Baal. There are these to stand with thee. Go back to thy duty, and anoint Elisha, the son of Shaphat, to be prophet after thee."

The need of Israel was not as the power of thunder and lightning; it was the still small voice of the Spirit of God. Because Elijah knew nothing of this method of pleading with Israel God must raise up another to do His bidding. So Elisha was chosen.

Now Elijah returned to Israel to find Elisha. He found him plowing with oxen in his father's field. When he came to the young man, the aged prophet placed his mantle upon him as a token. And Elisha understood that he was called of God to be the successor of Elijah. 

Straightway he went and bade his father and mother

farewell and followed Elijah to become his servant and his pupil.

AHAB AND NABOTH'S VINEYARD-I Kings 21 

When Elisha was called to the work of Jehovah, he bade farewell to his parents and went with Elijah to be his disciple and his servant.

As the two men traveled from one place to another Elisha came to know the problems of his people. He began to understand why some of them had forgotten the worship of the true and living God, for he saw that every settlement however small had its altar for the worship of Baal. But it was when they came to Samaria, the capital of Israel, and saw the great temple built by Queen Jezebel for the worship of Baal that Elisha began to understand the great mission for which he had been called. 

Day after day Elijah and Elisha mingled with the people of Samaria. On every hand they saw the power of King Ahab, for Ahab was a skilled warrior and an able statesman. Also the King was ambitious for the 

Kingdom of Israel. To further his power he kept a large army whose skill brought fear to the rulers of the small neighboring peoples. Because of this military power he had in league with the neighboring nations gone to war against the powerful King of Assyria and defeated him. From the King of Syria he had gained both land and great wealth in plunder in many battles. 

But it was the alliances of Ahab with the kings of Phonecia and Syria that had brought the greatest wealth to the city of Samaria.

It was not only the army of Ahab and the temple of Jezebel that brought dismay to the two servants of God, the magnificence of the king's palace with its splendor and luxury and wickedness amazed them. The streets of the city crowded with caravans bringing the

riches of many lands to its shops and bazaars and the gay crowds of eager buyers brought them great consternation. Now they knew the change that had come to the way of life of the people of Israel in the Northern Kingdom. When they had left the city of Samaria 

Elijah said to his pupil: "The house of Ahab must fall if knowledge of 

God is to live." 

Elisha answered his teacher. "It is understood! 

The house of Ahab must fall!"

Now it happened that in the days of his great power, Ahab built a palace at Jezreel. Nearby the palace was a beautiful vineyard owned by Naboth, a poor Ahab wanted the vineyard for the garden of the palace so he offered to buy it. But Naboth refused to sell the land because it had belonged to his fathers and he loved it. Then Ahab sulked like a spoiled child and would not eat because he could not get what he wanted. And Jezebel seeing the disappointment of 

Ahab mocked him saying: "Dost thou govern the Kingdom of Israel? Rise and eat and be merry. I will give thee the vineyard of Naboth." 

Straightway Jezebel sent letters in the King's name to the leaders of the city accusing Naboth of treason. 

And when Naboth came before them, false witnesses paid by the Queen accused him saying: "This man hath blasphemed God and the King."

The innocent man was taken and stoned to death according to the law. When the news was brought to 

Jezebel that her evil had been done, she said to Ahab: "Arise, take possession of the vineyard of Naboth; for Naboth is dead."

Just as Ahab went to take possession of the vineyard, he had coveted, Elijah appeared before him. "Hast thou found me, O mine enemy?" cried

Ahab.

"I have found thee," answered the stern old prophet, "because thou hast sold thyself to work wickedness in the sight of Jehovah. Behold, I will bring evil upon thee and thy house because thou hast made Israel to sin."

When Ahab heard Elijah foretell the ways of suffering that were to come to his house, he fasted and prayed and humbled himself before the Lord.

KING AGAINST PROPHET-1 Kings 22 

Jehoshaphat, the King of Judah, came to the court of Ahab seeking to establish peace between the two kingdoms. Since the division of Israel upon the death of 

Solomon hatred and jealousy had existed between them. 

At this time Judah was prospering and the worship of Jehovah was being established once more. For Asa the father of Jehoshaphat had done a great deal to undo the evil influence of the pagan wives of Solomon and the mother of Rehoboam. Jehoshaphat, also, was reigning wisely and righteously. Thinking to use the power of the King of Judah, Ahab gave to him his daughter, Athaliah, to be the wife of his son. 

Now Ahab felt that with the aid of his new ally, Jehoshaphat, he would be able to regain the strong fortress of Ramoth-Gilead, east of the Jordan, which was held by the King of Syria. When Ahab asked Jehoshaphat for his help the King of Judah answered: "My people are as thy people, surely I will help thee. Only let us first seek the counsel of Jehovah."

Then Ahab called all of the professional prophets together for counsel. Fearing the king they answered him with the words that he desired: "Go up against the stronghold of Ramoth in battle and the Lord shall deliver it unto the hand of the King."

But Jehoshaphat was not satisfied with this answer because he knew that many of the prophets of Israel were not of the house of Levi, for he remembered

that Jeroboam had chosen others to serve at Bethel and at Dan. So he said to Ahab: "Is there not here a true prophet of Jehovah that we might ask of him?" "There is one man," answered Ahab, "he is Micaiah, but I hate him for he has never aught to prophesy of me but evil."

Not daring to offend his ally, Ahab sent his servant for Micaiah. When the servant of the King delivered his message he warned the prophet to speak only good to the King like the other prophets had done. But when Micaiah was commanded to speak he answered: "I saw all Israel scattered upon the mountains as sheep that have no shepherd; and the Lord said, 'these have no master, let every man go back to his own. house.'

Ahab knew the meaning of the words of God's servant and in his anger he ordered his guards to return Micaiah to prison, and to give him nothing to eat but bread and water.

Thinking to protect himself, Ahab went to the battle against the King of Syria disguised as a common soldier; but the righteous King of Judah rode at the head of his armies dressed in his royal robes. In spite of all his care Ahab was mortally wounded by an arrow.

Propped up in his chariot the King watched the battle until he died at the time of sunset. At the word of the 

King's death the battle ended and each man returned to his own city.

The body of Ahab was brought to Samaria and buried with great honor. But as the soldiers washed the chariot in which he died in the pool of Samaria the words of Elijah were fulfilled: "In the place where the dogs licked the blood of 

Naboth shall dogs like thy blood."

After the death of Ahab, Jezebel continued her evil work against the worship of Jehovah. And her

1 daughter Athaliah began to follow her mother's example in the land of Judah. 

The need now to wipe out the house of Ahab was greater than ever.

THE PASSING OF ELIJAH-II Kings 2 

The time was at hand for Elisha to assume the work of his master and teacher, for Elijah was very old. The two men had come to Jericho when Elijah said to his disciple: "Tarry, I pray thee, for Jehovah hath sent me to Jordan." 

But Elisha would not leave his beloved teacher and he continued with him until they had crossed the river. Then Elijah said unto him: "What shall I do for thee? taken away from thee." 

Ask. before I be "I pray thee," answered Elisha, "let a double portion of thy spirit be within me." 

Elijah answered: "If thou seest me when I am taken away from thee, it shall be as thou desireth and the spirit within thee shall be as mine has been but with twice as much power. But if thou seest me not thou art not ready for the work of the Lord." 

Then as they continued on their way, Elisha saw a chariot of fire drawn by horses of fire descend from the heavens. And the chariot came near to them and passed between them and when it had gone Elijah was there with him no more. But when he took his eyes from the heavens whence the chariot had returned, Elisha saw on the ground at his feet the mantle of the prophet. 

THE EARLY WORK OF ELISHA-II Kings 2, 3 

Now Elisha went on his way to continue the work of Elijah strengthened by the testimony which had been given to him in the passing of his master. And as he went throughout Israel Elisha remembered the counsel

of the prophet, at all times he sought to teach men to serve God and keep His commandments. So Elisha went among the common people of the land in the villages and out-of-way places and his kindly deeds and his words of counsel brought comfort to many. 

One day in his journeyings Elisha came to the home of the widow of one of the loyal followers of Jehovah. 

He found the woman in great distress because a rich man to whom her husband owed money was about to take her two sons as bondsmen. When Elisha heard the sad story he said to the woman: "Tell me what thou hast in the house." "I have naught save a pot of oil," the woman answered. 

Then the prophet commanded the woman saying: "Go among thy neighbors and borrow many empty vessels and jars. Then shut the door upon thee and thy two sons. Pour the oil from your vessel into the empty ones." 39 The woman obeyed the strange command and gathered as many empty vessels as she could and took them into her house. Then she poured the oil from her jar and it continued to flow until all the borrowed vessels were filled.

Calling to Elisha she told him of the miracle that had happened and asked what she must do with so much oil. "Go," he answered, "and sell the oil and then pay thy debt to the rich man. With the rest, care for thyself and thy sons.'

In another village through which the prophet often passed there dwelt a wealthy woman respected by all who knew her. And the woman often asked the prophet to eat at her table after he had mingled with his own people, to hear more of his teachings. Then one day after the prophet had gone on his way she said to her husband:

"Let us build a small chamber for this holy man so that he might have a place to rest whenever he comes to our city." 

The next time that Elisha came to Shunem he found the room waiting for him. He was very grateful for this kindness and he sent his servant Gehazi to call the woman to see what might be done to show his gratitude. But there was no favor that the woman wished in return for her kindness.

When the woman had left the room Gehazi spoke to his master and said: "She has no child to bring her comfort." "Call her," Elisha said again.

Once more the woman came to the tiny chamber. 

Then Elisha told her that because of her kindness Jehovah would give her a son. "Nay, my Lord, a man of God, do not lie unto thine handmaid!" the woman answered in amazement.

The next time that Elisha came to the home of the Shunammite woman he was able to share the joy of the parents in the son that had been given to them. 

When the boy grew old enough he often went with his father and played in the fields. It happened that one day the sun was very hot and the child became very ill as he sat watching the reapers in the corn. The father sent a servant to carry the little son to his mother. 

But it was not long until the boy died. Without saying a word the mother carried the child to the prophet's chamber and laid him on the bed. Then taking a sarvant she set out to find Elisha who had gone to his home on Mount Carmel. 

Elisha saw the woman coming up the slope of the mountain, and he said to Gehazi: "Behold, yonder is the woman of Shunem! Run and meet her and say, 'Is it well with thee and with thy husband and with thy son?' "'

But the woman came on past the servant to where

Elisha stood. Then she poured out her sorrow in words of bitterness saying: "Did I ask a son of my Lord? Did I not say, 'Do not deceive me?" 

And Elisha understood the woman's sorrow and gave his staff to Gehazi and instructed him to return with the mother to her home.

But the Shunammite woman would not leave the prophet because she had little faith in Gehazi. When he came to the little chamber of the house in Shunem, he laid the prophet's staff upon the child as he had been instructed. But the child did not arise. 

After a time Elisha went with the woman to her home, for he knew that Gehazi had failed. Alone in the little room, seven times the prophet prayed to the Lord God to spare the life of the child. Then the child's body grew warm and he opened his eyes. Calling the woman to him, Elisha placed her son alive and well in her arms.

The word of the great power of Elisha was carried throughout the kingdoms of Israel and Judah, and many returned to the worship of the true God and many others who had not entirely forgotten the teachings of their fathers became strong and brave and kept the commandments of God more faithfully. It was the spirit of God renewed by the teachings of Elisha that kept the faith of Israel alive in the sad days that came to them later. The story of Naaman, the leper, as it is recorded in the Book of Kings, is an illustration of how God's spirit worked upon the hearts of mankind at this time.

NAAMAN, THE LEPER, IS HEALED—II Kings 5 

Naaman, a great captain of the armies of the King of Syria, was a leper. In all the land there was none to cure this dread disease and the brave soldier knew that

before long he would be compelled to leave his home and the wife he loved so dearly and go to dwell with those who suffered as he did.

The wife of Naaman had a little waiting maid who had been carried away from her home near Samaria by the soldiers of the King of Syria when they invaded the land. Day after day as she waited upon her mistress she was troubled to see the great sorrow that had come to the woman who was so very kind to her. 

Often she wished to herself that these people who worshiped strange gods might know Elisha and the wonderful things that he did through the power of 

Jehovah. Then one day she said to her mistress: "Would that my Lord might go to the prophet of

God in Samaria. He would heal him of his leprosy." 

The sincerity of the captive maid brought a gleam of hope to the great captain's wife. When Naaman heard of the works of faith of Elisha, the prophet of Israel, as his wife repeated the words of her waiting maid, he, too, felt a new hope. Immediately he sought the King, his master, for counsel. At once a letter was prepared and the King of Syria sent messengers with it to the King of Israel for his aid in behalf of his brave captain, Naaman. Then the captain set out himself with a large body of servants carrying gifts of gold and silver and costly raiment.

When the King of Israel read the letter of the King of Syria, he was puzzled at the words: "Behold, I have sent Naaman, my servant, to thee that thou mayest recover him of his leprosy."

Then the word went out among the people of Samaria that the King feared that the King of Syria was seeking an excuse to make war upon Israel once more. 

The word of the King's distress came to Elisha and he sent a messenger to him saying: "Let this Syrian captain come to me and he shall know that there is a prophet in Israel.'

When the King gave the message of Elisha to 

Naaman, the Syrian captain remembered the words of the maiden to his wife, so he went to seek the prophet of God. But Elisha did not go to meet the great captain as might have been expected. Instead he sent his servant to Naaman with a message saying: "Go and wash in Jordan seven times, and thy flesh shall be healed and thou shalt be clean."

Now Naaman was greatly angered at the treatment of Elisha and he exclaimed: "Are not Arbana and Pharpar, rivers of Damascus, better than all the streams of Israel? May I not wash in them and be cleansed?" 

Turning to his servants Naaman ordered them to drive away from the abode of Elisha and return to Syria. But the servants desiring to see their master cured of his leprosy if perchance the prophet of Israel had the power to heal, reasoned with him saying: "If the prophet had bid thee do a great thing wouldst thou not done it? How much rather then when he saith to thee, 'Wash and be clean!'"' 

The proud Syrian captain bowed his head ashamed of his anger, and his servants knew that he had taken their counsel. When they came to the Jordan, Naaman did as was bidden by Elisha and dipped himself into the water seven times. When he arose from the water the seventh time Naaman saw that his flesh was clean like that of a little child. With gratitude in his heart he turned back with his servants to Samaria to Elisha. "Behold, now I know," he said, "that in all the earth there is no God, save in Israel. Now, therefore, I pray thee, take an offering from thy servant." 

Elisha refused the gold, silver, and raiment Naaman had brought as a gift because his service was to 

God and through His power. Then the captain requested that he might be allowed to take some sacred

soil of Israel with which to make an altar in Damascus to Jehovah. "From henceforth," said Naaman, "I will offer no sacrifice, to any other god, but to Jehovah alone." 

Then he remembered that when the King of Syria went to worship the god Rimmon it was his duty to go also so he said to the prophet: "When the King goeth into the house of Rimmon he will lean on my arm and I needs must bow myself as he boweth to his god. In this thing may Jehovah pardon me?" "Go in peace," answered Elisha.

Gehazi, the servant of Elisha, had seen the rich presents that Naaman had offered to his master and wanted some of them for himself. So he followed the chariots of the Syrian as they turned homeward. When 

Naaman saw the runner following him, he turned back to meet him thinking something was wrong. "Is all well?" he asked, "All is well!" answered Gehazi. "My master has sent me to ask thee for a talent of silver and two changes of garment for two young pupils who have just arrived."

Naaman was so glad to be of service to Elisha that he gave Gehazi two talents of silver as well as the garments. When he returned to the house Gehazi hid the gifts that he had received from the Syrian captain. But when the servant of the prophet came in to his master, Elisha asked: "Whence cometh thou, Gehazi?" "Thy servant went no whither," Gehazi answered. "Did not my heart go with thee, and did I not see thee?" said Elisha. "Is this a time to take gifts of money and garments? The leprosy of Naaman shall cleave unto thee and thy race forever because of thy wickedness.'

When Gehazi went out from his master's presence he was a leper as white as snow.

THE FAITH OF ELISHA REWARDED—II Kings 6 

After a time the King of Syria made war upon Israel once more. The city of Samaria was surrounded and no food could be brought to the people. So great was their suffering that they ate all the animals and anything they could find. Even in spite of this Elisha counseled the people not to surrender their city to the enemy, promising them deliverance if they would but trust in the Lord. Jehoram, the King of Israel, thinking that this great suffering was the work of Elisha threatened to kill the prophet.

Elisha was in the house of some of the elders of Israel when the King found him. cried. "All this evil comes from Jehovah," the King "Hear the word of Jehovah," the prophet answered. "Tomorrow a measure of fine flour will be sold for a shekel, and two measures of barley for a shekel in the gate of Samaria.”

The servants of the king laughed in the face of 

Elisha and said: "If Jehovah could open the windows of heaven and pour down grain, this thing might be." "Behold," answered Elisha, "Thou shalt see it with thine own eyes, but thou shalt not eat thereof.” 

That evening four lepers from outside the gates of the city went to the camp of the Syrians to beg for food. But when they came to the camp there was not a man to be seen. The Syrians had fled, leaving everything behind, horses and chariots, tents and food. At the setting of the sun a great noise of thunder had been heard and the Syrians thinking this to be a sign of the anger of their god, Rimmon, over the conversion of

Naaman to the worship of Jahovah, had fled in great fear.

The beggars ate of the food they found in the first tent they came to. Then they went from tent to tent gathering plunder. After they had hidden much treasure they decided to carry the news to the people of Samaria.

At first the people thought that the Syrians had laid a trap for them. But when the word was brought that the Syrians had indeed left the country the people rushed out of the gates of the city to eat of the food that was in the camp. The next day when the supplies of the Syrians had been carried to the city a measure of flour and two measures of barley were sold each for a shekel as Elisha had foretold. And the soldiers who jeered at the prophet did not eat of the food for they were trampled to death as the starving people rushed through the gates of the city to eat the food of the Syrians.

In this way, the power of God through his servant Elisha was brought to Jehoram, King of Israel. But he did not cease his evil ways nor did he do anything to curb the evil work of his mother Jezebel. Now the people of the Northern Kingdom had become discontented and the whole land was filled with unrest. 

THE ALTARS OF BAAL DESTROYED-II Kings 8-11 

Elisha knew that the time had come to fulfil the commands of Elijah and calling one of his disciples to him, Elisha said: "Take this cruse of oil and go to the army at Ramoth-Gilead. Seek out Jehu, the son of Nimshi, and anoint him king over Israel."

When the servant of Elisha came to Ramoth he found the captains of the host together and Jehu was

among them. Pouring the oil on the captain's head he said: "Thus saith the Lord God of Israel, I have anointed thee King over Israel. Thou shalt smite the house of Ahab to avenge the blood of my servants at the hand of Jezebel. The whole house of Ahab shall perish."

Jehu gathered together an army and marched towards Samaria. And when King Jehoram heard that the army was nearing he went to meet it. The two armies met at Jezreel in the field that had been Naboth's. "Is it peace, Jehu?" cried the King. "What peace can there be while thy mother Jezebel fills the land with vileness and sorcery?" Jehu answered. 

Then Jehu drew his bow and Jehoram was struck and sank down dead in his chariot. "Cast his body in Naboth's field as God decreed, that the blood of Naboth may be requited," cried Jehu.

In the palace at Jezreel Queen Jezebel awaited the news of the battle. When she heard the news of the death of her son, she knew the end was near. But she was not afraid. She powdered her face, dressed her hair, put on her finest raiment, and seated herself in the window of the palace to await the coming of Jehu. And as he came to the gate at the head of his soldiers Jezebel mocked him saying: "Greetings, O murderer of thy master."

Thereupon Jehu commanded two serving men who stood by to throw the Queen to him. So they threw her down and she was trodden underfoot of the horses. Then he went into the palace of the King of 

Israel. But after a while he ordered his servants to bury what remained of the wicked woman for she was a king's daughter. And when the servants returned they said to their master:

"It is come to pass as Elijah, the man of God, foretold."

Now when Jehu ruled as king he sent men throughout the land to destroy all the altars of Baal, the priests of Baal, and all the worshipers. In this way by force and bloodshed was the worship of Baal destroyed in the kingdom of Israel.

But in the kingdom of Judah, the worship of Baal was growing rapidly through the wickedness of Jezebel's daughter, Athaliah. When her son Ahaziah died she ordered all his sons to be killed and made herself 

Queen of Judah. 

Now it happened that Jeosheba, the daughter of Athaliah, had saved a babe, the son of Ahaziah from the cruel death ordered by the Queen. For six years. she kept the child hidden in the chambers of the Temple with the aid of her husband, the high priest. 

When the boy, Jehoash, had reached the age of seven a great gathering of the priests of Levi was arranged at the Temple. Then the high priest brought forth the boy and anointed him as king and those who were gathered shouted in great joy: "God save the king!"

Athaliah hearing the great cheering hurried to the courtyard of the Temple. And when she saw the young boy standing before the great throng with a crown upon his head, she knew that the end to her power had come as it had come to Jezebel. At the command of Jehoiada, the high priest, the captains of the guard led the Queen outside the gates of the city and put her to death. 

The priests of Levi directed the destruction of the worship of Baal in all the Kingdom of Judah. A covenant was made with the new king and the people that they should be faithful to the worship of Jehovah. As long as Jehoash had the counsel and guidance of Jehoiada he ruled his kingdom wisely and with righteousness, and the people, too, were faithful to the covenant

they had made. The evil worship of Baal was ended in all the land of the people of Israel. 

The work of Elijah had been completed by Elisha. 

Elijah and Elisha had done more for the people of Israel than all the kings and armies of the two kingdoms. 

Now Elisha who was extremely old came to the end of his earthly labors.

The Prophets of Social Justice 

GOD,

OD, the Creator of the world and man, was ever mindful of what might happen to His children when He placed them upon the earth and had given them their freedom to choose between good and evil.

In the school of the wilderness at the foot of Mount Sinai He had given to the Children of Israel through His servant Moses the great law of life, The Ten Commandments.

For his progress and happiness man has to learn the laws of God for through them and them alone can he live righteously. It matters very little whether a man lives in a desert tent or in a crowded city, he lives with his own appetites, passion, and desires, he builds ideals as he gains ideas, and he lives only as he wills to live, choosing between good and evil.

There had been many great scenes in Israel's story; they had lived in the shadow of the great temples and pyramids of Egypt; they had tended their flocks and their fields among the pagan peoples of the land of Canaan; they had built a great nation with Jerusalem, the City of David for its capital; they had erected a temple at Jerusalem for the worship of Jehovah their God; and they had taken their place among the great nations of the ancient world. They had passed from the simple life of shepherds of the plains to that of the busy life of cities crowded with shops and a motley

band of traders from many lands. Much like the world of today they mingled with people of many different lands with different spiritual, national, and social ideals. 

But now scenes of tragedy darkened the glory of the past, the land of Israel was divided, the people, too, were not united; strife and rebellion existed among them; wars and bloodshed had weakened them, and unfaithfulness to God was bringing about its own sad punishment.

Israel was very human, out of the fabric of life that surrounded them from time to time, they gathered ideals and made new patterns of living. Good and evil came to them and they took according to their desires. 

How strange it was that the people to whom God had given the pattern for righteous living should be so wayward and so unfaithful! 

Because Israel was not faithful tragedy came to her. But because her people were still His children, God, the loving Father, sent prophets to teach them and to warn them, that they might not lose their eternal inheritance.

THE MESSAGE OF AMOS, THE HERDSMAN OF TEKOA-Book of Amos 

When Jeroboam the Second, the grandson of Jehu, became the King of Israel, great prosperity came to the Northern Kingdom. For this king was a great warrior and he was able to defeat the kings of Syria, Ammon, and Moab and take back all the land that belonged to the kingdom of David. These conquests brought great wealth in tribute money and in trade to Israel. 

But all was not well in Israel for the great wealth was in the hands of the merchants and the nobles. They lived in summer-houses and winter-houses decorated in the finest way and furnished with upholsteries of rare silks. Feasting, drinking, and pleasure took most
