of their days. The rest of the people, however, were crushed almost to slavery by those who were rich and powerful. The laborer was cheated of his rights for the fruits of his labor; for the poor man there was no justice because of dishonesty and corruption. 

Yet to all appearances all was well religiously in Israel. The wealthy brought rich gifts to the servants of Jehovah, paid large sums in tithes, and offered sacrifices freely and often. 

At this time there dwelt in Tekoa near Jerusalem a poor herdsman named Amos. All he knew was the life of the shepherds, ploughmen, reapers, and treaders of grapes he met as he followed his sheep along the hillsides and streams in the land of Judah. But from time to time there came to him the word of the evils of the rich and the wrongs of the poor in the Kingdom of Israel. As he grieved over the sufferings of his brethren he cried out in his heart: "They press down the poor in Israel as a cart is pressed that is full of sheaves. They place violence and robbery in their palaces. They turn aside the poor from his rights!"

About this time the prophet Joel went throughout the land of Judah warning the people not to imitate the ways of Israel, but to treat all men with mercy and justice. But when he went into Israel the people mocked him and said: "The Lord will make us glorious for He is our God only!"

When Amos heard the way that the message of Joel was received he brooded over the wickedness of Israel. Now God revealed to him in many visions the things that were to come first to Israel and then to Judah. 

And then God spoke to Amos saying: "Go! Prophesy unto my people Israel."

Leaving his sheep, Amos went to do as the Lord

had commanded him, to warn the people of the punishments that would come to them if they continued to keep their evil ways.

Amos went first to Bethel and he saw the worship of the golden calf, priests offering strange sacrifices, and the feasting and merriment of the rich. And he was filled with amazement for never before had he seen such things. He saw also the poor and the hungry and the oppressed with no one to comfort or to aid them. 

Straightway Amos went to the public place near the shrine of the golden calf and began to speak. Many paused to look at the strange figure dressed in rough sheep skin and then his words caused them to remain and listen.

At first Amos spoke of the evil ways of the peoples of Syria, Tyre, Phoenicia, Edom, and Moab, the neighboring peoples of Israel. Then he went on to speak of the great blessings of God to His people, Israel. Then in words of fire and denunciation he cried aloud: "Woe unto you who are at ease in Zion, and trust in the mountain of Samaria; ye that put far away the evil day; that lie upon couches of ivory; that sing to the sound of the viol; that drink wine in bowls and anoint themselves with rich perfumes; but are not grieved for the affliction of the remnant of Joseph, that sell the righteous for silver and the needy for a pair of shoes. For thus saith the Lord, 'I will raise up against you a nation, O House of Israel!' They shall take you captive and your palaces shall be spoiled. The altars of Bethel shall fall!" 

The rich who heard these words stood aghast at the impudence of a herdsman of the plains who dared to speak thus, but to the poor and forlorn who listened there came a ray of hope for the one who spoke seemed to them as a brother. Amos continued to speak this time, he was not threatening but pleading: "Seek ye the Lord and ye shall live! Seek not the

calf at Bethel! Seek Him that maketh the stars, The 

Lord is His name! Seek good, and not evil, that ye might live!" "The Lord God saith to you, 'I hate, I despise your feasts, and I take no delight in your solemn assemblies. Yea, though ye offer Me your burnt offerings and meal-offerings, I will not accept them; neither will 

I regard the peace offerings of your fat beasts. Take away from Me the noise of thy songs, for I will not hear the melody of thy viols. But let justice roll down as waters, and righteousness as an overflowing stream.' 

Now angry cries arose in the crowd and some even tried to seize Amos. Then Amaziah, the high priest of Bethel, came hurrying to the scene. Standing there before Amos in all the glory of his priestly raiment he heard the last fearful words: "Israel shall be led away captive out of this land.' 

Then Amaziah cried out: "O thou seer, go! Flee thee away into the land of Judah. Eat thy bread and prophesy there, but prophesy not again any more at Bethel, for it is the King's chapel and it is the King's court.

Looking at the high priest in all his adornment 

Amos answered: "I am no prophet, nor prophet's son, only a herdsman. But God called me and bade me prophesy to the people of Israel. Israel shall be taken captive. Moreover, thine own sons and thine own daughters shall die by the sword, and thou thyself shall die in the land of captivity."

Now Amos left the public place where he had spoken and passed through the gates of the city and came to his home in the hills of Tekoa. Once more he worked and mingled with the herdsmen and ploughmen as he had done before God called him. 

Day after day Amos waited for the word to come

to him of the effect of his words upon the people of Israel. But the only word that came to him was of the wickedness of the rich and the great sufferings of the poor.

At the close of his ministry Amos wrote down the words which God had inspired him to speak to Israel, that all those that came after might know that God required all men to live together as brothers in righteousness and justice.

HOSEA, THE PROPHET OF LOVE—Book of Hosea 

The people of Israel rejected the warning of Amos and wickedness and injustice grew apace. The poor were exploited, the weak oppressed, and the worship of God became little more than ceremony and sacrifice. So 

God raised up another prophet to Israel. 

The mantle of Amos fell upon Hosea as the mantle of Elijah had fallen upon Elisha. Just as Elijah had brought words of condemnation to Israel so also had Amos, but Hosea like Elisha was a tender-hearted teacher.

Out of the sad experiences of his own life Hosea came to understand the relationship of God, a loving Father, to His children, His sorrow for their waywardness and His plans for their salvation. Into the sacred record of his work, Hosea wrote a beautiful parable, tender and yet full of meaning, that Israel might come to know the nature of God's love for them:

A young Hebrew loved a beautiful woman and married her. For a time they lived together happily. 

Three children were born to them, two sons and a daughter. The husband was a loving father to his children, watching over them and teaching them that they might grow strong and brave and good. But the time came when the mother thought more of making herself attractive with a bright scarf for her head and with

chains of gold and bracelets and earrings than of the love of her husband and her children.

Then the time came when the wife of the Hebrew would rather walk in the streets of the city than to take care of her home and her children. It seemed that she thought more of the caresses of men drunken with wine than that of the loving fingers of her little ones. The father's heart, often very heavy now, and the little ones longed for their mother. One day she went to the city and did not return. Her husband went to find her and to beg her to come back to his home. But the woman answered: "The life of the city is gay and full of pleasure. 

Here men give me gold and silver and rich food and wine. I shall not return."

So the Hebrew returned alone to his home and his three young children. Now he must love them as a father and as a mother also. But each day he longed to see his wife and to hear her laughter, for he still loved her in spite of her wickedness.

After many days the sorrowing husband went to the city and he saw his wife ragged and disgraced walking in the market place. Now his heart was full of pity and he remembered only her youth and beauty and that she was his wife. So he went and bought her from those who had enslaved her in wickedness for the price of a slave, fifteen pieces of silver.

But when the husband brought the wayward wife back to her home he said to her: "You may live in my house but you shall not be my wife until you are as you were when we were first man and wife.”

Thereafter the man, as a father dealt tenderly with the woman, protecting her that she might become strong enough to resist temptation and teaching her of the nature of wickedness, that she might find her way back to purity and righteousness and happiness.

Many times as Hosea thought about the sad experience of life he tried to understand the cause of sorrow and suffering, what it was that separated men from the love and the goodness of God. In time he came to understand the nature of sin which is at the root of evil in men's lives, sin which separates one from God's love and goodness.

Hosea loved his people, Israel. He knew that 

Abraham had been chosen of God to be the Father of Israel; he knew how God had taught the people of 

Israel, how He had provided for all their needs, and had given them the land of Canaan for an inheritance. He knew also the waywardness of His people in the worship of Baal; he knew of the suffering and oppression, the luxury and wickedness that existed around him on all sides. And Hosea said to himself: "Israel is as a foolish woman who forsaketh a faithful husband. Israel forgot the Lord her God and gave her heart to Baal. There is no truth, nor goodness, nor knowledge of God in the land, naught but swearing and breaking faith and killing and stealing and committing adultery. Therefore, Israel must suffer that she might learn of God and His ways once more."

The words of Amos came to him and he remembered the terrible punishment that he had said would come to Israel. But when Hosea remembered the loving kindness of the Lord Jehovah he said: "The suffering that must come to Israel is for her own good, that she might return to the love of God and the love of righteousness.'

Whenever Hosea went abroad in the land he pled with the people that they might come to know the true and living God, and that they might understand that God desired of them loving obedience to His laws. When he saw the sacrifices offered at the altars of Bethel and the wickedness of the lives of those who offered them

his heart was full of pity for them. And as Hosea spoke to his people he said to them: "Come, O Israel, let us return to the Lord! There is no other true God but the God of Israel. "Thou art my people,' saith the Lord. 'I will love thee freely. 

I will heal thy backslidings. I will make thee to lie down in safety from thine enemies.'

But the people refused to listen to the loving message of Hosea as they had done to the stern words of Amos. Then Hosea went out among the people once more and cried out a warning to them: "The Lord will have no more mercy on Israel.

They have sown the wind, and they shall reap the whirlwind. They have ploughed wickedness, and they have reaped iniquity. Therefore, they shall be as chaff that is driven with the whirlwind. They shall not dwell in the Lord's land. Yet a little while and the Lord shall cause to cease the kingdom of Israel."

The Book of Hosea is a living parable. Hosea sees the great sin of Israel clearly, it is the sin of unfaithfulness. But what was more important, Hosea had learned that sin is the result of ignorance. Because 

Israel did not know God, they had substituted sacrifice for obedience and burnt offerings for righteousness, they worshiped because of fear or for protection and blessings.

Out of his own experiences Hosea had learned a great new truth about God, that He is a loving Father, long-suffering and forgiving, a God of love. This was the lesson that Israel had to learn. 

THE CALL OF ISAIAH—II Kings 15; Isaiah 6 

Uzziah became the King of Judah when Jeroboam the second was ruling over the Kingdom of Israel. When he saw the great wealth and prosperity that had come

to the people of Israel, Uzziah determined that his people should prosper also.

For many years Judah had had no share in the trade of Egypt and Assyria because she had no seaport. 

So Uzziah trained a small army and then he took the Mediterranean port of Eloth from the Edomites. Now 

Jerusalem, so long shut up in the mountains, became a door to the nations around Judah. Soon the great caravans from Assyria and Egypt began to pass through Jerusalem and large fleets of trading ships carried grain, wool, wine, oil, and spices to the great trading cities of Tyre and Sidon.

But the wealth of Judah like the wealth of Israel was in the hands of the merchants and the nobles, the men sowing and reaping the grain, watching the flocks and herds, or treading the grapes of the orchards for wine had no share in the wealth that their labors brought to the merchants who bought and sold their wares. But rather were they the poorer, for the merchants bartered for the lowest prices that they might make greater profits for themselves.

It was during these changing days in the Kingdom of Judah and in the city of Jerusalem that a youth named Isaiah was growing up. His father was wellborn and very wealthy, and he was a loyal worshiper of Jehovah.

Isaiah loved Jerusalem; to him it was David's city. The beauty of the House of the Lord as it stood in shimmering whiteness on the slopes of Mount Moriah filled his heart with pride. But it was when he was old enough to enter its sacred walls with his father and to take part in the ceremonies of the Passover that he came to know and appreciate the blessing of being born of the people of the living God. Like every other loyal Hebrew boy he was ready to defend Jerusalem and the 

Temple with his life.

But as he grew to manhood within the city, Isaiah

saw many changes, there were many strangers in the city, not the people of Israel who came from all parts of the two kingdoms to worship in the Temple, but travelers and traders of other lands. Then there were gay crowds of people dressed in rich silks and wearing ornaments of gold and precious stones. The sound of drunken revels often filled the air. Many times he had pondered over the words he had heard the prophets Amos and Hosea speak to the people in the public place of Jerusalem when he was a boy; but now he could understand them. The joy of his heart for his beloved city had gone and in his sorrow he said: "The land is full of silver and gold. It is full of horses and chariots. But it is also full of pride and revelry and injustice. is as a fading flower." 

The glorious beauty of Judah

The fame of King Uzziah spread far and wide among the nations, and the people of Judah heaped many honors upon him. As the time passed the heart of the King was filled with pride. But there was one great honor which he had not attained, he had not been made a priest of the Temple. Azariah the high priest was not able to bestow this sacred office upon the King, because the holy priesthood belonged to the sons of Aaron. Uzziah, however, would not listen to the servant of God but went to the House of the Lord and there conducted the sacred ceremonies. Even as he did so the white spots of leprosy appeared on his brow. When the priests of the temple saw what had happened they took him away. From then on King Uzziah had to dwell alone in a separate place. Jotham reigned in the place of his father and Judah continued to prosper. 

One day, at the time of the death of Uzziah, Isaiah was in the House of the Lord. He was thinking of the people of Israel and all that had happened in their long history, and his heart was sorely troubled over the grave problems of the day. Suddenly a great brightness sur-

rounded him and a marvelous vision came to him. He saw the Lord God in all His glory seated upon His throne and the angels around the throne were singing: "Holy, Holy, Holy, is the Lord of Hosts. 

The whole earth is full of His glory!"

Isaiah was shaken with fear at the vision, for when he saw the glory and majesty of God he understood the weaknesses of mankind and in dismay he cried out: "Woe is me! For I am undone; I am a man of unclean lips and I dwell in the midst of a people of unclean lips."

Then an angel came and cleansed the lips of Isaiah and pronounced him clean and his sins forgiven. Now 

Isaiah heard the voice of the Lord saying: "Whom shall I send and who will go for us?" 

And Isaiah said, "Here am I; send me.'

Isaiah had a great mission to perform; he was to be a statesman and a prophet. There were days of tragedy ahead for the remnant of Israel left in Judah, and God's work must not be destroyed from the earth. 

Because of his training Isaiah had been chosen as a counselor to the rulers of Judah that they might preserve Israel, not destroy it. The time was also one of great crisis for the people, they needed guidance to meet the social problems of the day and inspiration to help them to remain faithful to the worship of the only true God, the God of Israel.

THE PARABLE OF THE VINEYARD-Isaiah 5 

Isaiah went about among the people pleading with them tenderly to forsake evil and to return to the ways of the Lord. Those who heard the words of the young prophet as he spoke in the public places were amazed for they were beautiful and appealing, not words of fire and bitterness. One time Isaiah appeared in the street with a small harp and began to sing:

"My well beloved had a vineyard in a very fruitful hill: 

And he made a trench about it,

And gathered out the stones thereof, And planted it with the choicest vine, And built a tower in the midst of it, And also hewed out a winepress therein: 

And he looked that it should bring forth grapes. "And now, O inhabitants of Jerusalem, Ye people of Judah! 

Judge now, for yourselves,

What more could be done to my vineyard than that which I have done?

When I looked to find grapes that were good, why yielded it wild grapes? "And now let me tell you what I will do with my vineyard, I will remove its hedge thereof,

And I will break through its wall that it be downtrodden.

Yea, I will make it a waste, not pruned or weeded, And then it shall put forth thorns and thickets of brambles. 

To the clouds will I give command that they may not rain upon it. "For the vineyard of the Lord of hosts is the House of Israel,

And the men of Judah, his cherished plantation: 

He looked for justice, but behold; oppression; 

For righteousness, but, behold a cry.

The people of Jerusalem who listened to this beautiful parable in song must have understood it very clearly, for around their beautiful city in the hills of Judah were countless vineyards. If the intent of the Master

of the Vineyard of Israel seemed hard to think of, there was comfort to be had in the closing words of the parable: "For all this His anger is not turned away, And His hand is outstretched still."

ISAIAH, THE STATESMAN-II Kings 15-9 

Soon after Isaiah had begun his ministry he married a maiden of wisdom and virtue who loved God sincerely. And when a son was born to Isaiah, he named the child Shear-jashub which means "A remnant-shallreturn."

Now evil days were shaping themselves once more for Judah, for when Jotham the strong king died his son, Ahaz, a wild, ungovernable young man reigned in his place. He filled his palace with drunken friends and ordered incense to be kept burning before the idols of the heathen gods. Seeing the weakness of Ahaz, King Rezin of Syria and King Pekah of Israel invaded Judah and took away the port of Eloth. 

The people of Judah were unable to meet this great crisis for Ahaz was panic stricken. Instead of meeting the enemies bravely he decided to send to the powerful King of Assyria to come to his aid. 

While Ahaz was considering this foolish plan God spoke to Isaiah directing him to take his son and go to meet the King of Judah and counsel with him. Isaiah went to a place outside the city where the King and his guards were to pass. As the King came riding by Isaiah spoke to him as he had been directed: "Take heed and be calm; fear not neither let thy heart be faint because of these smoking firebrands, Rezin the King of Syria and Pekah the King of Israel. The evil planned against thee shall not stand neither shall it come to pass."

But Ahaz would not listen to the words of Je-

hovah. Instead he closed the doors of the House of the 

Lord and took all the treasures of silver and gold and sent them to the King of Assyria with the message: "I am your servant. Come up and deliver me.'

The powerful armies of Tiglath Pileser, the Assyrian, did not take long in seizing Damascus and in killing the rebellious King Rezin and in making Syria a part of Assyria. Then they came to Israel and placed a new king over the land. While Judah was left free she had opened the door to a powerful enemy. 

Then Ahaz realized the danger of Judah so he hurried to Damascus and paid homage to the conqueror. 

In payment of a yearly tribute Ahaz was able to keep his throne. When Isaiah saw what had happened to 

Judah he said in his great sorrow: "My people have gone into captivity because they have no understanding.'

Moreover, when Ahaz returned to Jerusalem he called Urijah, the priest, and ordered him to make an altar such as was used by the priests of Damascus; also he ordered that the worship and scarifices be patterned after the pagan worship of the Assyrians. And Urijah did as the King commanded. After these things had happened, Isaiah knew that it was useless to try to counsel with Ahaz so he spake to the king no more but withdrew to a place of peace with his wife and his sons. 

Many of his disciples followed him and Isaiah spent his time with them, teaching them the wisdom that had come to him from the Lord.

As long as Ahaz ruled over Judah the people paid tribute to Assyria. However, Hezekiah, his son, desired to regain the independence of his people. In Israel there was also a new king, Hoshea, who sought to free his land. The King of Egypt had stirred up Hoshea and the rulers of the small nations of Moab, Edom, Ammon, and Philistria to refuse to pay tribute to Assyria. The

counselors of Hezekiah urged him to join with Israel and Egypt and defy the King of Assyria. 

When Isaiah found out what was happening he left his home and his disciples and came to Jerusalem. 

Once more he stood in the public place and spoke to the people saying: "Woe to them that go down to Egypt for help and rely on horses, and trust in chariots because they are many and in horsemen because they are strong; but look not to the Holy One of Israel. The Egyptians are men and not God! Their horses are flesh and not spirit and they shall all fall together. Trust in the Lord forever; for in the Lord Jehovah is everlasting strength."

At this time the prophet Micah went among the villages of Judah speaking to the country men warning them of the evil to come to Hezekiah's alliance with Egypt and bringing to them a message of hope that they might remain faithful and not despair: "Many nations are gathered against thee, O Judah. 

But is the spirit of the Lord straightened that He cannot save He shall have compassion upon us. Out of Bethlehem shall He raise up a ruler for Israel." 

Even as the prophets Isaiah and Micah were speaking to Judah Shalmaneser, the new King of Assyria, was marching down upon Israel. He took Hoshea captive and laid siege to the city of Samaria. When Samaria fell, most of the people were carried away and scattered among the peoples of Mesopotamia. Then to make his conquest more complete the King of Assyria filled the land of Israel with people from other lands. 

In this tragic manner the Northern Kingdom of Israel came to an end seven hundred and twenty-two years before the birth of Christ.

In spite of the tragedy that had come to the Northern Kingdom the counselors of Hezekiah urged him to ally himself with Egypt and rebel against Assyria. Then the King of Babylon desiring to overcome the Assyrians

came to Hezekiah and urged him to join in the revolt. 

And the King of Judah received the Babylonian with much honor and respect. So flattered was he at the visit of the great king that he showed him all his treasures. Now Isaiah came and spoke to the King sternly warning him against seeking strength through war and bloodshed rather than through righteousness. And

Isaiah spoke further to Hezekiah of the things which had been revealed to him concerning the captivity of Judah that was to come. But Hezekiah did not listen to Isaiah, instead he allowed his servants to make a secret treaty with Egypt and Babylon against Assyria. 

When Sennacherib, the King of Assyria, heard of the rebellion of Egypt and Judah his mighty army marched through Philistia like a whirlwind burning and plundering as it went. The Egyptians sent an army against the invading Assyrians but it was defeated. 

Then Sennacherib turned his fury upon the land of Judah, one city after another was burned and twenty thousand people were captured with all their possessions.

The people of Jerusalem crowded to the Temple and began offering sacrifices thinking to prevail upon 

God to stay the ruthless conqueror. In great fear Hezekiah took all the money in his treasury, the gold from the doors and pillars of the Temple, precious stones, ivory, and slaves, and sent them as a peace offering to Sennacherib. The Assyrian took the tribute and then turned back to complete the defeat of the armies of 

Egypt. For a time Jerusalem was spared.

However, it was not long before Sennacherib sent his captains and a large army and demanded the surrender of Jerusalem for he was afraid to leave the city so strongly fortified. The men of Israel who had gathered to the walls of the city were stricken with fear when they saw the great hosts of Assyrians. One of the captains of the enemy came forward and called forth:

"Put not thy trust in Pharaoh, King of Egypt, a broken reed, neither put your trust in Hezekiah, King of Judah, for he shall not be alive to deliver you. Make treaty with the King of Assyria and pay tribute. Then shall every man eat of his own wine and fig tree and drink of his own well, until I take you away into a land like your own.'

In distress Hezekiah sent to Isaiah to seek his counsel; and the prophet bade the King to be brave and fearless and to make no answer. When the King came later to the Temple to pray Isaiah spoke words of comfort to him: "Be not afraid of the words thou hast heard; for the King of Assyria shall return to his own land.” 

Then Rabshakeh, the captain of the Assyrian host returned with a letter from Sennacherib saying: "Let not thy God in whom thou trusteth deceive thee. Hath the gods of the nations delivered them from the King of Assyria?"

Then Hezekiah went to the House of the Lord to seek the counsel of Isaiah but this time he came in great humility clad in sackcloth. And again Isaiah dealt tenderly with him and gave him words of comfort: "Be not afraid of the words thou hast heard. He shall not come into this city nor shoot an arrow there, neither shall he come before it with a shield. By the way that he came the same shall he return. "And the Assyrian shall fall by the sword, not of man; and the sword not of men shall devour him." 

As Isaiah had spoken so it came to pass for in the night the armies of the Assyrians camped outside Jerusalem were smitten by a plague and one hundred and eighty-five thousand lay dead; and the remnant led by 

Sennacherib fled in terror to their own city, Nineveh.

When the men of Israel saw the vast array of empty tents and the bodies of the Assyrians, they cried out in thanksgiving:

"God is our refuge and strength. 

And ever present help in trouble."

At last Isaiah saw that his work had not been in vain for now the King sought his counsel in all matters pertaining to the welfare of the people of Judah. King 

Hezekiah set himself the task of restoring the security of the Kingdom of Judah and establishing righteousness in the lives of the people. Isaiah, the greatest and wisest man in Judah, served his King as statesman and his people as a prophet of God.

ISAIAH, THE PROPHET-Isaiah 1-40 

After this the Lord God revealed many things to the prophet Isaiah concerning the things that would happen to Israel and to mankind. But the greatest of all was the revelation of the coming of Christ, the Messiah: "Unto us a child is born; unto us a son is given; 

And the government shall be upon his shoulder: 

And his name shall be called 

Wonderful, Counsellor, Mighty God, Everlasting Father, Prince of Peace."

And it was given Isaiah to know what would happen in the earth when the spirit of wisdom and understanding and the fear of Jehovah came to all mankind: "At that time the wolf shall dwell with the lamb and the leopard shall lie down with the kid, the calf and the young lion and the fatling together; and a little child shall lead them. They shall not hurt or destroy in all God's holy mountain, for the earth shall be full of the knowledge of Jehovah, as the waters cover the sea."

A vision of the establishment of the Kingdom of God in the last days was also given to Isaiah: "And it shall come to pass in the latter days, that

the mountain of the Lord's house shall be established in the tops of the mountains, and shall be exalted above the hills and all nations shall flow into it. And many people shall go and say, 'Come, ye, and let us go up to the mountain of Jehovah, to the house of the God of Jacob; and He will teach us His ways, and we will walk in His paths; for out of Zion shall go forth the law, and the word of Jehovah from Jerusalem. And He will judge between the nations, and will decide concerning many peoples; and they shall beat their swords into plowshares and their spears into pruning hooks; nation shall not lift up sword against nation, neither shall they learn war any more." 

Isaiah wrote a beautiful song, "A Song of Hope," for his people to bring them courage for the tragedy of the Exile that was to come. He wanted them to be comforted by the knowledge that the day and generation in which they lived was but as a moment in the eternity of God and that the promises made to Abraham would be fulfilled: "Comfort ye, comfort ye my people, saith your 

God. Speak ye comfortably to Jerusalem. Say unto the cities of Judah, Behold, your God! "Behold the Lord God will come as a mighty one, And His arm shall rule for Him: 

Behold His reward is with Him, And His recompense before Him. "He shall feed his flock like a shepherd. 

He shall carry His lambs in His arm, And carry them in His bosom, And gently lead those that are with young. "The grass withereth, The flower fadeth; 

But the word of our God shall stand for ever."

No other writer of the Old Testament has written with such beauty and with such power as did Isaiah, and the burden of his message to Israel and to all mankind was of the holiness of God. He saw the worship of God to be of the spirit not merely with outward form; that the holiness of man cometh from the things of the heart and the glory of God cometh from the holiness of His children.

THE MESSAGE OF THE PROPHET MICAH -Book of Micah 

Micah lived in the time of Hezekiah, King of Judah. Like the prophet Amos he was a country man. 

His home in the hills overlooking the land of the Philistines was at the southwest gateway of Judah. Not far away were the great trading cities of Gaza and Ashkelon on the shores of the great sea, the Mediterranean. 

Micah, who has been called the village prophet, wrote his wisdom and his inspiration in a few short pages, the Book of Micah. There we find the stern words of condemnation he spoke against the social evils of the day in which he lived. But among his words of wisdom, we find the most beautiful expression to be found in the Old Testament, as to the meaning of religion: "He hath showed thee, O Man, what is good; and what doth the Lord require of thee, but to do justly, and to love mercy, and to walk humbly with thy God." 

When Micah saw the great armies of Sennacherib on their way to fight the King of Egypt and then to lay siege Jerusalem, he was sorely distressed for the safety of his country. He began to understand the dangers confronting a small nation like Judah among such powerful and wicked conquering nations.

Often he would stand in the market place of the village when the merchants would come from Jerusalem

to buy the grain, wool, oil, and wine the people had to sell. He watched the crafty traders cheat the simple peasants and then go away chuckling. Many times the word came to Micah that the tax gatherers had taken away the orchard of an olive grower for a small debt, or that the sons of a poor farmer had been taken as bondsmen because of a mortgage on the land. And when Micah went to obtain justice for these poor unfortunate people he found priests and judges selling judgments to the rich and there was nothing that he could do. 

As he thought of all these evils which had come to the lives of his neighbors Micah said to himself: "Why do the powerful kill and why do those who are rich steal from others? Why are some of the land poor while others are rich?”

Then Micah left his home and went among the poor people of Judah seeking to bring them relief and comfort. To the oppressors he cried in anger: "Woe to them that devise iniquity and work evil. 

They covet fields and take them by violence and houses and take them away; so they oppress a man and his house, even a man and his heritage. "The good man has perished out of the land, there is none upright among men. They hunt every man and his brother with a net. That they might do evil, the prince asketh and the judge asketh for a reward. A man's enemies are they of his own house." 

The words of Micah condemning the evil ways of the rich and powerful in Judah were very bitter. But when he saw the misdeeds of some of the religious leaders of Israel his anger knew no bounds. All through its long history the servants of God, the prophets and the priests, had been chosen by direct revelation from the Lord or by the priesthood having His authority.

Now there were those who assumed this holy calling of their own responsibility serving without authority. 

But what was a greater evil still, they served the rich for hire. How could such false teachers be expected to condemn the evil of those who paid them for their services? To these false leaders Micah spoke fearlessly: "Ye shall have no vision; and it shall be dark unto you; for there shall be no answer of God to you.' 

Now Micah ceased his words of condemnation to the oppressors of his people and turned his words to the things that were to come to pass. He told Israel of the days of exile that were to come to them before long. 

He told them also of the coming of the Messiah, the Saviour, and of His works on the earth, and His influence upon mankind in the ages to come. "Out of thee shall one come forth unto Me, that is to be ruler in Israel; whose goings forth are from of old, from the days of eternity. And He shall stand and 

He shall feed His flock in the strength of the Lord." 

To Micah had been given a vision of the work of the Lord upon the earth in the latter days: "But in the latter days it shall come to pass that the mountain of the Lord's house shall be established in the tops of the mountains, and it shall be exalted above the hills; and people shall flow unto it. And many nations shall say: 'Come ye, and let us go up to the mountain of the Lord, and to the house of the God of Jacob; and He will teach us of His ways, and we will walk in His paths.' For out of Zion shall go forth the law and the word of the Lord from Jerusalem."

The prophet Micah closes the record of his teachings with a testimony of the mercy and loving kindness of God unto His people: "He retaineth not His anger for ever, because He delighteth in mercy."

THE REFORMS OF HEZEKIAH AND JOSIAH -II Chronicles 29-35 

The work of Isaiah and Micah, the prophets, began to bear fruit for King Hezekiah was able to do much good in the land of Judah because of their teachings. 

In the days when Hezekiah first became king he called all the Levites to come to Jerusalem and to prepare the Temple for the worship of God once more. 

Then he sent word to all the people to come and worship and keep the Feast of the Passover which had not been observed for many years. Many of the faithful came with great rejoicing to attend the Feast; many others neglected the call because they were not interested in worship any more; and many others mocked the King because they preferred to worship the new gods they had found.

Seeing the faithlessness of so many of his people, Hezekiah sent teachers out among them to show them the evil of their ways. But he did more than teach the people he sent his servants to destroy all the altars and idols that had been built for the worship of heathen gods.

After the destruction of the army of Sennacherib and there was peace in the land Hezekiah began to correct some of the evils that Micah had condemned, the injustice and oppression that made the poor people suffer.

It happened that the King became very ill and feared that he might die. He sought the Lord with tears and prayers that his life might be spared to finish the work of reform he had started. Then Isaiah came with a message telling Hezekiah that his prayers had been heard and his life would be spared for fifteen years to continue his good work for Judah. 

When at last the life of Hezekiah closed the people mourned him and honored him calling him the "Greatest King of Judah."

The next years in the history of the Kingdom of Judah are among the blackest pages in Israel's history. 

Perhaps if we remember there were people of many nations and many religions in the land, especially in the great city of Jerusalem, we can understand in a measure what happened. When Manasseh, the son of Hezekiah, became king he turned his back upon those who had brought about the reforms of his father. He rebuilt the altars to the pagan gods that had been destroyed; and he set up altars for the worship of the sun and the moon and the stars in the courts of the Temple. Once more little children were sacrificed to Moloch, even the King's son was not spared. The prophets and priests of the living God of Israel were killed or driven out of the land. The prophet Isaiah the greatest and wisest man in Judah, died a cruel death at the order of Manasseh. 

But an evil man does not live forever. When 

Josiah, the grandson of Manasseh, became king once more good began to triumph over evil in Judah. 

Following the pattern of the good King Hezekiah, Josiah removed from the land the altars to the worship of pagan gods: the altar of the fire-god Moloch, the devourer of little children, the altars of the sun god, and even the golden calves of Jeroboam at Bethel were destroyed. Now many of those who were faithful to 

Jehovah came to the aid of Josiah with new hope in their hearts, also the prophets and priests of Jehovah came from their places of refuge and began to preach once more.

Then King Josiah gave the command that the Temple be prepared for the worship of God once more. 

While Hilkiah, the priest, was setting things in order in one of the inner rooms of the Temple he found a strange book. Wiping the dust from the roll the priest called 

Shaphan, the King's scribe, to read the words inscribed therein. And as Shaphan read he discovered it to be

the Book of the Law and the Teachings of Moses. The servants took the roll to the King and read it aloud to him. When Josiah heard the words of the Law which 

Moses had commanded should be read to all the people once every seven years, his sorrow was very great for now he understood the extent of the faithlessness and the disobedience of Israel.

When the time came for the Feast of the Passover Josiah called the people from every part of the land to assemble in Jerusalem. Then he ordered that the sacred book be read to them. Standing before the people Josiah promised to keep the ancient covenant of the Israelites and all those assembled made the same promise. 

The days of the Feast that followed were seven days. of rejoicing for the people, such as had not been known since the prophet Samuel kept the Feast of the Passover and read the Book of the Law.

Afterwards Josiah sent teachers among the people to instruct them in all the details of the Law of Moses, that they might know that which was right. For in the Book of the Law were to be found laws to govern every activity of life: religious.laws, military laws, family laws, laws for officers of authority, laws of chastity, and humane laws.

When we read the Book of Deuteronomy we know the laws which were taught to the people of Israel by the priest and the teachers sent out among them by Josiah. Many of the laws were accompanied by punishments to be meted out to those who were disobedient. 

The faithful and the oppressed of Israel found comfort in the teachings of the Book of the Law: "High and low, rich and poor, all stand on an equality before Him." "Ye shall not respect persons in judgment, ye shall hear the small and the great alike." "Thou shalt not oppress a hired servant that is

poor and needy, whether he be of thy brethren or a sojourner within thy gates. "Thou shalt love thy God with all thy heart, and with all thy soul, and with all thy might." "These words which I command thee shall be upon thy heart; and thou shalt teach them diligently unto thy children. . . . And thou shalt write them upon the doorposts of thy house and upon thy gates.'

The influence of the reforms of Hezekiah and of Josiah was very great for now the people began to understand the teachings of the prophets; to obey the laws of God is better than to offer sacrifices. 

The repentance of Israel at this time was sincere and far reaching but the time left to them to make amends was very short.

FAITH IN THE REALIZATION OF GOD'S PURPOSES The Prophets of Israel's Spiritual Destiny THE 

HE fall of the tiny kingdom of Judah was inevitable, for within this small kingdom of less than two hundred thousand souls weakness and discord prevailed and without were the powerful nations of the ancient world struggling for supremacy. 

In the brief space of a century the Kingdom of Israel grew to greatness and prosperity. After the division of the land of their inheritance, the Kingdom of Israel had declined rapidly, being destroyed by the Assyrians in the year 722 B. C. For one hundred and fifty years longer the Kingdom of Judah had struggled to survive. In the year 586 B. C. Nebuchadnezzar completed his destruction of Jerusalem and carried thousands into exile to Babylon. 

The end of the national history of Israel was the opening of the way for the establishment of God's kingdom on earth, Israel's true destiny. The destiny of 

Israel lay not in supremacy among the nations of the world as a political kingdom, but as a spiritual kingdom, the kingdom of God.

The years before the fall of Judah were years of preparation for Israel, out of the darkness was to come a new light to the world, the new spirit of Israel. The prophets of God brought to their people the under-

standing that the events that were overtaking them were in the fulfillment of the purposes of the Lord.

The prophets Jeremiah, Zephaniah, Habakkuk, Joel, Nahum, Obadiah, and others of this period did not prophesy and preach in vain. Israel went into exile accepting the suffering as punishment for faithlessness and wickedness; and believing the promises made by the prophets that Israel would survive and be restored. 

But the restoration was not to be a national restoration, it was to be a restoration of Israel for its spiritual destiny, the fulfillment of the promise made to Abraham.

THE CALL OF JEREMIAH-Jeremiah 1 

Nearby the city of Jerusalem, almost in the shadow of its walls, was the village of Anathoth, where the priests lived. During the early days of King Josiah of Judah a sensitive youth was growing to manhood. 

Belonging to a family of priests since David's time Jeremiah knew little else than the worship of Israel. 

The dreadful days of Manasseh had left an ugly memory for the faithful among the people of Judah, a memory of idolatry and persecution. But now the new king was beginning to wipe out the idols and the wickedness from the very courts of the Temple. Even then many of the people still burned incense on their roofs unto the sun and the moon and the stars.

But in Anathoth life was still simple, disturbed only by the rumors of the happenings in Jerusalem. 

As Jeremiah came each day from the school of the prophets and passed by the gray olive orchards he often thought how much like one of the trees had Israel grown, gnarled and crooked instead of straight and beautiful. 

Then one day during his pondering he heard the voice of God saying: "Jeremiah, I have ordained thee a prophet unto the nations."

"Lord God," exclaimed Jeremiah, "behold I cannot speak; for I am a child." 

Then the Lord said unto him: "Say not I am a child; for thou shalt go to all that 

I shall send thee; whatsoever I command thee, thou shalt speak. Be not afraid of their faces for I am with thee to deliver thee. Gird up thy loins and go." 

Then Jehovah put forth his hand and touched the mouth of the youth and said: "Behold I have put my words in thy mouth. See

I have set thee this day over the nations, and over the kingdoms, to pluck up and to break down, and to destroy and to overthrow, to build and to plant." 

It was a great mission that had been given to Jeremiah, to teach Judah that God expected integrity in nations as in individuals, that nations should be judged and punished or rewarded according to their righteousness.

Even if the task was a hard and bitter one Jeremiah was not afraid for with his shyness of manner he had a brave spirit. Leaving his home and bidding farewell to the priests of Anathoth, he went to Jerusalem and in the public place and spoke to the people: "O Jerusalem, wash thine heart from wickedness that thou mayest be saved, that there shall not cease from thy cheek the voice of mirth and the voice of gladness. Thy safety is not long."

The people of Jerusalem paid little heed to the words of the young prophet, so he left the city and went about in the country preaching to the people that as a nation they might rouse themselves to an understanding of their condition.

It was about this time that the Book of the Law was found during the restoration of the Temple and the people began to heed the words of the prophets and help the reforms of King Josiah. Jeremiah worked eagerly that the change of heart of the people might not

pass away. For fifteen years the words of the prophet were received with gladness and all went well with Judah. In his heart Jeremiah hoped that the danger that had threatened his nation had passed away. 

But after the untimely death of Josiah a change came to Israel. Soon all the good that Josiah had wrought was undone. His son Jehoahaz reigned but four months and then he was carried away as a captive to Egypt. The new king, Jehoiakim, the second son of Josiah, was a wicked man like unto Manasseh and it was not long before idolatry and wickedness filled the land once more.

JEREMIAH'S TEMPLE SERMON-Jeremiah 7 

The new king was not only wicked, he was foolish, and he was unable to understand the things that were happening in the nations around Israel. Babylonia had taken the place of Assyria and had become the ruler of the land of the north as well as the east. 

Judah was already paying tribute to Egypt. What would happen if the Babylonians came as conquerors to the west and to the south to Judah and to Egypt? 

It was Jeremiah's task to warn his people of this new danger.

The Lord sent Jeremiah to stand forth in the court of the Temple and to warn the people of Jerusalem. 

It was the time of festival when he came there. The 

Temple Court was crowded and the prophet spoke out fearlessly the words that Jehovah gave to him: "Thus saith the God of Israel, 'If ye will not walk in my law I will make this city an heap of ruins. Amend your ways and your doings, then I will be with you and deliver you.'

Then a great tumult arose among the people who heard the words of Jeremiah, some agreed with the prophet but others threatened to kill him. The false

prophets spoke to the people denouncing Jeremiah for his words but some of the Elders spoke to the crowd reminding them that when a prophet of God spoke to Hezekiah he repented and did much good for Israel. 

While the confusion continued the friends of the prophet took him away to safety. After a while Jeremiah went to Anathoth to his father's home to rest among his kinsfolk and friends. But it was not long before an angry mob came and drove him out of his home back to Jerusalem.

THE MESSAGE OF THE BURNT SCROLL-Jeremiah 36 

It was about this time that the great king of Babylon sent his young son Nebuchadnezzar to conquer the nations of the west. When Egypt was defeated the people of Judah cried out in great joy. Then Jeremiah came forth and warned them that Nebuchadnezzar would soon come and destroy Judah. But instead the young prince returned to Babylon to be made the king for his father had died. The people of Jerusalem seized 

Jeremiah in anger and put him in prison. 

Then the Lord commanded Jeremiah to write on a roll all the things that had been given to him to speak to the people of Judah. So the prophet called Baruch, a young scribe, to make the record. And when it was finished Jeremiah sent him forth to read the words to the people.

When some of the friends of the king heard the words of the record they took it away from Baruch. 

Then they sent in secret and took Jeremiah out of prison lest the king in his anger kill a prophet of God. Baruch and Jeremiah were warned to flee to safety and to remain in hiding.

King Jehoiakim, however, heard about the roll. and demanded that it be read to him. As he listened to the reading of his scribe, Jehudi, the king flew into a

rage and took the roll and threw it into a fire that burned on the hearth. Then he ordered that Jeremiah and

Baruch be found and placed in prison. But they could not be found.

Jeremiah was instructed to take another roll and write upon it the words which had been written before. 

But this time there was to be added an even greater warning for the danger was very near to the gates of Judah.

Within the same year Nebuchadnezzar invaded the land and took the King a prisoner to Babylon. However, Jehoiakim was allowed to return to Jerusalem to rule over Judah for the King of Babylon. For three years Jehoiakim sent the tribute demanded by Nebuchadnezzar, but on the next year he rebelled thinking that the Babylonian king was unable to collect the treasure because he was making war upon other nations. 

THE POTTER'S CLAY-Jeremiah 18 

The people of Judah fell to rejoicing at what they considered the great wisdom of their king in rebelling against the King of Babylon. But the heart of Jeremiah was very sad. To bring comfort to His servant and to teach him a very great lesson the Lord sent Jeremiah to watch a potter at work molding vessels of clay. 

As the potter worked Jeremiah saw that when a vessel was spoiled in the molding or was not perfect the potter took the clay and remolded it sometimes several times, until at last it was the shape that he wanted. 

This was a great lesson for the prophet. Now he saw that the Lord God was slowly but patiently molding Israel to be the people He had planned them to be. 

It was not only a great lesson but a great comfort for now Jeremiah understood that out of the great suffering that was to come to Israel would come a refining and remolding of God's children into a new and more perfect pattern of living.

Taking a jar from the potter's wheel with him Jeremiah went among the people to teach them the lesson he had learned. When he came to the Valley of 

Hinnom where the people worshiped Moloch, the god of fire, like the Ammonites, he broke the jar in the sight of all the people and cried out to them: "Behold, O house of Israel! The Lord will break this people as one breaketh a potter's vessel. The Lord will bring to this city all that hath been pronounced against it."

Hearing of the words of Jeremiah the chief priest of the Temple had the prophet arrested and placed in stocks at the Bethlehem gate of Jerusalem that all might see him. As the people passed by him and mocked him, the hurt and sensitive spirit of the prophet caused him to say: "I will not make mention of the Lord, nor speak any more in His name." 

However, on the next day when he was released from the stocks the words of the Lord burned like fire in his heart and he spoke once more that which came to him to say.

JEREMIAH AND THE FALL OF JERUSALEM 

-Jeremiah 22-29

At last the fateful day for Judah came as Jeremiah had foretold, for Nebuchadnezzar brought a large army and took the city of Jerusalem. Jeremiah saw the Temple entered and its treasures taken. Then he saw the 

King and his family and all the choice men of Israel, soldiers, craftsmen, carpenters, and smiths, ten thousand of them, pass out of the gates of the city to captivity to Babylon. Only the poor and helpless were left in the city of Jerusalem and the farmers and peasants in the land of Judah. But the heart of the prophet was sustained in the knowledge that out of this suffering 

Israel would be perfected.

Now Jeremiah must be a comforter to his people, to those in Judah and to those in exile. To the people who had been taken in exile he wrote counseling them against rebellion and evil but to live in peace with all men. Then he wrote the message Jehovah had given him: "After seventy years I will revisit you, and you shall return to this place for the thoughts I think of you are thoughts of peace and not of evil, to give you hope. 

Then shall ye call upon Me and ye shall go and pray unto Me, and I will hearken unto you. Ye shall seek

Me and find Me, when ye shall search for Me with all your heart. "And I will be found of you and I will turn away your captivity, and I will gather you from all the nations and from all the places whither I have driven you, and I will bring you again into this place whence I caused you to be carried away captive."

The well-being of those who remained in the land of Judah gave the prophet much concern, for Zedekiah, the son of Jehoiakim, had been made the ruler of the land under Nebuchadnezzar. The new ruler of the people was both wicked and headstrong. The prophet counseled with him for the good of the people, to be loyal to the demands of the king of Babylon and not to bring any more suffering to the people. 

But Zedekiah did not listen to the counsel of Jeremiah but sought the aid of the King of Egypt to rebel against Babylon. Once more the prophet tried to warn 

Zedekiah of his folly, but this time Jeremiah was placed in prison for his trouble.

Some of the friends of the King now pled with him to put the prophet to death. The foolish King fearing to do harm to one of God's servants gave Jeremiah into the hands of these friends. Later, however, when Zedekiah found out that the prophet had been placed in an

old well he sent his servants to rescue him and return him to prison.

Even at this time the armies of Nebuchadnezzar were coming against Jerusalem because of the foolishness of the King. Now Zedekiah sought the counsel of the prophet as to what he should do. Once more 

Jeremiah appealed to the King to accept the yoke of the Chaldeans to save the destruction of the city and a terrible slaughter. But Zedekiah did not listen.

The army of Nebuchadnezzar broke down the walls of the city, destroyed the Temple and the palace of David, and burnt almost all of the dwellings. Many were killed and many escaped and fled to Egypt and of those remaining many were taken captive to join those already in exile in Babylon. Jeremiah, however, was treated with respect by the captain of the army who offered him peace and comfort in Babylon if he would accompany the captives.

But the prophet chose to remain with the remnant of his people left in Judah for there was a great suffering among them, some were homeless, families were separated, and many were destitute crying for bread amid the blackened ruins of the cities. 

In these dark days of destruction and sorrow Jeremiah did not despair, because like Isaiah he had the knowledge of the Savior that would come to Judah, a king of the line of David. As he went about among the remnant of the people who were left he sought to comfort them saying: "Behold a day cometh when the Lord will raise unto David a righteous branch, a king who shall reign. and he shall execute judgment and justice. Therefore be ye comforted."

After the terror of the destruction of Jerusalem had passed away many of those who were left decided to go to Egypt thinking to gain security and comfort.

Once more Jeremiah spoke out bravely reminding them

of God's promise to restore Israel to the land from whence they had been taken. But they did not listen to the counsel of the prophet and a large colony set out to make a new home among an idolatrous people. 

Of the last days of Jeremiah we know very little, some traditions say that he was taken to Egypt and later escaped to Babylon, while others state that he was stoned to death by his own people for opposing the migration to Egypt.

THE SPIRIT OF JEREMIAH Jeremiah 24, 32, 51 

The life story of Jeremiah is a record of unselfish and loyal service. He was more than a great prophet, he was a great teacher never ceasing to fight for the welfare of Israel.

Like many other patriots of his day he thought that the welfare of Israel lay in the security of the land of Judah. Then as he came to understand that the security of Israel lay in righteousness, not in possessions, he ceased to denounce and condemn them but went among them as a teacher. He suffered with his people all their adversity, he was persecuted, imprisoned, and stoned for his words, but he never ceased to comfort them and help them to understand that God's ways may be mysterious ways in the sight of men, but they are always part of God's eternal plans for the happiness of mankind.

The spirit of Jeremiah comes to us in the examples he used in teaching his people. After the first captives had been taken to Babylon, Jeremiah told his people of his vision of two baskets of figs, one of very good figs and one of very bad figs. The good figs could be used but the evil ones could not be eaten; likewise the good of Israel in capitivity would be used as a remnant which when refined would be used for good, the rebuilding of God's work, but the residue of Israel in

Judah and those who had gone to Egypt would become a reproach wherever they should go. 

To show the people his confidence in the fulfillment of God's promise Jeremiah went and bought a field in Anathoth as a symbol that houses and fields and vineyards should again be bought in the land by the people of Israel.

For the captives of Babylon Jeremiah wrote in a book the words that were given to him of the judgments of God against Babylon. Then the prophet commanded 

Seraiah to take the book to Babylon and to read it and then to bind it to a stone and cast it into the Euphrates. 

This was to be a symbol to Israel in captivity that Babylon would fall. Then Jeremiah pled with them saying: "You are learning a lesson in captivity and God will forgive you and will restore you to your land and the city of your captivity shall be destroyed. 

Jeremiah did not live to see his people return to their land but the great truths he taught have inspired the great religious leaders of the world since his time. 

Those great truths are: God is the God of the whole earth; God is a God of mercy; religion is a relationship between man and God; every man is responsible for his own actions.

ZEPHANIAH'S MESSAGE OF HOPE-Book of Zephaniah 

Zephaniah was a prince of the royal house of Hezekiah, and a cousin of King Josiah. He had had the good fortune of being one of the disciples of the prophet Isaiah. 

Like many other loyal patriots of his day, however, he saw with alarm the dangers that were threatening the small nation of Judah, a mere pawn in the struggles of the larger nations. But now a new enemy had suddenly appeared, hordes of barbarians had swept down from the Caucasus almost as far as the borders of Judah leaving a trail of blood and ashes behind them.

Surely this was the day of the judgment of Jehovah that had come to Israel! So thought the faithful leaders of Israel. On all sides was the evidence of what had happened to the worship of Israel, there were altars to pagan gods throughout the land. Some of the people even burned incense upon their house tops to the sun, moon, and stars. It seemed that Israel had copied patterns of worship from the peoples around them just as they had copied ways of building their houses and ways of living. What a tragedy for men to change without authority the things which God had revealed as the pattern of His worship! 

Zephaniah and other young patriots like him went throughout the land denouncing the people for their wickedness and warning them of the terrible doom awaiting them from the cruel conquerors. All these things were the judgments of God, cried Zephaniah as he went through Jerusalem with a lamp in his hand saying: "The Lord will search Jerusalem with a lamp!

He will find and punish those that do evil. Seek the 

Lord, the Lord is here to save thee." 

Later, however, Zephaniah came to understand that it was out of the tragedy that was to come to Israel that Israel would be cleansed. For God's people must be a righteous people living in humility and purity and dealing justly with all men. It was revealed to him that following the destruction of Judah a remnant of the people would remain in the land and a remnant would return from captivity and they together should establish themselves not as a nation but as a righteous people.

Then Zephaniah spoke to his people once more revealing to them the purposes for the sufferings that were to come and the vision he had been given of all the days to come even the latter days of God's kingdom on earth:

"At that time will I bring you in, at that time will

I gather you; for I will make you to be a name and a praise, among the peoples of the earth. Thus saith the Lord."

THE SPIRITUAL HOPE OF JOEL-Book of Joel 

The spirit of Israel did not die in spite of suffering and capitivity, rather did it grow strong. Those who were faithful accepted exile and persecution as God's will and awaited a brighter day when once more they would return to their beloved Jerusalem. The spirit of Israel was to grow into the spirit of Judaism, the spirit which has kept the Jews undaunted through the ages until today.

The words of the prophet Joel reveal the secret of the spirit of Israel, in spite of suffering and persecution, and even in disobedience they never forgot that they were the people for whom Abraham had been given. a sacred promise. The soul of Israel was a spiritual patriotism, a patriotism which grew stronger in adversity. They believed the promise of Joel when he said: "Judah shall abide forever, and Jerusalem for generation after generation."

But the most glorious promise of Joel is to be found in his words of the days far distant from the exile in Babylon: "And it shall come to pass that whosoever shall call upon the name of the Lord shall be delivered: for in Mount Zion and in Jerusalem there shall be those that escape, as the Lord hath said, and among the remnant, those whom the Lord shall call."

Joel ends his prophetic book with the words: "The Lord will be a refuge unto His people, and a stronghold to the children of Israel. So shall ye know that I am the Lord your God."

Through the prophesies of Joel for the future of his people and his religion, the courage of Israel was

sustained and the light of truth kept alive in the earth until the time of the coming of the Messiah, as Isaiah had foretold.

NAHUM AND OBADIAH-Books of Nahum and Obadiah 

The prophets Nahum and Obadiah arose as teachers in Judah during the ministry of Jeremiah. 

Nahum's words were issued as a condemnation against the evil power of Assyria and the punishment that was to come to that nation for the destruction of the Kingdom of Israel.

Nahum does not condemn his people, he encourages them to be faithful. assuring them: "The Lord is good, a stronghold in the day of trouble; and He knoweth them that put their trust in Him."

Most of the written record of Obadiah is a denunciation of Edom, the people of Esau, for attacking the descendants of Jacob during their days of great suffering. They had plundered the stricken cities, they had betrayed those fleeing from captivity and they had taken much of the land of Judah during the early years of the captivity.

The people asked the prophet why Edom survived while Israel and Judah had been destroyed. Obadiah warned his people that the day was at hand when all the heathen nations should perish and among them Edom: "And the house of Jacob shall be a fire, and the house of Joseph a flame; and the house of Esau shall be stubble, and they shall lick them up and devour them; and there shall not be any remaining of the house of 

Esau. For so the Lord has spoken."

Then Israel took comfort in the promise that a remnant of Israel should survive to continue God's work on earth.

While much of the written record of the prophets of Israel during the last days of the kingdom dealt with the great crisis of the day and the faithlessness of some of the people, there is one great truth they gave to the world, that God is loving and merciful working at all times for the salvation of all mankind. "Look unto God and be ye saved all the ends of the earth," is the teaching of these prophets.

INTERPRETING GOD FROM PERSONAL EXPERIENCE 

Three Prophets Perfect Their Understanding of God

THE measure of the mind of man has limited his understanding of God, His ways, and His purposes. 

Therefore, it has been necessary that God reveal to mankind from time to time those things which were necessary for human progress and eternal happiness. The prophets of God carried to mankind the knowledge which was revealed to them and thus God's purposes were fulfilled in the earth.

The prophets of God were as their fellow men, schooled by human experience. When the words of 

God came to them, therefore, even though they were sincere in purpose, many times it was difficult for them to understand the things that were revealed to them. 

God spoke many times to Abraham, the father of Israel, giving him a great message, but he did not understand how mankind in the ages to come would be blessed through his seed, the history of Israel for centuries was involved.

During the decline of the Kingdom of Israel there was much for the people to learn. It was as the lesson 

God taught to Jeremiah as he watched the potter at his wheel, often it was necessary to remold the clay into a new and more perfect form. Out of the experience of 

Israel as a nation of the ancient world they had not emerged as a righteous people. So now the great Cre-

ator must take His people and in another way mold them to their destiny in His eternal plans. 

To the prophets of Israel God revealed many things during the last days of the nation, of the true meaning of faith and obedience, of worship and righteousness, of rewards and punishments, and of His love for all mankind. Sometimes even the prophets failed to understand the things that were revealed to them but as a patient loving father, God continued to inspire, direct, and counsel them until they had learned the messages they were to deliver to His children. 

JONAH-Book of Jonah 

"Go ye into all the world and preach the gospel to every creature," was the command of Jesus, the 

Savior, unto His disciples.

The Hebrews thought of God, the Eternal Father, as theirs alone, and they thought of themselves as His chosen people.

The time came when God needed to teach the people of Israel that all mankind were in very deed His children, and that it was their mission to carry the knowledge of the Lord to all people. 

Up to this time the prophets had spoken only to Israel. So when God spoke to Jonah, a prophet of 

Judah in the time of King Jeroboam, the second, and commanded him to go to the people of Nineveh and warn them of their wickedness, we can understand the thoughts that came to the prophet; Nineveh was the great city of the Assyrians, the people were heathens, and they had no place in God's kingdom. 

Jonah did not go to Nineveh; instead he went to the seaport of Joppa and took a ship sailing for Tarshish. This foolish, wilful prophet, did he think that there was any place to escape from the presence of the Lord?

It was not long before a great storm arose and a terrific wind tossed the ship about as if it would break it to pieces. The sailors in their terror prayed to their gods to save them. Then they came to Jonah asleep in the ship that he might call upon his God for help saying: "What meanest thou, O sleeper? Arise, call upon thy God, that we perish not."

Then they cast lots being superstitious men, to know what had brought the trouble upon them. The lot fell to Jonah. The sailors asked him, saying: "Whence comest thou? Of what people art thou? 

What evil hast thou done that this storm followeth thee?"

Then Jonah told them his story and how he was seeking to escape from God. Now the sailors became more terrified than ever and asked Jonah what they might do to turn away the storm. Jonah found his courage and said to them: "Take me up and cast me unto the sea; for I know that for my sake this tempest is upon you.'

At first the sailors refused to do as Jonah requested, fearing to take the life of a prophet. But the storm grew worse and more terrible. Then they cast Jonah into the sea. Immediately the storm ceased. 

The Lord had prepared a great fish to swallow

Jonah. When three days had passed the disobedient prophet called upon the Lord for mercy and he was placed once more upon dry land. Then God spoke to 

Jonah and said a second time: "Arise, go to Nineveh, and preach unto it the preaching that I bid thee."

This time Jonah went to Nineveh, the great city of the Assyrians. We can imagine the wonder of Jonah at the things he saw in this city, one of the greatest of the ancient world; the temples of the gods of the sun and the moon and the stars, the palaces adorned with

statues and walls covered with brightly colored carvings telling of their kings and their great conquests; and the houses, streets, and shops crowded with peoples he had never heard of before. But Jonah was not afraid as he went through the city calling upon the people to repent and warning them that in forty days their city was to be overthrown.

The astonishment of Jonah must have been very great indeed when the King of Nineveh, upon hearing the words of the prophet, issued a proclamation to his people saying: "Let neither man nor beast taste anything. Let man cry mightily unto God; yea, let them turn everyone from his evil way, and from the violence that is in their hand. Who can tell if God will repent and turn away from His fierce anger, that we perish not?"

The King took off his royal robes and put on sackcloth and fasted with his people and the Lord God seeing the sincerity of the King and the people of Nineveh, spared them from suffering and the city from destruction. 

Instead of Jonah being thankful for the success of his mission he was angry with God for sparing the city; he wanted to see the heathens punished because he hated them so much. He was so angry that he asked 

God to take his life. "O Jehovah!" he cried, "did I not dread this from the first? Was this not why I fled to Tarshish?” 

But God answered him quietly, "Doest thou well to be angry?" And 

Then Jonah left the city to see what might happen, hoping that even yet disaster might come. as the sulky prophet sat there the Lord caused a wild vine with a gourd upon it to grow up to shelter him from the heat of the sun. Even now Jonah remained angry.

Soon, however, an insect came and injured the

gourd and the vine and it withered away and died. Then their came a hot wind from the desert and beat upon the prophet's head until he fainted from exhaustion. 

Again he asked that he might die. "Doest thou well to be angry about the withering of the gourd?" asked Jehovah. 

And Jonah answered, "I do well to be angry, even to the death." "Thou hast had pity on the gourd," said the Lord; "for the which thou hast not labored, neither madest it to grow; which came up in a night and perished in a night. And should not I spare Nineveh, that great city wherein are more than six score thousand persons in it who know not their right hand from their left?"

We are not told the answer Jonah made to the question God asked.

The Book of Jonah has been called a missionary story by some; by others a parable. The message of the experiences of Jonah is clear and unmistakable, however. Israel had to learn that God is the Father of all mankind, that all men are brothers and share alike in God's love and mercy.

But Israel did not learn this lesson, for if they had done so they would have become God's missionaries to the world. "For the love of God is broader than the measure of man's mind, And the heart of the Eternal is most wonderfully kind; 

And we make His love too narrow by false limits of our own

And we magnify His strictness by a zeal He will not own."

A PROPHET QUESTIONS GOD-Book of Habakkuk 

Habakkuk, the Hebrew prophet, lived in the last days of the Kingdom of Judah.

All that Josiah had done for the independence of his people had been undone. The people who had listened to the good king and the prophet Jeremiah and had forsaken their evil ways saw their enemies invading the land once more. After Josiah was killed his son was carried away in chains by Necho, the King of Egypt. Jehoiakim, the second son of Josiah, was made the ruler over Judah and was forced to pay tribute to Assyria. But what was more Jehoiakim was not a righteous man and soon injustice and oppression were rife in the land. However there were many who remained faithful and they cried out in distress to the servants of God: "We did right and still we suffer. Josiah reigned justly over us and he was killed. Why is this evil come to us?"

The prophet Habakkuk was bewildered by the questions and pled with the people to have faith in 

God's promises. 1 But many of the people did not obey the counsel of the prophet and turned to the worship of idols as they had done in the days of Manasseh.

As the prophet pondered over the problems of the faithful in the land of Judah he became more and more perplexed. Then he called upon God to give him the reasons for all that was happening to the people of 

Israel: 

"I cry out unto thee of violence; why are spoiling and violence before me? Why do the wicked beset the righteous? "Why do Chaldeans, that bitter and impetuous nation, march through the breadth of the earth to possess dwelling places that are not theirs?" 

Then Habakkuk watched and waited to see what answer was given to him for in his sacred record we read: "I will stand upon my watch and will set me upon

the tower and will look out to see what He will speak to me.' "And the Lord answered me and said, 'Write the vision and make it plain that a man may read it swiftly, for the vision is yet for the appointed time and it declareth of the end, and doth not lie. Behold, his soul is puffed up, it is not upright in him; but the righteous shall live by faith.'

Now the testimony of the prophet is strengthened for he knows that the arrogant and powerful tyrants who are enslaving Israel will perish, but that the righteous among Israel will endure. He knows that the destruction of the Kingdom of Judah is inevitable but that the work of the Lord will not be destroyed. 

Then the prophet prays that the Lord will renew

His loving kindness to His children, all in His own good time, as He did in the days of old. 

In a song of faith, understanding, and trust the record of the prophet closes: "For though the fig tree shall not blossom, Neither shall fruit be in the vines; 

The labor of the olive shall fail, And the fields shall yield no meat;

The flock shall be cut off from the fold, And there shall be no herd in the stalls: 

Yet I will rejoice in the Lord, I will joy in the God of my salvation."

The rejoicing in the soul of Habakkuk came from his understanding that the great purposes of God extend through the ages; that the events of the day and the hour are but part of a Divine plan and are not understandable to man whose vision is limited to but a meagre glance at that which has passed and of the things that are to come he knows nothing. He knew that it is for those who love God to stand and wait with faith in His truth, in His justice, and in His mercy.

JOB AND THE PROBLEM OF SUFFERING-Book of Job 

The exile of the children of Israel from the land of their inheritance and from the Temple in Jerusalem was a great sorrow. To those who had lived in obedience to the sacred and tribal laws of the Hebrews it appeared unjust and difficult to understand. It was true that many had been unfaithful to the worship of Jehovah and many had been very wicked. But the conquerors were idol worshipers and exceedingly wicked. 

Now it seemed that the teachings of their prophets had been reversed, instead of the wicked being punished and the righteous being rewarded, it appeared that the wicked prospered and that the righteous perished. Was this God's law?

The great problem that puzzled Israel through the suffering of exile and the years that were to follow was a problem that has troubled the minds of men and women in all ages of the world, the problem of human suffering. Why do people suffer? Into the answers to the question that have come to men there has been a shadowing of blame or punishment. Then came another problem, the source of the punishment. Does 

God punish evil doing with misfortune and suffering? 

Does God also reward righteousness with good fortune and prosperity?

The Book of Job was written by a writer who sought an answer to this great human problem. The message of a nameless Jew has challenged all who read his words because of the eternal truths they revealed and because of the beauty of their expression. 

The writer tells us:

There was a man in the land of Uz, whose name was Job, and that man was perfect and upright, and one who feared God, and turned away from evil.

Job had been greatly blessed; he had a goodly family of seven sons and three daughters; he had seven thou-

sand sheep and three thousand camels, five hundred yoke of oxen, and five hundred she asses, and a great household of servants.

In fact Job was the greatest man in all the East.

The family of Job often held festival together with feasting and merriment. And Job fearing lest his children might have been careless or done wrong in their gladness he made offerings and prayers for them unto God.

Now there came a time when the angels came to present themselves before the Lord and Satan, the Adversary, was among them.

The Lord said to the fallen angel: "Whence comest thou?" "From going to and fro in the earth," Satan answered. "Hast thou considered my servant Job?" said the
