Lord, "that there is none like him in the earth; a perfect and an upright man, one that feareth God, and turneth away from evil?" 

Satan answered: "Doth Job fear God for nought? Hast thou not made a hedge about him, and about his house, and about all that he hath on every side? Thou hast blessed the work of his hands, and his substance is increased in the land. But put forth Thy hand now and touch all that he hath, and he will curse Thee to Thy face." "Behold, all that Job hath, I give into thy power,' the Lord answered. "Only upon himself put not thine hand." ??

Satan went forth from the presence of God to prove the integrity of Job.

One day as Job sat in his house a servant came to him with the tidings: "A band of raiders fell upon thy servants and slew them with the sword and took the oxen that were plowing and the asses feeding beside them.'

Even while the servant was speaking another came and said: "God's lightning fell from heaven and burned up the sheep and the shepherds." 

Then a third messenger came to the presence of

Job and said: "The Chaldeans have come and taken thy camels and killed thy servants.'

But the worst tidings of all were brought by a fourth messenger saying: "Thy sons and thy daughters were making merry in the house of their eldest brother and behold there came a great wind from the wilderness and smote the four corners of the house and it fell upon them and they are dead.

Then Job rose up and in his great sorrow rent his garments. Bowing low to the ground before God he said: "The Lord gave and the Lord taketh away; blessed be the name of the Lord."

And Jehovah watching the suffering of his servant Job as the trials of Satan's proving came to him, sawthat His servant did not murmur or speak foolishly against God.

Once more the angels came to the presence of Jehovah and Satan came with them. Because he was not satisfied with the integrity of Job, Satan was given permission to test Job by bringing bodily suffering upon him. 

Going forth from the presence of Jehovah Satan smote Job with boils from head to foot. When the wife of Job saw his great suffering she exhorted him to curse God and die. But Job said unto her: "Thou speakest as one of the foolish women speaketh. Shall we receive good at the hand of God, and shall we not receive evil?"

The word of the misfortune and suffering that had come to Job came to his friends, and three of them, Eliphaz, Bildad, and Zophar, came to him to comfort him. And when they saw him covered with a loathsome

disease seated outside the city they wept to see the suffering of one who had been so rich and powerful. For seven days and nights they sat by him but spoke no words to him because the suffering of Job was so very great.

At last Job spoke, cursing the day he was born, questioning why life should be given to man if it be full of misery and suffering, and pleading that he be allowed to die and be at peace. Now the friends of 

Job who sat with him began to speak. Eliphaz rebuked Job for being overcome with his suffering and accept it as a punishment from God for his own wicked- Then Bildad spoke and then Zophar counseling Job to accept his punishment as less than he deserved and to repent of his evil ways. The friends dwelt at great length upon the goodness, justice, and wisdom of God as it is understood by the Hebrews. But their words had neither humility nor sincerity in them, they were merely words without knowledge. ness.

But Job was no comforted by the words of his friends for he knew in his heart that he had not done that which was evil in the sight of the Lord. And he answered them saying: "Teach me and I will hold my tongue; and cause me to understand. No doubt but ye are the people, and wisdom shall die with you. Yet I have an understanding as well as you. Have not I seen, have ye not seen, how the wicked prosper in their wickedness? “Though God slay me, yet will I trust in Him; but I will maintain my own ways before him."

A second and a third time the friends of Job pled with him to confess his sin that perchance God would forgive him and take away his suffering. And Job answers them: "How long will ye vex my soul and break me in pieces with words? Are ye not ashamed that ye deal hardly with me."

Then Job thinks of the days when God showered

His blessings upon him and tries to prove to friends that he has done his best: "I have not withheld the poor from their desire, or have caused the eyes of the widow to fail, or have eaten my food alone and the fatherless have not eaten thereof. I have not seen any perish for want of clothing, neither have I done any injury to the fatherless. Let

God weigh me in a fair balance and He shall know mine integrity."

The friends of Job thinking that Job's righteousness was only in his own eyes left him alone. 

Then Elihu, who had listened to much of the wisdom of the friends, spoke to Job, condemning him because he had defended himself instead of confessing his sins. Elihu bade Job to humble himself before the Lord and not rebel any longer. He tried to help Job to understand that God had many ways unknown to man to teach them His ways, by dreams and visions, by suffering, pain, and sickness, and by poverty and disaster. 

Then he condemns Job because he has spoken without knowledge, he has rebelled before he had understanding. 

When Job was left alone once more, there came a mighty rushing wind and God spoke to Job asking him many questions: "Where wast thou when I laid the foundations of the earth, when the morning stars sang together and all the angels shouted for joy? "Hast thou perceived the breadth of the earth? "Where is the way the light dwelleth? As for the darkness, where is place thereof? "Can'st thou send the lightnings that they may go, and say unto thee, 'Here we are?' 

Then Job cried out: "I have uttered that which I understood not. I have heard of Thee by the hearing of the ear but now mine eye hath seen Thee."

In the joy of the great understanding that had

come to him, he acknowledged the great truth that he had learned: "I know that my Redeemer liveth and that He shall stand at the latter day upon the earth, and though this body shall be destroyed, I shall in my flesh see God." 

Then Jehovah spoke to the friends of Job saying: "My wrath is kindled against you, for ye have not spoken of Me the thing that is right as my servant Job hath." 

The friends of Job came to him and they offered sacrifices to the Lord and Job prayed for them that their great sin in judging the ways of the Lord might be forgiven.

The trial of Job was. over and Jehovah blessed his servant with more than he had at the beginning. 

The message of the Book of Job is not an answer to the great problem of human suffering, it is the lesson which Job learned, to have faith in the ways of God, faith that trusts God in all things and does not insist on an understanding of His great mysteries. 

The children of Israel had to learn that the love of God is not a problem of reward, it is as Jesus taught men in the Great Commandment: "Thou shalt love the Lord, thy God, with all thy soul."

RIGHTEOUS LIVING IN TIMES OF CRISES 

The Comforters of Israel in Exile 

THE story of the Exile of Israel in Babylonia, the kingdom of Nebuchadnezzar, is not one of physical suffering, rather is it a period of exile, not a period of bondage. In fact many of the exiles under the advantages of the larger opportunities in the new environment became wealthy and prosperous. The suffering came through an isolation from the homeland and loved ones and from the worship of the Lord God in the Temple at Jerusalem. The knowledge of the destruction of 

Jerusalem and the House of the Lord added greatly to the feeling of complete exile from God and from their inheritance, the Land of Promise. 

But Israel in exile was not deserted. They had the testimony of the prophets of the Lord of the restoration of Jerusalem after a period of seventy years. Also the prophets and the servants of God were with them during the Exile bringing comfort and counsel to them and assuring them of God's mercy and loving care. 

Looking at the history of the people of Israel the Exile seemed to be a period of defeat. But it turned out to be a great victory. The victory of the spirit of 

God protecting and cleansing a chastened people in preparation for a great purpose, to help to establish the spiritual kingdom of God on earth.

At first the exiles gave way to lamentation and sighing for deliverance. But the words of comfort and

counsel of the prophets Daniel and Ezekiel helped them to remain faithful until the time appointed for their deliverance.

The miracle of the Exile was the religious faith of Israel that grew out of captivity and suffering. 

THE EXILE IN BABYLONIA-Ezekiel 1:3 

Ten thousand captives were led out of the city of Jerusalem under guard of the soldiers of the King of Babylon. It was Nebuchadnezzar's way of bringing the city into submission. But what was very significant the captives had been chosen carefully, the wealthy, the traders, the skilled craftsmen, and the warriors were among them for the pride of the conqueror of Judah was very great.

It was a long, hard journey for the grief-stricken captives. For three months they traveled on foot following the same trail that Abraham had taken from the land of the Chaldeans. At length the golden city of 

Babylon lay before them, a city of brightly colored pyramid-shaped temples, of immense statues of bulls and lions made of bronze, of paved streets lined with shops, and of ships tied up in the river docks. But the greatest wonder of all was the great palace of Nebuchadnezzar surrounded by the Hanging Gardens, a mountain of trees and flowers and fountains built to please his queen, a princess of Medea who longed for the hills of her native land in this level city of the plains of the twin rivers.

Over all the splendor of Babylon the Temple of Bel, the great god, towered. It was beautiful and strange, the colored terraces named for the planets of heaven with the golden altar on the pinnacle. But the memory of the beloved Temple on Mount Zion in Jerusalem was not dimmed for those who had partaken of its holiness.

Perhaps the greatest surprise of all came in the treatment afforded to the captives. Nebuchadnezzar wished the Jews, as he called the captives from Judah, to bring credit to his country so he gave them the opportunity of living as citizens, not as slaves. As a result many of them settled along the river Chebar near the city of Nippur, building homes and villages and following their own occupations. In time many began to prosper in this land of opportunity, and many became wealthy.

But the strange land was a place of exile in spite of its glory and its wealth. The hearts of the captives turned towards the homeland. Those who were faithful kept the Sabbath and worshiped God and were not influenced by the worship of the heathen gods of the 

Babylonians. A new note of hope and expectation came with the message of Jeremiah, that the power of the oppressor could not endure for ever, then they would be free once more.

Now more than ever did they strive to keep themselves a separate people, to preserve their racial purity. 

In the days of exile there arose among the people some of Israel's sweetest singers. Under the burden of their sorrow they sang: "By the rivers of Babylon,

There we sat down, yea we wept 

When we remembered Zion.

For there they that led us captive required of us songs, And they that wasted us required of us mirth, 'Sing us one of the songs of Zion.' 

How shall we sing the Lord's song 

In a strange land? 

If I forget thee, O Jerusalem, Let my right hand forget her cunning, Let my tongue cleave to the roof of my mouth, If I remember thee not:

If I prefer not Jerusalem.

O daughter of Babylon, that art to be destroyed; 

Happy shall he be that rewardeth thee 

As thou hast served us.'

From time to time the exiles heard the news of their own people left in Jerusalem and in Judah from the traders who went back and forth between Egypt and Babylonia.

EZEKIEL, THE WATCHMAN OF THE CAPTIVITY 

-Ezekiel 3. 27

The exiles were not left without God's guidance in the days of captivity. Ezekiel, a disciple of Jeremiah, and other priests and elders of Israel were with them.

It was Ezekiel, however, who became the chief comforter of the exiles during the first days of building a new home in Babylonia. He went about among the little settlements encouraging and comforting them. 

When the message came from the prophet Jeremiah counseling the people to live in peace, to make homes and cities in the new land, he did all he could to help them to understand and obey the counsel.

But there were those who were restless and resentful and they believed that they would soon be able to return to Jerusalem. In fact some of these restless people believed that those left in Jerusalem would be able to overcome the servants of Nebuchadnezzar and gain their freedom, then the exiles would be able to return. 

Ezekiel was gravely concerned about this rebellious spirit among the people in exile because the prophet Jeremiah had revealed the words given to him that the Lord would not restore the exiles to their own land - until seventy years had passed. Also, Ezekiel knew that if the captives in Babylonia followed the counsel

of Jeremiah they could be happy and contented in their new home.

One day as Ezekiel walked along the river Chebar a remarkable vision came to him. The heavens opened before him and a great cloud of brightness came forth.

From amidst the cloud four living beings came each like a man with four faces, the face of a man, the face of a lion, the face of an ox, and the face of an eagle; representing man with four great gifts the light of truth, the courage of a lion, the strength of an ox, and the power of an eagle to soar above the world. In this vision Ezekiel learned to understand the spirit of the Lord that was given to man as he saw the creatures soar between the heaven and the earth. 

Then there appeared in the cloud of brightness a figure representing the glory of God. In the sight of this great glory Ezekiel bowed to the earth. Then he heard a voice saying: "Son of man, stand on thy feet and I will speak to thee."

When Ezekiel arose the voice spoke further to him calling him to God's service for the people of Israel. But 

Ezekiel hesitated to begin his work at once and pondered over it for seven days. Then the voice of the Lord came to him again saying: "I have made thee a watchman unto the house of

Israel; therefore hear the word at my mouth and give them warning from me."

Then Ezekiel was given instructions as to how he should proceed to watch over the people of Israel in exile that they might be guarded from evil doing, from the dangers of following false counselors, and from the influence of the idolatry of Babylon. 

Day after day Ezekiel went among his people encouraging them to be faithful and to prepare themselves to take up the building of God's kingdom on earth when

the time came for them to be released from captivity to return to Jerusalem.

To impress this message upon the exiles Ezekiel loaded all his possessions on his shoulders. Carrying this burden on his back as the people had done as they left Judah, Ezekiel went from place to place speaking to his people: "Thus saith the Lord to you who are now in Babylon, I will put a new spirit within you that ye may be my people and I may be your God."

The word came to the exiles in Babylonia that Jerusalem had been destroyed. Before many months the rebellious king who had brought down the anger of Nebuchadnezzar upon the city in spite of the warnings of the prophet Jeremiah and was brought in chains to Babylon along with thousands of captives. 

Now it seemed as if the sorrow of Israel over the destruction of their beloved city and God's holy temple was almost too great to bear. Then Ezekiel gathered all of his people together and revealed to them a wonderful vision that had come to him.

God had taught Ezekiel in a vision how Israel might be revived and live again. He led Ezekiel into a valley covered with the bones of a large army killed in battle. The voice of God spoke to the prophet saying: "Son of man, can these bones live?" 

Ezekiel naswered, "O Lord God, thou knowest.' "Prophesy upon these bones," the voice spoke again, “and say unto them, 'O ye dry bones, hear the word of the Lord.. Behold, I will cause breath to enter into you and ye shall live; and ye shall know that I am the Lord!''

Ezekiel did as he was commanded and even as he spake the bones came together and were clothed with flesh and skin. Then the voice spoke again saying:

"Say to the wind, 'Thus saith the Lord God, breathe upon these slain, that they might live.'

As Ezekiel obeyed this command life came into all the dead men and they arose, a great living army. 

Also God instructed Ezekiel to say unto the people after he had told them about the vision he had received: "Behold, Judah is now a heap of dry bones in the midst of the valley. But the spirit of God as the breath of wind shall enter these bones, they shall rise and live. 

For thus saith the Lord, 'Ye shall dwell again in the land that I gave unto your fathers.'

For many years longer Ezekiel labored to keep the hearts of his people turned towards God. Many visions came to the prophet during these years concerning the return to Jerusalem and of the rebuilding of the Temple. 

As Ezekiel stood as a watchman guarding his people he did more than comfort and encourage them concerning the return to Jerusalem and the building of a new Temple, he taught them the law of individual responsibility. For the words of the Lord came to Ezekiel saying: "What mean ye when ye say, 'The father's have eaten sour grapes and the children's teeth are on edge?' "Behold all souls are mine; as the soul of the father, so also the soul of the son is mine. When the son hath done that which is lawful and right, and kept all my statutes, and hath done them, he shall surely live. The soul that sinneth, it shall die. The son shall not bear the iniquity of the father, neither shall the father bear the iniquity of the son. The righteousness of the righteous shall be upon him, and the wickedness of the wicked shall be upon him. 

It was by the teachings of Ezekiel and those who came after him that hope was kept alive in the hearts of the exiles through the long years of captivity.

FOUR HEBREW YOUTH IN EXILE-Daniel 1 

Nebuchadnezzar saw much to admire in the people he had brought to Babylonia as captives from Judah. 

One day as he went abroad in the land he was attracted by the manliness of the Hebrew youths he saw in the settlements of the exiles. Later he called unto him his chief servant, the Master of the Household, and said to him: "Choose from among the captives some youths who are well favored to look upon and skillful in wisdom that they might be educated and trained for my royal service.

Daniel, a noble youth and a descendant of the kings of Judah, was chosen along with three others whose names were changed to Shadrack, Meshach, and Abednego. Daniel's name was changed also. He was called Belteshazzar.

During the period of training the youths were to eat of the same food as was set at the King's table. This was a great challenge to the young men for according to the law of Moses only certain foods may be eaten and they must be prepared with God's blessing. But 

Daniel spake up courageously to the Master of the Household asking that they be not forced to eat the food which in the law of Moses was forbidden. 

But the King's servant answered: "My lord hath appointed your meat and drink. If he should see you unhealthy and different from the others ye shall endanger my head to the King."

The youths were not to be so easily turned away from obedience to the laws of their people, so they persuaded the steward who had charge of the food to let them eat herbs and fruit and drink water for ten days. "Then," said Daniel, "let our countenances be looked upon, and the countenances of the ones that eat

of the food of the King's table; and as thou seest, deal with us."

Because the Master of the Household had grown to admire Daniel for his honor and his integrity he permitted the steward to allow the youths to have their way. After ten days it was to be seen that the faces of the Hebrew youths were fairer and healthier than those who had partaken of the rich food and wine of the King's table.

Now the youths had their way in obeying the laws of Moses and keeping the commandments of God. They were faithful to the teachings of Israel. As the years of training passed they excelled in knowledge and in wisdom and were looked upon with respect by the wise men of Babylon.

THE DREAM OF NEBUCHADNEZZAR-Daniel 2 

There came a day when Nebuchadnezzar became sorely troubled over a strange dream that had come to him in the night but could not recall when he waked. 

He called all the wise men of Chaldea to his palace and said unto them: "I have dreamed a dream and my spirit is troubled to know its meaning." 

The wise men answered, “O King, live forever. 

Tell thy servants the dream, and we will show thee the interpretation." 

But the King said, "The dream is gone from me. 

If ye can tell me the dream and its meaning great shall be your reward and honor, but if ye tell it not to me ye shall be slain."

The wise men of Chaldea were sore afraid and they said to the King: "Never hath any king asked such a thing of his wise men. None can do it save it be the gods whose dwelling is not in the flesh."

Now the King in great anger ordered that all the wise men of Babylon be slain. So the soldiers of Nebuchadnezzar came to slay Daniel and his companions, because of their training they were called wise. But 

Daniel begged the captain of the guard to allow him to see the King. When he came before Nebuchadnezzar

Daniel promised that at an appointed time he would show him the dream and its interpretation. Then the four Hebrew youths went alone in secret and prayed to God that the dream would be revealed to them. 

That night the dream of the King of Babylon came to Daniel in a vision. And David arose and kneeled in thanksgiving before God: "Blessed be the name of God forever for wisdom and might are His. I thank Thee, Thou God of my fathers who hast given me wisdom and might, for thou hast made known unto us the King's matter." 

At the appointed time Daniel was brought into the presence of Nebuchadnezzar. Then he spoke of that which had been revealed to him: "The secret which the King hath demanded can neither wise men, magicians, or soothsayers show unto the King; but there is a God in heaven that revealeth secrets and He hath made known to thee what shall be in the latter days. "Thou, O King, sawest in thy dream a mighty image whose brightness was dazzling and whose form thereof was terrible. The head of the image was of gold, his breasts and arms of silver, his belly and thighs of brass, his legs of iron, and his feet part of iron and part of clay. "Thou sawest a stone, cut out without hands, which smote the image upon his feet, and brake them in pieces. There were the iron, the clay, the brass, the silver and the gold, broken in pieces together, and became like the chaff of the summer threshing floors and the wind carried them away that no place was found

for them. But the stone that smote the image became a great mountain, and filled the whole earth." 

The King was astonished at the words of Daniel for it was as he had dreamed. But the youth continued to speak: "This then, O King, is the meaning of thy dream:

Thou art the head of gold that hath built up a mighty kingdom. After thee there shall arise a kingdom less than thine; then a third kingdom of brass which shall rule over all the earth; and there shall be a fourth kingdom strong as iron, which breaketh all things, but mixed with weakness as the iron was with the clay. "Then shall the God of Heaven set up a kingdom which shall never be destroyed; it shall be the stone hewn without hands, and shall break in pieces all other kingdoms; but it shall stand forever."

The King was humbled in his heart that the Spirit of God in his servant Daniel had revealed the secret unto him. "Of a truth," said the King, "your God is a God of gods, and a Lord of kings, and a revealer of secrets.' 

Nebuchadnezzar honored Daniel by making him the ruler over the province of Babylon, and over all the wise men. And because Daniel requested it Shadrach,

Meshach, and Abednego were appointed to assist him. 

THE LOYALTY OF THE HEBREW YOUTHS-Daniel 3 

The power of Nebuchadnezzar was very great but he dreamed of becoming the emperor of all the kingdoms of the world. Much of the greatness that had come to him he believed was through the power of the god Mepodach, whom he worshiped faithfully in the magnificent temple in Babylon.

It seemed that after the strange dream and its interpretation by Daniel that the great King of Babylon set about making himself so powerful that the breaking

up of his kingdom as foretold could not happen. The first thing he did was to have a great image of himself in gold set up on the plains of Dura outside the city that all might see it. There it stood towering to the skies glittering in the sunshine ninety feet high.

When the time had come for the dedication of the great image all the chief servants of the vast empire were summoned. Princes, governors, captains, judges, treasurers, counselors, and rulers of the provinces were assembled on the plain to await the coming of the dawn. 

When the first rays of light touched the crest of the golden image a herald cried out in a loud voice: "To you it is commanded, O peoples, nations, and languages, that at the time ye hear the sound of the trumpet, flute, harp, sackbut, psaltery, dulcimer, and all kinds of music ye shall fall down and worship the golden image that Nebuchadnezzar the King hath set up, and whoso falleth not down and worshippeth, shall the same hour be cast into the midst of a burning fiery furnace."

There among the vast assembly were the three Hebrew youths who were the servants of the state. The words of the herald brought terror to their hearts. As they looked into one another's faces they knew without speaking the course they would take, the loyal children of Israel could not bow down and worship the golden image of an earthly king.

Now the sound of music broke upon the air and the people assembled bowed down in worship, all save three; these remained standing firm and straight with their eyes bright and unflinching. Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego remained faithful to the only true God, the God of Abraham.

After the ceremony was over the priests of the Chaldeans went before the King and reported what they had seen, saying: "O King, live forever! There are certain Jews

whom thou hast set over the affairs of Babylon, these men, O King, have not obeyed thee. They serve not thy gods, nor will they worship the golden image which thou hast set up.'

Nebuchadnezzar in great rage ordered that Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego be brought before him. "Is it true," he cried, "that ye do not serve my gods, nor worship the image which I have set up. Ye have still another chance, fall down and worship when the trumpets sound again. Then, if ye fall not down and worship the image, ye shall be cast the same hour into the burning fiery furnace. And who is that God that shall deliver you out of my hands?"

When the Hebrew youths heard the challenge the King had made to the God they worshiped they answered him without flinching: "O Nebuchadnezzar, we have no need to answer thee in this matter. The God whom we serve is able to deliver us from the burning fiery furnace and He will deliver us out of thy hand. But if not, be it known unto thee, O King, that we shall not serve thy gods, nor worship the golden image which thou hast set up. 

The King, seized with a terrible fury, demanded his servants to heat the great furnace used for the smelting of metals to seven times its usual heat and to cast the 

Hebrew youths therein.

When all was ready the guards bound the three and cast them into the burning furnace. So great was the heat that issued forth that the flames of the fire burned those that threw the Hebrew youths into its midst.

An angel of the Lord came and stood by the youths staying the power of the fire to hurt them and speaking words of comfort to them.

The King seeing the figures of four men in the flames of the furnace called aloud:

"Did not we cast three men bound into the midst of the fire? Lo, I see four men walking loose in the midst of the fire and they are not hurt.' "True, O King," the servants answered. 

Then Nebuchadnezzar came near to the furnace and cried out: "Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego, ye servants of the most high God, come forth and come hither." 

The three youths came forth from the burning furnace and the King and his counselors gathered around them and found that the fire had had no power to burn them, not even the hair of their heads were singed or did their garments smell of the fire. Nebuchadnezzar spoke to those assembled and said: "Blessed be the God of Shadrach, Meshach, and 

Abednego, who hath sent His angel, and delivered His servants that trusted in Him. Therefore, I make a decree that any people or nation which speaks anything amiss against their God shall be destroyed; because there is no other god that can deliver them."

The King promoted the faithful Hebrews to positions of great trust in the kingdom. As these servants of God went abroad in the land among their own people, the exiles from Judah, they were a living testimony of the presence of the Lord God among them in captivity. Also after the decree of the King the exiles were free to worship God as they desired and none dared to persecute them.

The faith of the children of Israel grew stronger as the days passed with the new courage that had come to them through Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego. 

NEBUCHADNEZZAR'S PRIDE HUMBLED-Daniel 4 

On two occasions the great King of Babylon had seen the power of the God of Israel manifest. But it seemed that even now the proud monarch could not for-

get his own greatness and his desire to be as a god. Still another warning was to come to him. 

Another dream came to Nebuchadnezzar, which like the first filled him with great fear and which likewise the wise men of Babylon were unable to interpret. Then 

Daniel was summoned to the presence of the King. "O Belteshazzar," the King said, "tell me the meaning of the dream, the visions of which trouble me greatly. I beheld a tree in the midst of the earth; and the height thereof was great. The tree grew, and was strong, and the height thereof reached unto heaven and the sight thereof to the end of all the earth. Its leaves were fair, and it bore fruit for all in plenty; the beasts of the field lay down in its shadow and the birds of the air dwelt in its branches. "Then a voice from the heavens cried aloud, 'Hew down the tree and cut off its branches, shake off its leaves, and scatter its fruit; drive the beasts from beneath it, and the birds from its branches. Nevertheless leave the stump of its roots in the earth, bound with a band of brass and iron, among the tender grass of the field and let it be wet with the dew of heaven until time be passed sevenfold.'' 

Then Daniel, whose name was Belteshazzar, was greatly troubled for the interpretation of the King's dream was of evil omen. But Nebuchadnezzar demanded that Daniel speak all that had been revealed to him.

After Daniel had explained to the King the meaning of each vision he continued to speak words of counsel: "O King, the Most High ruleth the kingdom of men, and giveth it to whomsoever He will. 

I pray thee, let my counsel be acceptable unto thee, and break off thy sins by righteousness and thine iniquities by showing mercy to the poor that there may be a lengthening of the tranquility."

But at the end of a year in spite of the warning of Daniel not to set himself up as greater than God, Nebuchadnezzar walked upon the roof of his palace and saw the glories of the great city and he said in his pride: "Is not this mighty Babylon which I have built by the might of my power and for the glory of my majesty?"

While the words were still on his lips a voice came from heaven, and these were the words: "O King Nebuchadnezzar, to thee it is spoken!

The kingdom is departed from thee. They shall drive thee from among men, and thy dwelling shall be with the beasts of the field and grass shalt thou eat. Time shall pass over thee sevenfold, until thou shalt learn that the Most High giveth the kingdom to whom He will."

In that same hour madness fell upon the great king and he fell to the earth and began eating the grass of the field. Then the people drove him out from among them. But after seven years his reason returned and he lifted his eyes to heaven and said: "I, Nebuchadnezzar, praise and extol, and honor the King of Heaven, whose works are truth, and His ways are justice. Those who walk in pride, He is able to abase.'

All had happened to the great King of Babylon as Daniel had foretold, his pride and folly brought him to humiliation; but as the stump of the tree was left to be nurtured by the dew of heaven, Nebuchadnezzar was given a chance to repent from his evil ways. Once more the King of Babylon was restored to his kingdom to rule with less pride and more mercy for many years. 

During the remaining years of the reign of Nebuchadnezzar Daniel followed the example of Ezekiel and labored among his people bringing words of counsel and encouragement to them that they might endure and grow strong for the days when their exile would be over.

THE FALL OF BABYLON-Daniel 6, 7 The kings of Babylonia after Nebuchadnezzar were not strong rulers. But a far more significant thing was happening without the kingdom than within. a new empire was arising. The Persians, a strong, small race of many tribes of mountain shepherds became united under a brave young king named Cyrus. Then they went forth and conquered a neighboring people, the Medes. It seemed as though the kings of Babylon enjoying the wealth and glory of Nebuchadnezzar were not mindful of what was happening around them. 

During all these years Daniel had no place in the affairs of the government of Babylon but lived among the exiles as a prophet and teacher following the death of Ezekiel.

It happened that when the month of festival came Belshazzar, the King of Babylon, made a great feast for the princes and lords of the land. For this great occasion he ordered that the gold and silver vessels which 

Nebuchadnezzar brought from the Temple at Jerusalem be used.

During the feast the sacred vessels which had been made for the service of the Lord were used by the drunken revelers of Belshazzar's feast. As they drank they paid tribute to their gods and ridiculed the God of the Hebrews because there were no idols of gold and silver, of brass, iron, wood, or stone for His worship. 

Suddenly amid the feasting and revelry there appeared the fingers of a hand writing upon the wall. Belshazzar saw the hand as it wrote. Terror stricken he called for the priests and the soothsayers and said to them: "Whosoever shall read the writing, and show me the interpretation thereof, shall be clothed in purple and shall have a chain of gold about his neck, and shall become the third ruler of the land."

None of the wise men or the soothsayers could read the writing because it was in a strange language. The 

King and his guests were shaken with fear, it seemed that the mysterious words upon the wall in front of them were words of judgment. Many were stricken speechless while others cried aloud in panic. The mother of the King hearing of the strange happening at the banquet hall sent a message to her son saying: "O King, be not troubled; there is a man in the kingdom in whom is the spirit of the holy gods. In the days of thy forefathers, Nebuchadnezzar made him the chief of the wise men of the land. Send for this man 

Daniel, that he might interpret the writing for thee. 

Daniel was brought and Belshazzar bade him interpret the meaning of the words offering him rich gifts for his services. Daniel answered the King saying: "Keep thy gifts to thyself, O King, and give thy fees to another. In other days Nebuchadnezzar ruled this land in glory and in majesty. All nations feared him. But when his heart was hardened in pride, the 

Most High God took his glory from him. Thou, likewise, Belshazzar, hast lifted up thyself against the Lord of heaven, thou and thy lords have drunk wine from the sacred vessels of His House. Moreover, thou hast praised the gods of gold and silver, of brass, iron, wood, and stone, that neither see, nor hear, nor know. But the God in whose hand thy breath is, and to whom all thy ways belong, hast thou not glorified. The hand was sent from Him, and this writing was written: "Mene, Mene, Tekel, Upharsin. "This is the meaning thereof: Mene, God hath numbered thy kingdom and finished it; Tekel, thou art weighed in the balances and found wanting; Perses, thy kingdom is divided and given to the Medes and Persians." 

That very night the mighty city of Babylon fell because the disloyal priests of Marduk, who were angry

at Belshazzar, opened the gates and let Cyrus, the Persian king, and his armies enter.

When Cyrus became the ruler over the kingdom of Babylon he did not take the people into bondage as the kings of Assyria had done to the people of the Kingdom of Israel and as Nebuchadnezzar had done to the people of Judah. He placed rulers over them and treated them with kindness that they might continue to be contented in their own lands. Over the kingdom of Babylon he placed Darius, the Mede, to rule as a regent in his stead.

DANIEL FACES THE LIONS FOR HIS FAITH—Daniel 6 

A new empire had arisen in the East, the empire of Cyrus, the Persian. Babylon had fallen as the Hebrew prophets, Jeremiah and Ezekiel and others had foretold. Daniel, who came with his parents and the captives of Nebuchadnezzar to Babylon, had seen these strange events and many others during the period of Exile. But the strangest of all was the fulfillment of 

God's promises to His people before the captivity. 

Now in the closing years of his life Daniel had become the trusted servant of Darius, the King placed over Babylon by Cyrus the Persian conqueror. However, when the various rulers of the provinces of Babylon saw that Darius depended a great deal upon the counsel of the loyal Jew, whose task it was to guard the King's treasury, they feared that he might be placed over them as their governor. So they made a plot against him. Going to King Darius they said: "We the princes of Babylon, the rulers of the provinces of the kingdom, have consulted together to establish a royal statute, and to make a decree, that whosoever shall ask a petition of any god or man for thirty days, save of thee, O King, he shall be cast into the den of the lions.

"Now, O King, proclaim and sign the decree, that it be not changed according to the law of the Medes and Persians.'

King Darius, remembering the treachery of the priests who had opened the gates of the city to the armies of Cyrus, thought the decree would serve to maintain order throughout the land, so he established the law. 

When Daniel heard of the decree he knew that he must choose whom he would obey, Darius or the God of Israel. Never faltering Daniel went to his room three times each day as was his custom and with his face turned towards his beloved Jerusalem prayed and gave thanks to God.

Now those who had plotted against Daniel, knowing of his custom to pray each day, came to spy upon him. Breaking in upon him as he knelt in prayer they seized him and took him before the King. "Hast thou not signed the decree, O King," they said, "that every man that shall ask a petition of any god or man within thirty days, save of thee, shall be cast into the den of the lions?"

Darius answered, "This is true according to the law of the Medes and the Persians which altereth not." 

Then said the princes of Babylon: "Daniel, the

Jew, a captive of the exiles of Judah, obeys not thee, O King, nor the decree that thou hast signed, but maketh petition three times each day unto his God."

Darius was greatly distressed when he saw the trap into which he had fallen. All that day he tried to think of some way to save his faithful servant, Daniel, from the penalty of the decree. When he pled with the princes to spare Daniel they answered: "Know, O King, the law of the Medes and Persians is that no decree or statute of the King may be changed."

The King was helpless to do other than the law

demanded. Turning to Daniel who stood brave and still before him Darius said: "Thy God, whom thou servest continually, He will deliver thee.'

Daniel was taken to the large pit where the lions were kept for the popular sport of hunting. The servants of the King saw the loyal Jew walk fearlessly into the den of the lions, and then they saw the King seal the door with his royal signet.

All that night King Darius spent in fasting and prayer. At the break of dawn he hurried to the den and unsealed the door. All was quiet and still, then in great fear the King called out: "O Daniel, servant of the living God, has thy 

God been able to deliver thee from the lions?" 

Then the voice of Daniel replied: "O King, live forever! My God sent his angel and shut the mouths of the lions that they have not hurt me; forasmuch as before Him innocency was found in me and also before thee, O King, have I done no hurt."

The king was grateful that the life of Daniel had been preserved. But to those who had plotted against 

Daniel he brought a swift and severe punishment.

Though they were the princes of Babylon he ordered them all to be cast into the den of the lions at once. 

Then Darius wrote a decree unto all peoples and na tions: 

"Peace be multiplied unto you! I make a decree that in every dominion of my kingdom men tremble and fear before the God of Daniel, for He is the living God. His kingdom shall not be destroyed and His dominion shall be even unto the end. "He delivereth and rescueth, and He worketh signs and wonders in heaven and in earth, Who hath delivered 

Daniel from the power of the lions."

THE VISIONS OF DANIEL OF GOD'S KINGDOM 

-Daniel 7-12

From time to time God gave to Daniel many wonderful visions showing him the things which were to come to pass. He was permitted to see the kingdoms of the earth that would come into being and then pass away. Then he was allowed to see the coming of Jesus, the Son of God, to be the Savior of mankind, His earthly mission, and His crucifixion and resurrection. But he saw that God's kingdom did not pass away in spite of all the evil that was to be brought against it, that it would endure until all mankind would come to know and worship the true and living God. 

But the greatest of all the truths that came to Daniel concerning God's purposes was the knowledge that was given to him that the life of man was eternal, that after the sleep of death man would awaken to everlasting life, those that had lived in righteousness to be clothed with glory and brightness even as the stars for ever and ever.

Daniel wrote of the visions he had received and his understanding of them that there might be a sacred record for his people and for all mankind of the things that 

God in His loving mercy had prepared for His children.

LOYALTY TO FRIENDS, FAMILY, AND PEOPLES 

The Loyalty of David, Jonathan, Ruth, and Esther

THE FRIENDSHIP OF DAVID AND JONATHAN 

-I Samuel 17, 20

ON 

Na hillside near Bethlehem, David, the youngest son of Jesse, was watching the sheep. David had much to think of for the word had come that the Philistines had marched through many of the nearby villages destroying the homes and stealing the flocks. It seemed to the young shepherd that it was so strange that the beautiful land of Canaan should have to be troubled with such cruel enemies. But David's heart was very sad on this morning for he had just watched his brothers go to fight with the army of King Saul. He, too, wanted to fight for Israel, but Jesse, his father, needed him to care for the sheep.

Even while David was dreaming a mountain lion got into the flock on the hillside. But his shepherd's sling was soon in action and the lion was dead. David tried not to forget his work again but daily practiced with his sling so as to guard his sheep better. 

Many weeks passed. Then one morning David's father called his son from the hills and commanded that he go to the valley of Elah twelve miles distant, where the Philistines and the Israelites were in battle to see how his brothers fared and to take them supplies of food.

When David arrived at the field of battle, he found the armies lined up on each side of the valley. Now the outcome of the struggle was to be decided by a single combat and the great Philistine champion, Goliath, was thundering out the challenge to the men of Israel: "I defy the armies of Israel this day: give me a man that we may fight together." 

Over and over again David heard the challenge, but no Israelite volunteered. As the days passed and 

David heard the insults of the champion at the cowardice of the soldiers of Saul he was ashamed and amazed. 

Then one day as he heard the challenge, David asked to be taken to King Saul.

The king of Israel stood before his armies with his son Jonathan by his side when the young shepherd came before him. Bowing low before the king David said: "My lord, let no man's heart fail because of this challenge. I will fight with the Philistine."

Saul gazed in wonder at the youth and then replied: "Thou art little more than a boy, thou art not able to fight. This Philistine has been a soldier all his days." "Thy servant kept his father's flocks," said David simply, "and thy servant has slain both lions and bears when they came to take the lambs. So shall it be with the heathen Philistine. Jehovah who delivered me from the lion and the bear will save me from his hand."

The king was so convinced by David's spirit that he took his own armor and his sword and placed them upon the youth saying to him: thee." "Go forth and the Lord God of Israel be with

But David laid off the heavy armor and the sword Saul had given to him because he knew not how to use them. Instead he took his sling from his shoulder and

LOYALTY 

287 went to a brook for some pebbles. As he gathered them 

David prayed: "O God, help me to defeat this Philistine, that the land be saved for Israel, even as Thou promised it to them."

With his shepherd's sling and five small pebbles, David went and stood before the Philistine. Scorn and rage seized the armor-clad Goliath as he saw the youth who had come against him. "Am I a dog," he shouted, "that thou comest after me thus. I will give thy flesh to the beasts of the field and to the birds of the air." "Thou comest to me," answered David, "with a sword and with a spear and with a shield; but I come to thee in the name of the God of Israel, whom thou hast defied. This day will Jehovah deliver thee unto my hand; that all the earth may know that there is a God in Israel."

When the Philistine came to meet David the youth slipped a stone into the sling and smote Goliath in the forehead. The Philistines saw their champion fall to the ground and as David took his sword, they fled in great dismay. Behind them followed the soldiers of

Israel to the gates of their cities, killing many and carrying away many weapons and much spoil. 

The captain of the King's guard brought David to Saul and the king honored him by making him his armor bearer and ordered that he live in the palace at Gibeah. 

The heart of Jonathan, the son of Saul, had gone out to David as he saw his courage, greater than that of all the armies of Israel. But it was when he heard the words of the youth armed with a sling and a few pebbles that Jonathan rejoiced greatly because he, too, knew that only the God of Israel could defeat the Philistine. Taking his cloak from his shoulders he placed it upon David and gave him also his sword and his

bow. From that day on Jonathan and David were knit together with a love greater even than that of brothers.

It was not long before Saul made David the captain over a thousand men as was Jonathan. Both young men went out into the land of Canaan to seek to protect the people from the plunderings of the Philistines. Word came back to the king from time to time bringing words of praise for the courage and leadership of David. But it was the valiant Jonathan who appreciated his gallant soldier-friend more than any other person. 

When David and Jonathan returned to Gibeah after one of their victories the admiration of Israel for the young captain brought forth great shouts of welcome from the men and songs of praise from the women. 

And when Saul heard the acclaim of Israel for David, it was not gratitude that welled up in the king's heart but jealousy. Then once again an evil moodiness took control of Saul's mind as it had done when Samuel the prophet warned the king of God's displeasure and His intention to give Israel another king. Day after day

Saul sat playing with his javelin. When David brought his harp and sang and played to Saul thinking to soothe him, the evil seemed to turn to madness. Twice Saul threw his javelin at David as he played, and twice David stepped aside in time to miss the weapon of death. Perhaps it was the knowledge that God was with David that made Saul desire to kill him, or perhaps Saul knew in his heart that it was David who had been chosen to rule Israel in due time. How strange that Saul, who knew of the Lord's ways, should think to thwart His plans for His people Israel! 

When Jonathan knew of the evil in his father's heart for David he sought to protect him at all times. 

If Jonathan knew that it was David who was to succeed Saul as King of Israel, there was no jealousy in his heart for he knew without a doubt that God was watching

LOYALTY 

289 over Israel. Once Jonathan spoke up bravely to his father saying: "Thy servant David has done nought to thee but good. He risked his life and slew the Philistine and saved Israel. Now thou wouldst slay him. Sin not so my father."

Saul yielded to the pleading of his son and for a time it seemed that all was well once more at Gibeah. 

But ere long the word came to the palace of more victories over the Philistines by David and his army. Then once more hatred darkened Saul's thoughts and he desired to kill David. Hearing that David had taken refuge with Samuel he sent messengers to capture him there. When the servants of Saul failed to find David. the king went himself to find him. But when he came to the abode of Samuel and heard the words of prayer and worship in the Holy Place, the desire to harm David left him and Saul stayed to worship with Samuel and his young prophets.

In time David's heart longed for the peace and security of his home, for the friendship of Jonathan, and for the favor of Saul so he returned to Gibeah. 

There he sought his friend to know what course to take. 

The two men decided upon a plan whereby they might know if Saul still meant evil against David. 

It was decided that David should be absent from his place at the King's table during the coming feast. 

If Saul was angry on being told that David had returned to Bethlehem for the yearly sacrifice instead of taking his place at the king's table, then the friends would know that all was not well. But if the king accepted the explanation of Jonathan they would know that there was nothing to fear. It was also decided how 

David in hiding should learn of the king's actions. 

So David went to a nearby place to hide. On the first day of the feast Saul did not appear to notice that

David was absent. On the second day, however, the king said: "Why hath David been absent from the feast both yesterday and again today?"

Jonathan explained that David had asked to be allowed to attend the feast with his own people at Bethlehem. Thereupon Saul's fury loosed itself against 

Jonathan: 

"Thou rebel, do I not know that thou art covenanted to this son of Jesse? Dost thou not know that while he lives there is no endurance for thee nor for thy kingdom? Wherefore, now, bring him to me, for he shall surely die." "Why should thou slay him?" cried Jonathan. "What wrong hath he done?" 

Saul answered his son by throwing his javelin at him. But Jonathan, thinking only of David, left the banquet hall in great haste that he might go to his friend to warn him.

Jonathan went to the hiding place of David taking with him a boy carrying a bow and a quiver of arrows. 

When they came to a large mound three arrows were sent through the air. Then David knew of the evil news his friend had brought for the arrows fell beyond the hiding place. Calling to the boy to gather up the arrows and then to return to his home, Jonathan waited for David.

The two men who loved one another so greatly wept bitterly. "Go in peace," said Jonathan sadly. "We have sworn that the Lord shall be between us and between our children for ever.'

David went on his way an outlaw seeking safety wherever he could, and Jonathan returned to his father to stand by and serve him to the death. 

But once more were David and Jonathan to meet. 

It was not long before a band of loyal Israelites

LOYALTY 

291 came to the aid of David. With four hundred brave soldiers he felt that he could go to the aid of his people against the Philistines. After he had brought help to the people Keilah, Saul knew where David and his men were hiding. So he set out to find him. But God warned David to leave the city. As he hid in the nearby hills Jonathan came to him. "Fear not," said Jonathan to the friend he had risked his life to find that he might comfort him. “God will not let my father harm thee. The day will come when thou shalt be king over Israel; and I shall be next to thee."

Once more the two noble friends spoke of the past, the covenants they had made with one another, and of the days when David would be king. They then parted but they were to meet no more. 

Jonathan continued to fight side by side with his father against the Philistines. Then came the great battle at Gilbod and Saul and Jonathan and two other sons were killed.

When David heard the news his sorrow was great beyond description. In his distress he remembered only the greatness of Saul, the King of Israel. But he remembered with gratitude and thanksgiving the beauty and strength of the love of Jonathan. Then into a song of lament David wrote his sorrow and his love: "Thy glory, O Israel, is slain upon thy high places! 

How are the mighty fallen! 

O Jonathan slain upon the high places! 

I am distressed for thee, my brother Jonathan; 

Very pleasant has thou been to me; 

Thy love to me was wonderful, Passing the love of women."

When the ancient Hebrew scribe placed the record of the friendship of David and Jonathan upon his sacred scroll, he wrote:

"The soul of Jonathan was knit with the soul of 

David, and Jonathan loved him as his own soul."

The story of the friendship of David and Jonathan has lived through the ages not because it was the friendship of a king's son for a shepherd youth who became Israel's greatest king, but because it is the record of the supreme loyalty which existed between two friends the loyalty that is born of greatness of soul, gallant, loving and sacrificing.

THE LOYALTY OF RUTH TO NAOMI -The Book of Ruth 

In the days of the Judges in Israel, the land of Judah was visited by famine. Because there was very little food in the land, many took their families and went to other parts of Canaan that they might not perish. So Elimelech of Bethlehem, thinking of his wife and his two sons, thought to take refuge in the land of Moab where food was plentiful. 

But Naomi, the wife of Elimelech, pled with her husband saying: "Do not take me from my own people to dwell among the heathen Moabites. 

Rather than see his loved ones suffer Elimelech took them to Moab, a journey of five days. There they found the vineyards, orchards, and grain fields yielding in great abundance. Soon the little family from Bethlehem was living in comfort.

Nevertheless, the hearts of Elimelech and Naomi were often sad because they could no longer sing the 

Lord's songs when they watched the Moabites worship the fire-god, Chemosh, and saw the altars burning before the great brass idols they were gravely anxious for their sons, Mahlon and Chilion, lest they forget to be faithful to Jehovah. 

In spite of her loneliness of heart Naomi made

LOYALTY 

293 friends with the women of the village. Soon they came often to seek the sad-eyed Hebrew mother, for her kindly counsel and her willingness to help those in need had become well-known. 

After three years the news came to Moab that once more food was plentiful in all the land of Canaan. 

Naomi begged to return to Bethlehem, but Elimelech was content to stay in Moab. It was not long, however, before Elimelech sickened and died and was buried in the hills of the pagan land. Once more Naomi pled to be taken to her home but her sons, now grown to manhood, had made friends among the Moabite maidens and desired to stay in the land indefinitely. 

It was not long before Mahlon and Chilion brought wives to the home of Naomi. The childish, impulsive laughter of Orpha brought much needed brightness to the little home, but it was the quiet trusting ways of Ruth that brought comfort to the lonely Naomi. As the years passed, the two young women came to love their mother-in-law for her loving kindness to them and the many things she taught them. 

But it was Ruth who came nearest to the heart of Naomi because it was to her that the lonely woman spoke often of her people, Israel, and the God she worshiped. 

Ere long a strange fever broke out among the shepherds of Moab and many died; among them were the sons of Naomi. The aged, lonely woman knew not what to do now to provide for herself. Not wishing to be a burden to the sorrowing wives of her sons she told them of her desire to return to Bethelehem. But 

Orpha and Ruth had no desire to be separated from 

Naomi, so they decided to accompany her to Bethlehem.

When all was ready for the journey, the three women set out upon the road to Canaan. As they came to the edge of the city Naomi stopped and spoke to the young women: "Go back, my daughters, each to her mother's house.

God bless you for your loving kindness to the dead and to me. May He grant you also another husband to care for you and to comfort you." 

Then she kissed them both tenderly in farewell. 

But Orpha and Ruth answered: "Surely we will go with thee to thy people."

Much as Naomi loved them and much as she needed their companionship and assistance, she spoke again to urge them to return to the homes of their mothers: "Nay, my daughters, why should you waste your lives in loneliness for my sake? It grieveth me that the shadow of my sorrow hath fallen upon you.'

Then Orpha weeping hysterically said impulsively, "Let us go with thee to Bethlehem.' ??

But Naomi knowing the childish spirit of her son's widow soothed her and persuaded her to return to the home of her own people. And Orpha turned back upon the road to the city. 

Turning now to Ruth, Naomi said to her: "Behold thy sister-in-law is gone back unto her own people and unto her own gods. Return thou after her." 

Now Naomi felt the arms of Ruth about her neck.

As she looked into the eyes of the aged mother, Ruth cried: "Entreat me not to leave thee or to return from following after thee! For whither thou goest, I will go, and where thou lodgest I will lodge. Thy people shall be my people and thy God shall be my God." 

Naomi seeing the strength of Ruth's love for her entreated her no more to return to Moab. The two women then continued their way to Bethlehem. As soon as they came to the gate of the city, a great crowd came to welcome Naomi whom they remembered and loved sincerely. When the news spread among the women of the little city of Ruth's kindness to her mother-in-law and how she had forsaken the worship of her

LOYALTY 

295 people to accept the worship of Jehovah, they received her, also, with great friendliness.

Then the two women went to the old home of the family of Elimelech and dwelt together with understanding and tenderness. When the harvest time came, Ruth, knowing their needs, said to Naomi: "Let me go to the fields and glean the grain that there might be bread in the house for the days to come." 

Naomi answered her, "Go, my daughter, go!"

When Ruth came to a large grain field outside the city she asked that she might be allowed to follow the reapers and gather the fallen grain. And as she gleaned, the master of the field, Boaz, came and seeing her asked of the steward: "Who is yonder maiden gleaning alone?" "It is the Moabite daughter-in-law of Naomi," the steward answered.

Now Boaz, who was a kinsman of Naomi, went to where Ruth was gleaning and spoke kindly to her saying: "My daughter glean in my fields as long as thou wilt. Follow the reapers with the other maidens and none shall harm thee. And when thou art athirst go to the water vessels and drink."

Ruth was amazed at the kindness of the master of the field and bowing low before Boaz, she asked: "Why should'st thou speak so kindly to me who am a stranger?" 

Boaz answered Ruth saying: "Thou art no stranger, my daughter. I have heard of all thy goodness to my kinswoman, Naomi. May Jehovah repay thee for thy kindness. May a full reward be given thee from the

Lord God of Israel under whose wings thou art come to trust." 

When the time came for the reapers and gleaners to rest, Boaz saw that Ruth received a plentiful supply of parched corn for her meal. Then going to the reap-

ers he bade them to respect the Moabite maiden and to see to it that her gleaning was easy by letting fall some ears purposely in her path.

At eventide Ruth returned to Naomi with a large supply of grain and a portion of the parched corn she had saved from the noonday meal. The women rejoiced at their good fortune. But when Naomi knew that it was in the field of the wealthy Boaz that Ruth had gleaned and heard the story of his kindness to her daughter-in-law, she was exceedingly thankful. 

Day after day Ruth gleaned through the barley and wheat harvests to provide food for herself and Naomi for the months to come. And Boaz watched Ruth and admired her humility and dignity as she worked among the young men and maidens in his fields. 

As Naomi saw the kindness of her kinsman Boaz to Ruth, in her heart the aged woman prayed that he might come to love the maiden who had brought brightness to her loneliness. Now more than anything else Naomi desired the happiness of her son's widow. 

And Boaz came to love Ruth and desired to make her his wife. It was the custom, however, in Israel that when a man died without children that his nearest kinsman had the right to buy his land and so take his widow also. 

So that Boaz might be able to marry Ruth, Naomi consented to sell the portion of land that was the heritage of Elimelech. And Boaz not wishing to break any law of Israel went to the kinsman of Ruth's husband and said to him: "Now, Naomi desires to sell the land that belonged to her husband and her sons. Wilt thou buy the land of her and take Ruth, who came with her out of the land of Moab, to be thy wife as is thy first right?" 

And the kinsman answered, "Take thou my rights both in the land and the woman. 

The people of Bethlehem rejoiced at the happiness

LOYALTY 

297 that had come to Ruth and to Boaz whom they respected greatly. And they cried to them with gladness. "May the Lord make this woman to be like Leah and Rachel for they builded the House of Israel and may thou, Boaz, prosper and be happy in thy children in Bethlehem." 

Happiness and comfort had come to Naomi at last, for in the home of the daughter she loved she knew peace and contentment. But it was when she saw the infant son of Ruth that the thanksgiving of her heart was the greatest. The waiting women when they laid the babe in the arms of the aged mother said: "Blessed be Jehovah, who hath not left thee this day without a near kinsman. He shall be the restorer of thy life and will comfort thine old age, for he is the son of thy daughter-in-law Ruth who has been better to thee than ten sons. May this child, Obed, be famous in Israel!"

Naomi took the child and loved it dearly, and she became its nurse caring for it with the tenderness Ruth's love had kept alive in her heart. And Obed grew to greatness for his son's son was King David, Israel's greatest king.

Many lovers of the Bible have wondered why this simple story of the loyalty of a young woman to an older woman, her husband's mother, should have been chosen for a place among the sacred historical and prophetic records of the Old Testament. Many explanations have been given by those skilled in the understanding of sacred literature. But one answer can be found in the human message of the story-that faith in the goodness of life brings with it loyalty to human ties and relations, and courage to give of oneself to others with unselfishness.

As long as human ties and relations are sacred things, the story of Ruth and Naomi will live and be loved because of its portrayal of a great loyalty.

ESTHER, THE QUEEN WHO SAVED HER PEOPLE—The Book of Esther 

In the days of Xerxes, the great Persian king, many Jews, descendants of those exiled by the fall of Jerusalem, lived in the great city of Shushan. 

The former kings of Persia, Cyrus and Darius, had been kind to the exiled Jews in their land so that they had prospered greatly. Even when the call of the prophets of Israel came for them to return and help to rebuild the city of Jerusalem and the Temple, they did not return to their own land.

Xerxes, whom the Jews called Ahasuerus, was the ruler over one hundred and twenty-seven kingdoms in the land from the source of the Nile in Egypt to India. 

His marble palace at Shushan was magnificently decorated with gold and silver and silken draperies. He ruled his people with selfish arrogance and there were many who feared him. Now it was that the Jews who had not returned to Jerusalem were sorely troubled. 

The time came when the King decided to extend his great empire by conquering Greece and its people, the Greeks. To make preparation for this new war he called all the princes of his empire to a great feast. 

Group after group came and the King showed them. his armies, and his great riches, that they might be impressed with his power and promise to help him in the war against the Greeks. While the princes and nobles were being entertained in the King's palace, Queen Vashti gave a feast for the wives of the King's guests in her palace.

During the feasting, when all were merry with much drinking, King Ahasuerus made a boast about the beauty of his wife. Thereupon he sent a command to the Queen to array herself in her royal robes and come to the feast to let his guests see how beautiful she was.

But Vashti refused to obey her husband's command to appear unveiled before the drunken revelers at the feast.

LOYALTY 

299 The Queen of Persia had been forced to choose between obedience to her husband and loyalty to the Persian ideal of womanly dignity; for in Persia it was the custom that a woman who exposed her face in public to the view of men was dishonored. The King's anger at his wife's disobedience was so great that he asked his counselors to decide on a punishment for Vashti. 

One of them thinking to provide an example for all the wives of the princes answered: "If it please the King let Vashti be queen no longer; let her be replaced by one who is more worthy of the honor, so that all wives shall give honor to their husbands. "Let a royal decree go forth demanding that all wives do their husband's bidding or be treated as Vashti."

The suggestion pleased the King and he commanded that Vashti be queen no longer. Then there was a need for a new queen so the word was sent throughout the land that the most beautiful maidens be sent to the palace that the King might select one to be queen. 

Among the Jews in Shushan was Mordecai of the lineage of King Saul whose foster daughter was Esther, a beautiful young maiden. Mordecai was a loyal Jew and he loved Esther greatly and he desired to see her in a position of security not only for her own sake but that she might be able to help her people if need be. 

So Esther was taken to the palace of the King to be placed among the maidens from whom the new queen was to be chosen.

When Esther was taken into the King's presence her beauty and lack of adornment pleased Ahasuerus so much that he set the royal crown upon her head and she became the Queen of Persia. But Esther told no one that she was born a Jew or that Mordecai was her kinsman, because it was as her foster parent had demanded.

Mordecai was able to watch over Esther from day to day because as a servant of the palace he knew all that happened. Now it was that two jealous officers of the court were plotting against the life of Ahasuerus and Mordecai discovered the plot. Esther told the King of the matter in the name of Mordecai. Thereupon the 

King, after he had further proof of the plot ordered the two servants to be hanged. Into the records of the country was written the account of how Mordecai had saved the life of the King; but no reward was given to the servant for his loyalty.

Now the most powerful servant in the King's palace was Haman, a man of great wealth and great ability. 

The King trusted this servant so much that he gave him authority over all others demanding that all bow to him in respect for his high office whenever they saw him. Out of fear all did as Ahasuerus commanded, all but Mordecai. For Mordecai was a proud and loyal 

Jew and would not bow in homage to any man. Therefore, Haman when he passed Mordecai about the palace doing the King's business and saw that he did not bow low to him in respect as did all the other servants, he was full of anger. But Haman hated the Jews also, because of their pride in their race and their loyalty to their religion.

So it was that Haman plotted that he might destroy all the Jews that were living in the land of Persia. But he did not know that Esther, the queen, was a Jewess. 

When the plans were ready Haman went before King 

Ahasuerus and said: "O King, there is a certain people scattered throughout your land. Their laws are different from your laws and they refuse to obey you. Because they are different from other people they are unprofitable to our kingdom. If it please the King let a decree go forth that they be destroyed. Then I will take their wealth of ten thousand talents and place it in the King's treasury."

LOYALTY 

301 Without as much as questioning his servant as to the people to be destroyed or their integrity, the King gave Haman the power to write the decree and to enforce it. And the word was sent throughout the land that on the thirteenth day of the twelfth month all Jews, every man, woman, and child, must be killed.

When this word came to the Jews in the land of Persia and in Shushan there was great mourning. They were not able to understand the royal decree for they had not disobeyed any of the laws of the land, they had paid willingly all tribute asked of them and they had dealt honestly with all men. Many dressed themselves in sackcloth as a sign of their sorrow, and fasted and prayed continually that this great evil be kept from them. But Queen Esther knew nothing of the decree that had gone forth among her people. 

Then Mordecai sent word to the Queen of the evil

Haman had plotted against the Jews. Also he begged her to go to the King and plead for herself and her people. 

But Esther knew the rules of the court and she was afraid to go before the King, so she sent a message to Mordecai saying: 

"All of the King's servants do know that if any should go into the inner court of the King without being summoned, he shall be put to death, except such as the King shall hold out the golden scepter. I have not been summoned to the King for these thirty days." 

Once more Mordecai sent his messengers to the 

Queen. This time he commanded her saying: "Think not that thou shalt escape the distinction of the Jews at the hands of Haman. If thou art silent at this time and deliverance shall come, who knoweth but what thou were made a queen at this time because of God's purpose to save His people." 

Then Esther faltered no more. To save her people she was willing to risk her own life. The messenger carried the word to Mordecai saying:

"Go gather all the Jews in Shushan in one place. 

There fast and pray for me for three days. I and my maidens will do likewise. Then I shall go into the 

King's presence. If I perish, I perish."

After three days, the Queen dressed in her royal robes went and stood in the inner court of the King's palace and when the King saw her standing thus, once more her beauty and dignity moved him and he held out the golden sceptre to her as a sign that she might enter. Then the Queen came near to the throne and touched the scepter. And the King asked: "What wilt thou? What is thy request? It shall be given to thee even to the half of my kingdom."

But Esther did not speak of the sorrow in her heart at this time. Instead she made the request that the King and his servant Haman attend a banquet she had prepared in their honor. So the King and Haman went to the Queen's palace and partook of the feast she had prepared. When the banquet was over, Ahasuerus said to the Queen: thee." "What is thy petition? for it shall be granted 

Esther answered: "If I have found favor in your eyes, O King, may you and Haman return tomorrow and eat with me. 

Then I shall tell thee my request."

Haman passed through the courts of the palace full of pride at the thought of the honor bestowed upon him by the Queen. But when he passed Mordecai at the King's gate and noted how this Jew continued unmoved with no show of respect for his greatness, Haman was filled with wrath. Coming to his home Haman told his wife and his friends of the honor that had come to him from the King and the Queen. Then he told them of the continued disrespect of the Jew, Mordecai. 

Then the friends of Haman and his wife urged him to have a gallows built and seek the King's per-

LOYALTY 

303 mission for the Jew to be hanged. Pleased at the suggestion Haman ordered the gallows to be built in readiness.

It happened that on that night King Ahasuerus was unable to sleep, so he sent for his scribe and ordered him to read from the records of the empire. Strange it was that he read to the restless king the account of the faithful service of Mordecai in saving the King's life. 

And Ahasuerus asked: "What honor and dignity hath come to Modercai for this service?" 

The servants answered, "Nothing hath been done."

When Haman came to the palace the next morning, the King asked him what should be done one whom the 

King desired to honor. And Haman, thinking the honor was for himself suggested that the man dressed in royal apparel mounted on the King's horse should be led through the streets of the city for all to see. "Haste thee then," said the King, "to do this to 

Mordecai who served so loyally."

Haman did as he was bidden for he dared do no other. Now it seemed that evil was beginning to prevail against him, and he returned to his wife and his friends to know what to do. But at once Haman was summoned to attend the second banquet of the Queen. 

This time when Ahasueras asked the Queen her petition Esther answered: "If I have found favor in thy sight, O my King, let my life and that of my people be spared; for we are sold, I and my people, to be destroyed, to be slain and to perish." "Who is he and where is he who dares to do such a thing?" cried the King. 

Esther answered: "It is this wicked Haman.”

When Ahasuerus knew that it was against the Jews, the Queen's people, that Haman had requested the

royal decree, his anger was greater than ever. Then one of the servants took the King and showed him the gallows that had been prepared for Mordecai. And the

King ordered that Haman be hanged thereupon without delay. 

Upon the death of Haman, Mordecai was made the chief overseer of all the land. But still the decree of death for the Jews was unchanged. 

Once more Queen Esther went before the King and falling at his feet besought him with tears saying: "If it please the King, let the decree to destroy the Jews be reversed. For how can I endure to see the evil that shall come to my people and the destruction of my kindred."

But it was written in the laws of Persia that a royal decree could not be changed. So the King sent messengers throughout the land to the Jews to arm themselves and to fight against the destroyers. When the thirteenth day of the twelfth month came the Jews were ready and powerful and none were able to destroy them. 

On the next day, the fourteenth day, a great feast of rejoicing was held by all the Jews in all the land of Persia. The feast was called the Feast of Purim or the 

Feast of Lots, because it was by the casting of lots that Haman decided upon the time for the destruction of the Jews.

Until this time the Jews celebrate the Feast of 

Purim as a time of thanksgiving, of gladness and joy because by the goodness of God they were saved from destruction through the loyalty and courage of Esther, the Queen of Persia.

To Jewish women the world over, Esther is a symbol of ideal womanhood, beautiful, brave, and faithful. But to all people the example of Esther is a supreme pattern of loyalty, the service that braves all obstacles, even death itself that good might come to others, individuals, groups, or nations.

SPIRITUAL VALUES FROM ORGANIZED RELIGION 

The Hebrews and Their Worship 

THUS saith Cyrus, King of Persia. Jehovah,

God of heaven hath given me all the kingdoms of the earth; and He hath charged me to build him a House at Jerusalem, which is in Judah. "Who is there among you of all His people? His

God be with him, and let him go up to Jerusalem and build the House of the Lord God of Israel." 

This amazing proclamation by Cyrus, the Persian conqueror, marked the beginning of a great period in the history of the Hebrews, the growth of Judaism, the religion of the Jews. 

Now after many centuries the descendants of Abraham had come to understand their true relationship to God, their Eternal Father. But what was more significant they knew that the kingdom of God was not an earthly kingdom with kings and thrones and wealth and power, but was a kingdom of righteousness guided by prophets, priests, and teachers who were God's direct representatives. The leaders of the Jews, therefore, believed that it would be through worship centered around the temple that Israel could be kept faithful to their heavenly King, and at the same time preserve their spiritual identity. Worship and ceremonial, covenants and laws were woven together in a pattern of religion called Judaism, the religion of the Jews.

The leaders of Israel during the years had made a record of the spiritual and racial experiences of their people: historical chronicles, books of scripture, books of law, books of wisdom and moral counsel, national songs and poems, and hymns of praise and devotion.
