From this vast body of literature the religion of the Jews received its inspiration and its expression and its laws. 

Before the Exile the Hebrews were concerned with their existence as a nation; after the return from captivity the interest centered about the preservation of their religious heritage, the worship of God and the obedience of the Law. Now Jerusalem was to be their Holy City.

Today Judaism exists much as it developed in the years after the Exile under the guidance of Ezra and Nehemiah, a Church with worship and ceremonies, coveenants and laws for the spiritual welfare of its people. 

THE RETURN TO JERUSALEM-Ezra 1 

The exile of Israel in Babylon was over. Cyrus, the Persian conqueror of the empire of the Babylonians, had issued a decree ending the captivity of the Jews. 

This great benefactor of the East was very different than the tyrant emperors of Assyria and Babylon, the conquerors of the kingdoms of Israel and Judah. He was of a tribe of the descendants of Japheth that had given rise to the nations of southern Europe. But what was more, his people did not make images of gods and idols to worship. This may have been the reason for his - liberation of Israel, or it may have been the desolation of the land which had once prospered under the thrift and industry of the people who had continued to keep alive the kingdom of David.

It was not long before a caravan of more than forty-two thousand Jews was ready. A few who had come to Babylon as children were among the returning

exiles. They could remember the beauty and glory of 

Jerusalem. Others of the second and third generation of captives cherished the memory of their fathers and looked forward with great expectation to life in the land which had been the inheritance of the descendants of Abraham. But there were those who did not care to return to the land of their fathers, because they were prosperous and well established in Babylon and now the religion of Israel meant little or nothing to them. 

With gladness and thanksgiving the exiles started on their way. Through the generosity of Cyrus they were taking back with them the treasures that Nebuchadnezzar had taken from the Temple. Also the wealthy Jews remaining in Babylon had provided large sums of gold and silver to help with the work of restoration of Jerusalem and the House of the Lord. 

The Journey across the desert led by Zerubbabel, a prince of the house of David, took months of weary travel for the mighty train of men and women and children, camels, horses, and cattle. As they came to the land of the inheritance of Israel they saw cities blackened and in ruins, they saw the remnants of those who had been left in the land of Judah living in loneliness and wretchedness, while in the kingdom of Israel was a people calling themselves Samaritans, a mingling of 

Israelites and Assyrians. But when they came to the city of Jerusalem and saw the walls broken down and the Temple and the buildings but piles of stones and rubbish they were overcome with sadness.

Some of the Jews returned to the homes of their fathers while many remained near the ruins of Jerusalem. It was a difficult task to make new homes amid the scenes of destruction, but they worked courageously. 

After seven months they all gathered at Jerusalem to keep the Feast of the Tabernacles and to plan for the rebuilding of the Temple. 

Setting up an altar on Mount Moriah amidst the

ruins of God's holy Temple the smoke of burnt offerings once more ascended into the heavens. Now it seemed that the struggles and disappointments of the past months were all forgotten for the spirit of holy worship brought with it a testimony of the place of Israel in the kingdom of a merciful and eternal God. 

During the days of the Feast the households lived together as they had done in the wilderness with Moses. 

The words of the psalmist, David, were sung many times during this period of worship and thanksgiving bringing courage and comfort with them. 

From now onward all the daily sacrifices and festivals were kept as in days of old uniting the people together in a new spirit. 

REBUILDING THE TEMPLE-Ezra 3-6 

As soon as they were able, the Jews began to prepare for the rebuilding of the Temple. As in the days of Solomon the giant cedars were brought from the forests of Lebanon, the masons and carpenters were skilled craftsmen of Tyre and Sidon, and the stone was hewn in the hills. At last all was ready to lay the foundation of the Temple and Zerubbabel, the governor of the colony of Jews, called the people together. 

It was a day of great rejoicing, the priests of Levi were assembled in their sacred robes and a choir of singers sang the psalms of David. When the first stone of the foundation was laid the people joined together in a great hymn of praise: "O give thanks unto the Lord, for He is good 

For His mercy endureth for ever." 

Some of the aged who remembered the glory and the grandeur of the first Temple wept aloud because of the simplicity of the new House of the Lord. But among the youth of the people who had been chosen to build the second Temple there was a spirit of great joy.

When the people of Samaria heard of the building of the Temple in Jerusalem they sent messengers to 

Zerubbabel saying: "Let us join you in building this House of the 

Lord. We seek your God, even as ye do."

But Zerubbabel thinking that the Samaritans had no real desire to forsake their images and worship the 

God of Truth refused their help saying: "Ye have nothing to do with us to build an house unto our God; but we ourselves will build unto Jehovah 

God of Israel."

This reply was to cause much difficulty for the builders of the second Temple, because many of the Samaritans were descendants of the Israelites of the Northern Kingdom who were not taken into captivity by the Assyrians. Even for five hundred years the enmity endured between the Jews and the Samaritans. 

And it became a familiar saying among both peoples, the Jews have no dealings with the Samaritans. 

The Samaritans found a way to stop the building of the Temple, they sent a letter to the King of Persia accusing the Jews of plotting rebellion against their ruler. Cyrus, the emperor who had freed the Jews from their captivity, was dead and the new king did not understand the decree of Cyrus freeing the Jews and directing them to rebuild the House of the Lord. He sent an official to Jerusalem to investigate the charges that had been made.

When the Persian arrived in Jerusalem and saw the building of the Temple he ordered the work stopped until he could investigate the decree of Cyrus. This was a great blow to the Jews for it took many months for a message to be sent to Babylon. 

At first the decree of Cyrus could not be found in the records of the Persian empire. Then King Darius ordered that a search be made among the clay books in the library of the city of Babylon. At last it was

found and the official was ordered not to interfere with the building of the Temple, but to help the people in every way as Cyrus had done.

But the people had become very discouraged by this time. It was almost impossible to make a living for themselves. The land had become like a wilderness during the long years of the exile, the hillsides no longer were covered with orchards, and several seasons of drought had killed the grass of the plains. Now after many years delay they seemed to have lost the spirit and the courage to finish the rebuilding of the Temple. 

Then God inspired two of His servants, the prophets Haggai and Zechariah, to bring new courage to the builders.

Haggai was an older man and he chastised the people for becoming faint-hearted. But the younger prophet, Zechariah, Spoke cheering words to them promising them success in their efforts and comfort in the days to come.

It was not long before the walls of the sacred building began to resound with loyal workmen. Then at last the long-delayed task was done. Zechariah rejoiced greatly as he looked upon the beautiful white temple. A vision of the coming of the Savior was given to him and he cried out to the people: "Rejoice greatly, O Israel! Behold thy King cometh. He is just and having salvation, lowly and riding upon an ass. He shall speak peace unto all mankind.

His dominion shall be unto the ends of the earth." 

When the time for the Feast of the Passover came, Zerubbabel gathered together all the people for the dedication of the Temple. The simple beauty of the building seemed to breathe the new spirit that had come to Israel. When they replaced the treasures of Solomon's temple, the vessels of gold and silver that Cyrus had returned to them, their eyes were filled with tears of joy.

But when they thought of the Ark of the Covenant

with its sacred treasures which was lost and its place in the Holy Sanctuary would be empty, they were sad even amidst their great joy. For seven days they sacrificed and gave thanks for the deliverance from their enemies, a deliverance which was like unto the deliverance of their fathers from Egypt.

THE SERVICE OF EZRA, THE SCRIBE-Ezra 4-10 

Many years after the prophets Haggai and Zechariah, and the governor, Zerubbabel, had passed away there arose no inspired leaders among the Jews. Once more the spirit of the people became careless and the word of God ceased to guide them. They were just a handful of people in a little colony, a land not more than twenty miles from end to end. All around them were heathen tribes. Their need was very great at this time.

In far away Babylon there lived many prosperous Jews. Among them was Ezra, a learned scribe, who knew the Law of Moses and was a faithful worshiper of Jehovah. When he heard of the sorry plight of his people in Jerusalem, he went to his master Artaxerxes, the ruler in Babylon, and asked to be allowed to go to their aid. The king was gracious to Ezra who had served him faithfully as a counselor, and gave him gifts of money and supplies to help him in his work. 

A band of many loyal Jews decided to accompany

Ezra, taking with them their possessions and much wealth. Artaxerxes offered to provide the caravan an armed guard for the perilous journey across the desert, for there were many robber bands preying upon those passing between Babylon and Egypt. But Ezra trusted in the power of the Lord to protect them. 

Before they started on the journey, however, Ezra requested all to fast and pray that they might journey

in safety. Then they set out unguarded and after four months they came to Jerusalem in safety.

It was as if a new light and a new hope had come to the lonely people of Judah. The wealth Ezra had brought meant a great deal, but the ability of the successful Jews from Babylon did much more to stimulate them to new ways of making a livelihood. It was a renewing of courage and enterprise, which was sorely needed to bring security and comfort to living.

But that was not all the mission of Ezra brought to the Jews. When he saw the carelessness of the people, the neglect of the worship of the Temple and the violation of the marriage laws of Moses, he was sorely grieved. Calling all the people to assemble before the 

House of the Lord, Ezra spoke to them of their shortcomings. The people were ashamed and wept bitterly at the words of the scribe. Then he called upon the men of Judah to gather in three days at Jerusalem. 

When they gathered before him on the appointed day, Ezra said to them: "Ye have transgressed, and have taken strange wives. Now, therefore, make confession unto the Lord 

God of your fathers, and do His pleasure, and separate yourselves from the people of the land and from strange wives.'

The men of the congregation who had transgressed answered Ezra saying: "As thou hast said, so must we do. Let us go to our homes and do this thing in order." 

What a difficult task Ezra had before him! Heathen women who had married Jewish husbands were sent away to their old homes. Wives and children were taken away and homes were left desolate. When loved ones are separated there is always suffering and such was the case throughout the little colony of Jews at the severe cleansing of Ezra.

Even if Israel was cleansed of a great sin against the Law of God, a great bitterness arose in the hearts of his countrymen and for a time Ezra could help them no more.

THE WALL OF JERUSALEM REBUILT-Nehemiah 1-3 

The word of the sad confusion and bitterness among the Jews was brought to Nehemiah, a wealthy 

Jew of the Persian court of Artaxerxes. Like Ezra before him he begged his master to allow him to go to Jerusalem and the service of his people. 

When Nehemiah came to Jerusalem and saw the ruins within the city and its crumbling walls he was amazed at the condition which had existed so many years after the Temple had been rebuilt. Calling all the responsible leaders he could find, Nehemiah said to them: "Ye see the distress that we are in, how Jerusalem lieth waste and the gates thereof are burned with fire; come and let us rebuild the walls of Jerusalem that we be no more a reproach."

Soon the work was under way for Nehemiah was a good organizer. Day after day the people toiled willingly and the walls rose higher and higher. 

The men of Samaria, hearing of the new activity in Jerusalem, tried to stop the work by force. Armed men were sent to harass the builders and to destroy their work. But the Jews were not to be hindered. Part of the men stood guard over those who were building to protect them from the Samaritans. 

At last the great wall was finished and the gates hung upon their hinges. Jerusalem was once more a protected city. Another great festival of dedication was called. Once more with singing and rejoicing they gave thanks to God for the new blessings that had come to them at the hands of Nehemiah.

EZRA AND NEHEMIAH ESTABLISH INSTITUTIONAL JUDAISM-Nehemiah 6-13 

It was the day of the dedication of the rebuilt wall around the city of Jerusalem. The people were assembled before the Water Gate and Nehemiah was speaking to them. As they listened to his words telling them once more, the story they knew so well, the destiny of Israel as the descendants of Abraham, they were very grateful for the new beginning that had come to them. 

Now it seemed that once more they could lift their heads in rejoicing for their beloved Jerusalem was rebuilt and secure within its protecting wall. 

Now Nehemiah commanded Ezra, the Scribe, to come before the people and read to them from the Book of the Law of Moses. As he read the words God had spoken to Israel as commandments, many bowed their heads and wept in humility and many knelt with their faces hidden for the remembrance of their shortcomings made them ashamed. Seeing this great spirit of repentance, Nehemiah spoke again to them saying: "Mourn not, nor weep! For this day is holy unto our Lord. The joy of the Lord is your strength."

It was indeed a holy day for Israel, a great event in the history of the world, for it was the beginning of Judaism. 

When it came the time for the Feast of Tabernacles the people gathered together once more. For eight days they stayed together listening to the words of the Law read and explained by Ezra, the Scribe, and Malachi and Nehemiah. At the close of the Feast the people made a covenant to obey the Law of God; to obey the law of the Sabbath; to abstain from intermarriage with heathen peoples; to obey the laws of tithes and offerings; and to observe the sacred ceremonies of worship and the days of festival.

All seemed well with the people now, the work

of Ezra and Nehemiah had borne fruit. Ezra continued to go among them as a teacher and an interpreter of the law. But Nehemiah felt that he must return to 

Babylon for he had delayed very long.

Some years passed and once more Nehemiah returned to dwell with his people in Jerusalem. He was greatly pleased with the loyalty with which some of the people had kept the covenant they had made to keep the Law. But he was sorely displeased to see that many did not even obey the law of the Sabbath. As he stood at the gate of the city on the Sabbath day he saw men going out to their fields and orchards to work and he saw traders coming into the city to sell their wares. 

Nehemiah and Ezra counseled together to find a way to help the people to keep the laws of the covenant. 

Then they realized that many of the people only came together on the three great festival days of the year, and that the law of the Sabbath was but the observance of a day of rest. They also realized that many had no opportunity to learn the sacred teachings of the prophets and they knew that a way must be found to guide and instruct the people. 

In all the small communities of the Jews Ezra established meeting places called synagogues. The people were to gather at the synagogue on the Sabbath to hear the reading of the Law. It was not long before this Sabbath meeting became an occasion for worship, hymns of praise were sung, prayers were recited as well as readings from the sacred records. Before long the synagogue became the very heart of the community, the Scribe or leader became counselor as well as interpreter of the Law.

Then the synagogues became the school of the communities. Before this only a few Jewish youths could go to the Temple for instruction, but now all could go to the synagogue schools. The youths were taught to recite the history of Israel, the lives of the great

leaders, and the words of wisdom and counsel of the prophets. As the droning words of the pupils as they recited the bits of history or the words of wisdom of Israel came to the parents of Israel, they too were quickened in spirit. But the education of the youth of Israel in the wisdom and law of their fathers was more than a preparation for the priesthood, it was the great force that was going to help to keep Israel faithful and enduring, the spirit of Judaism. 

Nehemiah gave much of his thought and care to the worship of the Temple. In the history of Israel from the time of Adam we have seen the place of the burnt offering in the expression of gratitude to the Lord God. As time passed the ceremonies connected with this form of worship became more elaborate, no longer were they the simple offering on an altar of stones as in the days of Abraham and Jacob. As Israel had become more aware of their weaknesses and disobedience they had begun to offer sacrifice as a sign of repentance and as a petition for forgiveness. In this way the worship of the Temple had changed and had increased in many of its ceremonies. The services of the priests of the 

Temple and the singers were a great part of the ceremonies. Nehemiah directed the worship of the Temple that it might become more sacred and at the same time more rigid. Now the priests of the Temple must conduct the sacred services and ceremonies to the glory of God and not for the share of the offerings that came to them as payment. The new dignity and sanctity of the worship of the Temple played a great role in the making of the pattern of religion that was being woven to make Judaism.

The refined worship of the Temple and the worship of the synagogue were not all that made up the religion of Judaism. Ezra and Nehemiah realized that additional rules and regulations were needed to help the people to obey the Law. From time to time many

rules were made limiting and directing the everyday lives of the people to the end that they render strict obedience to the Law.

In combining the worship of God with obedience to the Law a pattern of religion was made. Every loyal Jew who was faithful to the laws of Judaism became distinct from the rest of mankind. By observing the law of racial purity the Jews have preserved their religious identity from generation to generation, and on through the centuries. 

THE MESSAGE OF MALACHI—Book of Malachi 

The prophet Malachi lived during the time of 

Ezra and Nehemiah. During the absence of Nehemiah in Babylon he became gravely concerned with the carelessness of the people and the lack of sincerity of some of the priests in their duties connected with the worship of the people. He resented the attitude towards obedience and worship that did not give the very best to God. 

So he went about among the Jews urging them to obey and worship in sincerity and humility. 

When Nehemiah returned and ordered rules and regulations made with punishments attached that the people be disciplined into the worship of God and obedience to the Law, Malachi pled with the people to worship and obey because of their love of God, not out of fear. 

But Malachi agreed with Ezra and Nehemiah that it was better to have obedience under the direction of the priestly rulers than to have the people lapse into carelessness and be lost racially and spiritually. 

In the book which Malachi recorded his teachings and his prophecies in, we find many words of comfort and inspiration. Besides condemning those who were not loyal to the laws of marriage and those who did not worship sincerely, he pronounced his anger against those

who robbed God by withholding their tithes and offerings and those who dealt with their fellowmen without mercy and justice: "Thus saith the Lord of Hosts," he warned, “I will send my messenger and he shall prepare the way before me; and the Lord whom ye seek shall suddenly come to His temple. And He shall sit as a refiner and purifier of silver, and He shall purify the sons of Levi and purge them as gold and silver, that they may offer unto the Lord an offering in righteousness. "Behold I will send you Elijah, the prophet before the coming of the great and dreadful day of the Lord. 

And he shall turn the hearts of the fathers to the children, and the hearts of the children to the fathers, lest 

I come and smite the earth with a curse.'

The prophet admonished the people to live righteously and promised them many blessings: "Remember ye the law of Moses, which I commanded unto him for all Israel, with the statutes and judgments. "Thus saith the Lord of hosts, prove Me now herewith, if I will not open the windows of heaven, and pour you out a blessing; that there shall not be room enough to receive it. "A book of remembrance is written for them that feared the Lord, and thought upon His name. And they shall be Mine, saith the Lord of hosts, in that day when I make up my jewels."

THE PSALMS AND THE WORSHIP OF ISRAEL -Book of Psalms 

The Book of Psalms is the hymn-book of the Hebrews. It is a collection of one hundred and fifty of the choicest lyric poems composed by the people of Israel during their long history in expression of their understanding of Jehovah, their Creator and their God, as He had guided their destiny.

When the worship of the second Temple was organized by Nehemiah the religious songs of Israel were gathered together. Because many of them had been written by David the collection was named "The Psalms of David." The entire collection is divided into five sections each ending with a doxology to mark the division: Psalms 1-41; 42-42; 43-89; 90-106; 104-150.

The directions for the musical rendering of the Psalms in connection with the worship services of the Temple and the Tabernacle are elaborate and impressive, a great choir accompanied by harps, cymbals, and trumpets.

Many different occasions and religious emotions are expressed in the Book of Psalms. There are national hymns of praise, triumphal songs of victory, lamentations of defeat, processional chants for the days of the Feasts, ceremonial chants to accompany the sacrifices and offerings of the Temple, and there are hymns of many themes for the worship of the synagogues. 

From many of the Psalms we can learn the historical setting which inspired the writer. The psalm of Moses is a noble expression of the great prophetic leader of Israel, his understanding of God, the Creator, and the Savior of mankind:

Lord, Thou hast been our dwelling place in all generations. 

Before the mountains were brought forth, Or ever Thou hadst formed the earth and the world, Even from everlasting to everlasting, Thou art God. 

Thou turnest man to contrition; 

And sayest: "Return, ye children of men." 

For a thousand years in Thy sight 

Are but a yesterday when it is past.

Thou hast set our iniquities before Thee, Our secret sins in the light of Thy countenance. 

For all our days are passed away in Thy wrath;

We spend our years as a thought. 

The days of our years are three-score years and ten, Or even by reason of strength four-score years; 

So teach us to number our days, That we may gain a heart of wisdom. 

Return, O Lord; how long? 

And have pity upon Thy servants. 

O satisfy us in the morning with Thy mercy; 

That we may rejoice and be glad all our days. 

Make us glad according to the days wherein Thou hast afflicted us,

The years wherein we have seen evil. 

Let Thy work appear unto Thy servants, And Thy glory unto their children. 

And let the favor of the Lord our God be upon us;

And establish Thou the work of our hands upon us; 

Yea, the work of our hands establish Thou it. 

As the great procession led by David brought the Ark of the Covenant to Jerusalem a great symphony of singers and instruments filled the air: "Lift up your heads, O ye gates, And be ye lifted up, ye everlasting doors; 

And the King of Glory shall come in." 

-Psalm 24 

In Babylon the exiles sang in their sorrow: "By the rivers of Babylon

There we sat down, yea, and wept. 

How shall we sing the Lord's song 

In a foreign land?" 

-Psalm 134 

The theme of the Psalms is the spiritual understanding of Israel of God. His loving kindness and 

His goodness:

"God is our refuge and strength, A very present help in time of trouble." 

-Psalm 46 

David, "The sweet singer of Israel," sang of the majesty of the creations of God, the holiness of the Lord, and the infinite goodness and mercy of the Divine 

Creator: 

The heavens declare the glory of God, And the firmament showeth His handiwork; 

Day unto day uttereth speech, And night unto night revealeth knowledge;

There is no speech, there are no words, Neither is their voice heard.

Their line is gone out through all the earth, And their words to the end of the world. 

In them hath He set a tent for the sun, Which is as a bridegroom coming out of his chamber, And rejoiceth as a strong man to run his course. 

His going forth is from the end of the heaven, And his circuit unto the ends of it. 

And there is nothing hid from the heat thereof. 

-Psalm 19 

Lord, who shall abide in Thy tabernacle? 

Who shall dwell on Thy holy mountain?

He who walketh uprightly, and worketh righteousness, And speaketh truth in his heart; 

And slandereth not with his tongue, Nor doeth evil to his friend.

Nor taketh up a reproach against his neighbor; 

In whose eyes a vile person is despised, But he honoreth them who fear the Lord; 

Nor sweareth falsely to do harm, and changeth not;

He that putteth not out his money on interest,

Nor taketh a bribe against the innocent. 

He that doeth these things shall never be moved. -Psalm 15 

The Lord is my shepherd; I shall not want.

He maketh me to lie down in green pastures; 

He leadeth me beside the still waters. 

He restoreth my soul;

He leadeth me in the paths of righteousness for His name's sake.

Yea, though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death, I will fear no evil, For Thou art with me. 

Thy rod and Thy staff, they comfort me.

Thou preparest a table before me in the presence of mine enemies; 

Thou anointest my head with oil; my cup runneth over. 

Surely goodness and mercy shall follow me all the days of my life; 

And I shall dwell in the house of the Lord forever. 

-Psalm 23 

The earth is the Lord's, and the fullness thereof; 

The world and they who dwell therein. 

For He hath founded it upon the seas, And established it upon the floods. 

Who shall ascend into the mountain of the Lord? 

And who shall stand in His holy place? 

He who hath clean hands, and a pure heart; 

Who hath not lifted up his soul unto vanity, And hath not sworn deceitfully,

He shall receive a blessing from the Lord, And righteousness from the God of his salvation.

This is the generation of them who seek Him, Who seek Thy face, O Jacob. -Psalm 24

O Lord, our Lord, How excellent is Thy name in all the earth! 

When I consider Thy heavens, the work of Thy fingers,

The moon and the stars which Thou hast established; 

What is man, that Thou art mindful of him? 

And the son of man that Thou visitest him?

For Thou hast made him a little lower than the angels, And crownest him with glory and honor:

Thou makest him to rule over the work of Thy hands, Thou hast put all things under his feet. 

-Psalm 8 

The experiences of Israel were human experiences. 

The mind of man in all ages has searched for an answer to many questions: What is the purpose of life? What is the relationship of God and man? Does God care for the individual? What is man's eternal destiny?

Israel, too, searched for the answers to these universal questions. Out of their nearness to God in the meditations of their hearts they came to know the great truths of life. The expression of these truths in the Psalms has made them sources of comfort and inspiration to all who search the Holy Scriptures to find the light of truth. 

Commit thy way unto the Lord, trust also in him, And he shall bring it to pass. 

And he shall bring forth thy righteousness as the light, And thy judgment as the noonday.

The steps of a good man are ordered by the Lord; 

And he delighteth in his way. 

Though he fall, he shall not be utterly cast down;

For the Lord upholdeth him with his hand. 

I have been young, and now am old; 

Yet have I not seen the righteous forsaken nor his seed begging bread.

I have seen the wicked in great power And spreading himself like a green bay tree. 

Yet he passed away and lo he was not; 

Yea, I sought him but he could not be found. 

Mark the perfect man, and behold the upright; 

For the end of that man is peace. 

-Psalm 37 

As the hart panteth after the water brooks, So panteth my soul after thee, O God. 

My soul thirsteth for God, for the living God; 

When shall I come and appear before God? 

My tears have been my meat day and night, While they continually say unto me, Where is thy God?

When I remember these things, I pour out my soul in me. 

Why art thou cast down, O my soul? 

And why art thou disquieted in me? 

Hope thou in God,

For I shall yet praise him for the help of his countenance. 

O my God, my soul is cast down within me:

Therefore will I remember thee from the land of Jordan 

And of the Hermonites, from the hill Mizar.

Deep calleth unto deep at the noise of thy waterspouts; 

All thy waves and thy billows are gone over me. 

Yet the Lord will command his loving-kindness in the daytime, And in the night his song shall be with me, And my prayer unto the God of my life. 

I will lift up mine eyes unto the hills. 

From whence cometh my help? 

My help cometh from the Lord, Which made heaven and earth. 

-Psalm 42

He will not suffer thy foot to be moved; 

He that keepeth thee will not slumber. 

Behold, he that keepeth Israel 

Shall neither slumber nor sleep. 

The Lord is thy keeper; 

The Lord is thy shade upon thy right hand. 

The sun shall not smite thee by day, Nor the moon by night. 

The Lord shall preserve thee from all evil: 

He shall preserve thy soul. 

The Lord shall preserve thy going out and thy coming in 

From this time forth, and even forevermore. 

-Psalm 121 

Because experiences of Israel differ only in setting from the experiences of the rest of mankind the Book of Psalms is the hymn-book of all those who worship the Lord God, of Christian and Jew alike throughout the world.

BUILDING UPON THE WISDOM OF THE PAST The Teachings of the Wise Men of Israel 

THE Bible is the supreme book of literature; it has been accepted by the world as its noblest expression. What then are the marks of a supreme book?

It is a living book. Living because it is made out of the experiences of life, the actions of men and women in their greatest moments. A living book becomes immortal when its thoughts are carried by "winged words" stirring the souls of men to an understanding of truth. 

The Bible, the literary record of the Hebrews, gives to the world eternal truth expressed with words of lasting beauty. Noble men under spiritual direction learned the purposes and wisdom of God and recorded them in words and sentences that are unforgettable. Reflecting upon the experiences of life the Hebrews learned eternal meanings. With a clear, strong voice they proclaimed: "Thus saith the Lord, I am God and there is none else... Be ye holy for I am holy... Behold I set before you Life and Death, therefore choose Life."

Saint Jerome called the Bible "A Divine Library." 

Practically every type of literature is represented in it: history, biography, essay, drama, epistle, allegory, oratory, lyric, poetry, romance, and law. 

The Book of Proverbs and the Book of Ecclesiastes are among the wisdom books of the Bible. The sage, the preacher, and the scholar had a place in He-

brew life, to guide them in the quest for wisdom. Wisdom to the Hebrew was to "Know God." To understand God and His ways was the reward of knowledge and obedience.

Today men turn to the wisdom of the Hebrews that they might learn the greatest of all lessons, to live worthily and completely.

THE WISDOM OF THE PROVERBS-Book of Proverbs 

The Book of Proverbs has been called a library of wisdom, a handbook to successful living. It is a collection of aphorisms, brief statements of truth, from the records of Hebrew literature from the earliest times until after the restoration from exile. 

To understand the scope of this body of wisdom one has to remember the panorama of experience that made up the history of the Hebrews, also their attitude towards life as a great adventure in the plans and purposes of a divine Creator.

After following the story of Israel one comes very close to the spiritual consciousness of the Hebrews, their desire to know God, and to obey Him. Wisdom, then to the people of God, was to understand God's works and ways and use that knowledge as a guide to living. 

In the language of the wise men of Israel, successful living was the result of a well-trained mind, a skilled hand, and a disciplined will all working together for good. Wisdom built upon such a foundation is the same for all men; it serves the king on the throne as well as the workman at his bench; as well as to govern a state as to govern a household; to direct one's behaviour in the presence of the great as in the treatment of the foolish. "The Lord' by wisdom founded the earth and established the heavens," yet in the ways of 

God's humbles creatures man might learn wisdom.

The language of the Proverbs speaks to the heart

of man, to the peasant and to the scholar alike, because the truth expressed is the same for all and the simple words are understood by all. Many of the Proverbs are jewels of truth exquisitely set in words of beauty: "To be righteous is wisdom; to be wicked is folly." 

The wisdom of the Proverbs covers all phases of life: personal affairs, family relations, social relations, professional and business relations, and the relations of public life. In other words, one is able to find a proper guide to living in the truths of the Proverbs because of the values, dangers, restraints, controls, and responsibilities that are needed to steer life aright. 

Out of this record of Hebrew wisdom, which is in reality human wisdom, one may learn to play the game of life well. But wisdom is more than a book of rules, it is an achievement. Life is a great adventure and wise living makes it not only great but glorious: "Blessed is the man that heareth me. 

Watching daily at my gates, Waiting at the posts of my doors.

For whoso findeth me findeth life, And shall obtain favor of the Lord. 

But he that misseth me wrongeth his own soul.

Because life is more than a passing of years, a succession of hopes and fears, joys and sorrows, a guide book to living must have its foundations in the spiritual needs of man; it must be more than a code of ethics. The 

Book of Proverbs is a religious book, a spiritual guide 

"The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom, And the knowledge of the Holy One is understanding." "Happy is the man that findeth wisdom. 

And the man that getteth understanding."

"Her ways are ways of pleasantness. 

And all her paths are peace.

She is a tree of life to them that lay hold on her; 

And happy is everyone that retaineth her."

A WISE MAN'S RELIGION—Book of Proverbs 

To Know Wisdom

Wisdom is the knowledge of how best to conduct oneself in God's world: "A wise man will hear, and will increase learning; and a man of understanding shall attain unto wise counsels."-1:5. "When wisdom entereth unto thine heart, and knowledge is pleasant unto thy soul, discretion shall preserve thee, and understanding shall keep thee."- 2:10-11. "Wisdom is the principal thing; therefore get wisdom; and with all thy getting get understanding.—4:7. "For the commandment is a lamp; and the word is a light: and reproofs of instruction are the way of life."-6:23. 

The Source of Wisdom

God is the source of wisdom and the guardian of righteousness: "For the Lord giveth wisdom: out of His mouth cometh knowledge and understanding.”—2:6. "The fear of the Lord is the beginning of knowledge, but fools despise wisdom and instruction.-1:7. 

God and His Purposes 

God made mankind and has His purposes for them;

He defends and cherishes those who follow His way: "Every word of God is pure; He is a shield unto them that put their trust in Him.”—20:5. "The spirit of man is the candle of the Lord searching all the inward parts of the soul."--20:27.

"The way of the Lord is strength to the upright, but destruction shall be to the workers of iniquity.” 

On Religion 

The reverence of God is the basis of religion and all righteousness. "The fear of the Lord is the fountain of life." 

"Whosoever trusteth in the Lord happy is he.' -16:20. "To do justice and judgment is more acceptable to the Lord than sacrifice."-21:3.

On Education 

The plan of education we find in the book of Hebrew wisdom is for the mastery of the truths of life through knowledge and discipline: "Through wisdom is a house builded; and by understanding is it established, and by knowledge shall the chambers be filled with all precious and pleasant riches."-24:3, 4. "The way of a fool is right in his own eyes; but he that hearkeneth unto counsel is wise."-12:15. "How much better is it to get wisdom than gold! and to get understanding rather to be chosen than silver."-16:16. "Train up a child in the way he should go, and when he is old he will not depart therefrom."-22:6. 

On Right and Wrong

The understanding of values is necessary to determine the choices of life; conduct or behavior is a problem of choices, right or wrong: "Where there is no vision the people perish; but he that keepeth the law, happy is he."―29:18. 

He that walketh uprightly walketh surely; but he that perverteth his ways shall be known."-10:9.

"Treasures of wickedness profit nothing; but righteousness delivereth from death."-10:2. "A man shall eat good by the fruit of his mouth. 

He that keepeth his mouth keepeth his life."-12:2, 3.

Self Control and Integrity 

The wise man must not only know good and evil, he must learn to control his appetites and passions, impulses and desires, time and energies that they may yield the greatest good to himself and to his fellow men: "He that hath no control over his spirit is like a city that is broken down and without walls."-25:28. "Better is a dry morsel and quietness therewith, than an houseful of sacrifices with strife."-17:1. "Look not upon the wine when it is red; at the last it biteth like a serpent and stingeth like an adder." -23:31, 32. "Whoso committeth adultery lacketh understanding, his soul shall he wound and dishonor shall he get; and his reproach shall not be wiped out."-6:32, 33. "The integrity of the upright shall guide them; but the perverseness of transgressors shall destroy them.' 

The Qualities of Goodness 

Humility, truthfulness, diligence, kindness, and fidelity are some of the qualities which are builders of integrity. They are the qualities of life which are praiseworthy and of good report: "When pride cometh, then cometh shame; but with the lowly is wisdom."-11:2. "Lying lips are an abomination to the Lord; but they that deal truly are His delight."—12:22. "By mercy and truth iniquity is purged: and by fear of the Lord men depart from evil."-16:6. "Go to the ant, thou sluggard; consider her ways and be wise."-6:6-11.

"He that keepeth his mouth and his tongue keepeth his soul from trouble."-21:23. "He that is slow to anger is better than the mighty; and he that ruleth his spirit than he that taketh a city.' -26:22. "A word fitly spoken is like apples of gold in pictures of silver."-25:11. "The hand of the diligent shall bear the rule; but the slothful shall be under tribute."-12:24.

Home and Family Relations 

Home is a kinship group around a father and a mother. It is a preparatory school for life. The laws of God demand honor and respect for earthly parents even as the honor and reverence of God the Eternal 

Father is the foundation of righteousness: "A wise son heareth his father's instructions, but a scorner heareth not rebuke."-13:1. "A wise son maketh a glad father; but a foolish son is the heaviness of his mother."-10:1. 

Success and the Golden Rule 

What is Success? Not riches alone, but wisdom and a good name. Honesty, generosity, good will and unselfishness are the qualities that make a successful life. "A good name is rather to be chosen than great riches, and loving favor than silver and gold."—22:1. "Treasures of wickedness profit nothing: but righteous living delivereth from death."-10:2. "Better is a little with righteousness than great revenues without right."-16:8.

Friendship "Friendship is a social bond. Wisdom counsels care in the selection of friends because of their influence upon the life of an individual:

"A man that hath friends must show himself friendly, and there is a friend that sticketh closer than a brother."-17:24. "Wealth maketh many friends; but the poor is separated from his neighbor."-19:4. "Make no friendship with an angry man lest thou learn his ways and get a snare to thy soul."-22:24, 25. 

Brotherly Kindness

The love of one's fellowmen is the basis of justice and mercy. "To do unto others as one would be done by" is the golden rule of social living. It became the second great commandment of Jesus, the divine teacher: "If thine enemy be hungry, give him bread to eat; and if he be thirsty give him water to drink: for thou shalt heap coals of fire upon his head and the Lord shall reward thee."-25:21, 22. "He that oppresseth the poor reproacheth his Maker; but he that honoreth Him hath mercy on the poor."-14:31.

The Rewards of Wisdom 

"Happy is the man that findeth wisdom, And the man that getteth understanding.

For the merchandise of it is better than the merchandise of silver, And the gain thereof than fine gold. 

She is more precious than rubies; 

And none of the things thou canst desire are to be compared unto her. 

Length of days is in her right hand;

In her left hand riches and honor. 

Her ways are ways of pleasantness, And all her paths are peace.

She is a tree of life to them that lay hold of her; 

And happy is every one that retaineth her." 

---

THE BOOK OF ECCLESIASTES 

The Book of Ecclesiastes is the work of an Hebrew philosopher who called himself Koheleth, the Preacher. 

The Preacher was a philosopher and a pessimist. 

As he looked at the confusion of life at the time he lived, he saw many conflicting values, wealth and poverty, joy and sorrow, good and evil, honesty and injustice, freedom and slavery.

He asked himself many questions as he sought to understand what he saw. Is life worth living? What is the use of it all? What is man's highest happiness?

As he looked around him he tried to understand the motives of men, those who sought wisdom, pleasure, riches, or fame. Then he wrote his impressions in a series of essays. There is much wisdom in the words of 

Koholeth, for he lays bare the true values of the material things of life which men desire thinking thereby to gain happiness. 

The Quest of Wisdom

The preacher sets his mind to understand all the works of God that he might use the knowledge for practical purposes. But when he found there were things over which he had no control, crooked things that could not be made straight, he cried out in vexation: "All is but vanity and a striving after wind. For in much wisdom is much grief; and he that increaseth knowledge increaseth sorrow."

The Quest of Pleasure 

Turning from wisdom to pleasure Koheleth sought satisfaction. He built a place, furnished it with all the luxuries that wealth could buy, and lived in ease and contentment. But there he found no lasting satisfaction and he concluded:

"I hated life because all was emptiness and a feeding upon wind."

The Quest of Riches 

The writer decided that wealth earned by honest labor should yield happiness. But he found that this is not the case because wealth brings responsibilities and worries. His troubles are multiplied now so he said: "Better a handful with quietness, than both hands full with trouble and vexation of spirit." 

The Quest of Fame 

From all his experiences in life Koheleth examined the benefits that come from fame. He decided that good and evil come alike to all, the wise and the foolish. Man cannot see his fate so why seek fame or wisdom. Death alone is sure for all men, therefore it is wisdom to live joyfully to eat, drink, and enjoy life as it comes.

Righteousness Is Not In Vain In the Earth 

In his quest for the answer to his questions Koheleth enjoyed intellectual power, luxury, and wealth, he suffered trials, disappointments, and vexations, he found the limitations of knowledge and the understanding of God. But in all his experiences he never questioned the value of righteousness. He says: "I know that it shall be well with them that fear God." 

Throughout the essays of Koheleth we hear two voices, for the writer as he analyses his experiences in the quest of happiness in the realms of wisdom, pleasure, wealth, and fame, lets us know his thoughts. The pessimist, looking upon the disappointments of life, says: "Vanity of vanities, all is vanity."

But as the essayist looks upon the goodness of life, the creations of God and the works of man during the centuries as he made the earth his home, he gave to all mankind one of the greatest laws of successful living:

"Whatever thy hand findeth to do, do it with all thy might."

Then it would seem that the spirit of goodness became the conqueror for the closing words of Koheleth are among the greatest in the Old Testament: "Remember now thy Creator in the days of thy youth. "Fear God and keep His commandments for this is the whole duty of man."

THE SONG OF SONGS—Songs of Solomon The Song of Songs, called the Songs of Solomon, is a collection of songs of rare beauty singing the praise of the beauty and sacredness of marriage. 

To the people of Abraham marriage was a divine institution, that racial purity and spiritual integrity might be maintained among men. It is assumed, in the absence of any knowledge of the author, that the songs were written to be used as part of the festival of marriage of the Hebrews.

The story of the cycle of songs is very simple. 

Solomon saw and loved a beautiful maiden. He sought to dazzle her with the splendor of his palace. The maiden already in love with a simple shepherd of the hills finally repulsed the king and returned to her humble lover.

In the songs are exquisite pictures: brooks, hills, vineyards, and meadows, full of the color and music of nature. But the choicest lives express the theme of the collection of songs: "Set a seal upon thine heart, As a seal upon thine arm: 

For love is strong as death. "Many waters cannot quench love, Neither can floods drown it:

If a man would give all the substance of his house for love. 

It would utterly be condemned."

A beautiful interpretation has been placed upon the Song of Songs by those who seek to find a spiritual message through an understanding of the literary expression. The relation between God and His people is often compared to the relation of husband and wife, the comparison used by the prophet Hosea.

Beyond the Old Testament 

THE Bible, the record of an inspired race has for its message God's love for man, His offspring. The ever recurrent refrain, "Thine is the kingdom and the power and the glory," is like a golden thread running through every book bringing it together, from Genesis to Revelation.

The Old Testament record is a testimony of God's dealings with the Hebrews and their struggle for racial and spiritual integrity to merit their heritage as the chosen people of God. 

The organization of the sacred records of the Hebrews to form the Old Testament was an outgrowth of the organization of the religion of the Jews, Judaism. 

At first only the historical chronicles and the books of the Law of God were included in the canon of scripture. 

Later the books of the prophets were added and later still the books of wisdom. 

The closing of the Old Testament record with the Book of Malachi is not the closing of the sacred history of Israel. From that time until the birth of Christ is one of the most significant in the history of Israel and the world, the conflict between the nations of the ancient world and the nations of a new world, Europe. Under the influence of Greek and Roman culture many things happened to the Jews, storm tossed and beaten upon

new seas of adversity they continued to survive, as we see them in Palestine during the life of Jesus, the Christ. 

God had not left His children alone. Inspired leaders kept alive His truth among them. Men of courage gave their lives that the worship of God might survive against the paganism of a new world. Men of wisdom admonished their people to hold fast to the truths of life as they had been revealed to them by God's servants the prophets. The choicest records made during this period have been gathered together in a collection known as the Apocrypha. 

As we read the pages of the literature of the intertestament period, we know more of God's continuing love for His children, through His spirit until the time should be at hand for the coming of His Son, Jesus, the Christ, to bring to mankind a new testimony of 

His everlasting love.

THE CONTINUOUS STORY OF ISRAEL 

The time was ready at last for the third world empire to arise as foretold by the prophet Daniel. Babylon had fallen before the power of the great Persian conqueror. Now a new world conqueror had arisen, Alexander of Greece.

During the period of the decline of the kingdoms of Israel and Judah much had been happening in the Mediterranean world, two great races were growing up, the Greeks and the Romans.

The Greeks had developed into a people loving art and beauty. Throughout their little peninsular home the Greeks had built beautiful temples to their gods, theatres for their sacred festivals, and arenas or stadiums for their great athletic contests. To the Greeks the greatest thing in life was perfection, perfection of body and mind. While the Jews were in exile in Babylon, the Greeks were worshiping their gods and god-

desses in the beautiful temple of Athens, the Parthenon; and the young men of Greece were learning the wisdom of their great philosophers, for the Greeks had no religion, only philosophy. While the wisdom of the 

Hebrew was to "Know God," that of the Greek was "Know Thyself;" to the Hebrew, God was the measure of all things; but to the Greek, man was the measure of all things.

When Alexander of Macedon became the King of Greece he set out to conquer the world. Egypt, Palestine, Persia, all fell before his power, the nations of the ancient world yielded to a new and younger world, Europe. But what was more, Alexander determined that the conquered countries should have the benefits of the things which the Greeks had learned, philosophy, knowledge, and art. At the mouth of the 

Nile in Egypt he built a great city, Alexandria, and established a library of Greek literature there. Soon the Greek statues were seen in all the cities of Palestine. 

When Alexander's armies came near to Jerusalem, God warned Jaddua, the High Priest, not to oppose the invader as the Persians and Phoenicians had done but to open the gates of the city and go to meet him. 

When the Greek saw the high priest of God in his sacred robes leading a procession to greet him, he was greatly amazed. He honored the servant of God by offering sacrifices in the Temple and gave the Jews many privileges.

Alexander was not a tyrant; he took no captives and forced no Greek customs upon the people of the conquered lands. Now the Promised Land was divided into five small provinces: Judea, Samaria, Galilee, Trachonites, and Peræa, Gradually, however, the ways of the Greeks began to have a great influence upon the life of the Jews, especially the Greek plans of community life and government. But after the death of 

Alexander those who followed him were determined

that the conquered peoples adopt the pattern of Greek civilization.

When Antiochus Epiphanes, a Syrian tyrant, became the ruler of Palestine he attempted to force the worship of the Greeks upon the Jews. He built altars to Zeus in Jerusalem and abolished the worship of the Temple and the observance of the Sabbath day. Then he removed the high priest from the Temple and placed Jason, a Jew, who had turned Greek, over to the new pattern of worship.

Jason ordered all Jewish festivals and religious customs to be abolished. A gymnasium was built under the Temple itself. All Jewish youths were ordered to wear a hat after the fashion of the Greeks. Calling for all the books of the Law at the order of Antiochus, Jason proceeded to burn them and issued a decree that those who obeyed the Law should be killed. 

Many of the faithful Jews fled from the cities and went to live in the hills and in the wilderness away from the Greeks. Others burnt incense at the doors of their houses to deceive the Greeks. But there were many who adopted the new laws and customs without resistance. 

It happened that the soldiers of Antiochus came to Modin to force the people to sacrifice to the Greek gods. In that place there dwelt a faithful priest, Mattathias, and his five sons Judas Maccabæus, Jonathan,

Simon, Eliezer, and Johanan. The servants of the 

Greek ruler said to Mattathias: "Thou art a ruler and a great man in this city. 

Now, therefore, come and sacrifice upon the altars of the Greeks then thou and thy house shall be numbered among the friends of Antiochus and he shall bestow great favors upon thee and all thy house."

But Mattathias answered, in a voice all assembled could hear: "If all the nations that are in the King's dominions hearken unto him, to fall away from the worship of

their fathers, yet will I and my sons walk in the covenant of our father."

A Jew came forward at the command of a Greek officer to offer sacrifice upon the altar to the pagan god, Jeus, a Greek officer. Mattathias took his sword and slew the Jew and the Greek and then destroyed the altar crying: me." "Whosoever is zealous of the Law, let him follow Then the faithful priest and his five sons fled to the wilderness and many others soon joined him. The 

Greek soldiers followed the band of rebels until the Sabbath to attack them. When the Jews saw their enemies coming they said to one another: "Let us die rather than to break the law of the Sabbath."

As a result of their loyalty to the Law many of the followers of Mattathias were killed. But this did not daunt the leaders, on they went now openly fighting, destroying altars, and putting to death the disloyal Jews they found worshiping the Greek gods. The rebellion spread in spite of the disaster which seemed to shadow its supporters who were greatly outnumbered. 

On his deathbed Mattathias called his five sons to him and gave them his cause to follow; saying to them: "My sons give your lives to the covenants of your fathers. Among you all hath Judas been the strongest. 

Let him be the captain and fight the battle of God's people."

Judas, a man of faith and power, was a genius in battle. He carried the torch of freedom for the Jews. 

His followers called him "Maccabæus, the Hammerer,' because he harried the enemy with such great force. Under this brave captain many more loyal Jews joined the banner of freedom.

Four great armies were sent against Judas. The small band of faithful Jews said to their captain:

"How shall we be able being so few to fight against such a great number." 

And Judas answered them: "Saith the God of

Heaven, the victory of battle standeth not in the multitude of an host, but strength cometh from heaven. Be not afraid! Arm yourselves and be valiant! Remember how our fathers were delivered from the hosts of Pharaoh!" 

Stationing his men in passes in the hills Judas fought until the soldiers of the enemy were destroyed or had fled. Marching to Jerusalem they came to the 

Temple. It was a sad picture, the sacred altars were broken down and weeds and brambles were growing among its stately pillars!

Then Judas ordered the sanctuary to be cleansed, a new altar erected, and priests chosen to conduct the worship after the manner established by Nehemiah. For a time there was peace and freedom to worship for the 

Jews in Judea.

After the death of Antiochus, however, another great army was sent against Judas. Many were slain, among them the brave captain of the Jews. Then Jonathan and Simon rallied the armies of Judas and continued to fight until once more the Greeks were driven out of Judea. But the triumph was not to last long. 

Once more political independence became an evil instead of a blessing.

Upon the death of Simon, John Hyrcanus became the leader of the Jews, first as the High-Priest and later as King. The ideal of religious freedom was forgotten and this new ruler began to plan to build a great empire. 

He made war upon the people of Samaria and the Edomites. He set up a court and palace much as Solomon had done before him. The ambition of the grandson of the faithful priest who gave his life for the right to worship God was to enslave others for his own glory. 

The loyal Jews who understood the history of

Israel and knew that it was not their destiny to be a kingdom among the political rulers of the earth said to one another: "The business of the leader of the Jews is to protect the integrity of our ancient national faith. Why should a king of the Jews be concerned with the magnificence of a great court and foreign kings?"

These loyal Jews became known as Pharisees, they were the spiritual leaders of the Jews. But there were those among the Jews at this time who were enthusiastic about the new king and his interest in building up a royal court and establishing relations with the great new nations. This group became known as Sadducees. 

As was very natural there began to exist much bitterness between the Pharisees and the Sadducees. 

Now a new and terrible tragedy was to come to the Jews, the power of Rome. In a short period of time the Romans had become the military masters of Europe. 

Now the Roman general, Pompey, sought the conquest of Syria and Egypt also.

Meanwhile the grandsons of Hyrcanus were quarreling over the throne of Judea. The bitterness between the Pharisees and the Sadducees was creating much strife also. Some of the people desiring to end the sad condition in the land sent word to Pompey at Damascus asking him to come to the aid of Judea and end the civil strife therein.

Pompey, the Roman, came as a conqueror, not as a peacemaker. Thousands of Jews were killed, the walls of the city were broken down, and an army of soldiers placed on guard over the city. But the greatest humiliator of all came when Antipater, an Idumean of the tribe of Esau, was made the governor of Judea, Samaria, and Galilee.

The power of Antipater did not last long, for he was treacherously poisoned. His son Herod, one of the wickedest and yet one of the shrewdest of men, fled

for his life. In a few years, however, he returned to the country and with the aid of the Romans he made himself the King of Judea. 

Thinking to make himself in very deed the King of the Jews, Herod took for his wife a maiden of the family of Judas, the Hammerer. But this marriage with 

Mariamne, the Jewess, did not make the people respect him, it could not make them forget his lineage of the house of Esau.

Herod, like Solomon, thought to make Judea great by magnificence. He built a theatre and a stadium at Jerusalem, a palace at Jericho, and a new city and seaport called Cæsarea. But his greatest achievement was the new temple he built at Jerusalem. 

It was a beautiful structure with pillars of marble and roofed with the cedars of Lebanon. But the Jews had no part in the building of this temple, it was built out of vanity, not out of the love of God. Although the temple of Herod was built by the wealth of the Jews they had no such love for it as they had for the temple of Solomon.

Hatred and jealousy surrounded Herod, the Jews hated him and the Roman politicians envied him. As the years passed he became suspicious and cruel, people who served him were ordered executed upon the slightest pretext. Plots multiplied on all sides to rid the land of Herod. The Maccabees were slain, then Mariamne, and then the sons of Mariamne, all because Herod hated and feared the reverence of the Jews for the family of 

Judas, the Hammerer.

When Herod died his kingdom was divided among his sons. But later the son becoming the ruler over 

Judea was replaced by the Romans because of his inability and wickedness. The rule of the Roman governor was efficient and just. But the Jews had lost their independence. Never before in their history were they

as conscious of defeat as they were under the power of Rome.

However, now more brightly than ever there burned in their hearts the promise of Isaiah, of the Messiah who should come as their Savior and as their King.

GOD'S CONTINUAL REVELATION 

The close of the Old Testament record and the beginning of the New Testament record was a tragic period in the history of the Jews. But it was not without its great leaders, men of God and men of courage who sought to keep God's word alive in the hearts and lives of the people. However, the sacred and literary records of this period are not included in the Bible. 

Therefore, it is necessary to go to those records which have survived and known as the Apocrypha if we would learn of the spiritual life of the Jews of this period. 

Among these books the best known and the most inspiring are the Books of the Maccabees, the Books of Esdras, the Book of Tobit, the Book of Judith, and the Book of Ecclesiasticus. 

THE BOOKS OF THE MACCABEES 

These books comprise a very valuable historical record of the conflict of the Hebrew and Greek ways of life and thought. We have followed the courageous struggle of the Maccabees in the continuous story of Israel. It is a trustworthy historical record of the rebellion of the Jews against the domination of the Greeks over the worship of Judaism. 

Mattathias, the leader of the Maccabees, was a loyal and courageous Jew who defied the wicked designs of Antiochus Epiphanes to substitute the worship of the Greek gods for Hebrew worship. His five sons continued to fight courageously with great valor. But

the greatest of all the Maccabees was Judas, known as the "Hammerer.' 

The value of this record is not so much in the spiritual message of the books but in the historical details it provides for the understanding of the intertestament period of Jewish history. 

THE BOOK OF JUDITH 

The story of Judith in many ways is a companion of the story of Esther, it is a story of the heroism of a faithful Jewish maiden and her loyalty to the ideals of her people.

Holofernes, leading a large army of Assyrians, was laying waste to the lands of Syria. Many of the cities had surrendered rather than be destroyed. But the people of Bethulia resisted the invader. 

At last the suffering became so great in the city that the people demanded the leaders to surrender. But 

Judith, a loyal Jewess went before them and begged of them not to surrender, but to trust in the Lord. Then preparing herself with great care she took her maid and went to the camp of Assyrians.

When Judith came before Holofernes to plead for her people he was delighted with her great beauty and intelligence. For three days the woman remained in the camp of the enemy awaiting the decision of the captain. At last Judith was invited to dine with Holofernes in his tent. Later the Assyrian became drunken with much wine, and while he slept in drunkenness, Judith took his sword and severed his head from his body. Hastening from the camp Judith and her maid came to the gates of Bethulia bringing the people the word that they were free.

There was great rejoicing in the city of the Jews, the scene was never to be forgotten. The men of Israel came before Judith in their armor with words of praise

for her courage, but the women of Israel came and bowed in reverence to her and called her blessed. 

Throughout the book the courage, loyalty, and purity of Judith are extolled. Her loyalty to the laws of Israel is related in great detail, especially to the laws regarding food, for Judith and her maid carried food that was clean with them to the camp of the Assyrians that they might not break the law of Israel. 

As Esther saved her people by winning the favor of the Persian ruler, Judith served hers by destroying Holofernes. Judith like Esther became a great Jewish heroine, a pattern of national and religious loyalty to all Hebrew maidens.

THE BOOK OF TOBIT 

The Book of Tobit is an account of the experiences of Tobias, a Godfearing man, as he travels in foreign lands. The discussion of the paganism of the 

Egyptians receives much attention.

There is no doubt that the influence of Egyptian customs and thought had a great influence upon the Jews. During the period before the Exile many of the people of the Southern Kingdom took refuge in Egypt. 

Also many of those who were left in Judah after the captives had been taken to Babylon fled to Egypt. Then too there were many Hebrew scholars in the library of Alexandria at the time of the making of the Septuagint, the Greek translation of the Old Testament. 

The Egyptians had become masters of the art of magic. Many strange superstitions and dangerous practices had become part of their everyday lives because of their belief in this evil. The writer of the Book of 

Tobit had seen the wickedness of this influence and feared that the Jews living among the Egyptians might forsake the righteous ways of the worship of Jehovah. 

In extoling the truths of Judaism Tobias reveals the

power of goodness over the power of evil, the blessings. that are everlasting over the material gains of power and wealth.

There is much sound advice for living wisely to be found in this book written after the pattern of the 

Book of Proverbs. 

"It is good to keep close the secret of a king, but it is honorable to reveal the works of God. "Do that which is good, and no evil shall touch you."

THE BOOK OF ECCLESIASTICUS 

The Book of Ecclesiasticus is the work of a traveler and a scholar who lived in Jerusalem about two centuries before the time of Christ.

The work is a combination of essays and maxims covering the problems of the Book of Proverbs and the Book of Ecclesiastes, the wisdom of everyday living. 

Throughout the book there is a firm belief in God, the destiny of Israel, and the triumph of good over evil in the world. It is the work of a scholarly mind looking at life to find its values. 

Some of the most sincere expressions are found in the essays on Wisdom, Honor of Parents, Friendship, the Choice of Friends, the Discipline of the Mouth, and the Praise of Famous Men. 

Perhaps the most beautiful expression as well as the keenest insight is found in the essay on Friendship: "Sweet words will multiply a man's friends. 

If thou wouldst get a friend, get him by proving, and be not in haste to trust him. . . A faithful friend is a stronge defense; and he that hath found him hath found a treasure. There is nothing that can be taken in exchange for a faithful friend; and his excellency is beyond price. . . A faithful friend is the medicine of life; and they that fear the Lord shall find him. He that

feareth the Lord directeth his friendships aright, for as he is so is his neighbor also."

THE BOOKS OF ESDRAS 

The writer of the Books of Esdras is concerned with the answers to some of the questions that have puzzled the minds of men in all ages of the world. 

In the first book of Esdras we find King Darius searching to know the strongest thing in the world. At a great feast the King asks the question of his guests. 

There are many answers, the most significant being: 

Wine is strongest. The king is strongest. Truth is the strongest.

The King discusses the power of wine to strengthen the ties of pleasure and to weaken the powers of the individual. Its strength is to change the character, personality, and power of those who use it. It can even change the very heart of man until he is no longer human. No matter what a man's social position, wealth, knowledge, or integrity may be, wine has the power to level and to destroy. The strength of wine is great indeed. 

The second answer, "The king is strongest," is considered. The power of kings is very great. The lives and happiness of all under the rule of a king are in his hands. What can be greater than the power that can crush the life and spirit of man that God made in 

His own image, an eternal being! History has countless examples of such power.

Above all the answers to the question towers the third one chosen, "Truth is mighty above all things."

After discussing the values of truth this great ruler breaks forth into great eloquence and declares: "Wine is wicked, the king is wicked, the people are wicked, and wicked are their works and there is no truth in them; it liveth and conquereth for ever more.'

In the Second Book of Esdras the problem of sorrow is discussed, the sorrow that comes of the separation of loved ones by death. Here is a brave new note in Hebrew thought. Great mourning for the loss of loved ones is condemned, rather should there be rejoicing at the great gift of life and all that it brings to man. 

Therefore, life must be spent in creative action and not wasted or thrown away for, says the writer: "Now therefore keep thy sorrow to thyself, and bear with a good courage that which hath befallen thee. "For if thou shalt acknowledge the determination of God to be just thou shalt be united together again." 

The meaning of suffering and sorrow is not a problem of compensation alone, it is part of life; and the justice of God is not violated if one could see and understand all the things of life in their complete relationships. Only God has the knowledge of all things. 

Seeing all things from the beginning the Eternal God understands all. A thousand years in His sight are but a day. How then can man in a fleeting moment, in the life of an age understand all of its meaning? 

The message of Esdras on the value of things and on the understanding of God's purposes is an inspiration to men that they might obey the laws of God intelligently and not out of fear or in ignorance. 

THE MESSIANIC HOPE 

The centuries between the Testaments was a period of conflict for Judaism. Many fought as valiantly to overcome the influence of the culture of a new world as their fathers had done to destroy the influence of the paganism of the ancient world upon the people of Israel. 

It was the enduring hope of the coming of the Messiah as promised by the prophets of God that strengthened the faith and courage of the Jews during this period of great crises.

Isaiah had given to Israel a message of hope in the revelation of God's love of mankind, the coming of His

Son Jesus Christ to be the Messiah. The prophet Haggai had inspired his people to expect the Lord to come to His temple and establish His kingdom on earth. Zechariah had envisioned Israel restored with a king of the lineage of David ruling over them.

The people had prepared the Temple at Jerusalem for the coming of the Lord. But He had not come and it had been desecrated by the Greeks to their pagan gods. A new temple stood in the sacred place but it was the temple built for them by Herod. Yet they were not left hopeless, the promise of Isaiah had not yet been fulfilled.

Israel awaited the sign of the coming of the Messiah as Isaiah had foretold: "Therefore the Lord Himself shall give you a sign; behold a virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and shall call his name Immanuel."

While they still waited God's promises were being fulfilled, for to Joseph of Nazareth a messenger had been sent saying of Mary his espoused wife: "She shall bring forth a son and thou shalt call his name Jesus, for he shall save the people from their sins." "Now all this was done that it might be fulfilled which was spoken of the Lord by the prophet saying, 'Behold a virgin shall conceive and bring forth a son and they shall call his name Immanuel, which being interpreted is, 'God with us.' "' 

Thinking of God's promises and their fulfillment

Israel continued to look for a king as Isaiah had spoken of the child born of the virgin and his place in God's purposes as a ruler of mankind: "For unto us a child is born, unto us a son is given: and the government shall be upon his shoulder, and his

name shall be called Wonderful, Counselor, the Mighty 

God, the Everlasting Father, the Prince of Peace.

To the shepherds watching their flocks by night near Bethlehem the angel of the Lord appeared saying: "I bring you glad tidings of great joy which shall be to all people. For unto you is born this day in the city of David, a Savior, which is Christ the Lord."

A multitude of angels appeared in the heavens heralding the fulfillment of God's promises singing: "Glory to God in the highest and on earth peace and good will toward men.”

The Messianic promise being thus fulfilled, a new testimony of God was given to all mankind.

THE OLD TESTAMENT THROUGH THE AGES The Continuing Word of God 

THY word is a lamp unto my feet, a light unto my path."

David, the Psalmist of Israel, sang of the magnificence and goodness of God, the creator of the earth and man. As David lived in the solitude of the hills with his sheep, the mystery and majesty of God's world stirred his soul; as he mingled with the people of Israel he saw man as God's greatest creation, made little lower than the angels and destined for eternal happiness and progress. Out of the experiences of Israel and the spirit of God within him, David came to understand that only through loving obedience to God's laws could man achieve the destiny of God's eternal purposes: The words of David have served as a beacon light through the ages guiding mankind to learn to know their loving, eternal Father.

The spirit of God has guided the destiny of the world since its creation. When the earth was given to man for his home it was his to subdue and to rule for his happiness and progress. Throughout the ages the civilizations of the world have been of man's creation. 

Exploration and conquest, organization and leadership, science and invention have been combined to give man dominion over the world of nature. Change and decay may have removed the material evidence of the ages of the past, but always the ideals of yesterday are stamped

THE OLD TESTAMENT THROUGH THE AGES 

355 ineffaceably upon the works of tomorrow. Bit by bit we have been able to piece together the story of civilization. Thebes with its hundred gates, Babylon with its hanging gardens, and Nineveh with its mighty palaces are lost in the sands of the desert, forgotten empires of men who built with bronze and gold and human toil. 

The great civilizations of the ancient world the Egyptians, Babylonians, Persians, Greeks, and Romans, all passed away. On and on however, man has striven in order to make a world in which he could realize the greatest freedom and the highest development. The goodness of the world today is a living testimony of God's spirit keeping alive in the hearts of men the great purposes of life.

The Bible is the record of God speaking to mankind as He directed them in the early period of the world's history. As we read this sacred record we travel down the centuries with men, women and children who learned to know God and to understand the great truths of life. From time to time as a loving father the Creator spoke to them, directing their dwelling places, giving them counsel and commandments. Other times He spoke to them through His servants, His prophets and the Priesthood. Teachers arose among them, inspired and directed by the spirit of God teaching men to live wisely and well. With hearts turned to God's love the Hebrews made the sacred record of the Old Testament. The New 

Testament is the record of the life and teachings of Jesus the Christ. Guiding mankind to a knowledge of God the Bible has continued to speak through the ages to those who sought like David of old to know of life and its meaning.

As we read of the changing scenes in the history of Israel we come to understand the significance of the free agency of man, the gift of life was divine but its use by man was his responsibility. If we could have followed the children of Israel as Moses led them in the wilder- .

ness, or have stood with Deborah under the palm tree in Canaan giving counsel to her people, or have shared an evening meal with David and his loyal followers outlawed by the jealousy of a king, or have stood with Isaiah in the streets of Jerusalem as he saw the leaders from many lands in its streets and from many rooftops incense arising in the worship of pagan gods, then perchance the message of the Old Testament might become a little clearer. But the marvel of it would be that above the setting of time and place we would see men and women knowing love and hate, hope and fear, joy and grief, we would see them moved by the same appetites and passions, impulses and desires; and we would find them searching for the answers to the questions: What is life? What is its purpose? The great human document of the Bible speaks not for an age, but for all time, from the first earth-home to the present day men and women, parents and children, brothers and sisters, have lived together in happiness only as they have lived lovingly and righteously. 

God is ever speaking to mankind through the experiences of races and nations. He was with Israel in the wilderness and in exile just as He was with them in the days of Jerusalem's greatest glory. In the darkest days of the early history of Europe the light of truth was kept alive in the hearts of a few choice souls who walked alone guided by the words of the sacred records of the Bible in hidden places. In the Middle Ages many learned of God from the simple pictures of scenes from the Bible drawn by the monks for those who could not read. In the face of persecution and death men have never ceased to believe that the God of Israel lived and was guiding His work that it should not perish from the earth. When Isaiah saw the changing scenes of all the civilizations of the world, he was not dismayed. He saw the evils that come from ignorance and the destruction that results from wickedness but he knew that the light

THE OLD TESTAMENT THROUGH THE AGES 

357 of truth would remain, many times dimmed but never destroyed: "The grass withers, the flower fades, but the word of God abides forever."

The quest for a knowledge of God has not changed with the ages when man seeks to know God in his heart, the door is opened for the light to enter his mind and hear testimony of its reality. Elijah thought that 

God's word came to man as a voice of thunder, but he found out at Mount Sinai that the spirit of God was not limited to time and place, it came to man wherever he sought it as a still small voice. Many times as man has tried to solve the mysteries of nature, life may become more secure and complete. The light of truth has come to enlighten his mind and great good for all has resulted. 

Such is the story of freedom and progress throughout the pages of history. How strange to think the guidance of the divine Creator for His work be limited to one age of human history. As the poet Lowell writes: "God is not dumb that He should speak no more. 

If thou hast wanderings in the wilderness, And find'st not Sinai, 'tis thy soul is poor.

Slowly the Bible of the race is writ, And not on paper leaves, nor leaves of stone; 

Each age, each kindred adds a verse to it, Texts of despair and hope, of joy or moan.

While swings the sea, while mists the mountains shroud, While thunders' surges burst on cliff of cloud, Still at the prophets' feet the nations sit. 

That the word of God be kept alive in the earth many systems of religion have been organized using the Bible as a foundation. Religion based upon a belief in 

God has for its purpose the directing of men's lives that they might live righteously. From the Old Testament record we see that men thought of appeasing God by sacrifice. To render loving service to God was a difficult

lesson for Israel. It is still difficult for men to learn obedience to the great laws of life which mean self-control and sacrifice. Through religion men may be inspired to live in harmony with God's great purposes, but only as the love of God becomes in very deed a living thing in his life.

When Johannes Gutenburg beheld the work of a lifetime the first printed copy of the Bible he exclaimed: "It is a mighty torch of truth; soon it will light up the whole earth and ignorance and darkness will disappear." Since that time millions of Bibles have gone forth carrying the word of God to light the way of progress and guide men's feet in the way of happiness. Those who love the Bible the world over sing with thankful hearts the words of another psalmist inspired as David, the Psalmist of Israel: 

"O Word of God incarnate, O Wisdom from on high, O truth unchanged, unchanging, O Light of our dark sky; 

We praise thee for thy radiance, That from the hallowed page. 

A lantern to our footsteps, Shines on from age to age."
